## P: pop-up–PYO

### pop-up
 
 <i>adj.</i> [temporary and makeshift; as in pop-up mall, specific to post earthquake Christchurch, knowingly derivative ex UK]

>  The pop-up mall that I am not allowed to call pop-up in case Indignant of London asks His Bigwiggedness the lawyer to send me a letter, popped up. <i>(Nov 2, 2011,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

### popoiahakeke
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [Also eaten were] several <i>Fungi </i> [especially] ... four ... which grow on trees, - the Harori (<i> Agaricus adiposus </i>), the Hakeke, and the Popoiahakeke (</i> Polyporus <i> species, and the Pekepekekiore (<i> Hydnum clathroides </i>); also, three terrestrial ones, - the Paruwhatitiri <i>(Ileodictyon cibarium), the Pukurau </i> (Lycoperdon Fontainesii), ... <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/iii/32</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  [Also eaten were] several <i>Fungi </i> [especially] ... four ... which grow on trees, - the Harori (<i> Agaricus adiposus </i>), the Hakeke, and the Popoiahakeke (</i> Polyporus <i> species, and the Pekepekekiore (<i> Hydnum clathroides </i>); also, three terrestrial ones, - the Paruwhatitiri <i>(Ileodictyon cibarium), the Pukurau </i> (Lycoperdon Fontainesii), ... <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/iii/32</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

### popokatea
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Mohoua albicilla</i>, white head; sometimes misspelt popokatea the whitehead, a North Island insectivorous forest-dweller, similar to but smaller than its South Island cousin, the mohua or yellowhead. [Maori popokotea[o1-macron]]

>  This quartet of tieke, or saddlebacks, are vanguard to the main body of 20-odd popokatea, or whiteheads. <i>(Jan 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 55/16</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> 14. <i>Mohoua ochrocephala. G.R.Gray, </i>... Mohoua houa of the natives of Tasman Bay. <i>Quoy et Gaim. </i>Popokatea, natives of Cook's Straits. <i>Dieffenbach. </i> <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/189</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/189]

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> In the lower regions the forest was much enlivened by birds. I shot some parrots [fn Nestor meridionalis. Kaka of the natives.], pigeons [fn Columba spadicea, Lath. Kukupa.], and wattle-birds [fn Glaucopis cinerea, Gmel. Kokako.]. Among the branches of the trees hopped the neat fantail flycatcher [fn Muscicapa flabellifera, Gml. Piwakawaka.], and a social bird of a yellow colour, which has been depicted by Forster [fn Orthornyx heteroclytus, Lef. Popokatea.], and which had much the habits of the finch. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/29</i>)

>  I have seen the yellow-head, the popokotea or 'canary,' in the bush on Mount M'Kerrow, Wellington ... <i>(Jan 22, 1910,Evening Post,13</i>)

>  [The whitehead] is best known to bushmen, probably, as the bush canary, although it is not a canary. Its commonest name is popokatea. <i>(Dec 16, 1924,Otago Witness,6</i>)

>  [they] have recorded three sightings of the native, Popokatea, or Whitehead, in the Porirua Scenic Reserve - the first time the birds have been sighted in more the 100 years. <i>(Jan 19, 2005,Dominion Post,A4</i>)

>  I have paid special attention to the nests of three birds- the Native Canary (<i>Orthonyx ochrocephala </i>), the Robin (<i>Myiomoira albifrons </i>), and the Tomtit (<i>Myiomoira macropcephala </i>). ir Walter Buller says ... that the Blue Crow (<i>Glaucopis cinerea </i>) lays a single egg, and ... leaves the care of the chick to the to the Popokatea, or Canary (<i>Orthonyx albicilla </i>). <i>(1904,TrNZI,36/119</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> The <i>Certhidae</i>, or Creeper family of birds, possesses five species, none of which are very remarkable save the Pokokatea, a social bird not unlike the English finch in its habits. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/24</i>)

>  The Popokatea (<i>Mohoua albicilla</i>) is represented by another species (<i>M. ochrocephala</i>) differing in colour but so closely allied to it that the natives apply the same name to both; the Toutouwai (<i>Petroica longipes</i>) ... is represented by (<i>Petroica albifrons</i>) ... <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/iii/5</i>) [NOTES: (Buller Ornithology)]

>  Mohoua albicilla. Popokotea. Very common. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/160</i>) [NOTES: (Hutton Birds Great Barrier)]

>  <b>BULLER</b> 10. ORTHONYX OCHROCEPHALA. Gml. Canary. Yellow-head. Popokatea. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,8</i>)

>  <b>BUL.LER</b> ORTHONYX ALBICILLA {CLITONYX ALBICAPILLA}. (The WHITE-HEAD.) ... <i>Native names</i>. Popotea, Poupoutea, Popokotea, and Upokotea. <i>Ibid.</i> 103 {1888 2nd edn 1/56} ORTHONYX {CLITONYX} OCHROCEPHALA. (The YELLOW-HEAD.) ... <i>Native names</i>. The same [Popotea, Poupoutea, Popokotea, and Upokotea] as those applied to the preceding species [White-head]: `Canary' of the colonists. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,102,1888 2nd edn 1/53 ed.</i>)

>  19. <i>Orthonyx ochrocephala,</i> Gml. Canary, Popokatea. - Perhaps amongst the native avifauna this genus is one of the most puzzling to understand. <i>(Nov 1884,NZ Journal of Science,283</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  <i> popokatea</i>: whitehead, forest bird. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,175</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> WHITEHEAD <i>Mohoua albicilla Maori name</i>: Popokatea. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,209</i>)

>  <i>252. Mohoua albicilla</i> (Lesson) <i>Whitehead (Popokatea) </i>... North Island <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,67,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Whitehead Mohoua albicilla</i> (LESSON, 1830) ... OTHER NAME: <i>Popokatea</i>. <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,276</i>)

>  <b>WADDINGTON</b> Near [Rotorua], after a little climb, a small plateau was gained where the `porridge pot' was continually on the boil, the mud is relished by the natives, but its appearance was not tempting to us. <i>(1887,Notes of a Tour in the England of the Antipodes,31</i>)

>  ... porridge pots [frequently <i>passim</i>] <i>(1888,Geysers and Gazers,12</i>)

>  <i>(1862,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/782</i>)

>  He generally sees the whitehead, the Maori's popokotea, when he visits the bush behind Day's Bay. <i>(Jan 2, 1909,Evening Post,13</i>)

### poporo
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Popero, <i> Lindsay. Solanum aviculare.</i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> [<i> Solanum aviculare, Forst.] Poporo; Poroporo; Kohoho </i>. Also common in many parts of Australia ... The fruit is edible, and was made into jam by the early colonists. <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,482</i>)

### poporohe
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BULLER</b> ZOSTEROPS LATERALIS {CAERULESCENS}. (THE SILVER-EYE.) .<i>. Native names</i>. Tau-hou, {Whiorangi, Hiraka, Motingitingi,} Kanohi-mowhiti, Karu-patene, {Karu-ringi,} Karu-hiriwha, Poporohe, and Iringatau. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,[chck p. no. if in 1873],1888 2nd edn 1/77 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> The natives distinguish the bird as Tau-hou (which means stranger), or Kanohi-mowhiti (which may be interpreted as spectacle-eye or ring-eye). It is also called Poporoke [check = poporohe] and Iringatu, names suggested by its accidental or periodical occurrence. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/81</i>)

### popotea
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  In the centre [of the beautiful table top] is a group of birds, the tui and the yellow and white beaked popotea, and puriri, berries and flowers. <i>(Jan 23, 1889,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> ORTHONYX ALBICILLA {CLITONYX ALBICAPILLA}. (The WHITE-HEAD.) ... <i>Native names</i>. Popotea, Poupoutea, Popokotea, and Upokotea. <i>Ibid.</i> 103 ORTHONYX {CLITONYX} OCHROCEPHALA. (The YELLOW-HEAD.) ... <i>Native names.</i> The same [Popotea, Poupoutea, Popokotea, and Upokotea] as those applied to the preceding species [White-head]: `Canary' of the colonists. <i>Ibid.</i> 2/224 The Popo or Popotea (<i>Orthonyx albicilla</i>) is a little brown bird with a white head, which sings like a chaffinch, and principally lives about rata trees. We see them not infrequently. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,102,1888 2nd edn 1/53, 1/56 ed.</i>)

### popper
 
 <i>n.</i> a pneumatic tunnelling drill [ca1951 R.Gilberd (Northland) quarrymen's]

>  He considered that `poppers' (a small boring machine) should be abolished altogether as they caused dust. [in the coal mines]. <i>(Sep 15, 1911,Maoriland Worker,28/6/2</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> The air beat and shimmered with sound . . . [sic] the poppers, eating with their thin blue tongues into rock ... <i>Ibid.</i> 186 The air [in the hydro tunnel] was filled with a ceaseless roar of air-compressors, poppers, and the biggest air-picks they had ever seen, all new machinery ... As the men's hands steadied the poppers (the tongues of flame eating into rotten sandstone ...), you could watch their taut arms vibrate, their bodies tremble. <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,151</i>)

>  <b>McARA</b> It was not until considerably later that the `swing type' stoper with its pneumatic-feed-leg for drilling uppers, and known as the `popper', came into use. In it the drill was rotated in the hole by swinging the machine handle backwards and forwards. At first the miners did not like it, as they considered it `shook up' the roof too much, causing falls of ground, but it was light and easily transportable and was fast in good ground. It remained in use in the stopes for many years, until the much later introduction of the rotary stoper. At one stage the popper was given the rather undeserved name of the `widow-maker'. <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi,185</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> For the first time in my life I saw, and heard, pneumatic drills at work. They were referred to as `poppers'. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,85</i>)

>  The miners at Blackwater have refused to work the popper, and only surface hands are now at work. <i>(May 20, 1912,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  In theory the rock drill which figures so prominently in the dispute at Reefton is a Waugh, but in practice it is a "popper." It is worked by compressed air, and a hammer, stirking 500 blows a minute, pops like a Maxim gun on the quartz overhead. Hence the name "popper." <i>(May 27, 1912,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  Sir Arthur Guinness interviewed the Prime Minister this morning, and urged him to appoint a Royal Commission to enquire and report whether the "popper" drill could with safety, and especially in regard to the health of the miners, be worked by one man. <i>(Jul 22, 1912,Evening Post,7</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a small open boat with inboard, two-stroke engine with a characteristic `popping' sound [cf. dunger (Marlborough Sounds)ca1925] (See [cf. dunger](dou-dyn.md#dunger))

 <i>n.</i>

>  Gold Rush, 1868-1878' There, `poppet heads,' Big Pump, deep shaft;/ <i>(1939,Silhouettes of the Past,90</i>)

>  <b>MAY</b> POPPET HEADS: cross-pieces of timber above the mouth of a shaft on which rest the axles of the pulleys used for winding. At Ballarat and at Ross `Poppet Heads' covered the whole erection at the mouth of a deep shaft. <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,528,1962 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McARA</b> <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi (Glossary),329</i>)

### Poppy Day
 
 <i>n.</i> ?Brit Remembrance Day

### poppy show
 
 <i>n.</i> a girl's show of underwear [ca1950s (Kath Walls) ca 1930s DEH]

>  When I was a child, a well-used term for any female accidentally showing too much underwear was "poppy show" - just saying the two words ensured a rapid hitching of petticoat or smoothing of rumpled skirts. <i>(Jan 29, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),56</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> For example, when a girl's dress blows in the air, boys will claim they have seen a `poppy show'. This is a widespread term. <i>(1953,Unorganized Games,2/665</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Pin dips were also another popular amusement. `A pin or a bull or a button, To see a rary rary show show show!' (1875; Tahataki). A piece of glass backed by cardboard was covered with a piece of paper that could be flicked up to show the `rary rary show' or had a slit in it through which one could peep. One saw through the glass a variety of colors, pieces of colored paper, flower petals, pictures, and later pieces off cigarette boxes. Sometimes these had been arranged in some pattern, at other times they were kaleidoscopic only, and the perceiver had himself to add the configurations of intelligibility. The price for the view was always a pin `which we did not want anyhow but always accepted,' a relic from an age when such things were rare. Pin dips were known also as `poppy shows' (perhaps it was the similar flash of color that gave the same name - `the poppy show' - to the exciting experience of seeing a girl's dress blown upward by the wind; pins were not charged for flowers of this sort). In fact, the pin dip or the prick book may have developed from the poppy show. The poppy show was common before 1900 and the prick book after that date, a fact connected with the ever-increasing stock of picture cards and magazine pictures available to children. <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,223</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> The poppy shows with the Robertson tart in the pine plantation behind the hospital ... . <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,51</i>)

### poppy-chopping
 
 <i>n.</i> [cf. tall-poppy-chopping]

>  Unfair, you say? A bit of uncharitable stroppy-poppy-chopping? <i>(Apr 8, 2001,Sunday Star Times,F4</i>)

### popsicle
 
 <i>n.</i> body of a person who has died on a mountain

>  Because no one since Mark Inglis and Phil Doole had survived so many days at such high altitude, the crew believed they were probably looking for bodies, or "popsicles", as black-humoured mountaineers refer to fatalities. <i>(Feb 2006,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

### popular as a pork chop
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>pork chop</i>, phr. <i>like a pork chop in a synagogue</i> inappropriate or ill at ease; eg `That umpire's about as popular with the Kiwis as a pork chop in a synagogue.' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,87</i>)

### popular as shit
 
 <i>phr.</i> (coarse slang) [see also pie-picker]

>  <b>FAIRBURN</b> I'm going to be as popular as shit at a nightmen's picnic if it ever gets into print intact. <i>(Sep 11, 1935,Letters,97,1981 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>popular as shit at a nightmen's picnic</i> unpopular; A.R.D. Fairburn Letters, 1981 <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,87</i>)

### populationist
 
 <i>n.</i> (Maori) person supporting distribution of fishery assets on a population basis

>  He said it would result in "tribal infighting at its worst" as the populationists and coastliners kept squaring off. <i>(Sep 17, 2002,Dominion Post,A2</i>)

### pora
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a turnip cultivated by Maori. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  For vegetables there was plenty of pora, the Maori turnip, and puwha (rauriki) or sowthistle. <i>(1988,Tales of Pioneer Women,207</i>) [NOTES: Author writing of 1940]

>  <b>BEST</b> The other plant looked for by travellers along certain Maori routes in the interior of Otago was the pora. <i>(1925,Maori Agriculture,146</i>)

>  <b>BEST</b> Regarding the kumara and the pora; these were prized food plants grown by my ancestors in olden times .. They possessed the pora long before Europeans reached this land .. The pora mentioned by Tikao is evidently a turnip, and was probably obtained from early European voyagers at Queen Charlotte Sound. <i>(1925,Maori Agriculture,147</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> Maori cabbage

>  Pora is an introduced vegetable run wild. The word `pora' means a ship. The plant flourished up the rivers, and the first settlers called it `Maori cabbage'. The lists also call it Pora-kawa-kawa, but this is amalgamating two names, for the kawakawa is another vegetable run wild. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,65</i>) [NOTES: c1880 South Island Maori Lists `Wahi Mahika Kai' in 1945 Beattie Maori Place-names of Canterbury 65]

### porae
 
 <i>n.</i> an edible fish found mainly north of Cook Strait, the grey morwong of Southern Australian waters. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Por|ae, <i>s</i>. A fish so called. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,195</i>)

>  CHILODACTYLUS DOUGLASII. ... Native name - Porae. <i>(1875,TrNZI,7/244</i>) [NOTES: (Hector)]

>  <b>HAY</b> In the bays and tidal rivers are the mango or sharks - the most highly-prized food-fish - the tamure or schnapper, the whapuka or rock-cod, the kahawai or mackerel, the porahi or herring, the kanae or mullet, the patiki or sole, and many others. On the shores are oysters, mussels, cockles, mutton-fish, crabs, and other shell-fish in profuse abundance. In the fresh-water creeks are eels (tuna), lampreys (pipiharau), and whitebait (inanga). <i>Ibid.</i> 2/231 We prefer the substantial schnapper, the goodly whapuka or kanae, or the luscious poraki. [ceck transcr. if use:?= porahi = porae] <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/151</i>)

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> PORAE (<i>Chilodactylus Douglasii</i>). <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,80</i>)

>  Its nearest allies amongst New Zealand fish are <i>Cheilodactylus macropterus</i> (the tarakihi) and <i>Douglasi</i> (the porae). <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/6</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson)]

>  DACTYLOSPARUS [<i>Dactylopagrus</i> 1922 <i>ibid</i> V/92] DOUGLASII (Hector).<i> Porae</i>. Secured up to 40 fathoms among tarakihi. Uncommon at Russell, but more plentiful in Hauraki Gulf ... Distribution: Unknown south of Bay of Plenty. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/115</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Cheilodactylus douglasii</i> Hector. PORAE. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,38,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Porae <i>Nemadactylus douglasii</i> (Hector) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,407</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 407]

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Porae .. .. porae .. .. <i>Cheilodactylus douglasi</i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> PORAE [<i>Cheilodactylus douglasi</i> ] ... known as morwong in Australia. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,38</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> PORAE ... <i>Other names: Cheilodactylus douglasi</i> ; morwong (Australia). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,249</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Family CHEILODACTYLIDAE. The tarakihi,<i>Dactylopagrus macropterus</i> ... is commonest south of East Cape; the porae, <i>Cheilodactylus douglasii</i> .. is greenish-grey above and commonest north of Cook Strait; <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,222</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>TARAKIHI </i>(a) Scientific name: <i>Cheilodactylus macropterus.</i> (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Tarakihi. (c) Other common names: Tiki (Kaikoura); Porae (<i>Cheilodactylus douglasi</i> ) is normally lumped with Tarakihi and is known as Morwong in Australia and also as Sea Bream. (d) Principal localities where taken: [all except far south] <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,47</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> PORAE A big, green, delicious inhabitant of our warmer waters found in a variety of habitats from surf-beaches to rocky depths of up to 500 feet. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,181</i>)

>  <i>porae n</i>. NZ fish similar to tarakihi. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,585</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Porae </i>Nemadactylus douglasi <i>Also occurs off southern Australia, where it is known as the grey morwong. ... Most common along the north-east coast, straggling south to Cook Strait. </i> <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,103</i>)

>  [species name] porae .. [scientific name] <i>Nemadactylus douglasi</i> .. [other name(s)] morwong <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] porae 1568, 1601 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  <i>PORAE</i> (Morwong) <i>Nemadactylus douglasi</i> <i>Cheilodactylus douglasi</i> ... [242] In Australia this species is known as the morwong or douglas morwong ... <i>(1982,Collins Guide,241,1984 ed.</i>)

### porangi
 
 <i>n.</i> mad or foolish person [also porangi: madness a person who is mad or a fool] (See [also porangi: madness](../A#also-porangi:-madness))

>  Their reception has been variable; with the kingites, it has been very hearty; but with others they are looked upon as a lot of idle fools, "Nui Nui Mungarie and Porangi," who would do much better by staying at home, cultivating the soil, and looking after their stock, than wasting their time in gossiping expeditions over the country. <i>(Feb 12, 1863,Wellington Independent,2</i>)

>  The natives called him a "porangi," or talkat[i]ve babbler. <i>(Mar 13, 1879,Hawke's Bay Herald,2</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> `The combatants ... took especial pains to tell us that it was no fault of ours, but the porangi or `foolishness' of the Maori.'<i>Ibid.</i> vol. ii. p. 238. -->`Watanui said E Ahu was porangi, `a fool.'' <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,1/137</i>)

>  During the debate ... I frequently heard him called by leading men as Hori te Waru, Wiremu Tamihana etc. etc., `a porangi'. <i>(Jun 21, 1858,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/410,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (J. Morgan)]

>  <b>BAXTER</b> porangi <i>mad; madman</i> <i>(1982,Selected Poems,189</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> madness or foolishness (See [also porangi adjective](../A#also-porangi-adjective))

>  <b>Wakefield</b> `The combatants ... took especial pains to tell us that it was no fault of ours, but the porangi or `foolishness' of the Maori.'Ibid. vol. ii. p. 238. -->`Watanui said E Ahu was porangi, `a fool.'' <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,1/37</i>) [NOTES: recorded under porangi 'mad person' as well]

>  He now renounces his folly - the result, as he himself admits, of <i>porangi</i> or madness. <i>(Mar 30, 1866,Southland Times,3</i>)

>  <i>porangi n</i>. foolishness; silliness;-a. silly; crazy; cranky. [Maori] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,9</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>adj.</i> mad, insane; foolish, imbecilic [ca1900 Jane Morison, Marlborough (perhaps adopted from a stay in Taumaranui timber camps)]

>  <b>IHIMAERA & LONG</b> My Nanny, she used to be all right once. She never used to be porangi all the time. <i>(1982,Into the World of Light,233</i>)

>  Harry McIntosh,.. some time known to Wellingtonians as 'Porangi' has left Australia. <i>(May 18, 1912,NZ Free Lance,4</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> P|orangi, <i>a</i>. Hasty. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,195</i>)

>  DoC goes porangi [heading] <i>(Jul 2005,Deerfarmer,2</i>) [NOTES: publication <i>the deer farmer</i>, lower case]

>  We had words you didn't find in the dictionary but which we all used: "porangi", "whare", "taihoa". <i>(Jan 1992,North & South,110</i>)

>  <i>porangi</i>, out of one's mind; mad: other meanings, but this is the one intended when used by the Pakeha, generally in an abusive way. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/156</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> ... porangi or foolish <i>(May 23, 1840,Diary</i>) [NOTES: ATLMS]

>  The bearer is porangi to get off and makes me almost raruraru by his impatience. <i>(1847,McLean Papers,5/13</i>) [NOTES: (J.F. Riemenschneider)]

>  <b>MOSER</b> The majority ... did not appear to pay much attention, [one observing] ... that the koroheka (old man) was `porangi' (foolish) ... <i>(1863,Mahoe Leaves,38</i>)

>  <b>GRACE</b> He said he was a prophet and that we must go back. At first I thought he was a Hauhau; but, when I came up to some other Maoris, they told me that he was porangi (mad). <i>(Dec 18, 1867,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1867 Dec 18 T.S.Grace Journal in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 167]

>  <b>DOMETT</b> `'Twas nothing - he was not to mind her - she / Was foolish - was `<i>porangi</i>- and would / be Better directly - and her tears she dried.' <i>(1872,Ranolf and Amohia,435</i>)

>  A man who told such marvellous stories that he was deemed to be porangi or insane <i>(1882,TrNZI,15/435</i>) [NOTES: R. C. Barstow]

>  <b>DOMETT</b> 'Twas nothing - he was not to mind her - she/ Was foolish - was `<i>porangi</i>' - and would be/ Better directly <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,2/259</i>)

>  All the witnesses on one side declare the testatrix was `porangi' (mad). The other side say she was a `tohunga' and a prophet and had gifts of healing ... <i>(Jun 18, 1891,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/583,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (C.W. Richmond)]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Porangi, adj.</i> Maori word for <i>sad, sorry,</i> or <i>sick; cranky. </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,365</i>)

>  He t'e plurry Porangi, I t'ink. <i>(1917,New Zealand At the Front,161</i>)

>  <b>EADDY</b> (Northland `pidgin') Te Maori, he can't get to sreep. Bruddy well porangi. <i>(1933,Half Down,34</i>)

>  out of one's mind; mad: other meanings, but this is the one intended when used by the Pakeha, generally in an abusive way. <i>(Jun 1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/146</i>) [NOTES: (J.C.Andersen)]

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> He's porangi, that fellow, sick in the head <i>(1960,Maori Girl,49</i>)

>  <b>McCALLUM</b> If you mention these things to a pakeha wahine she think you are porangi. <i>(1963,Half-Gallon Jar,62</i>)

>  Some people called my nanny crazy, porangi. <i>(1977,Listener Short Stories,91</i>) [NOTES: Ihimaera `The Child']

>  <b>BAKER</b> We slipped into the easy way of calling a person <i>porangi</i> instead of crazy or stupid. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,42</i>)

>  <i>porangi</i>; out of one's mind, mad: other meanings but this is the one intended when used by the Pakeha, generally in an abusive way. <i>(1945,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/146</i>) [NOTES: JC Andersen Maori Words]

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Porangi</i>. (extremely) eccentric, crazy; (very) stupid: New Zealand coll.: late C. 19-20. B., 1941. Adoption of Maori word. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1141</i>)

>  <i>porangi adj.</i> out of one's mind, mad [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),16</i>)

>  <i>porangi adj. N.Z. inf</i>. crazy; mad. [< Maori] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,883</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 585; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 376]]

>  The class was in fits. The teacher marched him off to the headmaster where Patu did his porangi act again. <i>(Nov 12, 1983,Dominion,6</i>)

>  <b>MITCALFE</b> He would ... give Winston a shout, telling him to shut up, don'y be so porangi. [fn] porangi: mad <i>(1985,Hey Hey Hey,149</i>)

>  <b>MITCALFE</b> He would ... give Winston a shout, telling him to shut up, don'y be so porangi. [fn] porangi: mad <i>(1985,Hey Hey Hey,149</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>porangi</i> mad, eccentric, stupid; eg `Hey, there's a joker in the public bar's gone completely porangi.' Maori adopted colloquialism late C19. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,87</i>)

>  <b>DAVIS</b> `Hey, you gone porangi?' asked Tuku, taking a firm grip of the business end of his suntan. <i>(1964,Watersiders,133</i>)

>  Rawiri Taonui, the expedition leader, says all along the way Maori families and marae gave the team help and hospitality ... but maybe wondered if they were porangi. <i>(1997,Mana,Autumn 12</i>)

>  <b>EDWARDS</b> I decide to tone down a bit because maybethey think I am porangi! <i>(1992,Mihipeka,185</i>)

### porch
 
 <i>n.</i> [Jim writes: The question is whether the <i>British</i> use of porch is also wider than the dictionaries suggest. The absence of the terms sunporch etc from these dictionaries may be significant. I have noticed two (apparently) wider uses in Humphrey Carpenter's biography of Tolkien p.246 - "Now they had time simply to sit and talk especially on summer evenings after supper, on a bench in the front pocrch of Sandfield Road." [This is interesting for the author's phrase <i>in the porch</i>, whereas we would normally say <i>on the porch</i>. Also in Ruth Rendell, <i>Harm Done</i> p. 213. A 1987 Midlands Property Supplement has porch only in the sense of entrance porch (and then seldom); one or two ads have the term <i>storm porch</i> which was new to me. (Although porch and verandah are partly interchangeable, I think that the covered entrance to a meeting house - whare tipuna, wharenui - is always in English "porch", although it extends for the full width of the building.)]

>  The front-porch flavour of his songs is preserved in Steve Garden's subtle productiuon. <i>(May 24, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),42</i>)

>  <b>IRELAND</b> The old men, smoking on their porches / squint like experts / down the barrel of the bay. <i>(1987,Kevin Ireland : Selected Poems,1</i>)

>  <b>ASHFORD</b> In houses with more than one verandah, the verandahs - now called porches - adopted specialised functions, with names which included entry porch, sun porch, sleeping porch and screened porch. <i>(1994,Bungalow in New Zealand, The,37</i>)

>  <b>SALMOND</b> A small tea table for the porch is indispensable, as are also easy chairs, a goodly supply of cushions, and if possible a good hammock. <i>(1986,Old New Zealand,211</i>) [NOTES: Quoted from 1926]

See <i>Toadfish</i> and <i>Globefish. </i> <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,306</i>)

### porina
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>COWLEY</b> Night insects orbited the bulb on the end of the working lead, midges and flying beetles, fat porina moths. <i>(1979,Growing Season, The,149</i>)

>  The true grass grub, <i>Odontria zealandica</i>, occurs in association with the subterranean grass caterpillar, <i>Oxycanus cervinata</i> (formerly called Porina) ... <i>(1943,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,23/307A</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> It is a fascinating concept to envisage the control of weeds and insects - grass grub, porina, ... <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,348,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <i>porina</i> or<i> porina grub </i>n -s [NL] : a subterranean caterpillar destructive to turf in New Zealand <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1767</i>)

>  While porina larvae are still too small to be seen easily by the naked eye at this stage, [he] believes they will become a problem later in the year <i>(1993,Straight Furrow,11</i>) [NOTES: Month missing]

>  The depredations of the subterranean grass caterpillar [<i>Oxycanus</i> sp.] in the rye-grass and clover pastures, particularly in ... the east coast of the South Island, have been recognized for some years as demanding control measures. The damage ... is probably only second in importance to that done by the true `grass grub,' <i>Odontria zealandica</i>. Until recently, practically all damage done to pasture was attributed to <i>Odontria</i>, [310] and only recently have farmers commenced to distinguish between the two pests, and to refer to<i> [Oxycanus]</i> as `grass caterpillar,' or`<i>Porina </i>caterpillar.' It is unfortunate that the generic name <i>Porina</i>, which was coming into general use, must now be changed to <i>Oxycanus</i>, since the name <i>Porina</i> is preoccupied as a molluscan genus. <i>(1941,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,22/309A-310A</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [Ma.] a pasture-damaging native subterranean caterpillar, Wiseana hepialidae or the moth of Wiseana hepialidae.

>  Hence the renowned vegetable caterpillar is nothing more than a larva of Porina enysii, which has been attacked and killed by the parasitic fungus known as Cordiceps robertsii. <i>(Dec 4, 1905,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  I heard someone say that they were beginning to see traces of the Porina cervinata grub in a grass paddock or two on the plain. <i>(Jun 10, 1908,Otago Witness,85</i>)

>  The writer .. does not accept Mr. Hudson's view that it is the larva of Porina mairi, Buller, which is a very rare moth, whereas the "vegetable caterpillar" is extemely common in certain localities. The true host has, he considers, not yet been identified. <i>(Jul 6, 1904,Otago Witness,87</i>)

>  .. but Mr. Philpott has at last found that in parts of the South Island, at all events, the larva of the butterfly Porina dinodes is selected by the fatal fungus as its "host." <i>(Oct 9, 1903,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  The Porina moth makes its first appearance in early October ... <i>(Apr 1940,NZ Journal of Agriculture,245</i>)

>  Porina.. and grass grub are a serious hindrance. <i>(Apr 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,357</i>)

>  Follow carefully advice of Research Centres and Department of Agriculture in combating Grass Grub and Porina Caterpillar ... <i>(Jan 15, 1953,NZ Journal of Agriculture,52</i>)

>  Porina caterpillars become more difficult to control as they mature. <i>(Feb 24, 1966,NZ Farmer,18</i>)

>  Porina strikes when pasture is most needed. The surest way of checking for porina is soil sampling to a full spade's depth at different sites in the pasture. <i>(Apr 1968,NZ Dairy Exporter,37</i>)

>  Major pests are grass grub, porina and Argentine stem weevil. [subterranean caterpillar] <i>(1995,Grasslands Range of Forage and Conservation Plants,46</i>)

>  "The so-called 'vegetable caterpillar' (infested with the sphoerid [sic] fungus) is, I think, very probably the lava [sic] of this insect (Porina mairi). .." <i>(Sep 16, 1902,Hawera & Normanby Star,4</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i>(Mar 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,272/2</i>)

>  <i>(Apr 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,357/1</i>)

>  p - caterpillar <i>(Apr 9, 1969,NZ News,7/1</i>)

### porina moth
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [porina](pop-pyo.md#porina))

>  <b>GILLESPIE</b> The ones attracting most attention were the owlet moths, the caterpillars of which destroyed the first pastures and cereal and root crops. There were also porina moths (<i>Oxycanus spp.</i>) whose caterpillars also destroyed pastures; the bag moth (<i>Oeceticus omnivorus</i>) which left cigar-shaped cocoons hanging from shrubs and tree [sic]; and the ghost-like white moth ... of the flax grub (<i>Orthoclydon praefectata</i>) which eats longitudinal slits on the surface of [flax] leaves ... <i>(1958,South Canterbury,10,1970 ed.</i>)

>  One of the most destructive pests in New Zealand is the grass-grub or brown beetle (<i>Odontria zealandica</i>). This insect, together with the caterpillar of the <i>Porina</i> moth, has been responsible for the destruction of ... crops. <i>(1943,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,23/322A</i>)

### poriro
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  PORIRO. - Of unknown origin, not in Debrett's. The English form is used as an expletive or term of endearment, as the occasion demands. <i>(1917,New Zealand At the Front,xiv</i>)

### Porirua
 
 <i>n.</i> site of a mental institution; hence the euphemisms, "gone to Porirua", "sent to Porirua" and "in Porirua".

>  In the late 1930s, when Ngaere and Rita began nursing at Porirua Mental Hospital (originally called Porirua Lunatic Asylum). <i>(Nov 8, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),66</i>)

>  Madness was there, sure, on every street. But mad people were "sent to Porirua". <i>(Dec 2, 1999,Dominion,8</i>)

>  Porirua - universally pronounced Porarua back then - meant only one thing: the loony bin, the nut-house, the place where mad people were locked away, the place where, if you weren't good, you might wind up yourself. They might even come and get you and drag you there screaming if you didn't look out. <i>(Dec 13, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32</i>)

>  <b>25</b> To generations of New Zealanders, Porirua meant - in the politically incorrect language of the time - the loony bin. <i>(Sep 8, 2001,Evening Post</i>)

### Porirua hour
 
 <i>n.</i> the period before the evening meal or bedtime when children or pets cause minor chaos [Common in 1960s and still occasionally heard]

### Poriruvian
 
 <i>n.</i> a resident of Porirua

>  I probably won't still be living in Porirua, but I'll still be a hard-out Poriruvian. <i>(Jan 31, 2004,Dominion Post,E2</i>)

### pork and cheese
 
 <i>n.</i> Portuguese [ORIGIN: rhyming slang]

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Pork and Beans.</i> The New Zealanders called them <i>Pork and Cheese</i>. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,650</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> Portuguese [rhyming slang]

>  <b>MILLER</b> The Portugese or `Pork and Cheese' camp [at Sling] <i>(1939,Camps, Tramps and Trenches,85</i>) [NOTES: [1917 May 26] 1939 Miller C.T. and Ts. 85]

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> [at <i>Pork and Beans</i>] The New Zealanders called them<i> Pork and Cheese.</i> <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang</i>)

### pork and potatoes
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The Natives subsist on the food we have brought them, pork and potatoes; and till we came, they wandered over a fair portion of the earth, without knowing the use of it. <i>(Jun 17, 1843,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  As there are plenty of settlements to be passed on the road, and no lack of pork and potates, I do not anticipate they will arrive at their destination before the end of the week. <i>(Sep 15, 1871,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  Once or twice they had taken up the pork and potatoes from the native ovens and had found them nearly raw. <i>(Oct 30, 1915,NZ Truth,12</i>)

>  Groceries: Am living on wild pork and potatoes, instead, thank God. <i>(Jul 9, 1921,Ashburton Guardian,4</i>)

>  On the same day (Friday) our forces, minus the Arawas, proceeded on to the next village, which they found deserted, destroyed the village, captured several pigs and took them to camp, and had a sumptuous repast of pork and potatoes. <i>(Mar 5, 1867,Hawke's Bay Herald,2</i>)

>  These men through bad food and bad water were all down at one time with fever; so sick were some of them that they could not dress themselves, aad [sic] all the food they could get was Maori pork and potatoes. <i>(Jan 1, 1890,Observer (Auckland),9</i>)

>  In those days GBP5 was the price of a bag of flour, which was then looked upon as a delicacy, pork and potatoes being the chief and often only food available. <i>(Oct 29, 1907,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Very light winds almost becalmed ... our fare [on the schooner] very ordinary tea pork and potatoes for breakfast, dinner and supper. <i>(Mar 20, 1839,Journal,2/75</i>)

>  <b>MATHEW</b> After taking some dinner - the usual New Zealand fare, pork and potatoes - we left on our return in Fairburn's boat, ... <i>(Feb 26, 1840,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Feb 26 F.Mathew Journal in 1940 Founding of NZ 63]

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> ... had he not brought his own it could only have been the hospitality of pork and potatoes that we could have offered, for as yet we were destitute of any more civilized supplies. <i>(1881,Poenamo,4/7/306</i>)

>  The everlasting pork and potatoes of the Maori are giving place to the fine, fat, full-flavoured beef and mutton of Old England. <i>(Apr 18, 1851,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  Ex barque "Eleanor" and other late arrivals. Five tons fresh colonial flour, White and brown sugar in mats, coffee, Black and green tea, tobacco in 200 lb. kegs, Blankets, loaf sugar, piece goods, New Zealand pork and potatoes ... <i>(Jun 29, 1842,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,2</i>)

>  One's appetite here .. is always like a raging lion, going about seeking what it may devour, and finding generally pork and potates. <i>(Oct 1, 1842,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,120</i>)

### pork and puha
 
 <i>phr.</i> perceived as a favourite food of Maori and used almost as a catchphrase, with variants, occas. jocular such as <i>Pakeha and puha</i> (See [puha](pop-pyo.md#puha))

>  Our pork and puha boil-up is almost in this category. <i>(Jun 8, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),23</i>)

>  The biggest challenge has been getting the taste of pork and puha and fried food out of his mouth. <i>(Oct 15, 2003,Dominion Post,A2</i>)

>  <b>BOSWELL</b> What do we eat [in the Northland bush in the late 19 century]? Is it the usual pigeons and potatoes? Is it the pickled pork and puha? Is it even the dumplings with golden syrup? <i>(1955,Dim Horizons,41,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> <i>(1960,Maori Girl,75</i>)

>  <b>FRANCIS</b> `When this big bloke, the young chap's stepfather, got up in court, you could almost<i> taste</i> the puha and pork.' <i>(1964,Johnny Rapana,167</i>)

>  <b>STEAD</b> But before the pork and puha, the mussels and pipi and kumara and pumpkin were out on plates, beer was being dispensed. <i>(1999,Talking about O'Dwyer,44</i>)

>  It's clumsy in places, preachy in others, a real stew of a book, mixing pork bones and puha with polemic and poetry... <i>(Nov 16, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),19</i>)

>  <b>Ihimaera</b> 'I'll bring our dinner in soon. It's pork bones and puha.' As it turned out dinner was actually a mussel bisque, followed by swordfish steaks... <i>(2000,Uncle's Story, The,16</i>)

>  The programmers at Ruia Mai are an eclectic bunch; you can catch sleek soul, rap, dance, pop, hip-hop and pork-and-puha Maori traditional, complete with strummy guitars and large groups of mommas banging out the choruses. <i>(Aug 7, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),64</i>)

>  Whether it's an Irish stew, a delicious French pot-au-feu, or an old-fashioned Kiwi pork and puha, a boil-up will satisfy winter appetites. <i>(Jun 8, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),38</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> There would be kiwi and pigeon, wild duck and trout, pork and puha, potatoes and kumara - a great feast, with plum pudding to follow. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,109</i>)

>  The boil-up is perhaps better known to Pakeha as pork bones and puha, though as Mr Tamihere recounts it, the meat could be anything from a leg of mutton to beef brisket (other Maori tell me sausages are also common) while the green vegetables might be cabbage or silverbeet, grown in their back garden. <i>(Jun 21, 2003,Dominion Post,F6</i>)

### pork station
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McNAB</b> In February, 1840, a Mr. Walter Brodie chartered the schooner <i>Hope</i>, and proceeded ... to Chatham Island to farm there two pork stations. <i>(1913,Old Whaling Days,301</i>)

### pork sword
 
 <i>n.</i> penis [1960 Ken Arvidson-heard at Horotiu Freezing Works (also <i>mutton-dirk</i>) ca1973, 1977-8 Peter Jensen] (See [cf. mutton dagger](mob-mys.md#mutton-dagger))

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>pork sword</i> penis; eg `Exercise the old pork sword last night, did you, Batesy?' NZA and US. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,87</i>)

### pork(y)
 
 <i>n.</i> (in Maori use) a pig

>  <b>ANDERSON & AITKEN</b> Terms such as `moo-cow' and `baa-lamb' were not encountered once [among Maori schoolchildren]. In the light of this, it is interesting to note the common form `pork' or `Porky' for `pig'. This occurs several times and in contexts which would not lead one to suspect baby talk of any kind; <i>We just about got some pork.</i> This usage refers not to the meat, but to the living animals, and against this usage the form `pig' occurs only once. <i>Ibid</i> 105 <i>Please, it's a porky. I'm a pork</i> ... When they use [`pig'] themselves it tends to be only in derogatorative [sic] contexts; Get out of my sight, you dirty pig! <i>(1963,Speech and Idiom of Maori Children,38,1965 ed.</i>)

### pork-trader
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WODZICKI</b> Quoting? Angus 1847 Savage Life and Scenes. At that time there were parties of men called `pork-traders'. They used to go up the rivers and procure pigs from the natives in order to sell pork, either to people in the pakeha settlements or to captains of whalers and trading vessels. <i>(1950,Introduced Mammals of NZ,228</i>)

### porker
 
 <i>n.</i> a (wild) pig; a Captain Cooker [the few early (pre-1900) recorded uses suggest that it is a re-anglicisation of Maori <i>poaka</i>, itself an anglicised version of <i>pork(er)</i>, rather than a direct use of OED `young hog fattened for pork', etc (1657) - as elsewhere in NZ English (cf <i>baconer</i>). Perhaps the first of the following uses are merely whimsical. (cf poaka, porky) [?1838 cit. really poaka]]

>  It is to be observed that 20 poles of good ground, well cultivated, will yield a greater quantity of vegetables than can be economically used by a labourer, his wife, and three or four children; in which case the overplus must either be sold or employed in fattening a porker and a baconer. <i>(Jun 3, 1843,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,260</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> The<i> puorka</i> (so pronounced for pork or pig) is especially well acclimated to the country; ... <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/311</i>)

>  During the day we killed many pigs and at length so satiated were our party [of Mauries] that they cruelly murdered the wretched porkers only taking their hearts and livers. <i>(Dec 13, 1840,Journal of Ensign Best,267,1966 ed.</i>)

>  <b>POWER</b> ... long-legged, bumptious, Irish porker. ... If a Maori porker objects to go into a canoe, he is not forthwith kicked and punched ... but he is coaxed, and patted, ... <i>(1849,Sketches in NZ,68</i>)

>  The Maori word <i>poaka</i>, for pig, was probably given them by the Tahitian interpreter, Tupaea, who was with Captain Cook when he gave the New-Zealanders their first pigs. Had the Englishmen given a word they would probably have said `pig,' not `porker,' and the Maoris would have called the animals<i> piki</i>. <i>(1889,TrNZI,21/448</i>) [NOTES: (Tregear)]

>  <b>VOGEL</b> The Maori Porker <i>(1909,Tragedy of Flirtation,42</i>)

>  <b>SMYTH</b> The dogs were holding a `porker' somewhere <i>(1929,Girl from Mason Creek, The,169</i>)

>  <b>EADDY</b> How far to go for the wild porker [to a Maori in Northland. His Maoris use <i>te porker</i> frequently in Maori English.] <i>(1933,Half Down,48</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> `Captain Cookers', with sharp snouts and formidable tusks, waged constant war with a group of dogs ... that rushed towards ... food thrown out on to the open spaces [of the pa]. Running from place to place, the half-grown porkers led a precarious life ... <i>(1951,Confessions,34</i>)

>  <b>BOSWELL</b> ... when the boys had leisure to go hunting, it would be a joint of tender young wild porker; <i>(1855,Dim Horizons,23</i>) [NOTES: [Northland, ? 1890s]]

>  Three little porkers ... <i>(1891,Three in a Coach,22</i>)

>  "'Twas in the good old days of yore / That Waimate porkers abounded; / But 'tis my belief, they've all come to grief, / And this is what makes me confounded ..." <i>(May 18, 1881,Hawera & Normanby Star,3</i>)

>  Pigs: 29 yarded, all sold under hammer at exceptionally good prices: 2 weaners 18s 3d, 2 porkers 25s ... <i>(Sep 18, 1918,Poverty Bay Herald,5</i>)

>  For Sale .. Porkers (delivered if required at Slaughter House). <i>(Mar 13, 1855,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

>  But Sonny had already gone, charging through the brush, the ti-tree slapping and stinging at his face, his hands on the hocks and his head between the haunches of a Captain Cooker, almost as long as he was, five-foot-five. He'll be a tough porker, this one, Sonny was thinking, but smoke him in manuka and he'll keep the hui going for a day or two. <i>(Dec 1958,Te Ao Hou,25: 49</i>)

>  <b>CARR</b> He had a hunk of wood in his hand and a pocket knife - the wood to hit it over the head and the pocket-knife to bleed it once it was down. Only it wasn't a 50 pound porker, but a very angry full-grown boar which at that moment left the dogs and came galloping up the hill. <i>(1986,Diary of a Pig Hunter,43</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a wild pig.

>  There were numbers of wild porkers, all young and fat fern-rooters ... <i>(May 1922,Northlander,24</i>)

>  <b>TAIT</b> Wild pigs have been troublesome in the past, and will possibly need the attention of amateur hunters for many years, since the mountainous country close to the Urewera forests remains one of the porkers' best breeding grounds. <i>(1958,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol II,II 13</i>)

>  <b>DAVIS</b> "You need a pig dog for the porkers.." <i>(1962,Mutton on the Menu,145</i>)

### pornucopia
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Their hearts will go out to Alex when they read that Enrico, his computer cob, has become besotted with porn instead of healthy doses of traditional corn. And has Enrico added a new word to our language - pornucopia? <i>(Jul 19, 2000,Evening Post,4</i>)

### porohe
 
 <i>n.</i> the young eels or other fish

### porokaiwhiri
 
 <i>n.</i> pigeonwood, porokaiwhiri(a) <i>Hedycarya arborea </i> [aka poroporokaiwhiri]



 
 <i>n.</i> a tree, aka pigeonwood (<i>Hedycarya arborea</i>) [ORIGIN: Maori, aka porokaiwhiria, also archived ref to poporokaiwhiri]

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Porokaiwhiri, <i>Col[enso]. Hedycarya dentata. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> The spot fixed upon was .. under the shadow of a three-stemmed tawhero (<i>Weinmannia racemosa</i>), sheltered all round by close-growing porokaiwiria, torotoro, and other shrubby trees ... <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/12</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>H[edycarya] arborea, Forst.</i> .. A small tree 20-40 ft. high .. [600] <i>Porokaiwhiri</i>. <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,599</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Hedycarya arborea [(The Pigeon Wood)]</i> .. Maori name <i>Porokaiwhiria</i> or <i>Poporo-kaiwhiri.</i> <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,174,1940 p. 181 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Hedycarya arborea</i> Forst. .. Porokaiwhiri. <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/144</i>)

>  <i>porokaiwhiri,</i> a tree (Hedycarya arborea), pigeon-wood. <i>(Jun 1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/156</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i>(Oct 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,320/.3</i>)

>  <i>(Feb 1958,NZ Timber Journal,62/1</i>)

>  <i>porokaiwhiria n</i> -s [Maori <i>porokaiwhiri</i>] : a small or shrubby New Zealand tree (<i>Hedycarya arborea</i>) of the family Monimiaceae with opposite short-petioled leaves and inconspicuous flowers in axillary panicles followed by bright red drupes <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1767</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>HEDYCARYA ARBOREA, pigeonwood, porokaiwhiri</i>. A medium-sized tree, rather common in mixed forests except in eastern districts south of Banks Peninsula. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,36</i>)

>  Porokaiwhiri, or pigeonwood (hedycarya [sic] arborea), grows freely around Wellington and throughout most of New Zelaand with the exception of the lower east coast of the South Island. <i>(Feb 17, 2005,Dominion Post Indulgence,2</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Hedycarya arborea [(The Pigeon Wood)]</i> ... Maori name <i>Porokaiwhiria</i> or <i>Poporo-kaiwhiri. </i> <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,174,1949 p. 181 ed.</i>)

>  (from the <i>Australasian</i>) .. The yellow clusters of the spreading dark-leaved pura-pura .. the little fiery branches of the red porokaiwhiria; the rich luscious berries of the kotukutuku, or giant fucshia, whose flowers hang side by side with ripe fruit, and unopened buds. All these, and many another fruit of the woods, fell upon the warrior's path, and allayed their hunger. <i>(Apr 20, 1872,West Coast Times,2</i>)

### poroporo
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>ALLOM</b> The pura-pura <i>berry is another fruit much prized by the natives. ... The pura-pura</i> is beginning to be highly esteemed for preserving. <i>(1853,Letter,132</i>) [NOTES: 1853 Allom letter in 1853 Earp NZ 132]

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> To add to [the tui's] merits, he becomes such fine eating in the season of poroporo berries, that an alderman might quit turtle and dare the seas to eat a tui stew. <i>(1857,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/119</i>)

>  <b>LAMBERT</b> This was .... prepared with shark oil and the expressed juice of the <i>poroporo</i> shrub, a plant of the cape gooseberry species. <i>(1936,Pioneering Reminiscences of Old Wairoa,45</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> The interior [of skeleton of the ruined church] is filled with large poroporo trees ... <i>(Sep 3, 1853,Journal,8/118</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> ... I had to cut down a large and spreading <i>pura-pura</i> bush, that almost concealed the verandah ... <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/88</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Solanum aviculare</i> (also <i>S.nigrum</i>; also called bull-a-bull a) a fruit-bearing shrub of the nightshade family. b) its edible orange-yellow berry. BULL-A-BULL (etc.), =POTATO BERRY, the plant and its fruit [ORIGIN: Maori, [1919 Jul 29] Ver. (WB).= Vernacular names used by settlers supplied by W. Best, Otaki (And) <i>Solanum aviculare</i> burra burra]

>  <b>REEVES</b> Even in the North Island the average bushman will speak of the pukatea tree as "bucketeer", and not to call the poroporo shrub a "bull-a-bull" would be considered affectation. <i>(1898,Long White Cloud, The,404</i>)

>  ...I like the native poroporo. A lot of people see it as a weed - it does have a gothic weedy look - but the purple flowers are beautiful. <i>(Aug 24, 2003,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),19</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> ... bura bura ... beads of berries <i>(Oct 7, 1840,Diary</i>) [NOTES: ATLMS]

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Poraporo [sic] - berry, fruit <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/830</i>)

>  Poroporo (solanum aviculare) leaves were used by Mother Aubert to relieve "itch" and Maori also used them on bruises and skin infections, and used the soot from burned leaves for tattoo colouring. <i>(Aug 24, 2002,Dominion Post,F16</i>)

>  The tieke glides to a branch, then bounds through the poroporo shrubbery like a fast-forward version of its larger cousin, the kokako. <i>(Jan 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 55/23</i>)

>  <b>HUDSON</b> <i>Solanum aviculare</i> (Poro-poro, or Potato Plant). A shrub with very dark green pointed leaves, purple underneath, and bright purple flowers resembling those of the potato. <i>(1898,NZ Moths & Butterflies,140</i>)

>  Poroporo is a common, leafy soft-wooded shrub that grows throughout New Zealand. It is quickly growing colonising plant, reaching a height of three metres (ideal for native bush revegetation sites) and grows on the edge of coastal and lowland forests and along road edges and clearings. ... Reputedly having a mild acidic taste similar to the cape gooseberry, poroporo was cultivated and eaten by Maori and early European colonists who made jam from it. <i>(Oct 29, 2005,Dominion Post,E13</i>)

>  When transmitters attached to two kerer&#363; were tracked to a patch of poroporo, the remains of six more kerer&#363; were found. <i>(Jul 2004,Conservation Action,31</i>)

>  But there were sweetly innocent letters, too, from.. Kate of Christchurch, who wrote of the thrush that perched in the poroporo to inspect the ripening berries in late March... <i>(Dec 7, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

>  <b>Crowe</b> The fruits of the local poroporo taste like something between a sweet date and a tomato and were an important food of Maori... <i>(2009,Which Native Forest Plant?,57</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>SOLANUM LACINIATUM.</i> Poroporo (bullibul) is a soft-wooded shrub ... Two species are called by one Maori name. Two Latin names, <i>S. laciniatum</i> and <i>S. aviculare</i>, are both more than a hundred years old but it was only in 1954 that Baylis defined clearly the difference between the two species and then the two names came into general use. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,168</i>)

>  <i> poroporo </i>[s] <i>Solanum aviculare</i> Forst.f. <i>Solanum laciniatum </i>Ait. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,60</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Poroporo, n</i>. Maori name for the flowering shrub<i> Solanum aviculare</i>, Forst., called in Australia, <i>Kangaroo Apple</i>. Corrupted into <i>Bully-bul</i> (q.v.). <i>(1898,Austral English,365</i>)

>  Poraporo [sic] - berry, fruit <i>(Jul 29, 1844,21 Years with Boots and Pack,1844</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> I had to cut down a large and spreading pura-pura bush. <i>(1850,Savage Life,2/88</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Solanum <i>aviculare</i>, Forst. ... Nat. name, `Poroporo' in the northern, and `Kohoho' in the southern parts of the Islands, <i>Col. </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 182</i>)

>  <b>ALLOM</b> The pura-pura <i>berry is another fruit much prized by the natives. ... The pura-pura</i> is beginning to be highly esteemed for preserving. <i>(1853,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1853 Allom letter in 1853 Earp NZ 132]

>  They were lost to sight behind the pura puras - `Cassy' in a kit slung over Harry's shoulder. <i>(Mar 23, 1856,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/204</i>) [NOTES: (Emily Richmond)]

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> The poroporo, the nicest or least nasty of the wild fruits, is a sodden strawberry flavoured with apple-peel. <i>(1857,New Zealand (various authors/titles),136</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Poroporo, <i>Col[enso]. Solanum aviculare</i> and <i>nigrum. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)

>  As a cataplasm for ulcers they used the leaves of the kohoho or poroporo (<i>Solanum aviculare</i> ) - and for wounds ... they used ... also the hune, or pappus down of the large Bulrush, but merely as a protection against dust, etc. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/I/58/15</i>) [NOTES: Colenso(On the Botany of the North Island) in 1981 Brooker et al. NZ Medicinal Plants]

>  Poroporo. Solanum aviculare, Forst. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/iii/47</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

Also, the cooked leaves and herbaceous tops of [32] the Toi (<i>Barbarea Australis</i>), and of the Poroporo, or Raupeti (<i>Solanum nigrum</i>); and the baked inner stems and sago-like pith of the large black fern tree, Korau, or Mamaku (<i>Cyathea medullaris</i>). <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/iii/31</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  Soft aromatic <i>pepper-spikes</i> were seen;/ Potato-apples of the <i>Solanum aviculare</i> tall/ Rich-mellowing from their crude lip-burning green;/ <i>Ibid.</i> 2/337 Poro poro (potatoe, apples of) ..... <i>O</i>. Solaneae; <i>G</i>. Solanum; <i>S</i>. S. aviculare and S. nigrum. `Berry ovoid, edible, one to one and a half inches long; three-quarters of an inch thick. Sweet and rather richly flavoured when quite ripe. <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,2/109</i>)

>  <b>REEVES</b> [NZ Speech] .... and not to call the poro-poro shrub `bull a bull' would be considered affectation. <i>(1898,Long White Cloud, The,403</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Solanum aviculare (The Poro-poro)</i> ... The plant is often called by the settlers, the Bulli-bull. <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,366,1940 p. 389 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> PORO PORO (<i>Solanum aviculare</i> ), also called bullibull (a corruption of the Maori name), and potato berry. It is found commonly on the bush edge in lowland districts throughout both islands. It is a perennial leafy spreading bush 4 to 8 ft. high,. . . <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,152,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSEN</b> <i>poroporo</i>, a plant (Solanum aviculare) with beautiful large blue flowers and large orange-coloured berry eaten by boys and birds: often corrupted to bullybul. S. nigrum is raupeti. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/145</i>)

>  <b>LYON</b> Native fruits were few; the purapura, tawha and karaka are the only ones that occur to me. <i>(1952,Faring South,130</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>Solanum ... aviculare</i> ... PORO-PORO (darkened). <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,57</i>)

>  <i>poroporo n</i>. New Zealand flowering shrub, with edible plum-like fruit. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),623</i>)

>  <i>poroporo ... n</i>. N.Z. flowering shrub, with edible plum- like fruit. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),16</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> POROPORO (<i>Solanum aviculare</i> and <i>S. laciniatum</i>) ... a native consisting of two similar species. ... <i>(1981,Weeds of Roadsides,113</i>)

>  <i>poroporo n.</i> NZ flowering shrub with edible fruit. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,585</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <i>Solanum aviculare</i> var. <i>albiflora</i> <i>(1920,TrNZI,52</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [Ma.] a native plant, Solanum aviculare, cropped commercially in the 1970s and 1980s for its solasodine content.

>  Succesful establishment and management of the native poroporo (Solanum laciniatum and Solanum aviculare) in cultivated blocks may produce exportable quantities of solasodine, an alternative to present steroid raw materials. Poroporo is regarded as a weed in many parts of New Zealand. <i>(Jul 1971,NZ Journal of Agriculture,15</i>)

>  In two year's time (by 1981) Solanum..expects to have 1000 hectares of poroporo (Solanum aviculare) under cultivation. ibid. 34 The company spent more than $1 million developing an extraction process from a selected strain of poroporo ... <i>(Mar 1979,NZ Journal of Agriculture,33</i>)

>  <b>HINDMARSH</b> A little poroporo and second growth had made it a dark private place ... <i>(1997,Come Be a Pioneer,94</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (mistakenly, ?from consonantal similarity); piripiri

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> PIRI PIRI (<i>Aceara ... sanguisorbae</i>). This name is commonly corrupted into [called] bidi bid. In the North Island the plant is called hutiwai or utiwai and by mistake poroporo. It is a native species well known to all New Zealanders, and is a weed chiefly on land in process of being brought into occupation. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,99,1967 ed.</i>)

### poroporoaki
 
 <i>n.</i> (in Maori oratory) a panegyric or farewell, often to a deceased person; a funeral lament. [ORIGIN: Maori, also poroaki; also as verb]

>  "The question must be asked why, having been granted that respect on their arrival, they could not be afforded the dignity of a poroporoaki or farewell," she said in ruling that mental health worker Heather Burberry was dismissed unjustifiably. <i>(Jan 30, 2003,Dominion Post</i>)

>  So the conference ended as it began in the Maori protocol of poroporoaki with the three kaumatua who attended the whole symposium praising the organisers for their foresight... <i>(May 2, 2004,NZ Catholic,5</i>)

>  <b>EDMOND</b> He [a Maori friend] stepped forward and said a poroporoaki, a farewell to you [in your coffin] and then began to sing. <i>(1992,Autobiography of My Father,24</i>)

>  Ms Bullock was suspended in July after speaking to the media about an oral warning she was given for refusing to stand behind men at a poroporoaki in December. <i>(Oct 19, 2005,NZ Herald,A6</i>)

>  Your Editorial championing former probation officer Josie Bullock's wish to place her backside wherever she wished during a poroporoaki (Oct 12) has me puzzled. <i>(Oct 31, 2005,Dominion Post,B6</i>)

>  I would like to express my disappointment at your decision to follow the poroporoaki for my brother..with a letter.. suggesting that [he] had shown intolerance in supporting demands by student leaders that Paul Dunmore be brought to account for his "boycott Maori blessings" campaign. <i>(May 2001,AUS Bulletin,No. 49 : 11</i>)

>  How ridiculous that MP Turiana Turia believes in women being forced to sit behind men at powhiri and poroporoaki. <i>(Jul 6, 2005,NZ Herald,A18</i>)

>  Tonight is our poroporoaki to Auntie Puapua... We say goodbye to her, tomorrow...the four graves will become five. <i>(2005,Huia 6: Contemp. Maori Fiction,58</i>)

>  [She] complained to the media..because she was put in the degrading position of being asked to give up her front seat to a male at a poroporoaki, or farewell, for prisoners at a Probation Service office. <i>(Nov 12, 2006,Dominion Post,5</i>)

### porphyrio
 
 <i>n.</i> pukeko

>  Mr. Gould infers that [the Notornis] affects low and humid situations, marshes, the banks of rivers, and the coverts of dripping ferns, and, like the Porphyrio, doubtless enjoys the power of swimming but would seem, from the structure of its legs, to be more terrestrial in its habits than the members of that genus. <i>(Jul 8, 1908,Otago Witness,86</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> [The] native rat still lingers here and there [in the central North Island], although it has disappeared on the coast; the native dog has become a mongrel; ducks, teals, the red-billed porphyrio or pukeko, a large bittern, a tern, two kinds of gulls, cormorants ... are found here. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/362</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> `The crimson-billed porphyrio, that jerking struts/ Among the cool thick rushes.' <i>(1875,Ranolf and Amohia,213</i>)

>  <i>(1890,Victorian Statutes</i>) [NOTES: the Game Act' (Third Schedule)]

>  <b>DOMETT</b> With jetty black and violet blue,/ [274] Of the crimson-billed <i>porphyrio,/ </i> <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/273</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> Porphyrio, n. the Sultana-bird, or Sultana. The bird exists elsewhere. In Australia it is generally called the Swamp-Hen(q.v.). <i>(1898,Austral English,367</i>)

>  The takahe is closely allied to the pukeko (Porphyrio), and not far removed from the brown woodhen (Ocydromus), all these belonging to the family of Rails, which usually frequent more or less marshy ground, and in other countries are able to fly as well as other birds. <i>(Aug 25, 1898,Otago Witness,24</i>)

>  Scented dried skins of porphyrio, down from the albatross' bosom - / Never had Pua beheld gifts so bewildering to see. <i>(Dec 30, 1908,Otago Witness,81</i>)

>  <b>4</b> On rising in the morning we found the lake covered with waterfowl, among which were the beautiful porphyrio, ducks, and snipes, and also gulls, which feed upon a small fish that abound in the lake. Before our guides returned, I had shot a great many of the unweary pukeko, or porphyrio, which proved excellent game. <i>(Mar 30, 1844,Daily Southern Cross</i>)

>  At this place, along with the bones of the Moa and other extinct birds, were found those of dogs, seals, and many species of birds .. such as the .. porphyrio, and notably the notornis .. of which as yet only two living examples have been obtained. <i>(Sep 23, 1871,Otago Witness,2</i>)

### porpoise
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>HINTZ</b> At times one could fish the Whanganui mouth in slippers, standing dry shod on the beach and casting about twenty yards across the current, with fish splashing and porpoising close inshore. <i>(1964,Trout at Taupo,47</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [From the <i> Australian and New Zealand Gazette</i> 2 April] .. these remains were the right tarso-metatarsal bone of a member of the Penguin family .. and the left humerus of a small cetacean [sic], more nearly resembling that of a common porpoise than that of any other member of the order with which the author had been able to compare it. <i>(Jul 9, 1859,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,2</i>)

>  They returned with full loads in the afternoon, one of them bringing in a porpoise, which, with a good sized cod in its mouth, was placed in the head sheets, and did duty as an original description of figure-head. <i>(Feb 1, 1868,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Upokohue,</i> n. <i>Globicephalus melas, blackfish</i>, and <i>Cephalorhynchus hectori, porpoise. </i> <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,549</i>)

>  <i>Porpoises</i> and <i>dolphins</i> are most valuable for their skins and oil, ... <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/137</i>)

>  .. and that the shoals are harassed continually by porpoises, such predatory fish as barracouta, and countless flocks of sea-birds. <i>(1926,TrNZI,56/319</i>) [NOTES: (Young and Thomson)]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Hopuhopu ... Cephalorhynchus hectori, porpoise. </i> <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,58</i>)

>  [species name] porpoise .. [scientific name]- .. [other name(s)] unspecified. DOLPHIN <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  The dolphin and porpoise attain the age of thirty. <i>(Aug 31, 1842,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,3</i>)

>  In the <i>Auckland Times</i> of this week we perceive a letter signed "Robert D'Oyley," and written, we presume, by the Attorney of that name, regarding the capture of some porpoises at the Bay of Islands by an [sic] European, which porpoises were, it appears from the said letter, afterwards stolen by a native chief named Teraia. <i>(Nov 9, 1844,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  No porpoises were seen in the river during the trip, but one was heard of. <i>(Mar 6, 1883,Bruce Herald,3</i>)

>  The Marlborough Herald says a visitor to Picton a few days ago watched a shoal of porpoises follow the steamer Penguin to the wharf, and their appearance was the cause of shoals of herrings [sic] being run on shore, where they continue to be piled up like so much sand. <i>(Aug 2, 1907,Poverty Bay Herald,4</i>)

>  The Marlborough Herald says a visitor to Picton a few days ago watched a shoal of porpoises follow the steamer Penguin to the wharf, and their appearance was the cause of shoals of herrings [sic] being run on shore, where they continue to be piled up like so much sand. <i>(Aug 2, 1907,Poverty Bay Herald,4</i>)

### porridge pot
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) a small mud volcano, a natural pool of boiling mud

>  The party was next shown the Maori Porridge Pot, a hollow cone raised four feet above the level of the ground, half full of boiling clay, said to be edible; a portion was served out to all present, but no one asked for a second helping, so there will probably be plenty left for future visitors. <i>(Nov 22, 1882,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

>  ROTORUA, 25th October.The Porridge Pot, consisting of liquid mud, at Whaka, erupted to a height of 20ft to-day. This phenomenon occurs only at long intervals. <i>(Oct 26, 1912,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  Climbing up the side to see what was inside, we found a narrow ledge or margin all round encircling a boiling, spluttering mass of thick liquid mud, but for the colour, reminding me of a huge boiling porridge pot. <i>(Apr 13, 1893,Otago Witness,35</i>)

>  It is absolutely the finest film of its class ever shown embodying Panorama of Rotorua. The Porridge Pot. Whakarewarewa. The Valley of the Geysers. Pohutu at Play. The Boiling Cauldron. The Swimming Pools. Maoris Cooking in the Boiling Springs. The Brain Pot. <i>(Jan 26, 1915,Grey River Argus,5</i>)

>  <b>KERSHAW</b> `Pretty hot business in Auckland some years ago,' said Mac, ... look [ing] down on the twenty extinct porridge pots. <i>(1886,Colonial Facts and Fictions,250</i>)

### Port Craig cocktail
 
 <i>n.</i> [Excerpt from Wise: PORT CRAIG Remote locality on the western shoreline of Te Waewae Bay, on the Foveaux Strait coast of Wallace County, South Island... Crayfishing. Once the port for sawmilling settlement with a large timbermill but the site was abandoned when the venture folded in the financial depression of the 1930s. Named after mill hand John Craig, one of two men drowned at the mouth of the nearby Waikoua River. Reed's Place Names says: Well known at one time as having the largest timbermill in NZ. This was established by the Marlborough Timber Company. A recent pamphlet by Bremer gives a good account of the mill in 1927 but does not mention cocktails.]

>  <b>McCLENAGHAN</b> `I got on to Port Craig cocktails once' ... He explained, `You get a cup, drop in an egg, add a spoonful of Worcester sauce, an egg cup of methylated spirits, hold your nose and swallow rapidly.' <i>(1976,Travelling Man,47</i>)

>  <b>MILLER</b> They decided to try as a reviver a "Port Craig cocktail", made as follows:: Take a cup - or a round tobacco tin - and drop in one egg. Add one tablespoon of Worcester sauce and one eggcup of methylated spirits. Mix well, hold nose, swallow rapidly.<i> Ibid.</i> 177 The Port Craig cocktail was made and sampled. <i>(1967,Ink on My Fingers,176</i>)

### Port Egmont hen
 
 <i>n.</i> Antarctic Skua

>  <b>FORSTER</b> We saw a great Variety of bird this day viz. A white & sooty Albatross <i>Diomedea exulans & palpebrata</i>). The black & sooty Petrel, (<i>Procellaria aequinoctialis & fuliginosa</i>) the black banded Petrel (<i>Procellaria vittata</i>); the small white rumped Petrel (<i>Proc. pelagica</i>) & we saw likewise three times a <i>Port Egmont Hen</i>, which is commonly thought a mark of the vicinity of Land. [ed. fn] The birds listed here are, respectively, the Wandering Albatross; Light-mantled Sooty Albatross; White-chinned Petrel; Great-winged Petrel; <i>Pachyptila</i> spp; Wilson's Storm Petrel and Antarctic Skua. <i>(Jan 28, 1773,Journal,2/220,1982 ed.</i>)

### Port Nick
 
 <i>n.</i> sailors' and settlers' elliptical form of Port Nicholson, the early name for Wellington and its harbour. <i>Port Nick</i> became (1845 at least) Maori <i>Poniki, Poneke.</i>

>  Shorty after the first sale of land in the township, where sod and thatch whares were beginning to spring up, an old woman, a recent arrival, gravely asked a passer-by to tell her how long it would be before the township was as large as "Port Nick," at which he laughed aloud; but if things go on improving as they have done for the next few years, the old woman's question may not appear so ridiculous after all. <i>(Sep 11, 1869,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  <i>Port Nick n</i>. whaling sl. for Port Nicholson, Wellington. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),16</i>)

>  <b>FUSSELL</b> Poneka (Port Nick) <i>(1917,Letter from Private Henare Tikitanu,6</i>)

>  Port Nicholson, or as the sailors like to call it, `Port Nick' .... <i>(1866,Life & Recollections of an Early Colonist,II/</i>)

>  Hurrah for Port Nic. Legislation, / Enkindling the spark of taxation - / From Port NIC(k) of all spots in creation / We might hope for a LUCIFER match. <i>(Aug 8, 1851,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  My experience of Port Nick goes back to the days of the Sea Serpent, Esther, Salopian, Shepherdess, and similar craft, also Cemino's cutter Fly which were the usual means of communication between coastal ports. <i>(Oct 15, 1907,Poverty Bay Herald,6</i>)

>  "The deplorable state of Port Nick is now driving the rag merchants out of that melancholy city with their swags on their backs, and one lucky one has had the good fortune to find out poor insignificant Blenheim - a place unknown to Wellington in its palmy days, but soon found out when wanting a helping hand to lift it out of the mire into which it has sunk." <i>(Jul 18, 1879,Nelson Evening Mail,2</i>)

>  The incident adds another of the wrongs of Port Nick. <i>(Aug 13, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,7</i>)

>  He is perfectly amazed at the number of sceptics who now live on the shores of Port Nick. <i>(Feb 5, 1910,NZ Free Lance,4</i>)

>  We determined to return to our first love. "Port Nick" - as it was generally called <i>(Aug 28, 1890,Otago Witness,36</i>)

>  If Tom would really make such a coat as Wellington wore, his "utu" would be three of the finest pigs Poneke (Port Nicky) could produce. <i>(Dec 23, 1890,Otago Witness,18</i>)

>  From "Port Nick" to Waitara, by land and sea, the business of this district is done by Wellington. <i>(Jan 1, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,7</i>)

>  Along with earth tremors, Port Nick's waters rose and fell for at least eight hours. <i>(Aug 8, 1992,Dominion,10</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> ... started from Port Nick for Porirua <i>(Mar 14, 1840,Diary,1839 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WEEKS</b> Port Nicholson, or as it is here universally called, Portnic. <i>(1841,Journal,12</i>)

>  <b>CHAPMAN</b> He left Port Nick for Auckland <i>(1842,Letters</i>) [NOTES: (W.V.Brewer)]

 <i>n.</i>

### port price
 
 <i>n.</i> a nominal value per unit weight for a given species of commercial fish, and of which share fishermen are paid an agreed percentage.

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> The Seamen's Union represented the share fishermen in their negotiations. The 1938 award had left it to the parties to work out the prices on which the shares were based. This led to a schedule of species and port prices. <i>(2004,Hooked,237</i>)

### port sammy
 
 <i>n.</i> a portmanteau; a suitcase (esp. one already packed a trip) [ca1900 Marlborough (Jane Morison)] (See [port n](../P#port-n))

>  <b>HISLOP</b> One evening, on answering a knock on the homestead front door, he was met by a traveller with `port sammy' all set for the usual kind invitation to come along inside. <i>(1943,Pure Gold,107</i>)

>  <b>LAWRY</b> ... a classy gentleman's travelling chest. ... It had strong metal clasps and a lock, and a leather handle was attached to each end .... Ed nonchalently [sic] referred to this piece of elegant luggage as his `port sammy'. <i>(1980,Good Luck & Lavender,63</i>)

### port-a-bach
 
 <i>n.</i> a small portable cottage, intended for use as a holiday bach. [ORIGIN: blend]

>  William Giesen and Cecile Bonnifait designed their port-a-bach with Kiwi holidays at the beach in mind. .. The port-a-bach, designed to have minimal effect on its environment, is the result. <i>(Nov 5, 2007,Dominion Post,C1</i>)

### porta potty
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Quality household furniture... Porta potty. <i>(Dec 16, 1993,Napier Daily Telegraph,20</i>)

### portable broth
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>FORSTER</b> The prophylactics ... which were regularly served to the crew, namely portable broth, and sour krout, had a wonderful effect of keeping them free from sea-scurvy. <i>(1777,Voyage Round the World,1/105</i>)

### portable soup
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  At some time or other our neighbours in Australia will be glad to buy up the best of ours to remedy the defects which natural causes produce in their own herds; such as long journeys after full feeding, which will harden the muscles of the hindquarters, and render them more suitable for the portable soup boiler than for Christmas meat. <i>(Sep 29, 1857,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  On Sale at the Tea, Grocery & Italian Warehouse, Shortland Cresent .. Portable Soup ... <i>(Apr 22, 1843,Daily Southern Cross,1</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> Whereas we found the <i>Sowerkrout</i> so good, that we emptied about 60 Casks, & 60 boxes of portable Soup ... <i>(Feb 5, 1775,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,IV/722,1982 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> [fn] Should means be found of converting the meat into portable soup, the carcase of a sheep might ... be considerably higher than 10s. <i>(Aug 1862,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,IV/40</i>)

>  Captain Cook, always careful about the health of his men, had extra antiscorbutics, such as malt sourkraut [sic], salted cabbage, portable broth, saloup, mustard, marmalade of carrots, and inspissated juice of wort and beer added to the stores. <i>(Nov 30, 1893,Otago Witness,40</i>)

 <i>n.</i> [1964 J. Winchester [check] In NZ Railways does not necessarily mean a bloke who carries bags. Ticket clippers are porters.]

 <i>n.</i>
