>  Baker called for a "portergaff". <i>(Dec 1, 1917,NZ Truth,2</i>)

### porthole
 
 <i>n.</i> the small gate in a shearing shed through which sheep are released after shearing or dagging

>  <b>CARR</b> Fleece-picking. - As soon as a shearer has taken the fleece off and let the sheep go through the port-hole to the counting-out pen, the picker gathers the fleece up in such a manner that she or he can on getting to the rolling table, throw it out with the breech part at the right end. <i>(1913,Country Work,14</i>)

>  As he talked about this Mr Loughnan chanced to speak of shearers putting their sheep out through the "ports" or "portholes" of sheds and jotted down these words to check whether he had them on his lists. <i>(Feb 11, 1977,Press, The,19</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) an opening in a woolshed wall through which newly-shown sheep leave the board and enter the counting-out pen.

>  <b>CARR</b> As soon as a shearer has taken the fleece off and let the sheep go through the port-hole ... <i>(1913,Country Work,14</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> .. in the type shown there is no draught from the portholes, whereas with portholes on the outside wall the board can be very draughty and affect the shearers' backs. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,112</i>)

>  <b>STAPLES</b> The large woolshed with its many port-holes was passed before we pulled up at the door of the cookshop. <i>(1964,Cooks and Shepherds Come Away,10</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> porthole: the little doorway through which a shearer pushes his sheep into his counting-out pen. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,63</i>)

>  <b>THORNTON</b> Porthole The rectangular opening (with sliding door) facing the shearer on his stand through which newly shorn sheep are discharged to the yard via counting-out pens. Sometimes shortened to ports. [port; porthole] <i>(1986,NZ Heritage of Farm Buildings,256</i>)

### porthole fish
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Porthole Fish <i>Narooma raoulensis</i> (Waite) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,400</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 400]

### portobello
 
 <i>n.</i> a large flat brown mushroom

>  After growers have played with several names, including the ugly "brown flats", the unappetising "BBQ flats" and the inaccurate "field mushrooms", they have settled on "Portabello", which is already well known in the US and Europe. "Swiss Browns", another name used overseas, is the new names for brown buttons. <i>(Nov 24, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),43</i>)

>  A succulent main course chicken breast again came with.. a portion of white button mushrooms whose blandness did nothing to persuade me to go back to buying them in the supermarket, now that we have Swiss browns and portobellos. <i>(Apr 8, 2004,Dominion Post,B9</i>)

>  <b>FRANCIS</b> He sells all the sort of mushrooms the food world's gone mad about. Portobello, ceps, chanterelles, shitake, things like that. <i>(1999,Second Wind,50</i>)

>  And for mains (the Americans call them entrees), poached Maine lobster with spinach flan, wild mushroom ravioli and champagne sauce and sauteed beef tenderloin with roasted garlic potatoes, grilled Portabello mushroom, black beans and tomato scallion sauce. <i>(May 12, 2001,Dominion,7</i>)

>  The most accessible Wairarapa wine match is Martinborough pinot noir with locally raised lamb, though other contenders would be the local wild duck and pheasant.. or feral venison from the Rimutaka Ranges - especially when these meals are cooked with local field mushrooms or the flat brown portobellos from Parkvale Mushrooms. <i>(Jul 25, 2001,Evening Post,18</i>)

>  The mushroom (singular) proves to be a shining example of what a portobello should be, putting to shame any other members of this fungal family. <i>(Sep 21, 2003,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),17</i>)

>  [Hdg] Warm Portabello Mushroom, Roasted Pear & Blue Cheese Salad. <i>(Sep 7, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),43</i>)

>  Actually, these "brown flats", "gourmet browns", "portobellos" (call them what you will) are nothing more than brown button mushrooms which have been allowed to grow up. <i>(Oct 17, 2000,Evening Post,16</i>)

>  Patrons can sample Hotere's favourite seafoods with champagne, moi moi potato stew and Northland oysters, confit of mutton bird, lamb roasted in seaweed with wilted watercress and Portobello mushrooms and whitestone cheeses smoked and baked in flax. <i>(Oct 26, 2000,Evening Post,16</i>)

>  These big brown mushrooms... are an American strain of the standard cultivated mushroom <i>Agaricus bisporus</i>. In the United States, they are known as portobello. <i>(Jul 16, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),60</i>)

### portsammy
 
 <i>n.</i> (obsolete) a suitcase or travelling bag [cf. DNZE (earliest citation 1905)]

>  The Honorable William McCullough, M.L.C., has packed his little portsammy, and is ready to start on Saturday for another jaunt to England. <i>(Feb 22, 1896,Observer (Auckland),14</i>)

>  A particularly mean kind of theft was sheeted home to the culprits by a lady named Annie Dutton, at the Magistrate's Court, on Thursday, when a little woman calling herself Christina Atkinson and an individual named Thomas Atkinson were charged conjointly with having stolen a "portsammy" and contents. <i>(Feb 12, 1916,NZ Truth,3</i>)

>  Some of the shows he had in his portsammy were as follow: "Honey Girl" and "Mary" (Yankee productions), and "A Southern Maid," " The Naughty Princess," "A Night Out," and "The Little Dutch girl" (London successes). <i>(Jul 9, 1921,NZ Truth,10</i>)

>  .. and I was the first to open my "portsammy," but not before he told me. He only looked in, and threw the flap of the portmanteau over, and asked me if I only had wearing apparel in it. I said that was so, and he put a figure on the "porty" like 3, and said, "That's right." <i>(Oct 10, 1900,Otago Witness,66</i>)

>  - That an out-of-worker, who was departing from a boarding-house with his port-sammy at the barren heart of midnight, met the landlord at the gate, but the latter was so obfuscated that he offered to give the sloper a hand with his heavy cargo. <i>(Mar 2, 1901,New Zealand Free Lance,22</i>)

>  Then he got out and proceeded to descend while still hanging on to that beloved port-sammy. <i>(Nov 30, 1901,New Zealand Free Lance,20</i>)

### Portuguese Army
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Sir, - `The Gosling' ... warbles a satirical ditty on the Dinkums. (Applause.) Now listen. As an `Extra Unit' we hit Trentham like any mudlarks in the early winter of 1915. We were dubbed, inter alia:- The Trentham Battalion, Trentham Regiment, `Trents,' Lord Liverpool's Own, Earl of Liverpool's Own, England's Last 'ope, Fulton's Blind Half Hundred, The Portuguese Army, Rifle Regiment, N.Z. ### portulaca
 
 <i>n.</i> [see also wild portulaca, Curnow's curse]

>  During the showery weather, plant out young pansies, asters, zinnias, portulaca, or phlox Drummondii which have been nursed in boxes of seed beds. <i>(Dec 31, 1891,Otago Witness,5</i>)

>  There are quite a large number of seeds that can be successfully sown this month, but, unfortunately, most gardens can only accommodate a small number of them. Asters, alyssum, bartonia, calandrina .. poppies, portulaca, polyanthus ... <i>(Sep 18, 1909,Star, The,3</i>)

>  There were portulaca flowers growing wild in the stones and staunch succulents with heads like delicate big daisies. <i>(Jan 2, 2003,Dominion Post,C1</i>)

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> PORTULACA (<i>Portulaca oleracea</i>), called also pig weed, hog weed, purslane, and ice plant, is very common on garden paths etc., in Auckland, becoming less and less common as far south as Nelson. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,72,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <i>portulaca, wild</i> [s] <i>Portulaca oleracea</i> L. ... <i>portulaca [=] calandrinia: wild portulaca</i> [s] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,60</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> WILD PORTULACEA (<i>Portulaca oleracea</i>) ... This Asiatic weed of arable crops is common in many warmer coutries. In New Zealand wild portulaca occurs in gardens and cropping land. . . <i>(1980,Weeds of Crops,114</i>)

>  <b>HILGENDORF & CALDER</b> CURNOW'S CURSE (<i>Calandrinia ciliata var. caulescens</i>) is also called portulaca, horgan's weed, and purple calandrinia. It has spreading fleshly stems and somewhat fleshy leaves. <i>(1967,Weeds of New Zealand,78</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1926]]

 <i>abbr.</i> Post-settlement assets

### posher
 
 <i>n.</i> a copper-stick for beating clothes when washing them [Joh saw one of these for sale in Taupo "The man behind the counter said it was an English term; bsasically it was a broomstick with a hollow copper head, like a plunger in shape. The man said sometimes the copper head contained marbles so that madam could hear that the maid was indeed working. Ian Douglas (51 Liverpool St, Wanganui; dougie@xtra.co.nz) says the term used in England 60 years ago was "poss stick" a long wooden stick. "There was also a 'posser'which was a pole with a sort of vacuum creating hood on the end which was used to dump or dunk the clothes up and down and help shift the dirt."]

### POSIFoods
 
 <i>n.</i> point-of-sale individualised foods

>  POSIFoods, described.. as <i>Star Trek</i>-style technology, was launched last month. The name related to a new consumer-food category called "point-of-sale individualised foods". In much the same way that consumers could have their coffee flavoured in a complex range of strengths, flavours and styles, the POSIFood system could alter the formula of snack foods to suit health needs depending on a person's age, weight or physical condition. <i>(Jul 29, 2004,NZ Herald,C1</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### poss
 
 <i>n.</i> also poss stick

>  <b>ELDRED-GRIGG</b> Sadie was holding the poss stick, poking it up and down to stir the clothes in the copper, and Mum came out to inspect. `Poss them clothes, Millie,' she said. `Poss them good and proper.' <i>(1987,Oracles and Miracles,30</i>)

### Possibles
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The 8000 persons who were present at Athletic Park today to see the Possibles and Probables of the North Island battling for places in the All Blacks witnessed perhaps the finest game seen there since the war. <i>(May 27, 1924,Otago Witness,48</i>)

### possie
 
 <i>n.</i> a position, usually of advantage [(sports, incl. hunting, fishing) also pozzy, pozzie, posi]

>  <b>MASTERS</b> ... when he did come out it would be ... with one or two of the old hinds of his harems scatted to bark a warning, ... before I could get to a possie within range from which I could see him once more. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,56</i>)

>  <b>McCALLUM</b> We get into our possies [sc. maimais] about five o'clock ... and wait for daylight. <i>(1963,Half-Gallon Jar,39</i>)

>  <b>MANTELL</b> Cumming handed the line to Tommy who immediately returned to his possy by the shed, threw it into the sea. <i>(1978,Murder in Fancydress,76</i>)

>  .. the 30-year whitebaiting veteran says as he surveys Canterbury's Waimakariri River from his "possie". <i>(Aug 16, 2005,NZ Herald,A7</i>)

>  Whitebaiters can't officially own their patch of the river bank, but if you were cheeky enough to be fishing at somebody's else's "possie" when the "owner" turned up, the following conversation might well ensue: "I've been fishing here for 25 years and this is my possie." <i>(Nov 30, 2004,Dominion Post,E8</i>)

>  Just before half-time, Sydenham commanded a commanding "pozzy," and Davis, snapping the leather from a scrum, dodged over. <i>(Jul 31, 1915,NZ Truth,9</i>)

>  <b>Carr</b> Not having the right sort of dog and the "posi" to hunt in or the dedication to get amongst it all, combined to keep me from ever catching a pig until the week after I had picked up my three black pups. <i>(1986,Diary of a Pig Hunter,6</i>)

>  The cause of the trouble was that the 'bait preferred to travel up the creek rather than the river, and the wily whitebaiters were forever scrapping over possies along its banks. <i>(Dec 13, 1928,NZ Truth,9</i>)

>  <b>LACEY</b> There's few things in the world that look as stupid as a joker playin' out of his right possie on the footie field. <i>(1976,Loosehead Len's Bumper Thump Book,38</i>)

>  <b>Carr</b> Not having the right sort of dog and the "posi" to hunt in or the dedication to get amongst it all, combined to keep me from ever catching a pig until the week after I had picked up my three black pups. <i>(1896,Diary of a Pig Hunter,6</i>)

>  That Springbocks tour made national newspaper headlines. Fans camped out overnight to secure a possie in the packed standing terraces. <i>(Sep 18, 2010,Your Weekend,10</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a personal place; a secluded place [a personal, physical, space with sense of seclusion and exclusiveness; aka pozzy, posi, pozzie]

>  <b>ed Kidman</b> Murders, rapes, gang shootings and music teachers interfering with schoolboy saxophonists - the whole works, cases dating back through twenty years in the force, were all trotted out by Bill Harbidge from his possie on the La-Z-Boy [sic]. <i>(2006,Best New Zealand Fiction, The Vol 3,3/130</i>)

>  well, he had occupied their possie. <i>(Apr 21, 2006,NZ Herald,A11</i>)

>  I would include among New Zealand English words: possie: position or seat <i>(May 6, 1952,VUC First Year English Class</i>) [NOTES: (3 times)]

>  <b>DAVIN</b> `I've brought a picnic,' he said. `So you watch out for a nice little pozzy ...' <i>(1956,Sullen Bell,47</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Gotta get a good possie on the bank and see the boys win. Ya can put a ring around that one. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,60</i>)

>  <b>CURNOW</b> A city where we're all hoping like hell for the best possy on the dog's arse ... <i>(1972,Four Plays,101,1962 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (The Overseas Expert)]

>  <b>PEARSON</b> He had taken girls outside the [dance] hall before ... for a cuddle ... and sometimes more, if the night was fine, in a pozzy he knew of under the blackberries. <i>(1963,Coal Flat,31</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> possie: <i>a good possie</i>, a good position for seeing (the race). <i>(1964,Collected Stories,302</i>)

>  <b>FRAME</b> We found it to be divided into small plots like the private places or `possies' which my friends and I claimed for ourselves when in the midst of games we suddenly decided we wanted to be alone and inaccessible. <i>(1972,Daughter Buffalo,38</i>)

>  <b>MITCHELL</b> <i>pozzie</i>: Position. <i>(1972,Half-Gallon Quarter-Acre Pavlova Paradise, The,183</i>)

>  You'll hear in his bar-room `possie', / As a bloke came in for a `wet'. <i>(1926,Transport Workers' Song Book,70</i>)

>  <b>MARSH</b> Messing about on the scene of the crime ... She's going to find herself in a very, very uncomfortable little pozzy. <i>(1937,Vintage Murder,238</i>)

>  <i>possie, pozzie n. (Sl.)</i> position, physical rather than job or situation. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),16</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>pozzie</i>: A location in space or a POSITION. `I've got pozzie for the races - you can see everything.' <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,80</i>)

>  <b>GILDERDALE</b> <i>posse</i> Claim, position; as in `gum digger's posse'. <i>T.T. Garland's</i> Old Joe <i>(1937). </i> <i>(1982,Sea Change,241</i>)

>  Donors were territorially minded, and resented anyone nosying in on their "pozzies". <i>(Nov 6, 1982,Listener (New Zealand Listener),131</i>)

>  The 72 deluxe platinum seats are positioned for the best view in the house and have a possie for a bottle of plonk and a couple of wine glasses. <i>(Dec 5, 2002,Dominion Post,D1</i>)

>  The local topography completely blocks any trace of the signal from reaching the various pozzies where I find myself with a radio. <i>(Jan 7, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),42</i>)

>  <b>The Mixer</b> You'll hear him in his bar-room "possie". <i>(1926,Transport Workers' Song Book,70</i>)

>  <b>MILLER</b> There were about 12 "possies" where a jar could be set. <i>(1967,Ink on My Fingers,174</i>) [NOTES: brewing during prohibition]

>  Don't y'think it's a lovely little possey? I only just found it. <i>(1944,NZ New Writing,3/8</i>)

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> He had a pretty good possy on the verandah <i>(1948,Cunninghams, The,123</i>)

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> The autumn was mild for Gladston; the veranda, sunny and swept by cool breezes, was really a bonzer possy. <i>(1948,Cunninghams, The,114,1976 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DUFF</b> `What, is someone else using your possie?' `Two or three of the cows. When I got there last night there were three guns about five yards from me.' <i>(May 20, 1951,Shepherd's Calendar,31,1961 ed.</i>)

>  He finds a pleasant pozzie on the lawn sheltered from the hot nor'wester and explains his public timidity. <i>(Jan 15, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),24</i>)



 
 (with /s/ or /z/) <i>n.</i> a place of shelter (in wartime) [war time; sense of shelter as well as advantageous firing position; aka pozzy, posi, pozzie]

>  <i>possie n. NZ</i> & <i>Austral. colloq</i>. (also <i>possy</i>) position, place, job. [abbr.] <i>Ibid.</i> 590 <i>pozzy</i> var. of POSSIE. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,587</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 379]]

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>possie</i> useful position or hideaway, developed in dug-outs at Gallipoli; eg `That's a good possie you've got there for watching the birds from.' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,87</i>)

>  <b>BURDON</b> ... swell little pozzy this. <i>(1943,Outlaw's Progress,25</i>)

>  <b>MILLER</b> We repaired to our `possie' on the dispensary roof. <i>(Feb 2, 1917,Camps, Tramps and Trenches,11</i>)

>  [`<i>possie(s)</i>' very frequent] <i>(1918,NZEF Chronicles,<i>passim </i></i>)

>  <b>INGRAM</b> There are not enough `bivvies' about here to house us all ... I and two pals dug a possie out of the rim of a large shell hole and covered the top with odd pieces of corrugated iron. <i>(Oct 12, 1917,Anzac Diary,59,1987 ed.</i>)

>  `How many more miles have we got to go to get to this `possie' of yours?' asked the exasperated voice of a Digger. <i>(Feb 10, 1920,Quick March,27</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> This little possie is out on its own. <i>Ibid.</i> 652 <i>Possy</i>; occ <i>possie, pozzy </i>A position; esp. a dug-out, or other shelter: military, mainly Australian and New Zealand: from 1915. B. & P.; F. & Gibbons. <i>(1935,Slang Today,287</i>)

>  <b>FULLARTON</b> Those M.G. Blokes have camouflaged their `possies' well. <i>(1944,Troop Target,11</i>)

>  <b>SKEYHILL</b> Aust cit. omit 'e climbs up stunted pine-trees, / An' snipes away at us. / But 'e never shows 'is pozzy, / An' 'e never makes a fuss.] <i>(1916,Soldier Songs from Anzac,15</i>)

>  Jerry O'Dwyer had shot two crows from the new sniper's pozzy down at the creek. [Note: Pozzy or Possie - Australian warrior's short for "position", or lair. <i>Ibid.</i> 125 "Possie!" exclaimed the enquiring general. "What is a 'Possie'?" "That, sir," said the C.o., "is Australian for recess, either firing or sleeping. It's a contraction of 'position.". <i>(1916,Anzac Book, The,10</i>)

>  [He] announced that he saw legs passing behind a Turk sniping "possie" below the parapet. <i>(Apr 27, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,14</i>)

>  "Look out, Bill [Massey], Jerry's over and we're in a crook possie here!" <i>(May 15, 1923,Aussie (NZ Section),I</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> We occupy a <i>pozzie</i> <i>(1941,NZ Slang,62</i>)

>  <i>Possie, pozzie</i> - position. <i>(1944,US War Dept. Short Guide to NZ,39</i>)

>  He shot one after the other of the enemyas they sprang out of their undergroundH.E.-proof "possies". <i>(Aug 30, 1916,Chronicles NZEF,9</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a position, situation or predicament [ORIGIN: abbreviation, a personal but not physical situation; aka pozzy, posi, pozzie]

>  <i>(1953,Landfall,7/251</i>) [NOTES: (Baxter)]

>  <b>MARSH</b> Messing about on the scene of the crime ... She's going to find herself in a very, very uncomfortable little pozzy. <i>(1937,Vintage Murder,238</i>)

>  THERE are a lot of Civil Servants whose comfy possies fit them to a tea! <i>(Aug 15, 1925,NZ Truth,1</i>)

>  It leaves the average voter floundering for a comfortable grizzling pozzie, when politicians behave in a predictable manner, doing exactly what they always said they were going to do. <i>(Dec 30, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

### possum
 
 <i>n.</i> [attrib.]

>  Possum browsing is severely affecting the health of forests in the Tauherenikau and Waiohine valleys. <i>(Jun 23, 2005,Footnotes,13</i>)

>  [Hdg] Bellamy's: it's bye, bye to possum pie... [Caption] Possum pie maker Peter Salter gave Nick Smith a mouthful outside parliament - and National's conservation spokesman was as pleased as pie. <i>(Aug 8, 2004,Sunday Star Times,A10</i>)

>  At a private party in Balclutha one night, "'possum pie" was the chief dish on the supper menu, and those who partook of it are all agreed that it was par excellence. <i>(Jul 12, 1927,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,1</i>)

>  Their possum pies and whisky and maple lamb-burgers are a festival hit and the couple are enjoying the day. <i>(Dec 2005,NZ Geographic,80</i>)

>  Being tumbled in sawdust is just one of the 40 or so stages through which a possum pelt goes on its journey to becoming a soft lustrous fur. <i>(Jan 1992,NZ Geographic,No. 13/19</i>)

>  <b>HILL</b> Once a year, they [sc. the Moaville saleyards] become the venue for the Central North Island All-Comers Possum-Skinning Championships. <i>(1985,Moaville Magic,33</i>)

>  POSSUM boards, must be in good condition. Ph (07) 315 7765. <i>(Mar 17, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,17</i>)

>  The charismatic Maori driver of the Driving Creek train tells us that an 'Eyeful Tower' will be built at the terminus, to overlook the stunning Hauraki Gulf and serve "possum sandwiches - nothing flash". <i>(Jun 2002,AA Directions,Winter : 17</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a brush-tailed tree-dwelling mainly nocturnal, vegetarian marsupial introduced from Australia. (See [also opossum](../A#also-opossum))

>  Hitherto I have looked upon theintroduction of 'possum' into our NewZealand bush as a mistake. <i>(Oct 11, 1928,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> He was aware they'd killed the fatted possum, so to speak.They served up pork and fish, and three times they killed two cattle that fed the four hundred or so of them for nearly a week each. <i>(2000,Kokopu Dreams,89</i>)

>  A live opossum was belched forth from the printing machine from somewhere up in the top gear. <i>(Dec 3, 1937,King Country Chronicle,4</i>)

>  "Opossums become a Pest."...' 'Possums have been a tarnation nuisance ever since they were introduced into New Zealand. <i>(Feb 3, 1912,NZ Free Lance,14</i>)

>  <b>BACON</b> <i>possum</i>-The Australian marsupial introduced in the early days (in 1858, 1870, 1895) as a means of fostering a fur trade, but now one of New Zealand's greatest pests. Not to be confused with the American opposum which is carnivorous, the Australian possum is vegetarian. <i>(1963,In the Sticks,182</i>)

>  `I believe that the member for Tarawera is referring to the possum (Trichosurus vulpecula), not opossums (genus Didelphis) which are American marsupials,' [Agriculture Minister Moyle] said in his written reply. ... And for the name, you can be forgiven if you call our imported possum (the `brushtail possum,' to be precise) an opossum. The Aussie possum - there are about six types across the Tasman - looked so much like the American counterpart when first discovered by Europeans that it was called an opossum. As recently as 1981, scientists who turned up for the first New Zealand symposium on marsupials ... were sagely talking about possums in 12 of the papers presented while their colleagues talked about opossums in six papers. All were referring to the same nocturnal nuisance. One of the great achievements of that symposium was to finish up talking the same language after taking a vote that favoured for possum. ... The fur trade is pushing for `NZ phalanger' (a generic name for a group of marsupials) while possum stew exporters are trying to win acceptance for `Kiwi bear' in Oriental restaurants. <i>(Jun 28, 1988,Auckland  Star,1</i>)

>  <b>STUART C. MACDIARMID</b> In Australia and New Zealand the word possum is now almost universally accepted among professional zoologists and is the most widely-used colloquial name for the Australian phalanger of which the common brushtail possum, <i>Trichosurus vulpecula</i>, is an example. <i>(Oct 27, 1989,Letter</i>) [NOTES: STUART C. MACDIARMID, National Adviser, Health MAFQUAL]

>  [Advertisement] 2 Possum skins. <i>(May 3, 1851,Lyttelton Times,1</i>)

>  <b>SENIOR</b> [We] fed and stroked the Tasmanian 'possum [in Acclimatisation Society grounds at Christchurch]. <i>(1880,Travel & Trout,260</i>)

>  <i>Pos&#769;.sum</i> (p`os&#769;.s`um), <i>n. (Zoo.l.) [Add.] 2</i>. A phalanger.<i> [Colloq.] See PHALANGER, in the Dictionary. </i> <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2029</i>)

>  Gran'pa has been very excited lately about 'possums. <i>(Jul 11, 1921,Quick March,17</i>)

>  <i>possum n.</i> sl. abbrev. fr. opossum. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,9</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> After dinner he walked back to the back of the shack, where two little brown men were still fiddling with the earth-oven. `Looks like maggots to me,' he said ... <i>`Hou-hou</i> grub. Pretty good, eh?' ... `Pretty good, eh? No <i>hou-hou</i>, no 'possum, no wild pig, pretty soon we starve. ...' <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,147</i>)

>  <b>S,C. MACDAIRMID</b> Opossum is preferred by the industry (fur trade) as <i>possum</i> is thought to be perceived in North America as a colloquialism or slang word for the ratty little didelphid the North American opossum <i>Didelphis virginiana</i>, a very distant relation. <i>(Nov 2, 1989,Personal Communication</i>)

>  <b>LOCKLEY</b> `Heard a possum last night- ...' `You mean `opossum'. So did I, ...' `I mean <i>possum</i>! Don't be silly - fancy calling it `Oh-possum'! Just like you Kiwis, using long words ....' The argument was between my New Zealand grandson and his young Australian visitor. <i>(1980,House Above the Sea,109</i>)

>  At the possum farm, animals are caught live and `ranched': fed in close confinement for three months prior to killing...Ranch the wild animal for three months and the fur becomes a much better commodity, says Keber. <i>(Feb 6, 1982,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

>  Post-Primary School Bulletin <i>(1959,Post-Primary School Bulletin,12 No. 3/22</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,145</i>)

>  <b>HELMER</b> Darns six or seven faster'n a possum sucking eggs. <i>(1964,Stag Party,56</i>)

>  <i>possum n</i>. See <i>opossum</i>. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),16</i>)

>  <b>HASCOMBE</b> The 'possum sprang within a few feet of the barking dog ... <i>(1969,Down and Almost Under,151</i>)

>  Possum act earns fine [heading] A security officer outside Telethon headquarters at the Auckland Town Hall on Sunday was confronted with a drunken man dangling a decomposing opossum. <i>(Jun 28, 1983,Auckland  Star,4</i>)

>  <b>DAVIS</b> ... I'll tell you then - if only to give myself the satisfaction of not having to listen to your yakky-yak for five minutes... I was trapping possums. <i>Ibid.</i> 112 ... possums drop down in hundreds ... and me running about with a bit of a manku [sic] waddy clouting anything that moved. <i>(1964,Watersiders,111</i>)

>  G J seems to be wondering if L B is still true to him since he has been amongst the deer and the 'possums. <i>(Jul 1, 1899,Observer (Auckland),21</i>)

>  Have never seen such terrible-looking tucker ... like a long-dead possum in a swimming pool. <i>(Oct 14, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),68</i>)

>  The public would have taken to them like a dog to a possum. <i>(Aug 1, 2003,Dominion Post,C8</i>)

### possum dog
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a dog trained to hunt and kill possums.

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> My ornamental, but otherwise useless Irish Setter is a fantastic possum dog. A dog will put possums up trees very efficiently. <i>(1984,Possum Hunting in New Zealand,48</i>)

### possum fur
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  ...six of the featured designers used possum fur in their creations, either as trims and borders or, in one case, for a full-length coat. For these designers, and many other garment makers and fur sewers around the country, using possum fur is an eco-friendly practice. <i>(May 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 63/103</i>)

>  The problem of gathering possum fur in remote locations has been solved buy the invention of a portable plucking machine... <i>Ibid.</i> 108 For all its favourable qualities, however, possum fur is still an extremely difficult fibre to work with. <i>(May 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 63/107</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CRUMP</b> A possum-man is like a possum dog. They're a special kind of people. <i>(1971,Bastards I Have Met,99</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BAKER</b> 'There was a man here, a possum trapper, living by himself.' <i>(2000,Kokopu Dreams,137</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### possuming
 
 <i>n.</i> trapping etc. of possums [also opposuming; cf. possumming entered from DB (two m's); (HWO) ca 1949 Havelock; go opossuming, to go opossum trapping or (then illegally) poisoning;]

>  And later, when the "chaps" flourished sufficiently to dodge the Bible-class and to hanker after walking out with giggling girls, and after cigarettes, there were the white nights out on the ranges 'possuming. <i>(Jul 8, 1911,Evening Post,11</i>)

>  <b>GLEN</b> `Have you caught any possums yet?' asked Rex Findlay ... Eventually everyone at work began to greet me with the same question, `How goes the possuming?' <i>(1980,Bush in Our Yard,67</i>)

>  I like fishing, shooting, possuming and all that. <i>(Sep 6, 1987,Country Calendar (TVNZ)</i>) [NOTES: 1987 Sep 6 TV1 Country Calendar (West Coast)]

>  <b>SANSOM</b> On the runs, opossuming had always been a sideline. <i>(1970,Stewart Islanders,199</i>)

>  I was possuming up there and on my own for several months...I came down the river out the back of George Isley's farm at Galatea and slept in the tea-tree. <i>(Mar 10, 2012,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),11</i>)

### possummer/possumer
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a possum hunter.

>  It is instructive to note that at the time and maybe still, a Jacko was a West Coast appellation for both a possum and a possumer, which explains the suitable inappropriateness of the name for the column. <i>(Aug 2010,FMC - (Federated Mountain Clubs of NZ) Bulletin,31</i>)

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> I will also the use the terms 'hunter' or possummer rather than trapper when referring generally to people who hunt possums as I believe that the term 'trapper' is a misnomer considering the number of possummers who never touch a trap. <i>(1984,Possum Hunting in New Zealand,1</i>)

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> Very thick scrub such as gorse in amongst young pines is very hard to hunt and the possummer is confined to tracks and fire breaks. <i>(1984,Possum Hunting in New Zealand,16</i>)

### possumming
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the hunting and destruction of possums.

>  <b>GLEN</b> 'How goes the possumming?' <i>(1980,Bush in Our Yard,67</i>)

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> When you have decided to have a go at possumming and have obtained the necessary traps or poison licence, you now have to decide where to work. <i>(1984,Possum Hunting in New Zealand,17</i>)

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> Possumming from a back country base can be a tremendous experience. <i>(1984,Possum Hunting in New Zealand,21</i>)

### post and rail (fence)
 
 <i>n.</i> a fence of wooden rails held to posts; fencing made from wooden posts joined by rails. [also post and rail as adjective/modifier]

>  Chas and I put up post and rail fencing ... The first Overland Mail to the West Coast went through today. They had to borrow a horse from Enys to go on one stage. <i>(Jul 4, 1865,Chudleigh Diary,192,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <i>(1849,Notes on NZ</i>) [NOTES: [check cit]]

>  <b>COOPER</b> ... post and rail fences. <i>(1857,NZ Settlers' Guide,84</i>)

>  <i>Post and rai</i>l: Made from wooden posts joined by rails. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,28</i>) [NOTES: [check quot]]

>  <i>post and rails NZ & Austral.</i> simple kind of fence of uprights and rails. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,587</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a fence composed of posts and rails morticed together [often used in horse-grazing country.] [posts and rails Also elsewhere; aka post and rails; note also posts and rails usually in reference to components of fence rather than the fence; includes attributive use] (See [posts and rails](pop-pyo.md#posts-and-rails))

>  The land run over was in grass, with post-and-rail fences, and banks with double ditches and walls, altogether a "stiffer" country than has hitherto been attempted. <i>(Nov 4, 1865,Daily Southern Cross,6</i>)

>  <b>LOCKE</b> I thought your house was all shut in with the post-and-rail fence. <i>(1984,Kauri & The Willow, The,62</i>)

>  My outer fences are post and rails, ... and the inner ones of what is called in England staple and board. <i>(Apr 10, 1844,Dillon Letters,39,1954 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Dillon)]

>  Many of my scholars can now plough, drive the cart, grind with our horse-power mill, put up post and rail fencing, build chimneys, ... <i>(1852,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1852 in Wily and Maunsell Robert Maunsell (1938) 100]

>  <b>ALLOM</b> .. the only farm buildings requisite are, a strong post-and-rail stock-yard for the purposes of milking and occasionally mustering the stock. <i>(1853,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1853 Allom letter in 1853 Earp NZ 118]

>  <b>MONEY</b> .. I joined an old troop-mate [145] who had been post-and-rail splitting for some Maories at Rangitaiki.. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,144</i>)

>  <b>ROCHFORT</b> [The Station owner] has some excellent paddocks fenced in. The run is bounded on one side by the Turakina river, on the other three by a good post-and-rail fence. The sheep require but little looking after, as they cannot get over the boundary. <i>(1853,Adventures of a Surveyor,25</i>)

>  <b>KIRK</b> Totara post and rail fences are expected to last from ten to thirteen years ... <i>(1875,Durability NZ Timbers,6</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> Four varieties [of fences] are general in the North [Auckland]. They are post-and-rail, wire, wattle, and stake. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,189</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> As soon as maybe after his arrival, [the new settler] engages himself as a shepherd, or bullock-driver, or hut-keeper at a station, .. makes a hut of sod or wattle and dab, .. encloses [his land] with .. a post and rail or ditch and bank fence. <i>(1857,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,27</i>)

>  As a guide, so far, we should say that the common post and rail fences usually erected are "substantial" in the meaning of the ordinance, and that they ought to be held so till decayed. <i>(Mar 4, 1848,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  The coach struck a telegraph post, and the body of the coach charged through a post and rail fence, and was thrown 20 feet down the side of the hill. <i>(Mar 9, 1898,Colonist (Nelson),2</i>)

>  THE finest TRAINING STABLES in TRENTHAM; six acres good land, nine ton hay this year; good post and rail fences; four-roomed bungalow, conveniences ... <i>(Apr 12, 1919,NZ Truth,7</i>)

>  The animal went over the bank with the milk waggon, and fell a distance of 30 feet, tearing through a post and rail fence on the way down. <i>(Dec 3, 1906,Northern Advocate,2</i>)

>  192 Chas and I put up post and rail fencing. <i>(Jul 4, 1865,Chudleigh Diary,192</i>) [NOTES: (1950)]

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> The first fences were made of split totara posts and rails .. Post and rail fences took a very long time to erect. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,103</i>)

>  very fertile, free draining volcanic ash soil, fencing all post and rail and hedges. <i>(Jan 20, 2002,Sunday Star Times,C8</i>)

>  .. and 30 plus post and rail paddocks plus an 800 metre track ... <i>(2002,Sunday Star Times,C9</i>)

>  The .. Farm, consisting of about 30 acres of good land in high cultivation, with post-and-rail and quickset fences, and having a Dwelling house .. will be Let, on lease, from 1st January instant. <i>(Jan 6, 1849,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,177</i>)

### post and rail tea
 
 <i>n.</i> [aka post and rail(s) [check Burdon see if independent occurrence in NZ or Morris tradition]]

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> Many of the old station cooks, with the assistance of an `offsider' (mate) found no difficulty in cooking for fifty or sixty men. ... it has to be remembered that ... bread cost only 6d. or 7d. a 4lb. loaf, and tea (post and rails) could be bought for 1/- per lb. Some of these contract cooks were great fossickers and managed to find quite a lot of green stuff for vegetables ... <i>(1940,Te Waimate,91,1954 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Post </i>(p&omacr.st),<i> n</i>. [Add.] <i>Post and rail tea, or Posts and rails, </i>a decoction of coarse tea; - so called because the floating leaves resemble sticks or rails.<i> [Colloq., Australia] </i> <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2029</i>)

>  <i>post-and-rails (tea)</i> strong, coarse, inferior tea made in a billy (q.v.) with pieces of stalk or leaves floating in it. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),16</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a coarse drinking tea with pieces of leaves and stalk floating on the surface.

>  <b>MORRIS</b> Post-and-Rail Tea, slang name for strong bush-tea: so called because the large bits of the tea, or supposed tea, float about in the billy, which are compared by a strong imagination to the posts and rails of the wooden fence so frequent in Australia. [1851] <i>(1898,Austral English,367</i>)

>  <b>PFAFF</b> I forgot tea, 4s. .. but the quality; we used to call it Posts and Rails [on the diggings c1860s]. <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,43</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> ... the tea was `of the commonest kind'. The brand was Young Green Hyson, but the [missionary] settlers nicknamed the foul brew `post and rails'. This was a reference to the twigs which floated about on the surface, large enough to form the posts and rails of a wooden fence. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,16</i>)

>  But even that sparkling individual himself must be aware that "post and rail" tea and roasted corn coffee are "filth;" and sugar impregnated with arsenic is unfortunately "poison." <i>(Dec 23, 1869,Wellington Independent,5</i>)

>  Even Madame Melba is alleged to have imbibed smoking "post and rail" tea, but I expect the billy was silver, encrusted with diamonds. <i>(Apr 25, 1903,New Zealand Free Lance,19</i>)

>  <b>JAMIESON</b> In those days of my childhood it was all China tea, the common kind went by the name of post and rail. 'Post and rail' meant it was more stem and very little leaf. <i>(1911,Diary,27</i>) [NOTES: in Jamieson (1916) Old Memories]

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> .. bread cost only 6d. or 7d. a 4lb. loaf, and tea (post and rails) could be bought for 1/- per lb. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,91</i>) [NOTES: from 1954 edition: Many of the old station cooks, with the assistance of an `offsider' (mate) found no difficulty in cooking for fifty or sixty men. .. it has to be remembered that .. bread cost only 6d. or 7d. a 4lb. loaf, and tea (post and rails) could be bought for 1/- per lb. Some of these contract cooks were great fossickers and managed to find quite a lot of green stuff for vegetables ...]

>  <b>VANCE</b> 'post and rail tea' - tea stalks floating on top of the tea brew. <i>(1965,High Endeavour,52</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i>

<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) land fenced with post and rail fences. [i.e. settled land.]

>  The run was over a stiff post-and-rail country, giving considerable practice to the leaping capacities of the horses. <i>(Jan 20, 1866,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

 <i>adj.</i> after-ball

### post-colonial traumatic stress disorder
 
 <i>phr.</i> [also variants below]

>  The dollar, he reckons, is the result of post-traumatic stress after the Cullen-isation of New Zealand. <i>(Sep 30, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

>  He died of post-colonial stress disorder. No one was to blame except Pakeha. <i>(Oct 28, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),21</i>)

>  Maori are suffering from Post-Colonial Traumatic Stress Disorder, Associate Maori Affairs Minister Tariana Turia said today. <i>(Aug 28, 2000,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  Associate Maori Affairs Minister Taraiana Turia may have succeeded in introducing "post-colonial traumatic stress disorder" to New Zealand's English language... Ms Ramsden said that while post-traumatic stress disorder was a clinical disease, post-colonial traumatic stress disorder was a social disease. <i>(Aug 31, 2000,Dominion,2</i>)

>  After Tariana Turia's speech to the Psychological Society's conference in Hamilton, in which she coined the phrase "post-colonial traumatic stress disorder", it has become a matter of some urgency for New Zealanders of all races to speak out in defence of their history. <i>(Sep 1, 2000,Dominion,8</i>)

### post-hui
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  Post-hui progress [headline] Following Te hui a nga kaimahi o Aotearoa, regional offices report a variety of local activity. In Auckland, a special position on the section management committee has been created for one person responsible for following up resolutions from the hui. <i>(Jun 18, 1986,PSA Journal,7</i>)

### post-materialist
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  Many people are becoming less materialistic... "Our standard of living has increased so much we can, more or less, take [our next meal] for granted, and for these people, their interest is becoming post-materialist." Dr Wilson thinks most at risk would be those yanked back to the farm after a spell at university "exposed to all those nasty post-materialist concerns". <i>(Feb 25, 2000,Dominion,16</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (historical)

 <i>n.</i> machine for boring postholes

>  Post hole borer: machine to bore a hole in the ground in which the post is inserted. <i>(Mar 1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,29</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) an implement for boring holes in the ground for posts.

>  The 'Quick-Dig' post-hole borer. <i>(Oct 2, 1950,NZ Dairy Exporter,9</i>)

>  Post hole borer for tree planting.. <i>(Jan 18, 1951,NZ Farmer,11</i>)

>  New posthole borer: a new portable, hydraulic-powered boring machine is said to bore postholes quickly and easily in most types of ground. <i>(Oct 1974,NZ Journal of Agriculture,77</i>)

>  In the distance a figure with a blue cap wielded a motor-driven posthole borer. <i>(Jul 21, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),37</i>)

>  Our posthole borer didn't have a clutch and when you hit something underground .. it's swing you around on your outstretched arms like daddy used to at the beach. [post-hole borer] <i>(Dec 2000,Sunday Star Times,C3</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an implement for boring holes in posts for post-and-rail fences.

### posthole digger
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a posthole borer.

### postie
 
 <i>n.</i> [(Dunedin)]

>  <b>HISLOP</b> As I write this letter ... at Opoko the postie girl hands me a pair of ducks ... from my old friend Cecil. <i>(1943,Pure Gold,73</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) the person who delivers letters. (spg occas. posty) a Post Office worker who delivers the mail; (formerly) postman. [This term has, I consider, become general in N.Z. only after WW2, prob'y from ca1950; first as an urban endearment (everyone loves the postman or postwoman. - but cf the quot. below).]

>  He dropped out to become a postie and play in a band, giving it up when he realised he wasn't going to "cut it as a muso." <i>(Dec 5, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),43</i>)

>  He maintains a serious commitment to his postie round... <i>(May 10, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),11</i>)

>  Sir, - According to a recent report in `The Dominion' the Chief Postmaster at Wellington is reported to have used the term `posties' in referring to certain of his staff of postmen and postwomen. This is a slang term, derogatory and discourteous, like the term `cop' for police officer, `cockie' for farmer, and `rag' for newspapers. He should be asked to apologize to these diligent people who work on Saturdays when most other workers enjoy this day off duty, and when speaking about his staff should in future refer to them by thier correct and proper designation, postmen and postwomen. GEORGE H. DAVIS. New Plymouth. <i>(Jul 25, 1963,Dominion,4</i>)

>  <b>DAVIS</b> The sack as usual,' ... `Break it down, you're worse than '... Getting the wife's mother. But ... I did get the bullet in the end-through my brothers silly cow. <i>(1964,Watersiders,108</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,85</i>) [NOTES: [see quotation SLOT]]

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,79</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> `Halloo,' comes the cheerful trill, ... `Postie.' <i>(1979,Skin Deep,128</i>)

>  Some [N.Z.] words and senses get left out [of the Collins <i>Concise Dictionary</i>]. One might instanceM<i> pikelet, car conversion</i> and <i>Taranaki gate ; boatie</i> is in but not <i>postie</i> or <i>yachtie </i>(used recently in a 1YA newscast), <i>garryowen</i> but not <i>willieaway. </i> <i>(Aug 20, 1983,Listener (New Zealand Listener),101</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> `Hooray,' says the postie, cheerfully. `Hooray to you too.' <i>(1983,Bone People, The,103</i>)

>  <b>RICKERTS</b> [Keri Hulme speaks] It was hard yakka. Then I became a postie. I shifted over to the West Coast because there was a senior postie position over there. <i>(1986,Talking About Ourselves,22</i>)

>  <i>postie n. NZ & Austral. colloq</i>. postman. [abbr.] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,588</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>ELDRED-GRIGG</b> The postie biked past twice a day ... The milko would rattle past with his cart of a morning ... cause we was getting milk through the Charitable Aid Board [c1930]. Then there was the rabbit-o, who sometimes talked Mum into buying a couple of his little skinned carcases. <i>(1987,Oracles and Miracles,40</i>)

>  ... cheerful `posties' ... the `posties' branch <i>(Dec 18, 1951,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  I was disgusted when the `posties' carried the number plates for cars some years ago. <i>(Jul 25, 1963,Dominion,4</i>)

>  <b>SNOWDON</b> The postie brought me a letter from Ailsa this morning. <i>(1933,Through Open Windows,27,1937 ed.</i>)

>  I was working as a postie then. <i>(Feb 1997,North & South,18</i>)

>  A spokesman for NZ Post, commenting on posties then and now, says of this photo: "The walkbags are now made of nylon, not canvas and leather." <i>(Dec 14, 1996,Listener (New Zealand Listener),10</i>)

>  I was a postie from 1964 to 1970. The fastest postie in Christchurch. <i>(Aug 10, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),50</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a general postal or Post Office worker

>  <b>SLATTER</b> There was [postal] work [in the 1960s], hard satisfying work. Unloading parcel bags, huge cumbersome containers nicknamed pre-fabs, from a three-door Z. ... The sorters' school at the posties' pub known colloquially as Box 44 or the B.B.C., the Back Bar of the Cecil. The cheerful abuse of cobbers <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,85</i>)

### pot
 
 <i>v.</i> to kick a goal

>  Each match that year .. Thompson potted a goal for the Pirates. <i>(Jul 18, 1889,Otago Witness,27</i>)

>  Murray (Oriental) managed deftly to pot agoal. <i>(May 27, 1912,Dominion,5</i>)

>  The ball had emerged from a loose scrimmage [sic] on the 25 yards of the Clutha, when, after some active and dexterous play on both sides, Adams, a Union player, very smartly and very neatly "potted" a goal. <i>(May 26, 1882,Clutha Leader,5</i>)

>  MacFarlane converted M'Kenzie's try, while M'Kenzie himself very cleverly potted a goal. <i>(Jun 27, 1910,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  McSorriley and Leitch scored for the home team and H. Ferguson potted a goal for Ohai. <i>(Jun 10, 1930,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,2</i>)

>  I have seen a boy pot a goal and not know he was doing it. <i>(1888,Otago University Capping Book,206</i>) [NOTES: 1888 Latin macaronic in Otago Univ. Review in 1923 College Rhymes 21 -->1968 Slatter Pagan Game 206]

>  <b>BAKER</b> An old boy "chum" - son of a chief.. had joined the group by the roadside, to inform the young man, with whom he had in the past struggled many times on football grounds for the mastery of the ball, or the potting of a goal that he now was a missionary amongst his people. <i>(1884,Daughter of King,178</i>)

>  The advantage was shortly followed up by Laurenson potting a goal for the Kaikorai, thus increasing the score to 6 points. <i>(May 8, 1890,Otago Witness,2</i>) [NOTES: check page, 2 or 20-something]

>  The game had hardly started when Francis.. potted a splendid goal. <i>(Jul 11, 1928,NZ Free Lance,47</i>)

>  The Canterbury match was very interesting,.. the visitors sticking closely to the "potting" tactics as a means to score. <i>(Oct 1, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,12</i>)

>  Lusk potted a goal from the field in good style. <i>(Aug 22, 1887,Evening Post,2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a pit (See [(cf. pothole)](../(#(-pothole)))

>  Many of the larger chunks of ore are highly cavernous, and exhibit numerous `pots', sometimes empty, again containing earthy matter. <i>(1907,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No.3 (N.S.): 76</i>) [NOTES: (Bell-Nelson)]



 
 <i>n.</i> a drop-kicked (field) goal [kick]

>  Cresswell attempted a pot, but somehow or other he missed it, / <i>(1888,Otago University Capping Book</i>) [NOTES: 1888 Latin macaronic in Otago Univ. Review in 1923 College Rhymes 21]

>  As in England, drop-kicking is conspicuous by its absence, unless when resorting to a `pot'. <i>(1900,NZ Illustrated Magazine,3/237</i>)

>  .. in an even game with no tries on; he's certain to frighten the enemy with a `pot' which ... will skim the paint off the uprights. <i>(1908,British Rugby Team in Maoriland,33</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> .. Dave kicked his usual pot for them ... <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,74</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> The Ruamahanga forwards bullock into the ruck and Keith Maxwell, given the ball, takes a snap pot at goal ... <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,217</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Not daunted, Cooke took a snap pot at the goal but Norris marked it and kicked to touch at halfway. <i>(1907,On the Ball,109</i>)

>  <b>HOWITT</b> With time almost up and Wanganui ahead 12-11, Brown tried for a `pot'. The kick missed and Wanganui forced. <i>(1975,NZ Rugby Greats,20</i>)

>  <b>SMITH</b> A five-eighth had three pots at goal. <i>(1949,From N to Z,117</i>)

>  [Caption] Take a pot. <i>(Jun 6, 1928,NZ Free Lance,37</i>)

>  Francis.. made a clever sharp "pot," sending the ball well between the bars from about the 25 line. <i>(Aug 20, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,11</i>)

>  They marvelled too at the flypaper hands / and the outrageous 'pots' he'd so often land. <i>(Jun 11, 2005,Listener (New Zealand Listener),55</i>)

>  .. when a smart pass from C. Fairbrother to Baumber enabled that player to make a pot at goal, the ball falling a few feet short. <i>(Aug 22, 1887,Evening Post,2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a drink, a jar, a flagon; often pl, in phrase <i>a few pots</i>; or <i>a quiet pot,</i> a drink (esp. a beer) (The NZ usage seems more specific than OED sb1 2.b ellipt. A pot of liquor; transf. liquor, drink; drinking, potation. (also pl) cf <i>Cup</i> sb 10. 1583-1849. It may however be merely standard English. Part: usage seems not as specific as in NZ I have only heard this usage post WW2; ? introduced from Britain) [? cf WF n(5) + (6)]

>  <b>McCALLUM</b> [I] go down to the pub to have a few pots with the boys <i>Ibid.</i> I ... have just settled down to enjoy a quiet pot ... when ... <i>(1963,Half-Gallon Jar,18</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to inform on; cf. put one's pot on

>  <b>SLATTER</b> He's a moral to get potted - Shock treatment for the old brewers' asthma <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,164</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> cook in a pot

>  <b>DUFF</b> A woman of seventy asked me today why I don't `pot' my rooster. <i>(Jan 25, 1915,Shepherd's Calendar,14,1961 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> sc. crayfish, to catch in set pots

>  <b>WILSON</b> These runcrays are either males or juvenile females, and they are usually potted on the outer edges of the rough bottom where it merges into sand, or on light rough bottom where the rock outcrops are small. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,41</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a crayfish pot

>  <b>McGILL</b> He lived at Kaikoura and kept a few pots and took the kids out fishing. Not that he ate crays anymore. <i>(1985,G'day Country,120</i>)

 <i>n.</i> old man; parent [ca1949 Havelock (Len Climo) (fairly well-known in NZ. Perhaps brought back by 2NZEF, WW2) . rhyming slang]

 <i>v.</i> to attack, snipe at

### pot dip
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a round dip in which sheep are plunged to kill lice and other pests.

>  <b>RIDDOLLS</b> The pot dip: this type has been evolved to meet the needs of smaller flocks. <i>(1958,Farm Engineering,359</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> After the beautiful big long swim dip at Te Horoa, Ohinewairua had a dreadful little pot dip which was a horror among horrors. <i>(1973,Boot in the Stirrup,17</i>)

>  <b>THORNTON</b> An alternative to the long swim dip or race type was the 'pot.' <i>(1986,NZ Heritage of Farm Buildings,213</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### pot-licker
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a friendly retiring working dog, always cajoling for a feed.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> ... although his own dog was only a pot-licker he'd take him up on the bet. <i>(1975,Sixty Thousand on the Hoof,174</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> pot-licker: a back-door pensioner; a dog whose working days are over. It is not really a derogatory word, but implies freedom and latitude as reward for a lifetime of faithfulness. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,63</i>)

>  <b>BEACH</b> We all piled into Shane's short wheelbase Safari top Landrover, me in the back with the potlickers and headed out of Kaikoura ... <i>(1993,Over 30 Years Pig Hunting,102</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> The young would-be musterer, with the confidence of youth thought he recognised in Towser a sheep dog of great potential and so, with this pack of questionable pot-lickers, he joined the gang. [potlicker] <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,112</i>)

### pot-stirrer
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>pot-stirrer</i> political or social activist adjudged to enjoy upsetting the status quo; eg `That Minto's a real pot-stirrer, eh?' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,87</i>)

### potae
 
 <i>n.</i> a hat, head covering. [Maori potae[o-macron]] [ca1900 Auckland P.S.Ardern Quite commonly used for<i> hat. </i> ca1935 Havelock (? jocularly used; as also was <i>tile</i> from the popular song `Where did you get that hat?') ca1965 Jan Heard in Wellington `Crikey, cop that potae.']

>  His predecessor as Minister of Maori Affairs, Tau Henare, once said the most significant thing Mr Samuels did during that term was to get the rules changed so he could wear his famed potae (hat)in the house. <i>(Feb 22, 2000,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  The words<i> potae</i> `hat' and <i>puku</i> `stomach' are still current, but North Island words mainly. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,170</i>)

>  <b>LATHAM</b> ... and we made our own hats. Believe me, there were some beauts! One Maori girl's remark summed it up in a lot of cases - `Call that plurry thing a potae (hat)? Looks more like a mimi pot!' <i>(1986,WAAC Story, The,3</i>)

>  <b>IRVINE</b> Again, the New Zealander has drawn less upon Maori words than the Australian has called upon the Aborigine. However `puku' (stomach) and `potae' (hat) are freely used in New Zealand. <i>(1962,Australian Universal Dictionary,3</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Potai; A hat, cap, pot-lid; any round covering. [?sailor's hat] <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,195</i>)

>  hat - po'tai [potae] <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal</i>) [NOTES: c1826-1827 list of Maori words in c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) 111 ; also 1826-28 Boultbee Vocabulary in 1979 Begg 283]

>  <b>POLACK</b> Coverings for the head ( <i>potai</i>) are rarely made use of. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/180</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Potai - a hat, cap, pot-lid <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/380</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> How pretty she'll look in her new <i>potai</i>. Te-na. [fn] <i>Potai</i>, cap or hat. <i>(1881,Poenamo,2/3/71</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> ... my hat blew off, ... /I ran to retrieve my potai, ... <i>(1946,Farthest North,71</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> You could make a hat that would cost pounds in the shop, she breathed as she sewed the velvet to the crown .... when Eric came in. `Do you like my potai?' ... `What, can't you afford a decent hat?' <i>(1960,Maori Girl,147</i>)

>  <b>MACKAY</b> Mine Host set the example, and when that potai was returned to its bewildered owner, it took him some time to adjust the extra load. <i>(1961,Puborama,53</i>)

>  <b>PARKINSON</b> A Vocabulary of the Language of New Zealand. [...] Potai, <i>A feather ornament on their head. </i> <i>(1770,Journal,126</i>)

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> A hat Poti <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/338</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> Then raising their 'Poti' (Maori name for hat) they chanted good morning and trudged back to college. <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,87</i>)

>  I am a fan of the new Maori translation of Te Poti ro Potae (The Cat in the Hat) <i>(Apr 28, 2004,Capital Times,12</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> Sean jammed his green cocky's potae tightly on his head as the wind blew harder. <i>(2000,Kokopu Dreams,160</i>)

>  The potae (or "tea cosy thing" as Orange describes it) was on the head of Pita Wairua, who sat in as an adviser at Auckland University studies classes Orange attended in the mid-1970s. <i>(Oct 31, 2004,Sunday Star Times,C6</i>)

>  So - put on your potai in the lateststyle, / And smile, boys, smile. <i>(Sep 29, 1916,Chronicles NZEF,I/3 : 55</i>)

>  <b>STARK</b> hat - potai [potae] <i>(1986,Journal,111</i>) [NOTES: quote from 1827-28 BOULTBEE; grave accent on "o" of "potae"]

>  How pretty she'll look in the new potae. <i>(Jun 29, 1912,NZ Free Lance,4</i>)

>  I ket Winnie Hawkena's poti (hat) from the keyser. <i>(Apr 14, 1923,Aussie (NZ Section),X</i>)

>  <b>PARK</b> Ah, the King Country, the Rohe Potae, the brim of the hat, as the dispossessed Maoris scornfully called it. <i>(1992,Fence Around the Cuckoo,11</i>)

 <i>v.</i>

 <i>n.</i> (a. an orchid b. an old variety usu. a kidney grown from stock handed on by Maori people c. occas. (?Aust) poroporo)

 <i>n.</i> a Maori person who lives as a Pakeha

<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> black nightshade

<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> potato fern [?is this an Austr. entry taken over from Morris (368) potato fern; and native potato = a sort of yam: no other NZ reference]

>  <i>potato-fern; potato, native n</i>. fern with edible tubers, roasted and eaten. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),1968</i>)

<b>5</b> <i>n.</i>

### potato bird
 
 <i>n.</i> cuckoo

In many parts of Otago and Canterbury they were called by the early settlers `Potato-birds', as they invariably came on the scene as the potato-planting was going on; similarly, in the North Island they got the name of `Kumara-birds'. <i>(1904,TrNZI,36/134</i>)

>  In many parts of Otago and Canterbury they [long-tailed cuckoos] were called potato birds by the early settlers as they invariably came on the scene as the potato-planting was going on; similiarly, in the North Island they got the name of `Kumara-birds'. <i>(1940,TrNZI,36/134</i>) [NOTES: (Fulton)]

### pothead
 
 <i>n.</i> a worthless person, fool (a loan translation of Maori upoko kohua, i.e. someone whose head is fit only for the cooking pot) [(Finlayson 141) Pothead: translation of a Maori insult more serious than it sounds in English.]

### pothole
 
 <i>n.</i> [mining]

>  The former [lake] has no visible outlet, but was supposed by Dr. Hochstetter to have an underground communication with the latter [lake]. I am disposed to differ ... and to look upon [lake] Tikitapu as a mere pot-hole ... <i>(1877,TrNZI,9/10</i>)

>  The few plants I saw were growing on the edges of `potholes' - i.e., small pits from which gum has been dug - a rather unusual situation. <i>(1911,TrNZI,43/203</i>)

>  All hands.. are gone or going to theCreek.. which has often been pronounced"a duffer". Here.. a party got into a"pot-hole" and had got for two days' worksixteen ounces of gold. <i>(Mar 4, 1869,Westport Times,2</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Here and there strips of rocks could be peeled easily.. and slung down a nearby pothole - potholes were always handy, usually breathing out a cold clammy air from some ghastly Thing far below... The rim of green grass and thriving green branches of pothole-living trees, lured several beasts to their doom. <i>(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,109</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [mining]

>  The "pothole" may have become overlaid with some deposit, which induced the men to suppose that they had made a great discovery. <i>(Oct 12, 1865,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  Gardner and party (Venture Claim) got this week 87oz out of a pot-hole in one day. <i>(Dec 12, 1889,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  I intended washing up on Saturday, but coming across an extensive pothole on Wednesday night I have determined to bottom this before washing up. <i>(Feb 6, 1892,Tuapeka Times,3</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a vertical cave or sink-hole [ in which sheep can easily fall and starve to death.]

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> Over the years the potholes, with their smooth sides, had claimed many victims. The whole high block, known as the Blowholes, was riddled with pot-holes. <i>(1993,Station Country I,45</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> We would roam the hills after school and at weekends clambering over outcrops of rock, or finding potholes to throw stones down. <i>(2000,Mountain Farm, Bush Farm,39</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a depression created in kauri gumlands following prospecting.

>  The land is of a very broken nature, owing to it having been worked over for gum, which left innumerable pot-holes and hummocks. [pothole] <i>(Feb 15, 1912,NZ Journal of Agriculture,111</i>)

### potstick
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [sp. to early wool scouring] a long wooden pole used to stir and lift wool during the scouring process.

>  <b>BREMNER</b> <i>Potstick</i> wooden pole 6 feet long and 2 inches thick, used to stir and lift wool during the wash. <i>Ibid.</i> 17 The potstick woolwashers were the industry's founders. <i>Ibid.</i> A hot soak preceded the cold-water wash, and in the Medieval manner a strong arm wielded the six-foot potstick or the sluice fork stirring and lifting sodden wool. Scouring machines were a remote dream. <i>Ibid.</i> 34 Even in the mid-1960s, recalls Brian Redding, the present owner , `they were potsticking slipe wool from the fellmongery; via a wooden chute, water went into a box with a mesh base and a fellow stirred [35] the wool with a stick!' <i>(1985,Woolscours of NZ,9</i>)

>  <b>BREMNER</b> From the scouring tank, wool was pushed down a slide and swished around with pot sticks in perforated zinc washboxes ... [pot stick] <i>(1985,Woolscours of NZ,49</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. the old ewes, practically valueless, went to the works as potters. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,88</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> potter: animal only suitable for slaughter for its byproducts. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,63</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### pottle
 
 <i>n.</i> 1. see punnet. 2. a small plastic or cardboard container for chips, yoghurt, etc.

>  <b>TURNER</b> The words <i>pottle, punnet</i> and <i>chip</i> are all used for a container for strawberries and investigation of the regional distribution of these words might be of interest ... <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,178</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>pottle</i>: The South Island term for a SMALL CONTAINER. Originally applied to the little wooden boxes in which berry fruit was sold, it then transferred to the plastic boxes which replaced them. A further extension of the term applied it to small plastic containers of yoghurt and sour cream. (see: <i>punnet, chip</i>) <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,79</i>)

>  <i>pottle n. NZ</i> open cardboard or plastic container in which potato chips etc. are sold; closed container (for yoghurt). <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,589</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  Many people here in the South Island, where the best strawberries are grown, refer to a small strawberry container as a pottle. On the June 29 programme one of the teams gave the answer `pottle' to a question about what small strawberry containers were called, and were then penalised for a wrong answer. Their puzzled expressions were quite understandable to anyone living south of Cook Strait. <i>(Aug 13, 1983,Listener (New Zealand Listener),12</i>)

### pou
 
 <i>n.</i> post, pole, stake

>  [Hdg] Preparing pillars and Maori mats The entrance to Government House, ... will soon have a Taranaki flavour - carved pillars and woven mats made in the Te Atiawa (Taranaki) style Petone Technical Institute students carved pou out of a 100-year-old totara pile ... Te Atiawa women are still plaiting the mat to be laid at the main door. <i>(Oct 31, 1986,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  The pou, as it is known, also honours the memory of all Moriori ancestors. <i>(Jul 10, 2004,Dominion Post,E6</i>)

>  The house has at its heart two polished pou, vertical poles around which a spiral staircase winds, drawing the eye upward to the ridge of the house - just as in a wharenui. <i>(May 10, 2003,Dominion Post,F14</i>)

>  Mikara has travelled with whanau / friends from Rerenga Wairua and will keep walking to ki Whanganui a Tara (Wellington), "following the pou - making sure it's kept warm and safe." The pou whenua is Dame Whina Cooper's carved flagpole, born from Te Hapua to Poneke during the 1975 Land March. <i>(May 3, 2004,Salient,8/52</i>)

>  If they side with the aunties, "who to paint the pou? Who to call the dead home? Who to keep the fire burning?" <i>(May 11, 2001,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  <b>Ihimaera</b> There are a number of carved posts which support the structure of the meeting house; the main ones are the poutokomanawa, the heart post, and the poutahuhu, the ridge post. In the old days, the pou, which also included wall posts, were said to be living, sacred, able to talk. <i>(2007,Ask the Posts,23</i>)

>  Last year, for example, the couple created the carved wooden pou and woven tukutuku panel for the New Zealand embassy in Warsaw. <i>(Aug 3, 2006,Dominion Post,B7</i>)

>  When discussing the Pao Miere movement in the King Country, she forgets that the lettering and incisions on one pou (pillar) of the cruciform, assembly house included the date 1887, which was a deliberate record of the revitalisation of the movement by the leader and builder of the house, Te Ra Karepe. <i>(Apr 2, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),109</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a boundary post; a conservation warning

>  Emphasising the conservation ethic, local Maori have erected a pou rahui (above) on the bank of the Opanuku, visible to all who use the river. <i>(Nov 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 60/56</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a centre back pillar (Ryan) [ORIGIN: Maori]

 <i>n.</i> (See [pohutukawa](per-pop.md#pohutukawa))

### poua
 
 <i>n.</i> a mollusc

>  Very much interested in a large stratum of lignite to be seen under the sandcliffs [at Moiatoa], kapia still in ... Watched the karoros carrying up the pouas & letting them drop to break the shells... Saw my first korora (dead) <i>(Jan 30, 1861,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/685,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson)]

>  <b>IHIMAERA</b> The hill was a gigantic crescent of eleven terraces, like the poutama pattern, the wondrous Stairway to Heaven. <i>(1986,Matriarch, The,1</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a large moa (See [also pouakai](../A#also-pouakai))

>  I knew from various sources that the Morioris had a tradition of a great bird they called the pou[diaresis]wa. <i>(1897,TrNZI,29/163</i>) [NOTES: 1893 May Fortnightly Review 669 (H.O. Forbes) in 1897 TrNZI 29/163]

>  In this paper I will endeavour to prove that the large, mythological, and extinct bird the poua, traditionally spoken of by the remaining surrvivors of the Moriori people who inhabit the Chatham Islands, was not allied to the moa ... but was a species of swan, and belonged to the family <i>Anatidae</i>. <i>(1897,TrNZI,29/162</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> Post-European re-working of different legends about big birds.. seems even more evident in the names poua and pouaki. These were alleged by Tare te Maiharoa an influential South Island chief about the turn of the 20th century to be the true, ancient names for moas. <i>(1989,Prodigious Birds,91</i>)

>  It appears that the Morioris have traditions about a great bird, called by them Puoa... The Morioris have a song about it, and repeat the first syllable as a chorus, Pu, pu - Pu, pu - Pu, o-a, in a manner which recalls the hollow drumming noise made by the emu. The word is probably a contraction for Pu-pu-moa. <i>(1875,TrNZI,VII/117</i>) [NOTES: This is the article by Cockburn-Hood referred to by Anderson in "Prodigious Birds"]

### pouakai
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> Post-European re-working of different legends about big birds.. seems even more evident in the names poua and pouaki. These were alleged by Tare te Maiharoa an influential South Island chief about the turn of the 20th century to be the true, ancient names for moas. <i>(1989,Prodigious Birds,91</i>)

>  But he is Maori and, because of that, he and Hamish are able to recognise that the rampaging killer of the station's sheep is a feathery prehistoric eagle-taniwha, the Pouakai. <i>(Mar 9, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),62</i>)

>  Among the fearsome creatures that stalked New Zealand in days gone by was the pouakai - an eagle with a three-metre wing-span and the impact of a Mack truck. It preyed on the moa - and on humans - and is estimated to have been among the largest of all feathered predators. <i>(Aug 7, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),34</i>)

>  There is an ancient wonder-tale about a huge predatory bird called the pouakai.. This pouakai..was large enough to carry human beings off to its nest or lair... The name Hapagornis has been given to this prehistoric species. <i>(Feb 1938,NZ Railways Magazine,64</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a teacher [ORIGIN: Maori, (Maori ako = learn, teach)]
