### pouhaki
 
 <i>n.</i> a flagpole [ORIGIN: Maori, pohaki (x2) sic in citation? (from the net) Pouhaki Flagpole Although not always carved the Pou-haki is used primarily during Tangi funerals. During the tangi the flag is flown at half mast while the Tupapaku deceased is on the Marae. The Pouhaki is also used during major Hui conferences/meetings...]

>  It consists of two flagpoles, pohaki, and a treasure box, waka huia. One of the pohaki represents the natural world, from Papauanuku to the animals and birds of the forest. <i>(Nov 16, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,9</i>)

### poukai
 
 <i>n.</i> any of a round of annual hui attended by the Maori King (or, formerly, Queen) in Kingite territory [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Home in Waikato, Tawhiao sought solutions to Maori problems through the establishment of Maori institutions to deal with them. In 1885 he initiated the institution of Poukai, where the King would pay annual visits to King movement marae to encourage people to return to their home marae at least once a year. The first Poukai (originally called Puna-kai, or 'source of food') was held at Whatiwhatihoe in March 1885. It was a day for the less fortunate to be fed and entertained. The Poukai developed into an event which would later ensure direct consultation of the people with the King. <i>(1993,Dictionary of NZ Biography,2</i>) [NOTES: from the DNZB entry on King Tawhiao (R.T. Mahuta) (updated online version 2007)]

>  KOKOHINAU Marae in Te Teko had the honour of hosting, for the first time in the Mataatua area, the Maori King, Tuheitia on Sunday.... Mr Eruera said the king was attending a poukai, or assembly, and this was the 44th year it had been held at Kokohinau. ... A copy of a publication celebrating the 44 years of the poukai being held at Kokohinau was also given to the king by Katerina Waiari. Teriaki Amoamo, on behalf of Whakatohea and Te Whanau-a-Apanui, presented a ceremonial hoe waka (canoe paddle).Mr Eruera said the open debate session, which is what a poukai is all about, raised a few issues. One of those issues was brought up earlier in the day by Whakatohea and Te Whanau-a-Apanui who requested to share the poukai in the future. Mr Eruera said the request would have to go to hapu members to consider.A restored flagpole was also erected - hapu members, under the direction of carver Graham Junior Niao, carried out the restoration on the carved pole - and the special poukai flag was flown. <i>(Jan 18, 2007,Whakatane Beacon,1</i>)

### poukatea
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [pukatea](pop-pyo.md#pukatea))

### poultice
 
 <i>n.</i> a large sandwich

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>poultice</i> a large sandwich using everything in the fridge or pantry, akin to American dagwood without the toothpicks; eg `Looks like you've got the whole deli in that poultice, dear. Don't bolt it down, will you, or it'll be back to see you tonight.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,87</i>)

### poumarumaru
 
 <i>n.</i> a translation of English Board of Trustees [ORIGIN: Maori, (maru = shelter, marumaru = offering shade)]

>  The whanau, te poumarumaru (board of trustees) and pouako (teachers) demonstrate a strong commitment to their defined longterm aspiration. <i>(Jul 20, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,5</i>)

### pounamu
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> The best<i> pounamu </i>pendants were worn by the chiefs ... <i>(1941,Numismatic History of NZ,8</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> Te Pehi ... [was] killed treacherously by some of Tamaiharanui's tribe, among whom he had trusted himself, in order to barter muskets for Pounamu stone, ... <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,4</i>)

>  This strange weaving caused the young woman, with the sunset hair and the pounamu eyes, to be branded a witch and banished from the village where she was born. <i>(Feb 5, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),52</i>)

>  The Chairman turned on the pounamu floor. <i>(Jan 2, 2003,Capital Times,9</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> greenstone [alternate spelling: Poonamoo, Poenammoo, Poanamo, Poeananiamiou; note also inanga / inanga pounamu, whitish variety]

>  In the old days the cutting and working of pounamu (greenstone) was a most laborious operation, and it often took years to work a mere or axe to the desired shape and smoothness. <i>(Dec 21, 1904,Otago Witness,21</i>)

>  They found large quantities of the ponamu or green stone, some of which was clear as glass. <i>(May 6, 1863,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,3</i>)

>  No ponamu was found. <i>(Aug 27, 1853,New Zealand Spectator & Cook's Strait Guardian,3</i>)

>  [Photo caption] CASKET PRESENTED TO THE DUKE AND DUCHESS OF CORNWALL AND YORK. Made of greenstone, gold, and silver .. A souvenir of their Royal Highnesses' visit to the land of the Moa and the Pounamu. <i>(Jun 12, 1901,Otago Witness,41</i>)

>  .. In this river is found the stone used by the natives for rubbing down their ponamu, in quality resembling a Newcastle stone, though somewhat closer in the grain and texture, and has a fine cutting quality. <i>(Oct 28, 1848,New Zealand Spectator & Cook's Strait Guardian,3</i>)

>  .. During the time that he lay on the beach, he had, unknown to everyone, concealed beneath his shirt a "rakau-pounamu" or weapon made from a stone called "pounamu", which belonged to him and Taiaroa. <i>(Mar 31, 1852,New Zealander, The,2</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> Among the fossil productions of this country, we must likewise reckon a green stone, which sometimes is opaque, and sometimes quite transparent ... We asked for its native place, and they called it <i>Poenamoo</i> [e-dieresis], from whence the abovementioned part of the country obtained the denomination of Tavai Poenamoo [e-dieresis] ... <i>(1778,Observations Made During a Voyage,6</i>)

>  <b>McCRAE</b> They then remarked that that might be the way of the Pakeha (or white men) but with them the belief was was that their God had fished up New Zealand with three fish hooks made of Poeananiamiou a green serpentine ... <i>(1820,Journal,23,1928 ed.</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> .. a dark-gray and black-coloured granite, termed <i>onewoa</i> by the natives was made use of in its stead, or the green talc, <i>poeanamu</i>[a2-acute] and pieces of red jasper chipped off adroitly, resembling in shape our common gun-flints. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1.194</i>)

>  <b>MONRO</b> 1844 Monro Notes of a Journey (Nelson Examiner July 20 et seq.) in 1898 Hocken Contributions 261 <i>(1844,Notes of a Journey</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Monro Notes of a Journey (Nelson Examiner July 20 et seq.) in 1898 Hocken Contributions 261]

>  Mr. Fyfe had found a moa's egg, at the Kai Koras, while digging the foundation for his store. It is a foot long, about 6 inches in diameter, and 27 inches in circumference. The shell is the 16th part of an inch in thickness. A hole is drilled in the end of it, and the egg must have been considered of great value by the natives, as it was found deposited at the head of a skeleton, with a number of very large poenamu axes. Mr. Fyfe, we understand, is going to send them home to the British Museum. <i>(Aug 5, 1857,Lyttelton Times,5</i>)

>  <b>CAPT. RAVEN</b> They [natives] took to the woods, and we never saw them again [at Dusky Bay], nor did Mr Leith see any inhabitants during his residence at Poenammoo. He once found a fire, but the natives had fled at his approach. <i>(Nov 2, 1793,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1793 Nov 2 Capt. Raven to Lieut-Governor King in 1908 McNab Historical Records 1/179]

>  <b>McDONNELL</b> Coffee, sugar, indigo, and rice, would succeed well on the North Island, as also all the tropical fruits. New Zealand, more particularly Poenammoo, is yet but little known. <i>(1834,Extracts From a Journal,20,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PARKINSON</b> [South Island] He had on a flaxen garment .. and under it a petticoat, made of a sort of cloth which they call Aooree Waow[o-grave]; on his ears hung a bunch of teeth, and an ear-ring of Poonamoo, or green stone. <i>(Feb 8, 1770,Journal,119</i>)

>  ... their greatest branch of trade is for the green talk or stone (called by them Poenammoo) a thing of no sort of value. <i>(Nov 13, 1773,Cook's Journals,2/189,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BAYLY</b> The Indians are fond of green stone they have among them which they call Poanamo. It is a kind of Jasper. With this they make their Togie, or ads of & chissles to carve with, & bobs for their years [ears] & gorgets to hang at the breast. <i>(Jun 4, 1773,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1773 June 4 Bayly's Journal in 1908 McNab Historical Records 2/209]

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> P|ou n|amu; A green stone so called. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,195</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> [spg] `Manatungos,' or remembrancers .. are always either suspended round the neck or to the ear; they are mostly made of the ponamu, the green stone found only in the Southern Island. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),151</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> The native ornaments worn in the ears are pieces of the ponamu (nephrite, or oriental jade), which are called e tara ... <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/54</i>)

>  In [the Arahura's] bed, after freshets, is found the <i>poenamu</i>, in shingles or slabs. <i>(1846,Notes of an Expedition to Kawatiri and Araura</i>) [NOTES: 1846 Heaphy Exped. to Kawatiri in 1959 Taylor Early Travs. 241]

>  <b>ANGAS</b> .. the most usual ornaments are ear-drops made of pieces of nephrite or green jade, called <i>ponamu</i> by the natives ... <i>(1847,Savage Life,1/327</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> The natives procure .. quantities of a green jade, or serpentine, much prized by them for [314] the purpose of making war-clubs .. It is called by them `Ponamu', whence the name once assigned to the Middle Island <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,313</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> During the time .. he had .. concealed beneath his shirt a `rakau-pounamu' or weapon made from the stone called `pounamu', which belonged to him and Taiaroa. <i>`Te Wahi Ponamu'</i> or, `The Place of Green Stone'. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,21</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> The most prized ornaments amongst them are those manufactured of nephrite or green jade .. [158] called `poenamu' by the natives. <i>(1866,Polynesia,157</i>)

>  Serpentine occurs in two forms - as common Serpentine .. and as noble Serpentine (<i>Tangiwai</i>) in thin veins associated with Jade .. ordinarily known as Maori Greenstone (<i>Poenamu</i>) occurs <i>in situ</i> on several parts of the West Coast ... <i>(1868,Province of Otago in NZ, The,39</i>)

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> A magnificent Mere punamu .. cut out of the most beautiful, transparent nephrite. <i>(1867,New Zealand (various authors/titles),362</i>) [NOTES: (Morris)]

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> Poenamo [title] <i>(1881,Poenamo</i>)

>  It is called <i>pounamu</i> or <i>poenamu</i> by the Maoris, and `jade,' `jadeite,' or `nephrite,' by various writers, while old books refer to the `green talc' of the Maoris. <i>(1892,TrNZI,24/481</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Pounamu</i>, or <i>Poenamu, n</i>. the Maori name for <i>Nephrite, Jade</i>, or <i>Greenstone</i> (q.v.). In the second spelling the eis hardly sounded. <i>(1898,Austral English,369</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> The Maori greenstone (<i>pounamu</i>) called jade, jadeite, nephrite, etc., by Europeans, is a mineral very restricted in its deposits. .. one variety (<i>tangiwai</i>) is found at Milford Sound. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,248</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> `Pounamu' (greenstone) was only to be found in the creeks of the inland ranges of [40] the Middle Island, and hence called `Te wai pounamu.' <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,39</i>)

>  <i>Pounamu</i> (Nephrite, or Greenstone). - Used for ornamnental purposes. <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/36</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> .. here is an appropriate moment to tell the story of the jadeite or <i>pounamu </i>which first won celebrity for the West Coast. <i>(1926,Travel in NZ,(Vol II South Island) 84</i>)

>  <i>pounamu</i>, greenstone, chiefly (and the best) found in Arahura. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/156</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i>pounamu:</i> greenstone. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,175</i>)

>  <i>pounamu, poenamu n</i>. Maori name for nephrite, jade or greenstone (q.v.) [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),623</i>)

>  <i>pounamu ... n</i>. Maori name for a variety of N.Z. nephrite, jade, or greenstone [used by Sir John Logan Campbell as the title of a book (Poenamu)]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),16</i>)

>  New Zealand greenstone is either the mineral nephrite (Maori: pounamu) or bowenite (Maori: tangiwai). <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,1/875</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> 14 Pounamu = New Zealand jade, also called `greenstone' <i>(1983,Bone People, The,465</i>)

>  <i>pounamu n. NZ</i> greenstone. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,589</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  Two islands .. which [Cook] called Tovy Poenammoo; the literal translation of this word is `the water of the green talc' ... <i>(1773,Cook's Voyages,2/400,Hawkesworth ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Morris)]

>  There is a very interesting history attached to the transportation of this monster piece of pounamu here. <i>(Jun 17, 1876,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

>  <b> NOTICE</B>Kiaora koutouI, Iris Herewini would love to thank everyone that has come into my life and shared my passion and love through receiving my pounamu, may your journeys through life be a spiritual one... <i>(Feb 10, 2006,Whakatane Beacon,45</i>)

>  Kahu Hoeter is stunning as Tiri Mahana; her performance as luminous and richly hued as fine pounamu, shifting smoothly from past to present and anger to aroha. <i>(Apr 13, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),62</i>)

>  <b>FIRTH</b> Among the Maori, considerations of aesthetic interest helped quite materially .. to decide the relative worth of objects made of the <i>pounamu</i>, the nephrite so much prized for pendants, <i>mere</i>, and adzes. Most prized was the <i>kahurangi</i> stone, bright green, translucent, hard and clear; the <i>inanga </i>variety perhaps comes next, with its white milky-veined appearance, 389] followed by the <i>kawakawa</i>, dark olive-green, dull and opaque. .. <i>Tangiwai</i> (`tear drop'), <i>bowenite</i>, a softer stone of a clear pale green, with the appearance of drops of water in the texture, was less esteemed, though more beautiful in the European eyes, whilst <i>kahotea</i>, green, spotted or streaked with black, was an inferior type, and was used for small chisels, and for objects of subordinate interest. <i>(1929,Primitive Economics of NZ Maori,388</i>)

>  <b>JOSEPH</b> `The jade, the pounamu .. It's a beautiful stone .. Look,' he said, holding up a long hooked ear-drop of pearly translucence, `that's tau tau and the stone is called inanga. And this, the pekapeka - they say the shape comes from the bat. This is for the ear, too - the mako, like a shark's tooth .. And these,' - he ran his fingers along a row of .. nephrite adzes - `toki. See this one' - he held the heavy red-mottled adze in his palm - `that's the red kind, totoweka, woodhen's blood. The deep green, that's kawakawa.' <i>(1962,Pound of Saffron, A,113</i>)

>  It [sc. a Maori expedition] consisted of twenty <i>mokis</i>, well freighted with pounamu. .. The greenstone (pounamu) was largely used for crucifixes and Roman Catholic Church symbols. <i>(Jan 16, 1900,NZ Mines Record,233</i>)

>  These days, though, greenstone is the new gold. For more than 20 years, chunks of pounamu have been carted out of the mists. <i>(May 29, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),22</i>)

>  They travelled .. to barter .. slabs of the tuhua, or obsidian, his being the only stone capable of cutting the paunamu, or greenstone, found in the South. <i>(Feb 16, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,14</i>)

>  The next day we roll up our figurative sleeping bags and head south to Greymouth to search for pounamu. <i>(Jul 2004,AA Directions,Spring/59</i>)

>  Keri's novel has the preciousness of a piece of "kuru pounamu" in its apparent simplicity of shape, because it is concerned with the universal theme of aroha. <i>(May 12, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),60</i>)

>  I can imagine that the most abiding memories for a lot of viewers will be of the hapu doggedly cultivating their muddy clearing, or the two figures with lacerated feet shuffling across a scree-slope on their trek for pounamu. <i>(Sep 19, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),100</i>)

>  Although Maori regularly traversed the Southern Alps to collect pounamu (greenstone) from the West Coast, the first written accounts are those of European explorers. <i>(Jul 2000,NZ Geographic,47/85</i>)

>  Wanaka was at the crossroads of the trading routes for pounamu, and a school of learning - whare kura - was established at the main Wanaka settlement of Takikarara. <i>(Nov 2001,AA Directions,50/29</i>)

>  Jade, nephrite, greenstone, pounamu - these are all names for a stone most New Zealanders know and revere.. Maori were almost certainly unfamiliar with pounamu when they arrived in Aotearoa, but they brought with them the art of stone tool-making. <i>(Mar 2003,NZ Geographic,62/8</i>)

>  <b>McNAUGHTON</b> I am polishing my grand-dad's tohu. It is a / small piece of pounamu, translucent and / shaped like an elongated tear. <i>(1986,Countless Signs,106</i>) [NOTES: From Keri Hulme (1982) : Getting to the Coast]



 
 <i>adj.</i> Geol.

>  A fellow club member and a senior student in the Chemistry Department, Jack Mitchell . . . demonstrated to me the power of the petrographic microscope by showing me slides of the Pounamu rocks from the Griffin Range in north Westland. . . . <i>(Feb 1982,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,55/34</i>)

### pound
 
 <i>v.</i> impound

>  We heard that a lot of our cows were pounded last night [in Ch.Ch.]. There is some consolation in knowing that the cattle doe [sic] the fences more harm than the pound does. <i>(Jul 31, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,51,1950 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> used in various transf. uses and fig. senses (often graded between 1 and 19) to express various conditions of alleged intellectual handicap, mental bankruptcy, implying that a person is not all there; see also bob, quid, shilling; the formula is usu.: to be/pay (only) [x] bob (shillings) in the pound or not to be 20 shillings in the pound; cf. not the full quid; ?a synonym from punting for to be a shingle short, be two bob in the pound, to be sixpence in the shilling [1951 14 M 14 St Bede's, Chch 2 POUND, NINETEEN AND ELEVEN IN THE]

>  <b>DAVIN</b> He wasn't a bad bloke even if he was only fifteen bob in the pound. <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,118</i>)

>  <b>F W VOSSELER</b> Not twenty shillings in the pound, not to be up to standard. <i>(1951,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: (a tramper)]

>  <b>HAMILTON</b> Good God, said Neil, he was barely six shillings in the pound, used to talk all day about the colours of a motor-bike he had on the outer. <i>(1953,Till Human Voices Wake Us,147</i>)

>  <b>FRAME</b> <i>(1962,Edge of the Alphabet,XI</i>)

>  <b>McCALLUM</b> I come to the idea that this pakeha is only about eighteen bob in the pound, so I give him the brush off on the excuse that I must go home ... <i>(1963,Half-Gallon Jar,37</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Every small town has its characters: ... Joey who was only eighteen shillings ... <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,153</i>)

>  <b>OLIVER</b> ... or they say he isn't/ quite the full pound though/ useful enough in the front/ row ... <i>(1982,Poor Richard,24</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [ca1941-45 p.c. Riverside Community `Where one is kept preparatory to sentence']

>  <b>HAMILTON</b> ... it's a pretty rare specimen who won't do anything for a man in the pound. <i>Ibid.</i> 83 The exercise yard of the pound is the place where they used to hang and flog. <i>(1953,Till Human Voices Wake Us,72</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> solitary confinement; a punishment cell or area

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> The day before the following weekend I was locked up in the pound. A pound is for locking up animals. If only we'd been animals the RSPCA and all the animal lovers would have caused a public outcry - but we were only humans so no one worried. There had been no reason for punishing me - the official reason given was that I was put there as an assistant cleaner. The cleaner already there had virtually nothing to do. So the whole deal was hell of an unusual - in fact no crim I met had ever before heard of a situation in which an inmate who'd committed no offence had been sent to the pound - especially as an assistant cleaner. <i>(1971,Bullshit and Jellybeans,15</i>)

>  <b>MacKENZIE</b> The punishment block, known as the `pound' or the `dummy', was a prison within the [Mt Eden] prison. In the basement, through a steel grille and a wooden door, it was a dimly lit and silent annexe leading at its far end to the tiny execution yard. <i>Ibid.</i> 98 <i>pound</i> punishment block <i>(1980,While We Have Prisons,14</i>)

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> You're in <i>jail now, son!</i> Another peep from you and you'll be down the fookin pound. <i>Ibid.</i> 145 So I settled back to do my bird. Callander came up and found I was in the pound ... . In the digger the days go by pretty slowly ... .<i>Ibid.</i> 252 POUND (n) Detention unit. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,25</i>)

>  The tale about being in the "pound" is told and retold; the rebel has returned to the fold with stature increased. What isn't mentioned is the torturous difficulty the "hero" had in making it throught a few days on his own. <i>(Jun 22, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),31</i>)

### pound-keeper
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>ROLLESTON</b> `Mr Thomas Cowper,' announced the <i>Hawkes Bay Provincial Gazette</i> on the 15 October 1864, `shall be Pound-keeper at Wallingford'. <i>(1984,Master, The,21,1864 ed.</i>)

### poundage
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PETERSON</b> Many years earlier [?c1890s] Mother had sent Don to the Post Office for a 2/6 postal note. Unfortunately she forgot to give him the poundage. `That's another penny, Sonny,' said the Postmaster cheerfully. With eyes flashing fire the small boy held up the PN. <i>(1981,Glasshouses,7</i>)

### poupou
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [pou](pop-pyo.md#pou))

>  <b>ALEXANDER</b> Seeing that poupou and tukutuku are thetraditional means of decorating, I havetaken it that both must be employed .. The poupou in the Maori whare actually supported the roof. <i>(1951,Story of Te Aute, The,205</i>)

>  At the entrance a pair of eyes fix you. They are in the upper part of a carved panel, or Maori Pou-Pou, taken from .. inside a Maori Council Chamber that formerly stood at Tolago [sic] Bay. <i>(Oct 23, 1902,NZ Tablet,9</i>)

>  A valuable piece of Maori carved work in the shape of a poupou or wall slab from a house in Tologa [sic] Bay has just been placed in the Auckland museum. <i>(Sep 20, 1902,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  The fourth poupou challenges our people to build strong families and in turn strong communities. <i>(Oct 26, 2006,Wairarapa News,20</i>)

>  <i>poupou,</i> upright deeply-carved slabs forming the solid framework of a whare: between every pair of poupou is the panel of tukutuku work. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/157</i>) [NOTES: J.C.Andersen]

>  The poupou or supports, sometimes called puketara, are painted after very old Maori style. <i>(Oct 25, 1900,Timaru Herald,2</i>)

>  My socks slide on the polished floor as I follow Pare obediently from pou pou to pou pou. Each one represents an ancestor and many of them are from different tribes related to Ngati Kahungunu. She takes my hand and slaps it onto the genitals of one of the male pou pou. <i>(May 2000,AA Directions,12</i>)

>  "The poupou [perpendiculars] will be bold and thick and bear weight ..." <i>(Aug 10, 2005,NZ Herald,B6</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> It was his body [sc. Te Rangi-nukai] which was to be buried beneath the poupous - uprights supporting the framework of the whare. <i>Ibid.</i> 131 It was his body which was to be buried beneath the poupous - uprights supporting the framework of the whare - his death which was to celebrate the house-warming. <i>(1926,Tutira,95,2nd edition ed.</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> Rafters are supported below by upright slabs, poupou, which are often carved. <i>(1955,Maori Carving Illustrated,40</i>)

>  "In this house we're really trying to create new poupou you haven't seen in a book and attack new subject matter so it will drive the imagery in a new way." .. Across the back wall will be a line of poupou depicting ancestors from principal hapu ... <i>(Aug 10, 2005,NZ Herald</i>)

>  Carvers .. at Ohinemutu are at present on the last of the heavy pou pou slabs for the Wairoa .. meeting house. <i>(Jan 9, 1937,Morrinsville Star,4</i>)

>  [Photo Caption] A Carved Poupou from Ohinemutu. This carved poupou, or slab, forms part of the carved house at Ohinemutu, named Tama-te-Kapua, after the celebrated ancestor of the Arawa tribe. <i>(Dec 23, 1903,Otago Witness,49</i>)

### poupoutea
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BULLER</b> ORTHONYX ALBICILLA {CLITONYX ALBICAPILLA}. (The WHITE-HEAD.) ... <i>Native names</i>. Popotea, Poupoutea, Popokotea, and Upokotea. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,1888 2nd edn 1/53</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> ORTHONYX {CLITONYX} OCHROCEPHALA. (The YELLOW-HEAD.) ... <i>Native names.</i> The same [Popotea, Poupoutea, Popokotea, and Upokotea] as those applied to the preceding species [White-head]: `Canary' of the colonists. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,103,1888 2nd edn 1/56 ed.</i>)

### pour rights
 
 <i>n.</i> the right to supply liquor to a function in return for advertising rights

>  In exchange, the NZRFU, provincial unions and clubs give companies exposure through brand names on team clothing, signage at matches, team naming rights and pour rights at events and clubrooms. <i>(Mar 16, 2003,Sunday Star Times,A14</i>)

### pour-on
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a systemic chemical remedy that kills lice, keds etc when poured on the backs of animals.

>  The revolutionary pour-on systemic insecticide from Ivon Watkins Dow Limited opens up a new concept in cattle lice control. <i>(Jul 8, 1971,NZ Farmer,20</i>)

>  For the control of lice in beef cattle there is a wide variety of sprays, pour-ons, spot-ons and powders.. <i>(Nov 1975,NZ Dairy Exporter,45</i>)

>  Pour-ons have been used to control lice on cattle for several years. <i>(Mar 1979,NZ Journal of Agriculture,44</i>)

>  The term 'dip' is a hangover from when plunge dipping was the only option - it has latterly been joined first by spray-dipping and now by the pour-ons. <i>(Aug 1987,NZ Journal of Agriculture,36</i>)

>  With less need and dependency on the use of pesticides, insecticides, drenches, pour-ons and other drugs and remedies, by accepting a more balanced and broad-minded approach ... <i>(Oct 14, 1999,Taranaki Daily News,14</i>)

>  The Working Dog. Do NOT put the dogs in the sheep swim dip or shower or use any sheep pouron. This could kill the dog. [pour on; pouron] <i>(Oct 20, 2001,Lifestyleblock.co.nz</i>)

### pourage
 
 <i>n.</i> drinks supply [analogy with signage]

>  Montana, New Zealand's biggest wine-maker, won the pourage rights with <i>Lord of the Rings</i> backers New Line Cinema. <i>(Dec 11, 2001,Dominion,11</i>)

### pouraka
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> We dived for paua, sea eggs, and crayfish. Crayfish pots (called <i>pouraka</i>) were set at night. <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,124</i>) [NOTES: 1900 Horoeka in 1981 Sutton-Smith History of Children's Play 124]

### pouri
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  ".. he met the ploughing party after they had been turned off, who looked pouri and crestfallen ..." <i>(Jun 25, 1879,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> P|oudi, <i>a</i>. Dark, dull, heavy, sorrowful; <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,195</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> Very <i>pouri</i> from the idleness of the boys. <i>(Sep 7, 1832,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,258,1961 ed.</i>)

>  When it was rumoured that Morland was missing, she [Roro] was <i>pouri </i>- wouldn't eat, and cried all the time ... <i>(Dec 11, 1911,Triad, The,23</i>) [NOTES: A.A.Grace (The Fate of Morland)]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> My boys are all pouri, because they think we have taken the wrong ... <i>(May 19, 1849,Journal,6/92</i>)

>  <b>GRACE</b> They appeared quite satisfied that I had done all in my power for them; still, they were very pouri (sad). <i>(Dec 23, 1867,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1867 Dec 23 T.S.Grace Journal in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 169]



 
 <i>n.</i> sadness

>  Two Wanganui chiefs, who had been known to be most surly amongst the natives of Parihaka, appear to have got over their <i>pouri</i>. <i>(Dec 20, 1881,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  <b>WILY & MAUNSELL</b> ... `Mihi, the east wind comes. My husband is very full of <i>pouri. ...' </i> <i>(1938,Robert Maunsell,88</i>)

### pouriedie
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [puriri](pop-pyo.md#puriri))

### poutama
 
 <i>n.</i> a stepped pattern of tukutuku ornament on the walls of a native house, and of weaving mats, etc. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  The Rangiatea Church is being renovated, I hear, and the School of Art has been asked to reconstruct the arapaki work. It is I believe all poutama, and kakaho work, the real thing. We have assembled a lot of kakaho at Rotorua, material much cheaper than mock kakaho, but a little more difficult to handle. <i>(Mar 8, 1931,Na To Hoa Aroha,2/120,1987 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Ngata)]

### poutu kaua
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [pohutukawa](per-pop.md#pohutukawa))

### pouwaka
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  P|ou |aka; A box. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,195</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> A <i>powaka</i> [a's-acute], or hut raised on poles, with some elaborately carved facing boards, on which was executed several figures of an indelicate nature, stood in the centre of the village. <i>Ibid.</i> 2/32 The powaka [a2-acute] or village store has been described, in which the tribes keep their best apparel, ammunition, &c.; ... <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/91</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> [caption to carved buildings raised on posts] <i>Na[acute] Powaka, or Village depositaries of the natives. ... </i>[210]Next to the <i>Warre no te Rangatira, </i>or native palace, the village museum <i>(po[acute]waka)</i> in which the valuables of the community of the place are deposited, receives careful attention of the native Vitruvius. These erections are raised on four stout carved poles, the sides of the house are faced with boards, carved with a profusion of indelicate subjects, exaggerated in such a manner as to leave no spectator in any dilemma as to the actual meaning and intention of the artist. In these huts, or rather boxes (the literal meaning) are placed fowling-pieces, esteemed garments, ... <i>Ibid.</i> 1/228 The most elaborate carvings are bestowed on the boxes (<i>powa[acute]ka</i>) that serve to enclose feathers for decorating the head on gala days, and other portable trinkets. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/209</i>)

### pouwhakataki
 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori community liaison officer [ORIGIN: Maori, (from the net) A team of 23 Pouwhakataki are being recruited to work with M?ori communities and educators around the country. The Pouwhakataki (community liaison advisors) are a key element of Te Mana, to help M?ori get better access, participate more, and achieve better in education.]

>  Manager Pouwhakataki (two positions) ... As Manager Pouwhakataki you will be responsible for the day-to-day management of a medium sized team of remote, community based change agents, Pouwhakataki. <i>(Nov 23, 2005,Dominion Post,E5</i>)

### pouwhenua
 
 <i>n.</i> pole [cf. pouwhenua = long club in Williams and Ryan]

>  The carved pouwhenua, named after Ngati Awa ancestor Hikakino, was erected on Thursday, but is enclosed in plastic until tomorrow's ceremony. <i>(Apr 21, 2006,Whakatane Beacon,1</i>)

>  The young Maori who stole the sacred pouwhenua from outside the Tauranga Town Hall on Friday night is risking ostracism and death, says respected Maori leader Sir Graham Latimer. Sir Graham has issued a special plea to the young man to return the pouwhenua (a sacred post) and to consult a tohunga (Maori spiritual advisor). <i>(Sep 20, 1987,Sunday News,1</i>)

>  The pouwhenua is a black spike against the lightening sky, the flags are simple triangles ... everything is a carving in space. <i>(Jun 28, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>)

>  A Far North iwi plans to put up a line of 100 pouwhenua (poles) marking customary rights on Ninety Mile beach in protest at the Government's foreshore and seabed proposal. Te Rarawa will put up a continual line of pouwhenua or marker posts, along its coastal boundaries from Hokianga to Te Oneroa a-Tohe (Ninety Mile Beach). <i>(Dec 20, 2003,Dominion Post,A6</i>)

>  Two six metre-high pouwhenua (poles) to be erected near Parliamnent Buildings will "create an oasis of calm within the city centre - where people can sit and reflect"... The two pouwhenua will symbolise upturned waka standing on a recreated beach of crushed seashells. <i>(Oct 21, 2004,Wellingtonian,2</i>)

>  She said the trust had invited Ohope residents and other interested people to the unveiling of the pouwhenua, which was at 6.30am Sunday, April 22. <i>(May 2, 2007,Whakatane Beacon,5</i>)

### poverty plant
 
 <i>n.</i> so called from its ability to grow in the poorest soil. See kumarahou. [AND general Eremophila 1931] <i>Pomaderris elliptica,</i> =kumarahou

>  This plant of the fuchsia species which has an oval-shaped leaf of light-green color, and bears clusters of yellow flowers .... is always a sign of sterility [of soil] and is known by many settlers as the `poverty plant'. <i>(May 1869,Guide to Farming in NZ,29</i>) [NOTES: May - month or autnor's name?]

>  <b>MORRISON</b> Kumerahau is Poverty Plant .... / Shy plant that waves its yellow crest / Where even bracken scorns to grow. / <i>(1934,Poems (various authors),2</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> Kumarahou, P.elliptica ... branching shrub, petals crisped at the edges, yellowish-white. North Island: dry hills.] <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,249</i>)

### poverty weed
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Pomaderris kumeraho </i>A. Cunn. Common names: <i>Poverty weed, gumdigger's soap </i> Maori names: <i>Kumarahou, papapa </i> <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,84</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a wordy wowser; a talking do-gooder

 <i>n.</i> (See [pohutukawa](per-pop.md#pohutukawa))

### powder
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to sprinkle weedkiller on noxious pasture weeds.

>  <b>KERR</b> 'There's a couple of thistle patches up there and there you missed,' said Alf. 'Get back up there and powder them.' Variegated thistle is a big tough weed; even tough-skinned horses won't brave its thorns, and Alf's boys carried powder weedkiller to counter it. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,175</i>)

### powder puff
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a light, noisy dog, often a huntaway.

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> Many shepherds have the hard, slogging huntaways which have done, and still do, the hard work on the tough country, while many owners or managers get by with a team of powder puffs (light, squawking huntaways). <i>(1973,Boot in the Stirrup,37</i>)

>  <b>JANES & ROWE</b> .. as we returned to the hotel with our overnight bags, these four savage powder-puffs decided to charge out on the footpath and attempt to chew our legs off at the knees. <i>(1994,Great New Zealand Hunting Stories,137</i>)

>  It was time to take the maggot taxis up to the marrowbone country with the shandygaff, fly-flicker, powder puff, thistle-peeper, the wool classer and the grave-scraper. [powder-puff.] <i>(Nov 22, 2002,Dominion Post,C6</i>)

### Powdergate
 
 <i>n.</i> a Fonterra milk powder problem

>  Fonterra yesterday welcomed the Serious Fraud Office's investigations of the $50 million "powdergate" scandal, saying it would allow the matter to be settled "once and for all." <i>(Oct 16, 2002,Dominion Post,C1</i>)

>  "Powdergate" is a sobriquet that New Zealand's biggest industry earned for another kind of dirty business - an alleged $75 million corporate fraud... The Powdergate scandal has re-emerged to shake the $14 billion dairy sector.. and re-open old rivalry wounds among its parochial shareholders. <i>(Apr 3, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),24</i>)

>  [He]was one of seven charged by the SFO in March this year over their involvement in the "Powdergate" case. <i>(Jun 17, 2004,Dominion Post,C3</i>)

>  Four years after it sprang into the headlines, Powdergate - the persistent, but, for many, confusing, dairy-industry saga - will go to trial next year. ...[29] The powdergate seven allegedly breached New Zealand Food and Safety Authority's quality control systems, which underpin the country's $14 billion food export business. <i>(Jun 11, 2005,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)

### powdering
 
 <i>n.</i> a light snowfall

>  Light fall (of snow). (C.) <i>(Nov 18, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

### Powelliphanta
 
 <i>n.</i> a species of large, native carniverous land snail; named after the late Dr A W B Powell of Auckland Museum

>  We're excited to meet a live powelliphanta snail enticed out by the rain and sliding its way across the track near Billies Saddle. <i>(Mar 13, 2004,Dominion Post,E17</i>)

>  Endangered species such as the giant carniverous snail powelliphanta patrickensis live there. <i>(Jan 30, 2006,Dominion Post,A10</i>)

>  The land also contained a significant population of the nationally endangered giant land snail powelliphanta... <i>(Mar 13, 2004,Dominion Post,A12</i>)

>  She says snails, particularly the giant Powelliphanta species, are a great source of protein, so it should not be surprising that possums will seek them out, particularly in the high, less fertile montane forest where sustenance is harder to come by. <i>(Sep 27, 2003,Dominion Post,A19</i>)

>  The large, native carniverous land snail, <i>Powelliphanta,/i>, will often be seen on the tracks after heavy rain, feeding on equally large earthworms. <i>(1990,Tramping in South Island Forest Parks,64</i>)

### power board
 
 <i>n.</i> board at bottom of bed

A room mate was always a wife , trousers were always reamers, old boy was always pronounced as air bair, the board at the bottom of the bed was the power board, a shower was a soak, all other hostels were inhabited by morons, freshers answered the phone, ... <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,84</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (historical) a regional body elected to manage, mainly retail, distribution of electricity from State works (also Electric Power Board : often Power and Gas Board)

### powhaitere
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [cf kakariki](../C#cf-kakariki))

>  <b>YATE</b> <i>Powaitere</i> - A Parrot, or Paroquet. - Of these birds there are several kinds; all of them small ... nearly the same in plumage - a bright green; yellow or red under the throat and tail; and red or yellow about the head. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),54</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> 36. <i>Platycercus Novae Seelandiae/. Wagl.</i> ... Kakariki of the natives. <i>Dieffenbach</i>. Powaitere of the natives. <i>Yate. </i>Po-e-tere [e1 acute, e2 e3 grave] of the natives. <i>Less.</i> Very common in the Chatham Islands. - <i>Dieffenbach. </i> <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/192</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/192-193]

>  <b>BULLER</b> PLATYCERCUS NOVAE ZEALANDIAE. (RED-FRONTED PARRAKEET.) ... <i>ive names</i>kariki, Kakawariki, Powhaitere, Porere, and Torete ...[59] The Red-fronted Parrakeet is very generally dispersed over the whole country - but is more plentiful in the southern portion of the North Island than in the far north, where the yellow-fronted species predominates. <i>Ibid.</i> 62 PLATYCERCUS AURICEPS. (YELLOW-FRONTED PARRAKEET.) ... <i>Native names</i>. Kakariki, Kakawariki, Powhaitere, Porere, and Torete ...[59] The Red-fronted Parrakeet is very generally dispersed over the whole country - but is more plentiful in the southern portion of the North Island than in the far north, where the yellow-fronted species predominates. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,58,1888 2nd edn 1/137; 1/142 ed.</i>)

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> A parrot Powhytarnee <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/339</i>)

>  There are two names for Paroquet - Kakariki and Powhaitere. <i>(Feb 26, 1891,Bush Advocate,2</i>)

### powhiri
 
 <i>v.</i> to welcome with a powhiri [ORIGIN: Maori] (See [powhiri n](../P#powhiri-n))

>  They were powhiried at Te Puea Marae. <i>(Jun 1998,Cuisine,No. 22: 18</i>)

>  They got their big break in 1974 when they were called on to powhiri the teams arriving for the Commonwealth Games in Christchurch. <i>(Jun 1996,Mana,No.13: 9</i>)

>  But, on the morning of November 9, one year shy of 150 years later, they returned to powhiri (welcome) the peacable monks. <i>(Dec 14, 1996,Listener (New Zealand Listener),41</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a formal Maori welcoming ceremony on a marae or elsewhere. [ORIGIN: Maori] (See [also pohiri](../A#also-pohiri))

>  Te Ngakau advised that Sir George Grey should not be received with the usual powhiri or invitation from the women at the entrance to the settlement with the words, "Come here, oh guest," but should welcome him with "Come here, governor, your coming is your own work," meaning they will expect Sir George Grey to state his object in this meeting, and to take the initiative. <i>(Feb 2, 1878,North Otago Times,2</i>)

>  The Powhiri, Welcome and Conference Opening will start at 8:45 am on Tuesday morning. <i>(Nov 1997,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,114/11</i>)

>  If there had been a powhiri in the afternoon that would have been very acceptable, but that's not the way it's been planned. <i>(Feb 4, 2003,Dominion Post,A2</i>)

>  They were welcomed in a powhiri which acknowledged the coming together of Maori and pakeha historians, theologians, educators, students and those who wanted to discover and re-assess early relationships between Maori and the French and other early settlers before and after the signing of the Treaty of Waitangi. <i>(May 2, 2004,NZ Catholic,5</i>)

>  Te Kotahitanga members were on call 24 hours a day, to tear out to the airport, knock off a powhiri in the wind or rain or whatever, then shoot back home or to work. <i>(Jun 1996,Mana,No.13: 9</i>)

>  Up sprang these great semi-nude men, and accorded their guests a real powhiri welcome by their shouts of joy and their demonstrative caperings of delight, performed whilst retreating to a short distance. <i>(May 24, 1915,Poverty Bay Herald,9</i>)

>  As the party approached the Natives mustered in force, and began a "powhiri" or song of welcome, and an invitation was extended to them to be guests while they remained. <i>(Sep 27, 1880,Poverty Bay Herald,2</i>)

>  Waka Haka : Martin Bercie, a crew member from the double-hulled waka Te Aurere, performs a haka during a powhiri at the Museum of New Zealand, Te Papa, yesterday. <i>(Feb 26, 2000,Dominion,3</i>)

>  A powhiri, the lament usually reserved for the death of a great rangatira was performed. The coffin was covered with a Maori mat and borne by Cabinet ministers ... <i>(May 1984,Metro,124</i>) [NOTES: (Peter Shaw)]

>  Tomorrow there will be a powhiri (welcome) at the Town Hall. Guests are to include Governor-General Sir Paul Reeves, members of the New Zealand management committee of Te Maori, and representatives of the National Museum. Anyone can attend the powhiri. Guests are asked to be seated by 6pm. <i>(Aug 14, 1986,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  Powhiri: a formal welcome to visitors/manuhiri by the tangata whenua/hosts of the marae. <i>(Sep 17, 1986,PSA Journal,Conference Supplement 3</i>)

>  <b>IHIMAERA</b> Just behind the calling women, there was a group ready to [29] begin the powhiri, the action chant of welcome. .. They were waiting for the manuhiri, led by this woman in black, to return the call. <i>(1986,Matriarch, The,28</i>)

>  <B>POWHIRI</b> - FORMAL MAORI WELCOME-Men and Women have different roles.-Te reo Maori is key language.-Set seating arrangement reflects men and women's different roles. <i>(Jan 19, 2006,Dominion Post,1</i>)

>  Labour MP Shane Jones is distancing himself from Helen Clark's directive that women must be able to sit in the front row during state powhiri. <i>(May 26, 2006,NZ Herald,A6</i>)

>  It was decided that .. the formal welcome or powhiri take place on Friday, December 14th, at 7 o'clock. <i>(Dec 13, 1900,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

>  [Photo caption] A Powhiri or Dance of Welcome to the Visitors. <i>(Jan 14, 1903,Otago Witness,42</i>)

### powhiwhi
 
 <i>n.</i> a native convolvulus, with pale purple trumpet-shaped flowers. [Maori, used of various climbing plants]

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>Calystegia ... tugurionum </i>... Small Bindweed. PO-WHI-WHI (creeper). <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,51</i>)

>  <b>SALE</b> It was at this time last year I first saw the powhiwhi - a pale purple trumpet flower. ... It was a New Zealand native convolvulus, ... <i>(1982,Four Seasons,47</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [powhaitere](pop-pyo.md#powhaitere))

### poz
 
 <i>adj.</i> positive

>  <b>PYKE</b> I ain't poz about that. I've a notion that my little game is for a pretty big thing. <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,1/viii/31,(1889,1974) ed.</i>)

### PPA
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Protestant Political Association

>  From 1917, a newly formed Protestant Political Association canalised these discontents into a virulent anti-Catholic campaign, conducting a noisy agitation which provided a release for much pent-up tension. The PPA saw Irish events as part of a deep plot hatched in Rome against Protestant Britain and its Empire. <i>(Nov 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 54/24</i>)

### PPL
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Paid Parental Leave

>  Others have spent their Paid Parental Leave on ski-ing gear or house renovations .. When PPL was introduced, New Zealand caught up with the practice of most Organisation for Economic Cooperation and Development countries. <i>(Dec 14, 2004,Dominion Post,C4</i>)

>  Paid Parental Leave (PPL), the baby of Alliance leader Laila Harre, comes into force next month and provided you meet a range of criteria, you could be in the money... Early arrivals won't stop people from claiming PPL, as long as the due date is on or after July 2... If you want to share the baby-caring load, PPL can be transferred to a partner or another caregiver, but in this instance both people must meet the minimum standards. <i>(Jun 20, 2002,Evening Post,9</i>)

### PPP
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Public-Private Partnership - a public-private joint sector venture

>  The Government is drawing a line between the leaked document that was Ross Armstrong's downfall and its support for public-private partnerships in general... Informal public-private relationships are not new - governments have long invited business people to Wellington to discuss policy formulation or test ideas, often over lunch or dinner. But formal PPPs, in which a government enters into a contract with a private company to build new infrastructure, are not yet legal in New Zealand. <i>(Oct 22, 2002,Dominion Post,A2</i>)

>  To solve the problem, the state government extended a process widely used in Australia and several other countries and to be introduced to New Zealand: public-private partnerships... Mr Scott says the main reason PPPs are used overseas is to get around two main problems: an inability to raise funds; and the need to bring in building expertise from another country. <i>(Oct 24, 2002,Dominion Post,C7</i>)

>  PPP or PFI (private finance initiative, as it is also known), was introduced in Britain in 1992, and met with mixed success... What distinguishes PPP from more orthodox forms of IT contracting such as outsourcing and facilities management, is that the customer is only concerned with when, and importantly what, is provided - not <i>how</i> it is provided. <i>(Oct 28, 2002,Dominion Post,C11</i>)

>  [Hdg] Pushing public-private partnerships... PPPs became viable when the cheapest option was to contract the private sector to do certain parts of the work. <i>(Aug 23, 2003,Dominion Post,C10</i>)

>  There's no getting away from PPPs now - public-private partnerships. They occur when a government needs major capital works done, but the work is on a scale that would seriously unbalance the public balance sheet. <i>Ibid.</i> 24 Essentially, the PPP term entered the national lexicon only with the Birch report for Auckland City Council in 2001. Treasury had also once suggested that a PPP variant might be a way of financing construction of the new hospital planned for Wellington. <i>(Nov 2, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

### PPTA
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Post-Primary Teachers' Association [ORIGIN: acronym, also attrib]

>  You can set your calender by the withdrawals of the PPTA; they are as predictable as the four seasons. <i>(Apr 5, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

>  So many people have so many ideas that even if he clothed himself in white and gave all his wordly goods to the PPTA it wouldn't improve his popularity one jot. <i>(Jun 11, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),10</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>P.P.T.A.</i>: Not a strange version of the P.T.A., but the rather militant SECONDARY SCHOOL TEACHERS' UNION, the Post Primary Teachers Association. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,80</i>)

>  <i>P.P.T.A.</i> Post-Primary Teachers Association <i>(1985,Collins Compact Dictionary,379</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; nf. 1986 NZPOD]]

>  Post Primary Teachers Association (PPTA) advisory officer Rob Willetts says that young men simply don't see teaching as an attractive career option any more. <i>(Jul 21, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener)</i>)

>  Wairarapa PPTA representative Geoff Thurston says the srike action is more than just a pay dispute, though remuneration is one of the key demands. <i>(Sep 15, 2010,Wairarapa News,1</i>)

>  Does this amount to a hidden agenda for education? The PPTA's Cooney thinks so, but says the signals are contradictory. <i>(Oct 30, 1993,Listener (New Zealand Listener),36</i>)

### prad
 
 <i>n.</i> a horse

>  At the raffle which took place at the Empire Hotel on Monday evening for a well-known American buggy and the fast-trotting horse Charlie, there was a large attendance of ticket holders, and the fortunate winner of the prad was Mr. R. Sommerville of the Victoria Hotel. <i>(Jan 2, 1867,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  The Wellington Parson and the Country Prad. <i>(Dec 19, 1903,NZ Free Lance,6</i>)

>  One of them, who was mounted on a lumping big horse, rather too heavy for cross-country work, managed at length to scramble somehow over or through the bullfinch; but the other, after flinging his prad at it about twenty times, was compelled to give it best. <i>(May 14, 1877,Timaru Herald,3</i>)

>  The start was a good one, little George Williams made the most of it, and got his "prad" underweigh the quickest, leading round the bend by a length and a half; but at the back he pulled Poet back and let Libeller and Administrator lead him a bit. <i>(Jan 3, 1885,Observer (Auckland),12</i>)

>  Though their prad has to go out in open company from now on, he should prove capable of gathering in the right chips in his turn. <i>(Dec 2, 1926,NZ Truth,11</i>)

>  Jock, which is by Lucullus from a Tasman mare, is a promising cross-country prad. <i>(Sep 18, 1930,NZ Truth,12</i>)

>  "It's picking up money," declared supporters of Sandstream when that gelding was stripped for the Dannevirke Cup, and the money that came for Griffiths' prad testified that the horse's supporters believed in the ability of their nag to pull them through. <i>(Mar 19, 1910,NZ Truth,2</i>)

>  <b>SCOTT</b> If there were no races on we would beg or `borrow' out of a paddock a couple of `prads' and have a race meeting on our own account. <i>(1897,How I Stole 10,000 Sheep,6</i>)

>  The towel-horse is the safest prad. <i>(Aug 17, 1905,NZ Truth,9/7/8</i>)

>  <b>COOZE</b> He put what he had on his favourite prad. <i>(1946,Ten Bob Each Way,9</i>)

>  If you can afford to waste your dough on these prads surely you can pay the amount mentioned above. <i>(1963,Home Life,2</i>) [NOTES: [Hori]]

>  <b>McCALLUM</b> I tell my pakeha friend that I will put ten bob each way on this prad. <i>(1963,Half-Gallon Jar,22</i>)

>  Owing to power shortages during the Northisland winter and the lateness of thestart of last races at many fixtures, thepoor "prads" have to be able to see theirway home. <i>(Aug 10, 1955,NZ Truth,25</i>)

>  As Mrs Hedley and the "old prad" are alsoout for life, the "ring in" did not do the crowd behind the ex-New Zealander, Rawmore, much, if any good, when that gelding won. <i>(Feb 22, 1908,NZ Truth,2</i>)

>  The "jim" went on the Karamu prad, but the jockey became unseated during the race and the second favorite [sic] running off the course let the "roughie" to win comfortably. <i>(Jun 11, 1910,NZ Truth,2</i>)

>  Tom's prad, supposedly skittish, caved ina bit when 17 stone of body and baggagehit it. <i>(1935,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,IV/93</i>)

>  Park Prads Poor in Quality and Quantity .. There is some class to a few of the prads, but most of the fields are composed mostly of has-beens and left-overs. <i>(May 30, 1934,NZ Truth,3</i>)

>  Seeing the prad leading in the straight, bookie thought he would go the back way to town, but Madame Day-and-Martin, who had one eye on the race and the other on her cash, set sail. <i>(Aug 25, 1898,Otago Witness,36</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> esp <i>to prad along</i>, take it easy; go at an easy, or steady and comfortable, pace

>  <b>R MASON</b> <i>prad</i>: J.W. Butcher. To prad along, to walk at a steady, comfortable pace. <i>(Jun 7, 1952,Personal Communication</i>)

### prairie
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TUCKETT</b> ... the land appeared to be one continued prairie ... Fine groves of timber ... scattered over the prairie ... <i>(May 24, 1844,Diary</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)1844 May 24 Tucketts Diary in Hocken Contributions 223]

>  This is a vast grassy prairie, about 12 miles long and 56 miles broad ... <i>(1849,Notes on Otago</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  <b>HALL-JONES</b> Waiau ... To the east, undulating prairies covered with grass ... <i>(1971,Mr Surveyor Thomson,65</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)1850s in 1971 Hall-Jones Mr Surveyor Thomson 65]

>  A less observant youth having lost his way a few nights ago, in the neighbourhood of the capital, was cheered in the loneliness of the prairie, by the appearance of a man approaching.. <i>(Apr 12, 1851,Lyttelton Times,5</i>)

### prairie grass
 
 <i>n.</i> prairie grass introduced from eastern South America. ... (See [grass](go-gra.md#grass))

### prat
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Prat one's frame in.</i> An Australian and N.Z. variant of <i>prat</i>, v., 2: C. 20. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,656</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>prat</i> to talk to someone; perhaps from English C19 colloquialism `praty', talkative, from Southern English `prate', idle talk; mostly NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,88</i>)

### prawn
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>YATE</b> Those saltwater fish] most plentiful, and of greatest note, are, soles, mackarel, cod-fish, a species of salmon, whiting, snapper, mullet, bream, skate, gurnards, and a few smaller kinds, some not near so large as a sprat; with an abundance of crayfish, oysters, shrimps, prawns, muscles, and cockles. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),71</i>)

>  We were in want of a name for the little prawns like shrimps, and called them `squid.' <i>(1895,TrNZI,28/53</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Golden prawn Plesioika martia</i> ... Not well known. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,153</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Jacknife[sic] prawn Hymenopenaeus sibogae</i> ... Somewhat similar to the king prawns of Australia and the royal red shrimp of the United States. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,153</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Royal red prawn Aristaemorpha foliacea </i> <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,153</i>)

>  [species name] aristaeomorpha foliacea .. [scientific name] <i>Aristaeomorpha foliacea </i>.. [other name(s)] royal red prawn <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Sabre prawn Campylonatus rathbunae</i> ... Widespread, more common around southern New Zealand. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,153</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> a fool or simpleton

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>prawn:</i> An expression largely confined to Southland. It implies a certain deficiency in cognitive skills in the individual so described. DUMMY <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,80</i>)

### prawn killer
 
 <i>n.</i> The shovel-nosed lobster, <i>Ibacus alticrenatus </i> (fam. Scyllaridae, called"bugs" in Australia), characterised by lobster-like body with part of two frontantennae flattened into large serrate plates. Related to a similar species in South Australia also erroneously called prawn killer and quite different from the Eastern Australia prawn killer which doeshave the chelae to crunch prawns.

>  [species name] shovelnosed lobster .. [scientific name] -- [other name(s)] prawn killers or spanish lobster <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> Prawn Killer. <i>Ibaccus alticrenatus</i>...This is of a dull salmon colour and growsto about 100 mm long... In SouthAustralia a related species is termed the"Prawnkiller". <i>(1987,Native Animals of NZ,35</i>)

### prayer-book
 
 <i>n.</i> a small holystone

>  The large, soft smooth sandstone with ropes attached at each end, was slid fore and aft over the decks by the crew. Smaller stones, called "prayer-books" were used by hand to get into the narrow corners. <i>(Dec 2001,NZ Memories,No. 27/6</i>)

### pre-Adamite
 
 <i>n.</i> an early name for settlers who had arrived in what was to become Canterbury before the `Pilgrim' settlers (e.g. the Deans) [noun and adjective uses]

>  <b>SMITH & ANTHONY</b> 'And these bells are reminiscent of a time even beyond the Pilgrim period, being the generous gift of the Rhodes family, the heirs of three "Pre-Adamite" brothers who cast their lot in with old Canterbury, and throve mightily.' <i>(1907,Christchurch on Avon (New Zealand),19</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> He had further aroused the hostility of the leaders of the settlement by his decisions on certain complicated `pre-Adamite' Banks Peninsula land claims, and by his support of Sir George Grey's cheap land policy. <i>(1947,Story of Canterbury, The,183</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> Caverhill was one of the most remarkable of the pre-Adamite settlers (those who arrived before the first four ships) in Canterbury. <i>(1975,Early Canterbury Runs,ch ix 270,(4th ed.) ed.</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> [Mrs Dean's memoirs-1860's] <i>(1930,Early Canterbury Runs,5</i>)

>  <i>Pre-Adamite</i>.- Old nickname for the families who arrived in Canterbury before the settlement. (Mrs Deans's Memoir.) <i>(Nov 18, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: (Acland Gloss.)]

>  <b>REED</b> To the Hays and Sinclairs and other `pre-Adamites' - as those few who had arrived before the `Pilgrims' came jocularly to be called - these ships represented shops, schools, churches, ... <i>(1947,Story of Canterbury, The,55</i>)

>  <b>ED. WARD</b> The term `Shagroon' was used to describe pastoralists and stock-drivers who came to Canterbury direct from Australia and without the blessing of the Cby. Association. The Cby Assoc. settlers were `Pilgrims', while those established in Canterbury before their arrival were `pre-Adamites'. <i>(1951,Journal,132 [fn]</i>)

>  Messrs Schaef and Sons are just finishing a substantial two storey brick building .. and Mr. White has nearly completed his new boot-shop next door. These will be handsome substitutes for the pre-adamite structures recently consumed. <i>(Apr 11, 1895,West Coast Times,4</i>)

>  This palace, swayed by balmy breeze, is always on a rock;/ The lavatories and the drains/ The nuisance-man would mock;/ The furniture, pre-Adamite,/ Remains in statue quo;/ The bath reminds you there's no need/ To Rot-a-mer to go. <i>(Sep 8, 1900,NZ Free Lance,9</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [general; before Adam, pre-human]

>  For the present we are all Adamites, or pre-Adamites. <i>(Dec 31, 1859,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,2</i>)

>  The Vice-Chairman said he was not going to propose an antediluvian toast (Mr Julius - "Pre-Adamite, I said"). <i>(Nov 17, 1864,North Otago Times,1</i>)

>  <b>PASCOE</b> [Birds S.W.] There are two varieties of this pre-adamite looking bird, the king or crested penguin ..... <i>(1957,Mr Explorer Douglas,286</i>) [NOTES: ca1899 (1957) Pascoe Mr Explorer Douglas 286]

>  <b>MAY</b> Apart from Loweriston's story, and Toms's entry of the river afterwards named the Buller, whatever the sealers learned went unrecorded. A few names on the map, a rotted hulk here and there, a legend or two, were the only traces the pioneer explorers ever discovered of these West Coast pre-adamites. <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,30,1962 ed.</i>)

>  <b>STACK</b> <i>(1972,Through Canterbury,18</i>) [NOTES: (editorial note)]

### pre-condition
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to fatten or prime stock for sale.

>  <b>GORDON</b> We were on tenterhooks about how how we'd go about pre-conditioning the cattle and getting through the health testing. <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,158</i>)

### pre-empt
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>pre-empt n.</i> abbrev. fr. pre-emptive right. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,9</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [sp. use] to lay claim to land, often by spotting or gridironing strategic areas.

>  <b>STEWART</b> These schemes [of squatters] were all directed to pre-empting key positions so as to render access of settlement by the small settlers impossible. <i>(1940,William Rolleston: a New Zealand statesman,60</i>)

>  <b>STEWART</b> Other forms of spotting consisted of pre-empting the only land with a supply of water so as to render adjacent land of no value but the owner of the water. <i>(1940,William Rolleston: a New Zealand statesman,61</i>)

### pre-empted
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> Canterbury land ... was magnificent sheep-country. Fortunes in it; but since the Government was now putting all pre-empted land up for sale it was necessary to buy it in. <i>(1938,Promenade,245</i>)

### pre-emption
 
 <i>n.</i> The collocations pre-emptive right, right of pre-emption, pre-emptive land, were all used in the early days of the Colony when land-purchasing was keen; a legal or historical word, modified in Colonial usage formerly well-known but now dropped in popular speech. --

>  <b>POWER</b> In the United States the pioneer has always had the right of pre-emption, for there is no one better entitled to it than he who gives the land a marketable value. <i>(1849,Sketches in NZ,123</i>)

>  The arguments used ... appear to be based upon an assumption of a right afecting unalienated native territory by virtue of the Crown's right of pre-emption. <i>(Oct 13, 1858,Memorandum by Native Secretary</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Oct 13 Memorandum by Native Secretary in 1949 Rutherford ed. Select Documents 105.23]

>  <b>DALZIEL</b> pre-emption: Right to purchase before anyone else. <i>(1968,Sir Julius Vogel,32</i>)

>  ... but the Chiefs ... yield to Her Majesty the exclusive right of Preemption over such lands as the proprietors thereof may be disposed to lienate - at such prices as may be agreed ... <i>(Feb 6, 1840,Treaty of Waitangi,(1877, reprinted 1976) ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Feb 6 from a facsimile English version of the Treaty]

### pre-emptive
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CRAWFORD</b> Another four acres were to be planted on the `Pre-emptive' (which by then was freehold) ... <i>(1981,Station Years,126</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>GRIFFITHS</b> Why shouldn't he, the first arrival, have the right to take up his pre-emptive option? ... he made the most of it when he appeared before the Waste land Board on February 11 and 13, 1863. `It was about four months ago,' he told the board ..., `that I realised I would be upset by gold-diggers. As a result I have been forced to apply for my pre-emptive rights sooner than I would otherwise have done.' <i>(1971,King Wakatip,97</i>)

### pre-emptive right
 
 <i>n.</i> [possible distinction re prerogative of crown]

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> This was the interpretation put by the Governor (Sir George Grey) on the land clauses of the Treaty of Waitangi, commonly known as the `pre-emptive' right of Her Majesty under the Treaty. <i>(1910,Reminiscences,51</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> <i>(1930,Early Canterbury Runs,26</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) land purchased with a pre-emptive right.

>  All Mr Acland's houses are on preemptive right so he gets off all the taxes, ... a direct inducement not to buy freehold. <i>(Apr 19, 1864,Chudleigh Diary,132,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PINNEY</b> There was a house and woolshed on a pre-emptive right; there was also a seam of coal. <i>(1981,Early Northern Otago Runs,70</i>)

>  <b>PEACHE</b> That some of the pre-emptive rights may have to be bought up ... still the amount should not be much. <i>(Jul 4, 1876,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1876 July 4 Peache Jnl in 1970 Gray Quiet with the Hills 47]

>  .. the law was plain enough as regards the exact or at all events approximate position of the 80 acre pre-emptive right. <i>(Dec 10, 1859,Hawke's Bay Herald,4</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> In eighteen months nearly all the run except the pre-emptives had gone. <i>(1946,Early Canterbury Runs,37</i>)

>  <b>PINNEY</b> There was a house and woolshed on a pre-emptive right. <i>(1981,Early Northern Otago Runs,70</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a right of a land purchaser to buy public land on condition that it be improved

>  "Your committee now proceed to state the principles which, in their opinion, should form the basis of a law to be enacted by the Imperial Parliament, for the settlement of the rights of property in New Zealand .. 5. That the holders of lands not in cultivation, whose titles shall have been admitted by the commissioners, shall thereupon have a pre-emptive right of purchase from the crown, at a price not less than 20.s per acre. .." <i>(Jan 2, 1841,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,2</i>)

>  Mr Driver brought Mr Brunton's application for a pre-emptive right on run 62 before the board. <i>(Jul 3, 1875,Otago Witness,10</i>)

>  The natives in this district are preparing a petition against the Crown's resumption of the pre-emptive right. <i>(May 5, 1884,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)

>  <b>WAITT</b> The largest run-holder has only a pre-emptive right <i>(1856,Letter,13</i>) [NOTES: 1856 Waitt Letter to Joseph Thomas 13]

>  As to the preemptive right, please send me the best description you can of what we should apply for. We have a right to <i>`80 acres at one of the stations and 10 acres at each of the other stations erected upon the run'</i> ... It seems, however, that there must be a station of some sort at the place where preemptive is applied for; but it would be easy to have a hut put up at any place where we wanted to take our land. <i>(Apr 20, 1863,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/37,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (C.W. Richmond)]

>  <b>G M WILSON</b> The settler was allowed a pre-emptive right for every 50,000 acres or so which he could choose where he liked. In this way the owner of this station got about 4,000 acres pre-emptive right on this run at [pound]1 per acre. <i>(Feb 2, 1873,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1873 Feb 2 G.M.Wilson Diary in 1973 Wierzbicka Wilson Family 164]

>  A communication was received from the Surveyor-general in regard to the balance of pre-emptive right on pastoral run 208, Wairaki, the Otago School Commissioners having desired that the land be proclaimed an education reserve. <i>(Oct 31, 1900,Otago Witness,30</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> Every [Canterbury] run-holder has a preemptive right over 250 acres round his homestead, and 50 acres round any other buildings he may have upon his run. <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,viii/115</i>)

>  <b>W.G. REES</b> Why shouldn't he, the first arrival, have the right to take up his pre-emptive option? ... he made the most of it when he appeared before the Waste land Board on February 11 and 13, 1863. `It was about four months ago,' he told the board ..., `that I realised I would be upset by gold-diggers. As a result I have been forced to apply for my pre-emptive rights sooner than I would otherwise have done.' <i>(Feb 1871,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1863 Feb W.G. Rees in 1971 Griffiths King Wakatip 97]

>  The runholder was also allowed a pre-emptive right of purchase on his run of eighty acres for his principal station and ten acres for each out-station. This concession ... proved later to interfere with the fuller settlement of the country, as the runholder, in his phrase, `picked the eyes out.' <i>(1898,Hocken Contributions,168</i>)

>  <b>EVANS</b> The pre-emptive right was the right of a lessee to freehold one square mile of land. This became the station headquarters. <i>(1962,Waikaka Saga,117</i>)

>  <b>SEWELL</b> The stockowners talk big about what they mean to do one of these days in return for those pre-emptive rights which they have secured to themselves. <i>(Mar 12, 1853,Sewell Journal 1853-1857,209</i>) [NOTES: in McIntyre (1980) Sewell Journal 1853-1856]

>  Every run-holder has a pre-emptive right over 250 acres round his homestead, and 50 acres round any other buildings he may have upon his run. <i>(1860,In Memoriam,95</i>) [NOTES: in BUTLER First Year (1863)]

>  After selection most of the runholders were granted pre-emptive rights for residential purposes. <i>(1910,Tapanui Station,11</i>)

>  <b>ACKROYD</b> A pastoral licensee could use his, and rarely her, pre-emptive rights to lock up the land along a mile of road for the cost of the fencing, say GBP60, when to buy the same land outright would cost a settler GBP600 .. in some cases huts and fences would be erected to obtain a pre-emptive right over the adjacent land after which they were pulled down and put up elsewhere to enable fresh claims to be made. <i>(1993,Politics, Economics & Pastoral Land,11</i>)

### pre-European
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> I have always regarded obsidian ... as the most important article of trade and barter in pre-European days. <i>(1941,Numismatic History of NZ,15</i>)

>  She [Rangitoto] was also a parrot reserve for the Maori chief Te Peretu in pre-European times. <i>(2001,Huia 4: Contemp. Maori Fiction,12</i>)

### pre-Fleet
 
 <i>adj.</i> of the time before the Great Fleet q.v.

>  ... pre-Fleet culture <i>(Apr 2, 1963,NZ News,2/2</i>) [NOTES: [OEDS]]

>  The cave paintings in the South Island suggest some connection with the motifs of carvings recently dug up in the far North, and have to be re-examined in the light of a suggestion that such pre-Fleet peoples such as Waitaha ... were responsible for the paintings ... <i>(Sep 20, 1935,Na To Hoa Aroha,3/200</i>) [NOTES: (Ngata)]

>  <b>PHILLIPS</b> It is possible that all whale hunting was done by the pre-Fleet people ... <i>(1966,Maori Life and Custom,30</i>)

>  <b>DUFF</b> The question now arises as to the date at which the Chathams had their last contact with New Zealand. It can be fairly confidently stated as pre-Fleet, since Moriori traditions include none of the well-kmnown canoes of the Fleet. <i>(1956,Moahunter Period of Maori Culture, The,18</i>)

### pre-jigger
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>MAHONEY</b> [Bush camps] developed in the pre-jigger era as an alternative to the expense of having a lokey and carriage assigned specially to commuting. <i>(1998,Era of the Bush Trams in NZ, The,58</i>)

### pre-kidding
 
 <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of goat-shearing] prior to kids being produced.

>  The animals are shorn twice a year, with pre-mating and pre-kidding excellent timing for ease of management and to preserve fleece quality. <i>(2002,Lifestyle Farmer,June/July 28</i>)

### pre-lamb shear
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to shear ewes prior to lambing.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> They now pre-lamb shear with machines ... <i>(1975,Sixty Thousand on the Hoof,102</i>)

>  <b>MAF</b> 19 Some farmers do not intend to pre-lamb shear this year. <i>(1989,BLANK ENTRY,19</i>) [NOTES: Economic Evaluation of Agroforestry in NZ App.]

>  <b>MAF</b> A high proportion of hill country farmers pre-lamb shear. <i>(1989,BLANK ENTRY,31</i>) [NOTES: Economic Evaluation of Agroforestry in NZ App]

>  <b>MCRAE</b> Makarora and Dingle Burn were going to pre-lamb shear that year. <i>(1998,On the Run,13</i>)

>  With lambing on its way, farmers who pre-lamb shear found themselves in a real bind. <i>(2002,Wool Grower,Spring 23</i>)

### pre-lamb shearing
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the shearing of ewes in warm areas during winter [in order to lamb more easily]. (See [pre-lamb shear (verb)](../P#pre-lamb-shear-(verb)))

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> Since the introduction of pre-lamb shearing, which takes place in August, the shearing hours have been from 7.30 a.m. to 5.30 p.m. <i>(1982,Blue Cliffs,227</i>)

>  This [hut] was built at approximately the same time as the additions to Sutherlands [sc. the 1960s], mainly because of the change to pre-lamb shearing. <i>(May 1994,NZ Historic Places,47/36</i>)

>  We had our pipe-opener for the year's mustering with the bringing-in of the ewes for pre-lamb shearing. <i>(Sep 1961,NZ Farmer,10</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> There was a time during the last war and just after when blade shearers became so scarce that it looked as if the art must die, but the advent of pre-lamb shearing revived the interest ... <i>(1974,Kingdom in the Hills,90</i>)

### pre-Maori
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>McNEISH</b> ... in other words a forgotten or forbidden hunting ground of pre-European - possibly even pre-Maori - times, tapu, of course, possibly a resting place of ancestors, which would explain the extreme reluctance of the natives to enlarge on the legend. <i>(1970,Mackenzie,29</i>)

>  Outside this sequential linkage of ancestor tosuccessive descendant cultures, there is no evidence for any pre-Maori element in New Zealand. The supposed pre-Maori and non-Maori Moriori of mainland New Zealand as depicted by earlier writers are simply the early Maori. <i>(1984,Te Maori,61</i>)

### pre-Pakeha
 
 <i>adj.</i> of the time before European settlement

Roberts <i>Kohikohinga</i> (1929), etc. and the jocular uses <i>pakehas</i> (the `has' being accented) and <i>pakehasn't</i>, for a rich and penniless white man respectively. (These uses may have occurred in print, but I have not seen them; only heard them.) <i>(1945,Journal of the Polynesian Society,54/228</i>) [NOTES: (Baker)]

### prefab
 
 <i>n.</i> 1. a prefabricated building, esp. a class room: abbrev. of prefabricated building, in New Zealand originally chiefly applied to such Army huts during and after WW2; thence, and now almost specifically, to pre-fabricated classrooms (the idea for these originally deriving from the availability of surplus prefab. army huts) which have been since about the mid forties, such a feature of school-building practice to meet expanding school rolls.>esp. temporary educational

>  <b>ASHTON-WARNER</b> I see again in mind my rickety rafteredrocky prefab that spilt the melting frostin the spring. <i>(1963,Teacher,220</i>) [NOTES: 1963 edition]

>  Siberia, the name given to freezing prefabs on the outer fringes of Parliament buildings, houses lowly Government back-bench MPs. <i>(Jun 4, 1989,Dominion Sunday Times,2</i>)

>  <b>ASHTON-WARNER</b> When the pre-fab where I teach comes into view, I run into something that does more than renew life in a garden. <i>(1958,Spinster,4</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> ... past the mushroom growth of prefabs sneered at by the genteel Victorian villas opposite, .. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,40</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> The pre-fab, stifling in February ... a criss-cross of rafters and beams; pinus and pinex and unpainted corrugated iron, all bolted and jointed and thrown up behind the trees close to the boundary of the school ... A purely temporary structure ... <i>Ibid.</i> 172Put in a quote for touching up the pre-fab before Fletchers hear about it. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,99</i>)

>  <b>ASHTON-WARNER</b> My schoolroom was that pre-fab I wrote about in <i>Spinster</i> ... I saw workmen dismantling the prefab ... <i>(1979,I Passed This Way,17,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> Ruatapu has a sawmill, New Forest has, appropriately, red pine for prefabs ... Maimai is logs, Reefton coal. <i>(1985,G'day Country,149</i>)

>  Standard one [in 1950] was in a prefab, and the real standards ... were in the main school building. <i>(May 1987,Metro,50</i>) [NOTES: Roger `Auckland Life']

>  The prefab was one of several old buildings due to be demolished. Polytechnic Principal ... said the fire was deliberate. <i>(Sep 19, 1988,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  <b>POSTGATE</b> ... of the nature of superior `pre-fabs'[of a wartime scientific settlement staff houses] about it. <i>(1953,Ledger Is Kept, The</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [postal workers' use]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> There was [postal] work [in the 1960s], hard satisfying work. Unloading parcel bags, huge cumbersome containers nicknamed pre-fabs, from a three-door Z. There was slots, there was lumps, there was packing fragies in bottlos, there was the tying down of the north line for a clearance, the scissors, lead seals and squeezers. The sorters on call-back flicking circulars with precision. The volcanoes of letters on the facing-up table, the checker chanting the reds onto the lorry, the checker calling the overseas in, London 45 ... The queue of men working at the parcel depot or the postal siding ... Tieing down the cargon. ... The sorters' school at the posties' pub known colloquially as Box 44 or the B.B.C., the Back Bar of the Cecil. The cheerful abuse of cobbers. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,85</i>)
