### Premier
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The Premier was interviewed at 10.30 o'clock this morning by a deputation representing the flax milling industry. <i>(Apr 17, 1890,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  .. we ought to feel thankful that we are blessed with a Premier who has the courage to let the colony know his opinion of such meddlesome traders. <i>(Mar 23, 1895,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  Reference was made to the Government's cheap money scheme, and the Premier enlarged upon the benefits which he contended had resulted therefrom. <i>(Dec 8, 1898,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  At this meeting Lord Howick was the only minister present besides the premier, apparently as the organ of the colonial Office. <i>(Aug 21, 1839,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,4</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> In the constitutional debates of 1854 during the first session of the New Zealand Parliament, the term `Prime Minister' was frequently used in a somewhat casual fashion. This explains in part why it was some years before the term `Premier' came into general usage. William Fox (q.v.) who took office in July 1861, was described in the <i>Gazette</i> as Attorney-General `with first seat in the Ministry'. In November 1864 Weld (q.v.) was gazetted as holding `a seat in the Executive Council and the office of Premier'. Stafford (q.v.) his successor, in October 1865 was termed `First Minister'. In June 1869, when Fox again headed a Ministry, he was described officially as `Premier and member of the Executive Council.' Thereafter the term `Premier' was regularly used. Nevertheless, in the Schedule of the Civil List Act of 1873, provision was made for the salary of the Leader of the Government `being the Prime Minister'. The first Premier to use the title `Prime Minister' officially was Richard John Seddon (q.v.). It was used in the <i>New Zealand Year Book</i> of 1900 and the Imperial Conference of 1903 confirmed the practice. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/868</i>)

>  Wellington. Interviewed the premier Sir John Hall ... Dined at Bellamys ... <i>(1880,Chudleigh Diary,289,1950 ed.</i>)

>  Deputation to the Honble the Premier/ ... A deputation of representatives of the Trades and Labor [sic] Unions waited on the Hon the Premier at Government Buildings ... <i>(Sep 1, 1890,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/559,1960 ed.</i>)

>  The preparation of this Handbook was undertaken by direction of the Hon. John Balance, Premier of the Colony, in the beginning of May last. <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,iii</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> ... they practise the mission which the Premier in jaunty abandon has mapped out for them, ... <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,227</i>)

>  <b>HULL</b> [a character Massey speaks] I want to be Premier, and wear a Windsor dress;/ <i>(1907,College Songs,26</i>) [NOTES: 1906 May in 1907 Hull College Songs 26]

>  Had a meeting with Mr Seddon at 2 p.m. Dr. Hugh Walter Pigeon introduced the Premier to all of us ... <i>(Mar 27, 1906,Chudleigh Diary,432,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <i>The Premier's Private Secretary</i> I was `grafting' in the office long after the `boss' had left; ... <i>(Sep 11, 1911,Triad, The,45</i>)

>  `Bob' Semple - dubbed `the Demon of Dissension' by those who want the mob tame flunkeys - said at Waihi that New Zealand's Premier was being gushed over by the `snobs.' <i>(May 26, 1911,Maoriland Worker,12/6/4</i>)

>  <b>DALZIEL</b> Premier: Prime Minister, leader of the Government. <i>(1968,Sir Julius Vogel,32</i>)

>  Atkinson. Major, Egmont, (Premier and Colonial Treasurer). <i>(1877,NZ Parliamentary Debates,XXV/v</i>)

>  There is no accusation brought against the Premier. <i>(1877,NZ Parliamentary Debates,XXV/46</i>)

>  The Premier said the House.. had decided that the resolutions should not be considered. <i>(Sep 1, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

>  <b>MULGAN</b> His idea of a premier [was] a man who could quote Latin and speak with a cultivated accent. <i>(1934,Spur of Morning,267</i>)

>  Restrained borrowing, whether on a municipal or national scale, is inseparable from the financial adjustment system of a youthful country like ours, and the Premier was not far wrong at Taranaki the other day when he computed the requirements of this Dominion within the next five or six years at sixty millions. <i>(May 16, 1929,NZ Truth,6</i>)

>  The Premier said that he was never an advocate of separation, and could not support the resolution. <i>(Sep 9, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

>  In his reply on the Tariff Bill at 5 o'clock this morning.. the Premier was interrupted by loud snores proceeding from two of his ministers. <i>(Sep 8, 1900,Egmont Star,14</i>)

>  The Premier, when speaking at Dunedin a few weeks back.. stated that.. he had not been able to carry out his full intention. <i>(Dec 1, 1899,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)

>  Last evening the Right Honourable the Premier spoke in the sylvan township [sc. Eltham], and for fifteen or twenty minutes that Town Hall was the scene of excitement which it would be hard to describe in detail. <i>(Dec 2, 1899,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)

>  The Premier outlined the proposedlegislation for 1899. <i>(Jun 2, 1899,NZ Times,6</i>)

### presently
 
 <i>adv.</i> At directly this etym. comment: [OED2 6c dial and US 1851 `shortly' applies generally in NZ rather than 6 a. `immediately'. Compare the use ofpresently.] The following is moved from the DIRECTLY entry for check at PRESENTLY: presently. In NZ presently usually means `in a short while' (as OED 4), but I have heard it used occas. as immediately (OED 4). At <i>presently</i> 4, OED notes `In blunted sense (gradually weakened from 3): ... soon, shortly. (cf ANON 5, BY and BY 4, and colloq use of <i>directly, immediately,</i> and nearly all advbs of the same kind.) Now the ordinary use.'; W3] [The OED would seem to belie the citation from the Listener]

>  This was in the days when "presently" meant "later" - not "as of now" as it seems to today. <i>(Jul 26, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),11</i>)

### press
 
 <i>v.</i> to compress wool into bales; to operate a wool press (See [wool press](../W#wool-press))

>  .. some merchants have them to press wool bales and save freight thereby. <i>(Sep 25, 1858,Taranaki Herald,3</i>)

>  My wish is to see a proper use made of all the natural resources, and I would even go so far as for instance to use the pressure of the water, before it is supplied to the ships, or before a street is filled with it, to press wool, or do some other service on the wharf. <i>(May 27, 1880,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  My employment was chiefly in packing the woolbales which is very heavy work. You put from 90 to 120 fleeces in a bail which way [sic] from 300 to 350 lbs. It is hard to press the wool into so small a compass. We sheared some 850 sheep to-day. <i>(Nov 24, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,67,1950 ed.</i>)

>  `Press' and `sweep' are technical terms for necessary operations in the woolshed at shearing time. The former refers to pressing the fleeces into tight-packed bales ... to facilitate the transport of an otherwise bulky commodity. The latter is the process of keeping the floor (the `boards'), on which the sheep are shorn, clear of loose ends of wool, dirt and excrement. <i>(1945,NZ Geographer,1/39</i>) [NOTES: (Pascoe `Canterbury High Country')]

>  <b>WILSON</b> At the main entrance of the shearing-shed, which is a long building with pens down the centre, and an outlet at each end, stands a wool-press, and behind this are the bins into which the fleeces, after having been classed, are placed preparatory to being pressed. <i>(1894,In the Land of the Tui,242</i>)

>  368 9548 sheep shorn, 139 bales pressed. I think sheep and wool will be up to last year; run sheep much better. Ewe and paddock sheep not so good. Lice in wool and [dust from] ploughed ground all through Ewe mob. All the paddock sheeps wool must be scoured. <i>(Nov 15, 1889,Chudleigh Diary,1950</i>)

>  <b>PERRY</b> The skirtings of the neck and shoulder can be called first pieces, and the britch ends made into second pieces. The `bellies' can be placed in a pack hung on the shearing-board, and when two of these are filled they can be pressed. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,121</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> While one press was being filled and pressed, the already pressed bale would have the cap clipped (not sewn), and then it would be branded and wheeled away and another empty bale inserted <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,113</i>)

>  Pressed the remainder of the `Firsts' scoured wool, in the afternoon. <i>(Aug 5, 1868,Puketoi Station Diary</i>)

>  My press men are two fools. Abner pressed more wool in 40 minutes than they did from 2 to 6. <i>(Nov 16, 1878,Chudleigh Diary,274,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>T.C. KIERNAN</b> Being short of bales or bags could not press the wool, so had to pack it the best way I could. <i>(Feb 26, 1879,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1879 Feb 26 T.C. Kiernan Diary in 1921 Guthrie-Smith Tutira 127]

>  Pressed the first bale of this seasons wool with the Williams press. 580 lbs Rams wool Romney. <i>(Nov 28, 1882,Chudleigh Diary,315,1950 ed.</i>)

>  I then muster for Brown and Engst shears last. I put Manuel and Lyne on to shear to. ... I press. The general hand Hobbs picks up and rolls fleeces. <i>(Dec 9, 1884,Chudleigh Diary,331,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS & REEVES</b> [`A New Chum's Letter Home'] I'm engaged in the baling and pressing of wool, / And when picking up fleeces my hands are quite full. <i>(1889,Colonial Couplets,9</i>)

>  Work Wanted .. I can kill, crutch, dag, eyeclip, woolroll, press wool .. and do lots of odd jobs about a station ... <i>(Aug 26, 1915,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  As it takes about one revolution and a half of the big wheel to press a bale, it can be seen that the operation is a quick one. <i>(1986,AJHR,H-5/7</i>)

 <i>phr.</i> to hongi

>  <b>POLACK</b> Every one of them [sc. Maoris] came up to press noses, which I enacted with a few of the foremost; but respect for the shape and position of this ornamental member, made me abridge the ceremony, or I should never have retained its pristine form, so eager were these new friends to greet and welcome my arrival. <i>Ibid.</i> 85 Each presentation was accompanied by the pressing of noses (e'ongi) and expressions of `Friend, welcome; how are you all,' &c. `Ekoro airamai tenarakokoe[a2-acute],' in a whiningtone, as if each were about to condole for the loss of their entire generation. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/75</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> On entering one of the enclosures ... these visitors commenced their salutations by pressing noses with each in succession, and then sat down in silence; the women setting up a tangi, or `crying of welcome'. <i>(1847,Savage Life,1/237</i>)

>  <b>T.S.GRACE</b> ... they should shake hands and `hongi' (press noses [possibly ed.]) in the presence of all, be good friends and neighbours, ... <i>(Dec 29, 1853,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1853 Dec 29 T.S.Grace letter in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 35]

>  <b>ANGAS</b> The usual mode of salutation amongst the New Zealanders is that of pressing their noses together. <i>(1866,Polynesia,150</i>)

>  <b>ROSIER-JONES</b> Maude turns to Tui. `How are you?' `Kapai. Ke te pai.' Tui presses noses with the old lady,then Maude. <i>(1866,Cast Two Shadows,88</i>)

>  <b>LINKLATER</b> This old lady cannot speak English, but she has asked me to say that she would like to press noses with you. <i>(1953,Year of Space, A,201,1954 ed.</i>)

>  ... after the speeches were completed an invitation was made to rub noses, shake hands and have kai. <i>(Nov 12, 1988,Dominion,3</i>)

### press the flesh
 
 <i>phr.</i> [in ODE as 'chiefly Amer.'; Soljak below is wrong about the origin (TD)]

 <i>n.</i> coll. nickname for a Presbyterian [ca1948 C.D.Orsman `Appears quite a common term, esp. at primary and secondary school']

<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a woolroom, or area in a woolshed where wool is pressed and stored.

### presser
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) a press conference

>  You certainly come away from a Helen Clark post-cabinet presser considering yourself told. <i>(Jul 2, 2005,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> wool-press operator

>  James Bradley, a wool presser, deposed that the deceased and others were working in the shed about a quarter to 12 on Saturday morning. <i>(Feb 23, 1893,Otago Witness,22</i>)

>  Wanted - Wool Presser, 30s. <i>(Nov 29, 1906,Wanganui Herald,7</i>)

>  <b>CARR</b> The boss Maori undertakes to supply all the rouseabouts (as shed hands are termed) i.e., fleece pickers, wool rollers and `Sheep oh!' the [sic] owner or manager generally gets his own pressers and expert (where machines are run). <i>Ibid.</i> 15-16 Pressing. - When there is sufficient wool in one bin, the pressers (generally two) get to work. One gets into the `box' as the lower portion of the patent presses is called, while his mate ... hands him ... the fleeces. These are placed in tiers, and tramped down until the lower box is full. An upper box is then swung ... or [16] lowered on to the lower one, and the process repeated. When full, the `monkey' or cover is put into position, the spears (wire rope) affixed, and the two men lever the cover down until the top box is clear. <i>(1913,Country Work,14</i>)

>  "That's nothing", the presser informed me. <i>(Oct 15, 1947,NZ Observer,21</i>)

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> Two pressers were paid 17/- a bale. <i>(1982,Blue Cliffs,89</i>)

>  The pressers had put in a bale. <i>(Dec 2, 1897,Otago Witness,58</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a member of a shearing gang who presses wool into bales.

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> One classer, two rollers, two fleece-pickers, two pressers, one penner up, make the shed contingent ... <i>(1951,NZ High Country,27</i>)

>  <b>STAPLES</b> The tablehand, a retired farmer, picked off the dirty bits and rolled the fleece and it was picked up by the presser. <i>(1964,Cooks and Shepherds Come Away,117</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> wool book: the responsibility of the pressers. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,92</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> Twelve shearers on the board of the main shearing shed, with another 12 people around them - rousies, penners and pressers. <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,118</i>)

>  <b>HINDMARSH</b> We had two of them in the shearing gang as pressers a couple of months ago ... <i>(1997,Come Be a Pioneer,100</i>)

>  <b>WATERSON</b> At morning smoko time the presser came back to the woolshed ... <i>(1999,Some Buggers I've Seen,82</i>)

>  woolled-up: when the shearers' output exceeds the ability of the woolhandlers, classer or presser to keep up. <i>(2001,Woolpro Glossary of Wool Terms,42</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (Rural) wool-pressing

>  <b>CARR</b> Pressing. - When there is sufficient wool in one bin, the pressers (generally two) get to work. One gets into the `box' as the lower portion of the patent presses is called, while his mate ... hands him ... the fleeces. These are placed in tiers, and tramped down until the lower box is full. An upper box is then swung ... or [16] lowered on to the lower one, and the process repeated. When full, the `monkey' or cover is put into position, the spears (wire rope) affixed, and the two men lever the cover down until the top box is clear. <i>(1913,Country Work,15</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the compressing of fleece-wool into pockets or bales.

 <i>n.</i> wool presser, one who operates a wool press

I put on young Wood as pressman as he was near at hand ... and put on my two station boys as fleece boys. The shed did not stop one minute. <i>(Nov 4, 1889,Chudleigh Diary,368,1950 ed.</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### pressure-cooked
 
 <i>adj.</i> hasty ; hastily accomplished; rushed (with suggestion that the haste is unnecessary, and perhaps harmful to proper accomplishment)

### pretties
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Marbles had a language of its own. There were names for the marbles, the games, and all the various aspects of the play. There were terms for marbles such as `taw' or `shooter', referring to the marbles used to fire with; `dubs', `dates', `stakes', `dukes', or `changers', referring to the marbles put down to be fired at. Then there were the terms referring to particular kinds of marbles: for example, agates, aggies, aggotties, American alleys, blood alleys, bomb-squashers, bonies, bonsers, bottlies, bull's eyes, bum-squashers, cat's eyes, chinkies, connies, duckies, eggies, glassies, Greeks, milkies, molly-bars, mulley-bars [mullibars: large marbles], peelies, peewees, piss-wees, pretties, Scotties, Scottish alleys, smokies, stinkies, stonies, stripies, tom-bobblers, white alleys, and in more recent years ball-bearings, chippies, plastics, and woodies. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,174</i>)

>  <b>KEITH & MAIN</b> Marbles were the currency of childhood and there were an astonishing number of names for them, some referring to the material or colour, others to value or function in the game. There were agates, aggies, American alleys, blood alleys, bomb-squashers, bonies, bonsers, bottlies, bull's eyes, bum-squashers, cats-eye, [sic] chinkies, connies, duckies, eggies, glassies, Greeks, milkies, mollies, peelies, peewees, pisswees, pretties, Scotties, smokies, stinkies, stonies, stripies, tom-bobblers and whities. The march of technology added ball bearings - ballies or steelies - which were unbeatable when it came to smashing an opponent's miserable glassie. World War II[roman] brought `clayeys' often imperfectly spherical they were pathetic terracotta substitutes for real marbles. <i>(1984,NZ Yesterdays,288</i>)

### pretty quick
 
 <i>n.</i> a quail

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Thus the quail which said `Pretty Quick, Pretty Quick' in rapid repetition in the day (so much so that they were sometimes called Pretty Quicks) in the evening said `I'll put it out, I'll put it out.' <i>(1956,Pioneer Recollections,49</i>) [NOTES: 1947-1948 Beattie Pioneer Recollections (1956) 49]

### preventive detention
 
 <i>n.</i> an indeterminate sentence introduced by the Criminal Justice Act 1954 for offenders repeatedly convicted of certain crimes. [Jim Cameron writes : This replaced the previous sentence of <i>reformative detention</i> (1910) and <i>declaration as habitual offender</i> and <i>habitual criminal</i> (1906). None of these terms is recorded in DNZE. Its scope has been protean. In its present form (Criminal Justice Act 1985 as amended) it is a sentence of indefinite detention with no fixed maximum that may be imposed on persons aged 21 or more convicted of certain sexual and other serious offences. A sentence under the same name existed in England from 1908 to 1967.]

>  At the other end of the scale is the indeterminate sentence of preventive detention. A sentence of the same name existed in Britain from 1908 to 1967. Introduced in New Zealand in 1954 to replace the sentence of reformative detention (1910) and declaration as an habitual offender and habitual criminal (1906), it remains very much alive here. <i>(Jan 1999,NZWords,2</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a present (See [also pressie](../A#also-pressie))

>  Those strangely infantile condensations - `prezzies' and `rellies' (for presents and relatives) were bandied about on the radio quite a bit recently. It looks as if they are going to join veges, ciggy, brekky, cardy - and all the rest as OK idiom. But there is one antipodean expression that charms people internationally ... That is the word `naughty', for sexual intercourse. <i>(Jan 10, 1989,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  Buying native trees and ferns as prezzies helps protect the habitat for our endangered wildlife. <i>(Dec 19, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),23</i>)

### pricker
 
 <i>n.</i> get/have the pricker: to be upset, annoyed with, often in a sulky manner; in the general sense of temper, sulks (variously get one's pricker up, get the pricker (with), give somebody the pricker, to become, get, make, somebody, angry, annoyed, nettled) [OED says Aus & NZ.]

>  <b>WILSON</b> `You deserve everything you get.' `Now don't get the pricker, Len.' She smiled and patted my hand. <i>(1984,South Pacific Street,69</i>)

>  <b>J B CAMERON</b> ca1945 (W.Cameron) and heard in Wellington 1952 Aug 27<i>get one's pricker up</i>, to get one's temper up<i> get the pricker,</i> get angry, nettled, or sulky; fed up (Suggests<i> pricker</i> = pricks = spines : ie get one's back up (as a cat etc), or <i>get hairy</i>) <i>(Aug 27, 1952,Personal Communication</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>pricker</i> , phr.<i> get the pricker with</i> to be angry with; ref. Gordon Slatter; eg `Watch out, mate. The Sarge has really got the pricker with you.' c. 1930. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,88</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> You'll come a gutzer son I ses... Got the pricker with me. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,91</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> Even if they're only up against Italians, front-line troops get the pricker if you sneer. <i>Ibid.</i> 311 They were all beginning to get the pricker a bit. No wonder. On the go since Syria. <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,296</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Got the pricker with me. Slingin off at me he was. You're up the boo-ay he told me. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,91</i>)

>  [Hori] I'm feeling as cheerful as a Fijian in a freezing chamber ... What is really giving me the pricker [=getting me down; low spirits] is the fact that I have got the plurry toothache. ... the boss tells me to use more tact when I write to pakehas 'cause they are not as tough as Maoris and get the pricker very quick. <i>(Jan 1963,Home Life,2</i>)

>  <b>McCALLUM</b> Then Eisenhower getting the pricker [with Kruschov] and not making the apology. <i>Ibid.</i> 76 I sit in [the doctor's] waiting room for about an hour ..., and I have just about got the pricker when a ... girl ... comes in and says the doctor will see me now. <i>(1963,Half-Gallon Jar,28</i>)

>  <b>FRANCES</b> `Miro's got the pricker,' Ben said, flopping his lips in a grin. `He cleared off by himself.' <i>(1964,Johnny Rapana,94</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> <i>Get the pricker</i> is equivalent to English slang <i>get the needle</i> `get annoyed'. <i>(1966,Australian & NZ English [TS],122</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Got the pricker with me because I put his weights up - A terrible stick man, that Tank - Woppit Upper - <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,163</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> Boxer got to his feet, his fist bunched. `Now, now, Boxer, don't start getting the pricker. ...' <i>(1975,Breathing Spaces,107</i>)

>  <b>O'SULLIVAN</b> That joker's been giving me the pricker for weeks. <i>(1985,Shuriken,35</i>)

### Prime Minister
 
 <i>n.</i> . [[? date] G.H. Scholefield (ed) NZ Parliamentary Record [?p] [Mr Seddon was first Premier to assume the title, which he did in 1899. It was officially used in the N.Z. Yr Bk of 1900+5 the Parliamentary debates of 1902, it was used in the constitutional debates in the 1st session of the 1st N.Z. Parliament in 1854, + it appeared in the schedule of the Civil List Act of 1873.] [check this] The operative date for the introduction of this term to official documents was August 1900, although from context in NZPD in which it was used (more than once) by Prime Minister Seddon (who was still addressed in Parliament as Premier during the debate), it appears to have been in some general use before that date.]

>  PRIME MINISTER GIVES AN AT HOME. An at home at Hatfield House was attended by five hundred. <i>(Jun 21, 1902,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)

>  With respect to the allegations regarding sly grog, the Prime Minister said that the Government believed in the strict administration of the law ... <i>(Jul 17, 1913,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  `INFORMATION,' of South Otago, disputes `Snippet's' statement (April 26) that Richard John Seddon was New Zealand's first Prime Minister on the grounds that the term `Prime Minister' was not used officially till New Zealand obtained Dominion status on September 26, 1907. THE term `Prime Minister' was first used in New Zealand during a constitutional debate in New Zealand's first Parliament in 1854, and was subsequently used on many occasions during the late 19th century. In 1873, for example, in a schedule to the civil list, provision was made for the leader of the Government `being the Prime Minister.' The title `Prime Minister' was adopted officially in 1899 and Richard John Seddon was the first New Zealand Premier to use it. It was officially used in the New Zealand Year Book in 1900 and in Parliamentary debates in 1902. <i>(Jun 7, 1960,NZ Truth,42</i>)

>  <i>(1971,Speeches & Documents,42</i>)

>  <i>(1982,NZ Almanac,49</i>) [NOTES: [for styles PM Premier]]

>  New Zealand ... Prime Minister, Colonial Treasurer etc, Rt. Hon. Richd. J.Seddon. <i>(1901,Whitaker's Almanac,520</i>)

>  It will be noted that last year's Conference was opened by the Governor-General, followed by speeches by the Prime Minister (Hon. G. W. Forbes), Hon. J. G. Coates and Mr H. Holland, so you will see that the prestige of the [Farmers'] Union is very high. <i>(Apr 26, 1932,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,3</i>)

>  If I become a member of the Cabinet, or if accident made me Prime Minister - I doubt very much whether I should be equal to such an onerous position. <i>(1877,NZ Parliamentary Debates,XXV/49</i>)

>  THE PRIME MINISTER .. Mr Massey stated last night: "It is impossible for me to say that the results of the election have been satisfactory either to the Dominion or to the Government Party ..." <i>(Dec 12, 1922,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,1</i>)

>  This opportunity now given will prove the sincerity of those who.. have said to me "As Prime Minister of this country, you are underpaid. <i>(1900,NZ Parliamentary Debates,CXII/333</i>)

>  An Act to amend the Law relating to the Salaries and Allowances of Responsible Ministers of the Crown... There shall be payable.. the annual sum of eight thousand pounds for.. the annual salaries of Ministers, as follows:- The Prime Minister.. .. .. GBP1,600. <i>(1900,NZ Statutes,64 Vict. / 00</i>) [NOTES: Check page]

>  By another statute the annual appropriation for Ministers' Salaries is fixed at the sum of GBP8,900 of which GBP1,600 is for the Prime Minister... The Executive Council now consists of.. Right Honourable Richard John Seddon, P. C.., Prime Minister. Ibid. 27 Following previous practice, the wording is: 'Executive Council.. Rt. Hon. R.J. Seddon, P.C., LL.D., Premier. <i>(1900,NZ Official Yearbook,27</i>)

 <i>adj.</i> [relating to primers, earliest classes at primary school]

 <i>n.</i> the foot rags worn by swaggers

### prison camp
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical) a low security prison [Jim writes: a historical form of penal institution in NZ. The first prison camp was at Milford Sound in the 1890s; subsequently there were <i>tree-planting camps</i> in the North and South Island. See annual reports in AJHR series H-20. Prison camp may be an international term in this sense as well as in relation to war prisoners. NZOD defines it ambiguously as a camp for detaining prisoners of war <i>or of the State</i>.] (See [prison farm](pop-pyo.md#prison-farm))

>  The prison camps at Hanmer, Waipa and Waiotapu are doing excellent work. <i>Ibid.</i> 16 Seven prisoners were transferred during the year,1 to the prison camp for first offenders at Waipa. <i>(1912,AJHR,IV/H-20: 7</i>)

>  The tree-planting prison camp at Waiotapu has now got beyond an experiment, and is turning out a great success. <i>(1903,AJHR,H20 : 3</i>)

### prison farm
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical) a low-security prison incorporating a farm on which the inmates were employed.

### Prisoners' Base
 
 <i>n.</i> a children's game

>  <b>GEE</b> "Come on," I would say, "we're going to play Prisoners' Base". <i>(1972,In My Father's Den,52</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> We had a lot of games like prisoners base where you had two teams and you tried to collar one another for prisoners, like a spider catching a fly. <i>(1981,Speaking a Silence,8</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Others wanted [to play] `Prisoners' Base' or `Chivvy', `Brewer', `Egg-in-the hat', `Duck-stone', `Red Rover', or `Kingy-seeny'. The boys did not know where this last game came from. They did not know its proper spelling or pronunciation. `Kingy-seeny' it was to them and they did not care whether that had a meaning or not. This rough and tumble game was the most popular of all. <i>(1905,Bush Boys of NZ,84</i>)

### private school
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  * A private school for the superior classes. Principally for the children of the lower classes, paying about 6d. and 9d. per week, and private schools. <i>(Jan 20, 1844,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,2</i>)

>  A nine-roomed dwelling, owned by Mr. Lyanar, and occupied by Misses Donnellan and others as a private school, was destroyed by fire at midnight. <i>(Jun 29, 1905,Wanganui Herald,7</i>)

>  Mr. Ellis was formerly well known in New Plymouth in educational matters, he having carried on a private school here many years ago, among his pupils being a number of well known citizens of to-day. <i>(Nov 15, 1900,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  While on the subject of education, it is gratifying to state that, independently of private schools, there is another public one, connected with the United Christians, where, we, believe, a very large number of children are taught. <i>(Jan 21, 1843,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,183</i>)

>  What would it cost if they went to private schools? I don't think any private school would do it at GBP2 per head, per quarter - GBP24 per annum for the lot. <i>(Feb 22, 1884,Wanganui Chronicle,3</i>)

>  I marvel much that before now no pen has been employed against a practice which I consider unseemly and very injurous - namely, the public examination and awarding prizes to female children at private schools. <i>(Jan 6, 1871,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  At the Education Board's Conference a resolution was passed, "that while protesting against the establishment of private schools, yet, if private schools are to be allowed, the authorites connected with each proposed school shold [sic] notify the Education Board of the district of their intention to establish the school, and should submit to the Board information regarding, (1), the buildings in which it is proposed to hold the school, and the accomodation provided thereby ..." <i>(Sep 27, 1919,Grey River Argus,4</i>)

>  He had supported the Private School Bill, which applied principally to the Catholics. <i>(May 8, 1890,Otago Witness,24</i>)

>  <b>MURDOCH</b> It is noteworthy that private schools tend to pay more attention to the claims of music. <i>(1944,High Schools of NZ, The,148</i>)

>  WANTED, A MIDDLE-AGED MARRIED LADY as Governess to assist in a Private School. A pious person of evangelical sentiments would be preferred. <i>(Jan 28, 1853,Daily Southern Cross,1</i>)

>  The net result is that private schools are past becoming the order of the day, where the children of the rich are taught under brighter and better conditions and in a more congenial environment; in fact, the general surroundings of the private school, in every respect, especially lighting, heating, ventilation, sanitation, etc., outmarches the public school at every turn. <i>(Aug 14, 1920,NZ Truth,3</i>)

>  Snobbishness is growing in the North Island and a sort of false pride, for people are going without comforts to enable them to send their children to private schools, when our public school system, especially for boys, is one of the best things we have in New Zealand. <i>(Mar 29, 1932,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,3</i>)

>  St Kentigern headmaster Nigel Troy is one of several private school principals who have noted the growing tendency for both parents to work so that the income of one can be dedicated to getting a child through a private school. <i>(Jan 25, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),24</i>)

### pro
 
 <i>v.</i> to take a pro[hibition] order out against (a person) (See [cf. pro order](pop-pyo.md#pro-order))

>  <b>DAVIN</b> `What the hell nonsense is this? Why not?' I felt myself blustering, in front of the friends for whom I'd just ordered the first round. `Sorry, I'm not allowed to serve you.' `Why not, I say?' `Well,' he looked at my friends and tried to say it quietly but they heard all right, `your wife's had you pro'd.' [199] Imagine it, a prohibition order taken out against me. And they say it's a man's world, when a woman can stop her husband buying a drink. Not just buying one either, she can even stop him from getting his friends to buy him one. <i>(1959,No Remittance,198</i>) [NOTES: [refce WW1]]



 
 <i>n.</i>

### Probationary Assistant
 
 <i>n.</i> a Teachers College graduate in the first (probationary) year of teaching. [short form is P.A.]

>  <b>BACON</b> <i>Probationary Assistant</i>-New Zealand student teachers normally do two years' training in one of the country's Teachers' Colleges. During this time, they work about five separate monthly practice periods in selected schools. Their third year of training is as Probationary Assistant in a larger school. If this probationary year's service is satisfactory, trainees receive their Teacher's Certificate, and are then eligible to apply for a teaching position anywhere in New Zealand or in New Zealand's Island Territories. <i>(1963,In the Sticks,182</i>)

>  <b>PEARSON</b> <i>Probationary Assistant</i>. J.W. Perry. The term said to have become official in the `Regulations for Public Schools: Salaries, Grading, Staffing, etc. ' as amended by Regulation 6 of the Amended Regulations under the Education Act 1914 made on the 22/3/1926 (Previously known as <i>Student Assistant</i>) <i>(1963,Coal Flat,8</i>)

### procesh
 
 <i>n.</i> procession [general, not university, procession]

 <i>v.</i> to form or participate in a procession
