### proddy dog
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal, offensive) a Protestant

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>Proddy dog</i> Protestant, offensive, usually from Catholic children, inviting counter-insult `Catholic dogs stink like frogs'. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,88</i>)

### proddyhopper
 
 <i>n.</i> (obsolete) Catholic school children's abusive term for a Protestant

>  <b>McCARTHY</b> It was only natural that we `Cattledogs' [from Marist Brothers School, Tasman Street, c. 1916] were frequently ambushed by those `Proddyhoppers' [from Mount Cook State School]. <i>(1973,Listen...!,21</i>)

>  <b>KEINZLY</b> `It was the proddyhopper, Sister -' `What is a <i>proddyhopper</i>, Augustine?' `A protestant kid.' <i>(1960,Tangahano,165</i>)

>  <b>PARK</b> We children had a kind of good humoured rivalry, admittedly, often shouting, 'Yah yah, Cattledog or 'Proddyhopper' as the case might be, but that was all. <i>(1992,Fence Around the Cuckoo,94</i>)

### prodigal duck
 
 <i>n.</i> (recipe)

>  <i>PRODIGAL DUCK</i> Stuff a fillet of pork with prunes and apples. Pot roast very slowly ... <i>(1936,Recipe Book [St Mary's],26</i>)

### produce
 
 <i>v.</i> v.i. [I put the nips into the fellow in charge for a feed. He was a quean to look at, yet he produced; but when I tried to put the nips in for a franc he said he had been stung before, so you see I came a gutser.]

### producer board
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LEVY</b> Information gleaned from these surveys statistically collected and analysed by the Board's staff, will enable the Producer Boards to review better the detailed farming structure, ... <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,349,1970 ed.</i>)

>  Councils differ from the old Producer Boards not merely in being Producer- Government partnerships, but in controlling (or at least having the power to control) all wholesaling in their produce - i.e. both overseas and local. <i>(1945,Korero (AEWS Background Bulletin),2(23) : 16</i>)

>  This is the first time in 70 years of producer boards that one has breached its obligations. <i>(Jan 29, 2006,Sunday Star Times,D2</i>)

>  Five producer boards, theoretically owned by farmers and enshrined in statute, cover the main areas of New Zealand agriculture, the Dairy Board, Meat Producers Board, Wool Board, Apple and Pear Marketing Board and the Kiwifruit Marketing Board. <i>(Oct 29, 1992,Dominion,8</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a specific developmental and/or marketing body of primary products such as wool, meat, dairy products and fruit.

>  .. we must abandon any remaining statutory powers the producer boards possess and maybe the producer boards themselves. <i>(Jul 20, 1995,NZ Farmer,8</i>)

>  <b>JACKSON & MCROBIE</b> The first primary producer boards were set up in the wake of the collapse of the commandeer system (q.v.) following the end of World War I. In the years that followed [the establishment of the meat and Dairy Boards] producer boards were set up to oversee the production and export of fruit, honey, poultry and hops. After the [second world]war producer boards were re-established and extended to include wool, eggs, kauri gum, maize, barley, potatoes, bobby calves, pork, apples, berry and citrus fruits, tobacco, and as recently as 1988, kiwifruit (q.v.). <i>(1996,Historical Dictionary of NZ,57</i>)

>  Looking forward, Wiggins agrees that the days of the producer boards are numbered. <i>(2001,Wool Grower,Winter 4</i>)

### production
 
 <i>n.</i> milk production

>  While long lactation is an important factor for high production per cow, consulting officers occasionally advise early drying off to build up reserves of feed for winter. <i>(Feb 10, 1960,NZ Dairy Exporter,52</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (graffiti term)

>  Graffitti Speak... Production : Several pieces by different artists done at the same time on the same wall or other site. The sections join to be one large mural. <i>(Nov 30, 2000,Evening Post,19</i>)

### production index/PI
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a measure of an animal's level of production [esp. dairy cows].

>  As a rule, use BI for breeding and production index for culling. Effective BIs and PIs are not available unless a farmer herd-tests and identifies progeny through the local livestock or breed association. When looking at cows' production, remember that PIs rank cows within a herd. <i>(Jun 1982,NZ Dairy Exporter,5</i>)

>  The PI shows a cow's productive ability but it gives a poor estimate of her breeding ability or genetic merit. <i>(Jun 1994,NZ Dairy Exporter,99</i>)

### professional
 
 <i>n.</i> a professional thief

>  <b>TONKIN</b> There are a class of men known as the `professionals' - that is, men who live by stealing and swindling, and who have been in prison again and again - who take a special delight in destroying their clothing whenever they can do so without fear of detection and punishment [to get a new issue]. <i>(1980,Dunedin Gaol,9</i>) [NOTES: >c1875 Otago Guardian in 1980 Tonkin Dunedin Gaol 9]

### proficiency
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical) short for proficiency examination [so far as I know NZ only (HWO) `Neither of us never got our proficiency' (R. Mason) -]

>  <b>LOCKE</b> Some went through "Proficiency" but that didn't get them into High School, because there wasn't a High School in a hundred miles. <i>(1984,Kauri & The Willow, The,153</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> .. it was quite a good school, and Mr Duncan said that Carly, though a nervous little idiot, was safe to get her Proficiency. <i>(1938,Godwits Fly, The,37,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>THOMAS</b> At the age of twelve and a half years [c1904] I had passed the final school examination for my `certificate of proficiency' ... <i>(1970,Way Up North,70</i>)

>  <b>THOMPSON</b> They had left school early, he at 14, she at 13, both having obtained their proficiency. <i>(1980,All My Lives,11</i>)

>  <b>FRAME</b> She was in her Proficiency Year at school, and all the talk at home was of whether she'd get her proficiency,.... <i>(1982,To the Is-Land,84,1982 ed.</i>)

>  In my last year at primary school, 1935,the Proficiency exam, which was thequalification for going to secondaryschool, was abolished. <i>(Aug 8, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),64</i>)

>  <b>GIBBONS</b> Any pupil who had gained proficiency could leave.<i>Ibid.</i> 25 Proficiency was a Junior Free Place. <i>(1989,Recollections (various authors/titles),22</i>)

>  <b>GIBBONS</b> Any pupil who had gained proficiency could leave. <i>(1989,Recollections (various authors/titles),22</i>)

>  The test of "proficiency" is not made sufficiently wide to discover the true intelligence of the candidate. <i>(Nov 3, 1926,NZ Observer,7</i>)

>  Proficiency and competency certificateshe considered an unwanted evil, and othermeans infinitely more efficient could befound to judge the capacity and work ofthe child. <i>(Jul 15, 1919,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  I obtained my proficiency last year, and I think I will be going on to the High School this year. <i>(Feb 12, 1924,Otago Witness,71</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> The principle of "accrediting" and ofabolishing the "proficiency" examination,which was the entrance examination intosecondary schools, became part of thepolicy of the Labour Party and of thefirst Labour Government, 1935-49. <i>(1983,History of the University of Auckland, A,181</i>)

### proficiency certificate
 
 (see also proficiency examination, proficiency exam, proficiency) <i>n.</i> (See [proficiency examination](pop-pyo.md#proficiency-examination),[proficiency](pop-pyo.md#proficiency))

>  It was resolved that every member endeavor to urge upon parents having children with the proficiency certificate to enrol them. <i>(Apr 20, 1910,Hawera & Normanby Star,8</i>)

>  The most important [resolutions] are: (1) That in secondary schools all pupils not disqualified by age be given the opportunity of obtaining the proficiency certificate qualifying for free education; (2) that all now attending secondary schools who have passed the proficiency examination, and who have obtained scholarships be put on an equal footing with those eligible for free education next year, providing that they have passed the proficiency examinations, and are within the prescribed age. <i>(Jan 14, 1903,Otago Witness,18</i>)

>  [The Minister for Education] agreed that, at any rate for a proficiency certificate, there should be a uniform examination throughout New Zealand. <i>(Apr 10, 1909,Evening Post,15</i>)

>  If a boy secures his proficiency certificate it by no means follows that he is therefore prepared mentally to receive the education.. provided by a secondary school. <i>(Nov 3, 1926,NZ Observer,7</i>)

>  The following pupils have succeeded in gaining certificates of proficiency or competency in the annual examinations conducted by the Hawke's Bay Education Board ... <i>(Dec 20, 1920,Poverty Bay Herald,3</i>)

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> .. I completed my primary education, at the age of thirteen and gained, by examination, the Proficiency Certificate which in those days marked the high point, and usually the end, of formal education for most children. <i>(1983,Memoirs</i>)

>  The photograph of my mother .. "dressed top the nines" at the camp, may be of interest as well as her Khandallah School Proficiency Certificate of which she was very proud. <i>(Dec 2004,NZ Memories,No. 51/67</i>)

### proficiency examination
 
 <i>n.</i> [aka proficiency exam, see also proficiency certificate] (See [proficiency certrificate](../P#proficiency-certrificate))

>  The most important [resolutions] are: (1) That in secondary schools all pupils not disqualified by age be given the opportunity of obtaining the proficiency certificate qualifying for free education. (2) That all now attending secondary schools who have passed the proficiency examination, and who have obtained scholarships be put on an equal footing with those eligible for free education next year, providing that they have passed the proficiency examinations, and are within the prescribed age. <i>(Jan 14, 1903,Otago Witness,18</i>)

>  Candidates [for the Gibbons' scholarship] must .. obtain at least 75 per cent of marks at the proficiency examination ... <i>(Jul 5, 1907,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

>  A member of the [school] committee was also appointed to act in conjunction with Mr. Swinbourne at the proficiency examination. <i>(Dec 2, 1908,Hawera & Normanby Star,5</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> You know you've got to get strong and pass your proficiency exam and get your scholarship. <i>(1948,Tidal Creek,20,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>CHRISTIE</b> I was still only fifteen and had left school with the gaining of the Proficiency Examination which entitled me to enter high school but my parents did not know that I was also eligible for free travelling costs to the nearest college. <i>(1987,Candles and Canvas,139</i>)

>  In my last year at primary school, 1935, the Proficiency exam, which was the qualification for going to secondary school, was abolished. <i>(Aug 8, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),64</i>)

>  Proficiency and competency certificates he considered an unwanted evil, and other means infinitely more efficient could be found to judge the capacity and work of the child. <i>(Jul 15, 1919,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> The principle of "accrediting" and of abolishing the "proficiency" examination, which was the entrance examination into secondary schools, became part of the policy of the Labour Party and of the first Labour Government, 1935-49. <i>(1983,History of the University of Auckland, A,181</i>)

### prog
 
 <i>v.</i> to poke (out)

>  <b>MARIE</b> Get a stick, let's prog it [sc. the ball]. <i>(1906,Alexander Rudolph,35</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> food; sweets, recorded in NZ 1851-1879

>  Two packing cases and a log,/ A cupboard - all bereft of prog -/ A shelf of bottles - but no grog -/ My weary senses battled. <i>(Feb 14, 1851,Lyttelton Times,8</i>)

>  <b>THATCHER</b> (The Caledonian Gathering) And the booth they visited for prog,/ And sometimes took a nip of grog;/ There was Grieve, one of your famed Town Board,/ McIvor too his orders roared/ <i>(1864,Invercargill Minstrel,6</i>)

>  <b>THATCHER</b> He's joined with little Francis, who owns a monstrous dog, / And it must cost an awful lot to keep the brute in prog, <i>(1869,Wit and Humour: Local Songs...,10</i>) [NOTES: Sights at Grahamstown]



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> to steal [ca1940 (Mainly schoolboys')]

### progress party
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> Instead, it urged, the Government should divide disputed land and buy whatever proportion belonged to the `friendlies' or `the progress party', as the settlers called the `land sellers'. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,121</i>)

### Progressive Association
 
 <i>n.</i> [ca1930 at least]

### progs
 
 <i>n.</i> spectacles [ca1953 Wellington (? origin)]

### Prohib
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical, informal) a Prohibitionist, a supporter of national prohibition [ORIGIN: abbreviation, [This was a coll. abbrev. current from, say, the late 90's to WWI when the prohibitionist movement was strong. Prohibitionist (OED from 1854) very frequent in newspapers of the time eg 1905 June 24 Truth 1-2] -->1905 Aug 12 Truth 8/1/6 ... the Prohibs. ...]

>  A Christchurch Prohib, who lately returned .... <i>(Sep 30, 1905,NZ Truth,15/4/5</i>)

>  Prohib. spouters. <i>(Aug 12, 1908,NZ Truth</i>)

### prohibition order
 
 <i>n.</i> an order made by a court to prevent a person's drinking intoxicating liquor (See [cf. pro n](../P#pro-n))

>  An elderly man from Inglewood was fined GBP1 (costs 9s), for procuring whisky at New Plymouth during the currency of a prohibition order. <i>(Apr 19, 1907,Taranaki Herald,7</i>)

>  For having procured liquor during the currency of a prohibition order, Alfred Millen was fined GBP5; in default, 30 days' imprisonment. <i>(Jan 7, 1908,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  Among the other provisions of the Bill may be mentioned clauses prohibiting the sale of liquor to children apparently under sixteen years of age. Also, one prohibiting the sale of liquor to habitual drunkards. Any two Justices may issue such a prohibition order, extending over twelve months, and may, if they think fit, renew any prohibition of [sic] the end of the term. <i>(Jun 18, 1881,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)

>  The issue of a prohibition order, granted on the application of a runner of one of those cheap boarding-houses. <i>(Aug 12, 1905,NZ Truth,8/7/1</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> `What the hell nonsense is this? Why not?' I felt myself blustering, in front of the friends for whom I'd just ordered the first round. `Sorry, I'm not allowed to serve you.' `Why not, I say?' `Well,' he looked at my friends and tried to say it quietly but they heard all right, `your wife's had you pro'd.' [199] Imagine it, a prohibition order taken out against me. And they say it's a man's world, when a woman can stop her husband buying a drink. Not just buying one either, she can even stop him from getting his friends to buy him one. <i>(1959,No Remittance,198</i>)

>  Three cases on Wednesday last at the Dunedin Police Court were chiefly remarkable for the number of people run in for muzziness and fracturing their prohibition orders. <i>(Apr 2, 1910,NZ Truth,6</i>)

>  Formerly the fact of a prohibition order being issued against a man, meant that he got more to drink than ever he did, but this is all changed now, and the prohibited person, who is found on licensed premises, is deemed to be guilty of a criminal act. <i>(Apr 11, 1896,Mataura Ensign,2</i>)

>  "I see you have not had a prohibition order for some years. Do you think it would help you?" he asked. <i>(Feb 25, 1926,NZ Truth,7</i>)

>  For instance, when you see green snakes in carpet slippers sitting on the end of the bed, do you try to guess what they're doing or do you immediately take out a prohibition order? <i>(Dec 4, 1930,NZ Truth,8</i>)

### projagenate
 
 <i>adj.</i> difficult

>  <b>MASON</b> <i>(1952,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: b. R.Mason heard 1952 <i>Not too projagenate</i>, not too difficult, not unreasonable]



 
 <i>adj.</i> a fanciful coinage (like, eg., obstropulous) distorting <i>procrastinate</i> and used almost as a c.p. by young soldiers. Training college students. [ca1945 (Army and Training College) Don't projagenate now. Hurry up.]

### projagenent
 
 <i>adj.</i> (too) thrustful, interfering; having the unfortunate knack of being always in the way.

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Prejaganint. (Too) thrustful, interfering; having the unfortunate knack of being always in the way: New Zealanders': from ca. 1912. Perhaps a corruption of <i>prejudiced. </i> <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,657</i>)

>  New Zealanders have their curiously sounding slang. `Prejaganint,' `snitcher' or `snozzler' (a good specimen), ... <i>(Apr 2, 1938,Press, The,18</i>) [NOTES: McNab NZ Slang]



 
<b>2</b> <i>adj.</i> sticking out a mile ca1930's-1940's

### Project Crimson
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) a charitable conservation trust established to protect pohutukawa and rata in New Zealand

>  Three years ago, with the help of Project Crimson, Clive began propagating seedlings from the rata on his property, and hopes that eventually South Wairarapa will again be ablaze with its crimson flowers. <i>(Feb 2007,Forest & Bird,1</i>)

>  Over the last decade, Project Crimson support of East Cape pohutukawa plantings has been enthusiastically welcomed by local communities who have cared for the flourishing trees. <i>(Jun 2007,Conservation Action,43</i>)

### Project Employment Programme
 
 <i>n.</i> usually abbreviated to P.E.P. a scheme in the early 1980s for the employment (and training) of the otherwise unemployed.

### Project K
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Leanne McCarthy, Hutt valley director of youth development programme Project K. is another quick to vouch for Umaga'a loyalty. <i>(Jan 14, 2006,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  Judith Hutchinson joined the Project K mentoring programme in 1999 while working at Carter Holt Harvey as a positive and pro-active way of helping more of New Zealand's youth reach their potential. She loved it so much, she mentored three students. <i>(2006,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://projectk.org.nz/?go=m_profiles&sp=pkAccessed 15 Feb 2006]

### proline
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Not for me the noisy proline/ but the quiet of the auger and fang-spanner - / long tom and jim crow,/ strumming on the old banjo./ ... [fn] <i>long tom: long blade used for placing measured amount of gravel under sleepers jim crow: large horseshoe-shaped tool, with screw, used for bending rails banjo: shovel </i> <i>(Mar 1, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),11</i>) [NOTES: (David George `Euchre Weather')]

### prong
 
 <i>n.</i> cash, money. [1953 Heard Wellington : Run out of prong. Could you lend us some.]

### pronging
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  And I haven't heard "pronging" to refer to sexual intercourse for a very long time. <i>(Mar 4, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),40</i>)

### pronker
 
 <i>n.</i> a show-off [see ODE to pronk (Afrikaans)]

>  He was puzzled when a workmate called him a pronker. At first he thought he had been identified as a plonker, but found the word pronker was chosen deliberately. The South African verb pronk means to show off, as a springbok might do when it makes a spectacular leap, perhaps to score a try to win the World Cup. <i>(Jan 5, 2002,Dominion,12</i>)

### prop
 
 <i>v.</i> to propose

>  It was propped to me that I write a book. <i>(Sep 20, 1999,TV1,Crossfire</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> pole to hold up a clothes line

>  <b>THATCHER</b> In London the swells in the park drive all round, / But the diggers drive here with a pick underground; / And the props that they use, surely everyone knows / Ain't the same used in London for hanging up clothes. / The women use fans* there, the men use them here, / Though to cockneys that statement may sound very queer, / And the fellows that frequently hang round hotels, / Out here are called loafers, at home are called swells. -->[p 89 fn] * A fan is a machine for pumping air down the shafts. <i>(1875,NZ Songster,No.3/88</i>) [NOTES: London & the Diggings]

>  <b>RENeE</b> The good old days:.. Props falling down from a line of newly-washed sheets so the sheets drag on the muddy ground. <i>(1998,Yin & Tonic,110</i>)

>  The clothesline was a length of number 8 wire strung between two trees, the prop made from a Manuka (tea tree) pole. <i>(2003,NZ Memories,No. 44/32</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>THATCHER</b> If you ever get into a fight, ./ Of course you'll not forget to skite, / And learn to prop well with the right, /And your opponent you'll annoy. <i>(1875,NZ Songster,No.5/64</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (of prison inmates) to hold a sit-down or other protest strike

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> Because we were late starting practice we all decided to prop for ten minutes extra ... <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,89</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a sit-down strike

>  Sit-down strike. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,252</i>)

>  After Alofoe's suicide, A Block inmates began `a prop'. One man started a hunger strike and others planned to join him after 39 days (then the average time span between Paremoremo's suicides), and refuse food indefinitely. ... The original unpublished plan involved a mass `prop' by all Paremoremo inmates. But many were not enthusiastic about a strike on this scale, and feared the consequences. <i>(May 12, 1958,NZ Times,5</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to stop suddenly

>  <i>prop n.</i> of a horse, to stop suddenly. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),623</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Prop, v.</i> of a horse: to stop suddenly. [1870] <i>(1898,Austral English,370</i>)

>  <i>prop n.</i> a sudden stop, esp. of a horse;- v.i. to halt suddenly, esp. of a horse. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,9</i>)

>  <i>prop ... NZ & Austral.</i> sudden stop by horse going at speed. <i>2 v. ... NZ & Austral.</i> (of horse) come to a dead stop with forelegs rigid. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,604</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <i>prop v.</i> in Australia, stop suddenly and change direction; in N.Z. two outside men in three-man front row of a rugby scrum. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),16</i>)

>  <i>Prop</i> (pr`op),<i> v. i</i>. To stop suddenly; to jib; - said of a horse. <i>[Local, Australia]</i> <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2029</i>)

### prop up
 
 <i>v.</i> be propped up: be put together, structured, made

>  <b>THOMSON</b> He had an insatiable desire to see what was inside any creature he came across, to find out how they were `propped up', as he put it, how and where they `stowed their dinner', to locate their `pumper' and `breathers', and other `gear', as he called their organs, and to see how they `shifted' themselves. <i>(1905,Bush Boys of NZ,13</i>)

### property
 
 <i>n.</i> farm, land

>  They take the present opportunity to offer their services to their friends and others as Agents for the Management of Landed Property, the Purchase and Sale of Merchandise, and the Superintendence of Shipping and other Agency Business. <i>(Aug 21, 1839,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,1</i>)

>  GEORGE FISK, WANGANUI, AUCTIONEER, AND COMMISSION AGENT FOR THE SALE AND PURCHASE OF LIVE STOCK, HOUSE, AND LANDED PROPERTY. <i>(Oct 5, 1864,Daily Southern Cross,8</i>)

>  An important sale of property in the Auckland district has just been completed. The estate known as the Whakatane Company's run in the Bay of Plenty district .. comprises eight thousand acres freehold and twelve thousand leasehold land, and is partly stocked. <i>(Jul 29, 1890,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  A. E. Austin, Tuatapere, offered GBP1 per annum for lease of unused roadline (2 acres) running through his property. <i>(Nov 22, 1932,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,4</i>)

>  <b>BELL</b> Frank Ashwin, to whom the property belonged, was a New Zealander born, the son of one of the early settlers. His father, who owned a large farm .. in the open country, some fifty miles nearer the coast, had .. started him in life on his own account here in the bush, by purchasing this property of about five hundred acres ... <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,10</i>)

>  COUNTRY PROPERTIES. WAIHEKE - Farm of 368 acres, fronting the Owaawaroa Bay, 150 acres in grass, remainder light bush; two Houses and Out-buildings erected thereon. <i>(Aug 26, 1872,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

>  FREEHOLD PROPERTY AND STOCK. MONDAY, OCTOBER 24th. ON THE PROPERTY, OTAUTAU, At 12 o'clock. <i>(Oct 18, 1910,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,1</i>)

>  Look at West Coast Farmers' property list on page 2. <i>(Jun 22, 1908,Hawera & Normanby Star,5</i>)

### property order
 
 <i>n.</i> a court order appointing a manager of the property of someone not competent to manage their own affairs in relation to property. [Jim Cameron writes : Introduced by the Protection of Personal & Property Rights Act 1988, s.31.]

### prophet
 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori seer

>  The Maori prophet is disappointed with his reception by the Waikaremoana natives, and has returned to Ruatahuna. <i>(May 15, 1906,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  Rua, the Maori prophet, is expected at Wairoa tomorrow on his way to Wellington to interview the Ministers. <i>(May 13, 1908,West Coast Times,4</i>)

>  Four native followers of Rua, the "prophet," charged with cattle-stealing at Paeroa, were admitted to probation at the Supreme Court to-day, Mr. Justice Edwards stating that the men had been led away by a false prophet. <i>(Aug 6, 1908,Hawera & Normanby Star,5</i>)

>  He don't like it when the Maori have the real live prophet that come out in the open and do the proper miracle in front of everyone's eyes, eh? <i>(1977,Listener Short Stories,17,1977 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1944 Finlayson in 1977 N.Z. Listener Short Stories]

>  <b>COWAN</b> Kimble Bent, the <i>pakeha-Maori</i>, related to me that when Te Ua was in Otapawa pa ... a <i>ruru</i> flew from the forest at dusk and perched on he ridge-pole of the house in front of which the prophet was sitting. <i>Ibid.</i> 2/72 Kereopa te Rau ... and Patara Raukatauri were the two prophets despatched by Te Ua early in 1865 to convert the tribes of the East Coast to the Pai-Marire faith. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/7,1983 ed.</i>)

>  THE PROPHET RUA. <i>(Apr 4, 1916,Grey River Argus,6</i>)

>  <b>PAYTON</b> Here lives the great Te Whiti, the Maori prophet and fanatic .. occasionally the spirit moves him to stir up his people to rise against the <i>pakeha</i>. <i>(1888,Round about NZ,293</i>)

>  The famous temple of Rua, theRingatu prophet. <i>(May 6, 1950,Southern Cross (Wgton),8</i>)

>  The most famous circular building ofrecent times was the temple of theMaungapohatu prophet, Rua. <i>(May 6, 1950,Southern Cross (Wgton),8</i>)

>  Not long ago we described how the famousHimiona of Motiti lost his mana as aprophet. <i>(Dec 29, 1899,NZ Times,7</i>)

>  At this time .. bands of native fanatics ..were massing themselves together atParihaka, and singing paeans to thepseudo prophet Te Whiti. <i>(Jun 2, 1883,Auckland Weekly News,15</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a nickname for the Australian supporters of a pastoralist or sheepfarming regime in early Canterbury settlement as against those (mainly newly-arrived English 'Pilgrim' settlers who looked rather towards agricultural small-farming (See [Pilgrim](per-pop.md#pilgrim),[shagroon](sa-sho.md#shagroon))



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farmer from Australia, arriving in Canterbury after the earlier 'pilgrims' from Britain.

>  Gaily the pilgrim harnessed his plough, / When he had built up a roof o'er his head; / Singing, `From Albion hither I come;' / `Land of mine! land of mine! grow me some bread.' Proudly the prophet flourished his crook, / When he had landed his sheep from the west; / Singing, `From Philipland hither I come;' / `Silly men! silly men! wool pays the best.' <i>(Aug 9, 1951,Lyttelton Times,7</i>) [NOTES: (`Pilgrims and Prophets')]

>  <b>THOMSON</b> ...An old Australian squatter, who visited the Canterbury settlement in 1851, divided the inhabitants into pilgrims, shagruns, and prophets; the first were the original colonists, the second were people from other parts of New Zealand, and the last were settlers from Australia. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,2/186-187</i>)

>  `Pilgrims and Prophets'. [title of Poem] <i>Ibid.</i> 9. The pilgrim toiled for his crop. ... Shortly the pilgrim and prophet agreed The plough and the crook couldn't live far apart. [NOTE 1866] <i>Ibid.</i> 101 [Notes] During the first year of the Settlement, Canterbury was invaded by a body of Sheep-farmers from Port Philip (Victoria), who were regarded by the Settlers partly with admiration as successful colonists, and partly with horror as disbelievers in the art of colonization and the `Canterbury System'. ... One of the visitors, who seem to have delighted in the <i>soubriquet</i> of `Shagroon', ventured to prophesy, in a letter which found its way into print, that the `Canterbury Pilgrims' would soon be ruined ... The `pilgrims' sought no harsher revenge than to nick-name their Port Philip visitors `prophets', and to take example from their experience in pastoral pursuits. <i>Ibid.</i> 125. During the first year of the Settlement, Canterbury was invaded by a body of sheep-farmers from Port Philip (Victoria) who were regarded by the settlers partly with admiration as successful Colonists, and partly with horror as disbelievers in the art of colonisation and the `Canterbury System'. The visitors found the country admirably adapted for sheep-farming, and they treated with some contempt the raw emigrants from England who looked to agriculture as the foundation of their fortunes. One of the visitors, who seem to have delighted in the soubriquet of `Shagroon', ventured to prophesy, in a letter, which found its way into print, that the `Canterbury Pilgrims' would soon be ruined and their country left for occupation by his more enlightened comrades. The `pilgrims' sought no harsher revenge than to nickname their Port Philip visitors `prophets' and to take example from their experience in pastoral pursuits. The Canterbury pasturage system was quickly adapted to the requirements of sheepfarming on a large scale, and `pilgrims' and `prophets' were soon merged into one class of Canterbury runholders. <i>(1866,Canterbury Rhymes,5</i>)

>  <i>Prophet</i>.- Nickname of the Australian squatters who came down to Canterbury in 185(1) They were caled Ps. because they prophesied ruin for the farming pilgrims. It was they who finally persuaded Godley to authorise the squatting system here. See also <i>Shagroon</i>. (C.R., p. 5) <i>(Nov 18, 1933,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: Acland Gloss]

>  Not only did Australian sheep arrive, but during the 1850-60 period many Australian squatters (disgruntled by the fickle rainfall) also sold or abandoned their properties and migrated to New Zealand. These men arrived with capital to invest, with experience in colonial farming, knowledgeable of the peculiar habits of the Merino sheep, and with whole-hearted support of a `squatting' policy of land tenure based on the occupation of large areas of grazing country at low rentals. They became known as the `Prophets' because they forecast failure for the small-area arable farm plans of the established settlements. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/231</i>)

>  Shortly the pilgrim and prophet agreed, The plough and the crook couldn't live far apart .. Quickly the prophet bred up his flock. As he defied dogs, scab and catarrh. <i>(1851,BLANK ENTRY,5</i>) [NOTES: in REEVES Canterbury Rhymes (1883)]

>  <b>THOMSON</b> An old Australian squatter, who visited the Canterbury settlement in 1851, divided the inhabitants into pilgrims, shagruns and prophets; the first were the original colonists, the second were people from other parts of New Zealand and the last were settlers from Australia. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,II 186</i>)

### prophetess
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [Ranghiaita] is of the Ngatiawa tribe, the son of an old prophetess of great renown. <i>(Dec 23, 1843,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,1</i>)

>  Wi Popata and Mr. White had nothing left but their bones; their flesh was chopped up, and taken for food for the Hauhau prophets. The eyes and hearts were given to the prophetess, whose name was Maria. She ate them. <i>(Aug 12, 1867,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

>  During this conversation I had gradually moved close to the village where I was quartered, and put myself under the protection of a prophetess who had guaranteed to protect us until our tribe came back from an inland journey, on which they then were. <i>(Jun 19, 1865,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

### proppy
 
 <i>adj.</i> of a horse,given to propping (See [prop v](../P#prop-v))

>  Garotter, to the eye, looked pretty well when stripped, but directly he settled into his canter the proppy manner in which he went with his fore legs showed that he was anything but what he should be. <i>(Jan 14, 1865,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,3</i>)

>  Bruin looked none the worse for his gallop on the previous day, but Portsea was very "proppy" at starting, though shook it off when he warmed up. <i>(Oct 10, 1895,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  He was a bit "proppy" the first time of asking, but he strode along nicely afterwards, and put in a good jump the second time. <i>(Jul 9, 1906,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  Manu is getting very proppy now, and he finds a great difficulty in jumping off on level terms with his field. <i>(Sep 27, 1919,NZ Truth,7</i>)

>  Master Strowan was a bit proppy at Wanganui, and he will always do better when the sting is out of the ground. <i>(May 28, 1920,NZ Truth,11</i>)

>  The 9st. 9lb., and the severe gallops she has lately had told against old Hatred, and her proppy preliminary did not gain her many admirers. <i>(Jan 5, 1874,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  The brute went short and proppy, and the minds of those who had backed him seriously misgave them. <i>(Dec 31, 1881,Observer (Auckland),245</i>)

>  <b>AYSON</b> The horse was so `proppy' that Thomas decided he was past using as a stock horse, so he had to leave him to his fate. <i>(1937,Thomas,96</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>adj.</i> shaky

>  During our run up, the engine came twice to a dead stop. She was old, very old and proppy, and it was amusing to see the passengers get out and look on at the engineer patching her up for a fresh start. <i>(Dec 16, 1880,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  The lame, proppy and useless utterances expressed by the last speaker (George Fisher) beat anything I ever heard. <i>(Aug 3, 1899,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

>  He would be a welcome acquisition to the professional ranks if he decided to go on with the game for he stands well up to his man relying on short, proppy punches to score his points. <i>(Aug 7, 1920,NZ Truth,8</i>)

>  But it is not only Bob's memory that would appear to be weak. He would seem to be pretty proppy on his pins, judging by their appearance on Monday at the stadium, and the paltry support they provided to the poor old bloke. <i>(Jan 8, 1910,NZ Truth,237/1/2</i>)

### props
 
 <i>n.</i> a gambling game (? introduced by American diggers)

>  <b>LOVELL-SMITH</b> In a Supreme Court case, which came before the late Mr. Justice Chapman, father of Sir Frederick Chapman, a voluble witness was giving evidence, stating that he was having a game of `Props' (a gambling game played with shells) with someone, and Joe Mills said this, and Joe Mills did that, and so forth, pouring out a stream of talk, when His Honour interrupted him ...`Now, first of all - What are Props? And who is Joe Mills?' <i>(1931,Old Coaching Days,14</i>) [NOTES: [c186?] in 1931 Lovell-Smith Old Coaching Days 14]

### propsticks
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  It appears that the lad had been directed to remove the prop-stick from a loaded dray before the horses were put into the shafts. Consequently, the poor fellow was overwhelmed by the whole weight of the dray ... <i>(Oct 27, 1877,North Otago Times,2</i>)

>  They had neglected to put down the back propstick, and the horses during the night had come poking about the shafts, as horses used to the road will do, and tipped up the load on to the sleepers. <i>(Feb 8, 1894,Otago Witness,49</i>)

>  Smyth directed the man to prop up the dog cart, so that he might endeavour to get a little rest in it until daylight; but after a time the prop-stick gave way, and the unfortunate fellow was thrown out, and severely shaken and bruised. <i>(Oct 18, 1864,Wellington Independent,3</i>)

>  <b>MITCHELL</b> It was in 1872, while carting wheat into Timaru from Cave Valley way, I composed the following piece, ... Hold hard, Jock, put the prop-sticks down,/ And let the cattle spell./ O for a gill of Campbellton,/ Or a pint of Tennant's ale,/ <i>(1889,Rhymes and Rambles,25</i>) [NOTES: [1872] in 1889 Mitchell Rhymes and Rambles 25]

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> PROPSTICKS: To hold up shafts of dray or stretcher while horses feed. <i>(1961,Friends in Chains,188</i>) [NOTES: c1900-1920 A Teamster's Glossary in 1961 Henderson Friends In Chains 188]

### prospect
 
 <i>n.</i> a trainee for a 'black' gang; junior or probationary gang member

>  Usually, a gang trainee for a 'black' gang is a <i>prospect</i>, whereas a gang trainee for a 'white' gang is a <i>probie</i>. <i>(Oct 1999,NZWords,2(2) : 2</i>)

>  <b>MARRINER</b> The prospect scrambles to obey. <i>(2001,Stonedogs,71</i>)

>  Among gangs junior members or `prospects' often admit ownership of drugs as a gang initiation, Mr Keefe said. <i>(Oct 21, 1988,Dominion,1</i>)

>  ... the police want to question ... Hirawani (22), an unemployed Mongrel Mob prospect ... <i>(Nov 26, 1989,Sunday Star (Auckland),A3</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to look for gang recruits

>  <b>'K'</b> We back the buggy up the hill and prospect downstream for a practicable crossing-place. <i>(1885,Visit to Lake Rotoaira, A,8</i>)

>  He was busy prospecting for the Mob. <i>(Dec 28, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),44</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a trial sample of ore or dirt containing gold [goldmining, the sample]

>  He appeared in very high spirits with his prospects, and departed again on Thursday with the articles he required, and having secured an extra prospecting claim, and made a claim for the bonus offered for the discovery of a new gold field. <i>(Dec 1, 1868,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  We have a prospect washed while the Chairman of the company points out the boundary of the claim. <i>(Mar 6, 1901,Otago Witness,37</i>)

>  The account of the Gully which he gives is, that fair prospects have been found there, and his opinion is that a party can make an ounce a man, as the sinking is very shallow, and the working dry. <i>Ibid.</i> 16 This morning a man .. showed me a prospect which he had obtained from several dishes of washing-stuff, in a claim on the Waitahuna. It consisted of about [17] half-a-dozen specks of gold - about half a grain ... <i>(1861,NZ Goldfields,13,1976 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1861 Sep 20 in NZ Goldfields 1861 (1976) 13]

>  <b>McCRAE</b> .. found some nice prospects of fine gold ... <i>(Nov 11, 1861,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1861 Nov 11 McCrae to Provincial Council in 1948 Cowan Down the Years in the Maniototo 34]

>  About twelve or fifteen miles below the junction .. we first obtained payable prospects. We tried a number of small bars that we thought would pay about an ounce a-day by working with a cradle. <i>(Aug 23, 1862,Otago Witness,7</i>)

>  <b>HODDER</b> We set to work .. to `prospect' for gold .. We found that all the others had obtained as good a `prospect' as ourselves. <i>(1862,Memories of NZ Life,72</i>)

>  <b>T.B. ARCHIBALD</b> We would have been successful at least in getting a good prospect [.] <i>(Sep 29, 1862,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1862 29 Sept Letter from T.B. Archibald in 1863 Appendix Journals (Pyke Report Goldfields)]

>  <b>BARRINGTON</b> We laid in a little stock of provisions and .. carried a portion into the bush and buried it till some of the party should return .. if we got any prospect. <i>(1863,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1863 Barrington Diary in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 392]

>  Many were the prospects washed, varying from a few grains to .. 3 cwt. ... <i>(Jan 5, 1866,West Coast Times</i>) [NOTES: 1866 Jan 5 West Coast Times in 1914 Pfaff The Diggers' Story 127]

>  <b>MONEY</b> .. our claim proved a `duffer'. No less than fifteen shafts did we sink .. but found a prospect of nothing sufficient to encourage us in working it. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,99</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> He watched me washing a prospect in a dishful of stuff. <i>(1874,Colonial Experiences,147</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> .. `several specks of fine gold from the surface wash' on the Molyneux; `several specks of fine gold' at the Bannockburn; `fair prospects' at the Lindis; `nice prospects of fine gold' in the Mareburn, and so on throughout. <i>(1887,History of the Early Gold Discoveries  in Otago,60</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> the result of the first or test-dish full of wash-dirt. [1890] <i>(1898,Austral English,371</i>)

>  <b>PFAFF</b> We went up the creek to prospect .. We pushed on into maiden country, i.e. the Right Hand Branch, and got very good prospects. <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,43</i>)

>  <b>HISLOP</b> .. his first dishful did produce two very nice flat match-head samples [of gold]. `What is this heavy prospect doing so close to the road in that innocent looking wash?' I said. <i>(1943,Pure Gold,55</i>)

>  <b>MAY</b> PROSPECT: (<i>noun</i>) the yield of gold won from a dish of wash-dirt, or from the bottom of a shaft, or from roughly reduced quartz. <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,528,1962 ed.</i>)

>  <i>prospect v.i.</i> search for gold;<i> n</i>. result of the first test-dish of washdirt. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),16</i>)

>  <b>COOPER</b> We commenced operations, and discovered .. several fine leaders containing burnt quartz. Our neighbours as they passed all congratulated us on our prospects, but we shall see. <i>(Nov 7, 1867,Digger's Diary,10,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Pros&#769;.pect </i>(pr&obreve.s&#769;.p&ebreve.kt), <i>n. [Add.] 7</i>. [Perh. directly from PROSPECT, <i>v. i.] (Mining) (a) </i>The gold or other mineral obtained by washing a sample of wash-dirt in a dish. <i>(b) </i>The gold or other mineral obtained by crushing and washing a sample of ore. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2029</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [goldmining, the possible outcome for the site / the proposed site itself]

>  <b>SWAINSON</b> Gold in minute quantities was found by every [prospecting] party almost on the surface of the soil; ... Early in November, however, the prospect began to be of a more promising character : one party being reported to have washed out two or three ounces of gold, including several nuggets, and to have averaged fair daily wages; ... <i>(1853,Auckland,94</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [goldmining, gold found when sampling a site]



 
 <i>v.</i> search for gold [goldmining]

>  At the Land Board two licenses [sic] to prospect for tin in Alford Forest, and one license [sic] to prospect for gold in the Hororata district, were granted to-day. <i>(Sep 14, 1883,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

>  A license [sic] to prospect for gold has been granted to a party of four in the Wilberforce district. <i>(Feb 8, 1884,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)

>  Reefton, December 14. A Diamond Drilling Association has been formed [here] to prospect, with the aid of the Government diamond drill and a subsidy. <i>(Dec 14, 1906,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  One of his mates informed me that so far as he knew Nelly was never near Rangitoto, and only prospected in the Pirongia ranges, where he professed he had found gold, though the statement was never supported by substantial proof. <i>(Aug 13, 1872,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> ... we managed ... to prospect most of the creeks ..., though with no success as regarded any discovery of `the colour'. I am myself of opinion that the bed-rock on the eastern side of the dividing range is at so much greater depth [28] ..., as to render it unlikely that it would ever pay for the labour of sinking for a prospect, but time will shew. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,27</i>)

>  .. the annexed tabular report of the work at this Gold-field since my arrival. .. wherever I and the Messrs. Ring have `prospected' on the Kapanga .. we have found gold, and that the greatest number of flakes in the prospecting dish, appeared upon washing spme earth yesterday at a bar about half a mile lower down the stream .. and below all the present diggings. <i>(Dec 10, 1852,NZ Government Gazette,V/30/183</i>) [NOTES: (Heaphy's Report) Kapanga River Diggings,/ Coromandel harbour,/ 4th November, 1852.]

>  I went there [to Arataonga] yesterday `prospecting', and found gold in the first pan washed. <i>(Nov 10, 1852,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1852 Nov 10 (Auckland) in 1853 Earp NZ 255]

>  <b>SWAINSON</b> `I went there [to Coromandel] on yesterday,' says the reporter, `prospecting, and found gold in the first pan washed. Out of about six pans washed four gave gold specks and grains. ...' <i>(1853,Auckland,103</i>)

>  <b>HODDER</b> We set to work .. to `prospect' for gold. .. We found that all the others had obtained as good a `prospect' as ourselves. <i>(1862,Memories of NZ Life,72</i>)

>  A party of sawyers .. prospected for gold and found a speck. <i>(Apr 16, 1864,Nelson Evening Mail</i>) [NOTES: 1864 April 16 Nelson Ev. Mail in 1952 Dec 1 Marlborough Express p6]

>  <b>TINNE</b> [It] is to be .. the great alluvial gold-field of this island. It has not been `prospected' yet, from the reluctance of the natives to mining encroachments ... <i>(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,43</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Prospect, v.</i> to search for gold. In the word, and in all its derivatives, the accent is thrown back on to the first syllable. This word, in such frequent use in Australia, is generally supposed to be of Australian origin, but it is in equal use in the mining districts of the United States of America. [1861] <i>(1898,Austral English,370</i>)

>  .. the goldfield was quickly prospected and its boundaries determined. <i>(1931,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,12/159</i>)

>  <i>prospect</i>: to search for gold. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,175</i>)

>  <i>v.int.</i> to search for gold; n. result of the first test- dish of washdirt. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),623</i>)

>  However, one man named Williams, an expert flax-dresser, took the <i>Perseverance</i> prospecting; he was rewarded by finding flax ... <i>(1971,NZ Heritage,1/277</i>)

>  <b>T.B. ARCHIBALD</b> We prospected the bars and the banks. <i>(1862,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1862 Letter from T.B. Archibald in 1863 Appendix Journals D/7/2 (Pyke Report Goldfields)]

>  <b>BARRINGTON</b> We started up Stony Creek prospecting the creek as we went till we reached its head .. but could find no gold. <i>(Dec 26, 1863,Diary of a West Coast Prospecting Party,393</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> .. we managed .. to prospect most of the creeks .. though with no success as regarded any discovery of `the colour'. I am myself of opinion that the bed-rock on the eastern side of the dividing range is at so much greater depth [28] .. as to render it unlikely that it would ever pay for the labour of sinking for a prospect, but time will shew. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,27</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> Well, boys, when you've quite done prospecting our claim I'll trouble you to clear out. <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,1/v/19,(1889,1974) ed.</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> Here again, we met a party ... who were returning from the South, having prospected several rivers near Mount Cook; ... <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,96</i>)

>  <b>KNOX</b> .. the reefs have been prospected to six or eight hundred feet below the sea-level ... <i>(1888,Boy Travellers,217</i>)

>  .. aided by .. an old alluvial miner - the locality was diligently prospected, and one or two `colours' are said to have been found in the wash in the bed of the Mangaone Stream . <i>(1893,TrNZI,25/354</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> I've prospected-up everyone of them gullies, and never seen the colour yet. <i>(1873,Station Amusements in NZ,191</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (merely a further example of 2?)to look for wapiti in an area where they have not previously been reported or shot for. [1965 Bruce Branwell <I<TS</i>. of <i>Wapiti Hunters</i> (Note by Les Cleveland) 69 A party prospected for wapiti in the area between the Wapiti river and the Doon Valley. <i>Ibid.</i> 71. Five blocks and two prospecting blocks were offered in 1933. [Adj. usage is more common in the phrase <i>prospect blocks</i>, hence] 71 Three new coast blocks were opened to enable better coverage of the peninsulas between the alternate sounds. These were known as prospect blocks. The first parties to stalk these areas were, in most cases, entering country no stalkers had previously covered.]



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> to explore, search about

>  <b>HAY</b> Seeing the open door, he [sc. a pig] resolved to prospect a bit, and accordingly entered the shanty. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,1/140</i>)

>  <b>BUICK</b> The summer of 1895 was a particularly dry one, and ... the settler who owned the section adjoining ... was prospecting for a better supply of water. <i>(1937,Moa-Hunters of NZ,136</i>)

>  <b>'K'</b> We back the buggy up the hill and prospect downstream for a practicable crossing-place. <i>(1885,Visit to Lake Rotoaira, A,8</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a test-search of a place, or of material, to discover its propsective yield of gold [goldmining; the search]

>  [Photo caption] Examining a prospect in Rawlston and Merrilees claim. <i>(Dec 31, 1902,Otago Witness,38</i>)

>  We are indebted for the following graphic description of the result of a `prospect' to a gentleman who has been a successful digger on the Tuapeka, notwithstanding his education and habits of life being such as apparently to disqualify him for manual labour. The energy with which gold-digging is pursued, and how little is cared for personal comfort in the excitement of a prospect, <i>(Aug 10, 1861,Otago Witness</i>) [NOTES: 1861 Aug 10 Otago Witness in 1887 Pyke History of the Early Gold Discoveries in Otago (1962) 31]

>  The result of their several prospects was 3.5 dwt of gold <i>(Apr 16, 1864,Nelson Evening Mail</i>) [NOTES: 1864 April 16 Nelson Mail in 1952 Dec 1 Marlb. Expr. p6]

>  <b>PYKE</b> Borrowing a pan (<i>Anglice</i> : tin-dish) from some neighbourly miners, he progressed up the Gorge trying `prospects' in many places, and at length he was satisfied to `peg out'. <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,1/iv/17,(1889,1974) ed.</i>)

>  <i>v.int</i>. to search for gold; n. result of the first test- dish of washdirt. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),623</i>)

>  <b>MILLER</b> I was on gold all the time. I had sunk my first prospect hole at the lower end of a shallow depression ... and had struck the deepest part of the lead. It was a narrow gutter, no more than a foot wide at the most, and rich in heavy gold. <i>(1946,There Was Gold,69</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> 3.as n. and attrib, esp. in <i>prospect block </i> [[no citations] 1965 Bruce Branwell T.S. of <i>Wapiti Hunters</i> (Note by Les Cleveland) [see PROSPECT v. below]]

### prospecting
 
 (stress on first syllable) <i>n.</i>

>  [doubtful n Many of these had been much employed in `prospecting', ... <i>(Jan 12, 1853,NZ Government Gazette,6/1/3</i>) [NOTES: (Heaphy's Report from Coromandel)]

>  <b>WEKEY</b> The mere prevalence of blocks of `cement', as it is technically termed, ... forms no sure indication of the presence of a lead, but if the `cement' be found strewn over a limited tract, round which the <i>bed-rock</i> crops out ... it may then be taken as a guide in prospecting. <i>(1862,Otago As It Is,21</i>)

>  The policy of the Government at that period [1856] appears to have been adverse to the encouragement of what is termed `prospecting' <i>(1863,AJHR,D-6/2</i>) [NOTES: (Pyke Report Goldfields)]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Prospecting, verbal n</i>. and<i> adj.</i> See <i>Prospect, v.</i> [1890] <i>(1898,Austral English,371</i>)

>  <b>EVANS</b> ... during thelatter years of his life he devoted his life to prospecting. <i>(1862,Waikaka Saga,85</i>)

>  <b>BLYTH</b> <i>Prospecting</i>: Searching for mineral lodes or deposits. <i>(1935,Gold Mining Year Book,11</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SWAINSON</b> Several `prospecting' parties now started in various directions, all eager for the search [for gold]; ... [NB several persons had lately returned from California] <i>(1853,Auckland,87</i>)

>  Some of the many prospecting parties who are now ransacking the mountains are meeting with temporary success, but it is only in small ravines or blind leaders. <i>(Oct 16, 1861,NZ Goldfields,45</i>)

>  <i>Pr&obreve.s&#769;.p&ebreve.kt-&ibreve.ng)</i>, a. & n. <i>from PROSPECT,</i> v. i. <i>Prospecting claim (Mining)</i>, a specially large claim, allotted by the warden of the gold fields to the first miner discovering gold in a new district; - called also <i>reward claim</i>. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2029</i>)

### Prospecting Association
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Yesterday afternoon a number of Te Aroha residents .. proceeded to Waitoa in order to judge whether there had been any tampering with the stuff at the shafts, Mr. Pond in his report to the Waitoa Prospecting Association having stated that the unnatural gold found by him came from heaps of stuff at the mouth of the shaft numbered 12. <i>(Nov 11, 1887,Otago Witness,13</i>)

### prospecting claim
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  A prospecting claim was granted them, and the next day Major Croker started to inspect the spot. <i>(Dec 21, 1861,Otago Witness,2</i>)

>  The Commissioner of Crown Lands has been applied to for, and has issued, a protection license for a quartz reefs prospecting claim on the Tararua Ranges near Palmerston North. <i>(Jan 10, 1881,North Otago Times,2</i>)

>  Two prospecting claims were pegged out. <i>(Dec 10, 1900,Grey River Argus,3</i>)

>  1/2 share No 3 north-west of Prospecting Claim. <i>(Aug 20, 1872,Grey River Argus,3</i>)

>  At last sitting of the Warden's Court there were applications for prospecting claims for 100 acres by E.H. Donaldson, 100 acres by C. Low, 100 acres by Driscoll and party, 100 acres under dispute but which was settled out of court by parties combining, and an additional 300 acres applied for, making a total of 1100 acres between Karamea and Ugly River, a distance of two miles, and with gold showing freely on all these claims. <i>(Jan 28, 1915,Grey River Argus,4</i>)

### prospecting dish
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Gold from Karori. .. The stone, taken from all parts of a 14ft reef, has been roughly broken up with a hammer so as to liberate the precious metal, and the particles resulting from this operation carried by means of water across a ripple-table, the stuff collected in the ripples being then panned off in an ordinary digger's prospecting dish. <i>(Sep 23, 1895,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  2 HHDS. HOLLOWARE, comprising:- Prospecting Dishes and Dippers. <i>(Sep 13, 1861,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  "It was nothing unusual for some miners to carry about with them loose gold in their vest pocket, so that when a stranger wanted to see a prospect washed, a quantity was cunningly mixed up with the stuff in the prospecting dish, thus making sure that the prospect would not fail for want of 'salting.' ..." <i>(Apr 19, 1873,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  Included in Heavy Shipments recently landed are .. Mining Steel, Gelignite, Dynamite, Blasting Powder, Detonators, Fuse, Drill Hammers, Prospecting Dishes, Picks, Shovels, and Mining Goods. <i>(Feb 20, 1906,Colonist (Nelson),3</i>)

>  Long crow-bars, or iron-shod handspikes, are indispensable, as also a prospecting dish with bulging rim outside, and buckets or pumps for clearing out the water. <i>(Nov 13, 1852,New Zealander, The,2</i>)

>  ".. On former occasions when unemployment has been forced upon mill-hands scores have gone back to pick and shovel and unearthed sufficient gold to keep the wolf from the door. Such may be the case in the near future, although the majority of the young men employed in the bush have never had experience with the prospecting dish." <i>(Jun 1, 1921,Ashburton Guardian,4</i>)

>  Kapanga River Diggings,/ Coromandel harbour,/ 4th November, 1852. .. the annexed tabular report of the work at this Gold-field since my arrival. .. wherever I and the Messrs. Ring have `prospected' on the Kapanga .. we have found gold, and that the greatest number of flakes in the prospecting dish, appeared upon washing some earth yesterday at a bar about half a mile lower down the stream .. and below all the present diggings. <i>(Dec 10, 1852,NZ Government Gazette,V/30/183</i>) [NOTES: (Heaphy's Report)]

>  <b>COWAN</b> Sutherland and Mackay, with guns, slash-hooks, and prospecting-dish .. were clambering the rocky side of Lake Ada. <i>(1935,Hero Stories of NZ,273</i>)

>  <b>HISLOP</b> A day with a prospecting dish is real good fun, and will often give the novice puddler visions of gold that will put to shame the [21] goose that laid the golden eggs. <i>(1943,Pure Gold,20</i>)

>  "I tested the quality of the wash in a great many places by panning it off in a prospecting dish, and found the quantity of gold in the same extremely regular, and for dredging purposes I have not the slightest hesitation in pronouncing it exceptionally well payable and easily wrought ..." <i>(Sep 5, 1889,Otago Witness,19</i>)

>  Henry Hughes, Limited, patent and trade marks agents (authorised by N.Z. Government) reports the following list of patent applications in New Zealand as compiled from Government Gazette of 6th March .. H. McLeod, miner's prospecting dish ... <i>(Mar 12, 1913,Poverty Bay Herald,4</i>)

### prospecting licence
 
 <i>n.</i> [mining; right to hunt for gold mostly but also iron, copper, tin etc; cf exploration licence.]

>  The natives are issuing prospecting licences at GBP1 per year. <i>(Apr 19, 1873,Otago Witness,14</i>)

>  Nineteen gold and one copper and tin prospecting licences were granted at the meeting of the Canterbury Waste Lands Board on Thursday, the former at Malvern and the latter at Alford Forest. <i>(Aug 31, 1883,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

>  V. E. McColl (Mr. Lawry), prospecting license, 100 acres, Waitua. - Granted. <i>(Dec 2, 1913,Grey River Argus,3</i>)

>  Prospecting licences, he thought, should be free, and he would encourage local industries, where needful, by supplementary aid, and would even go to the length, in the case of flax, of offering a piece for twenty or thirty tons, from time on time, should the merchants not come forward. <i>(Oct 8, 1869,Nelson Evening Mail,2</i>)

>  John Burton, application for prospecting licence. - Granted. <i>(May 12, 1920,Grey River Argus,4</i>)

### prospecting party
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Another diamond prospecting party has left for the Malvern Hills. <i>(Apr 19, 1883,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)

>  Mr. Nixon, who belongs to the prospecting party that started from here a short time since returned to Blenheim on Tuesday last, for the purpose of procuring blasting powder, tools, and other materials. <i>(Dec 1, 1868,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  The "simple alteration" that Mr. Wilson suggests in limiting the holdings of the companies, and further states that small prospecting parties or syndicates are compelled to man their leases continuously whereas the mining companies can lock up hundreds of acres and yet not run the slightest danger of forfeiture. <i>(Jul 6, 1914,Grey River Argus,6</i>)

>  A prospecting party recently left Dannevirke for that part of the range lying due west of the town. <i>(Nov 15, 1909,Poverty Bay Herald,4</i>)

>  We know of no instance in which a Government prospecting party has discovered anything, unless, indeed, it were the art of getting an easy living at the public expense. <i>(Jul 31, 1868,West Coast Times,4</i>)

>  From old experience the writer has found that prospecting parties are shams, and during a term of much over twenty years he has never known one of these expeditions prove serviceable, or in any way be the means of opening up country. <i>(Nov 10, 1874,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> [We] fell in with an encampment .. mostly occupied by one man, the only representative of a prospecting party, which had left one of their mates behind to look after .. the horses during their absence. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,100</i>)

>  A gold prospecting party in that same country got lost, with the head of the geological party with them. <i>(Dec 31, 1925,NZ Truth,9</i>)

>  Invercargill has already dispatched a prospecting party to the West Coast; at Orepuki the miners' association are advertising for a prospecting party; and a public meeting, presided over by the mayor, has been held at Riverton, a miners' association formed, and a public subscription set on foot. <i>(Nov 17, 1894,Tuapeka Times,2</i>)

>  And as these vessels seem best adapted for washing soils impregnated with minute particles of gold, generally termed <i>dust</i>, it may not be out of place to give an idea of their form and application, for the benefit of such as may be inclined to try their luck on the newly discovered gold fields at Coromandel, or for prospecting parties elsewhere. <i>(Nov 2, 1852,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

### prospecting track
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Messrs. Firth and Clarke have commenced work on their special claim, and are now having trial parcels broken out from the various reefs that were cut when the county prospecting track, by the Buck reef, was made. <i>(May 21, 1887,Te Aroha News,2</i>)

>  UPPER MOONLIGHT PROSPECTING TRACK. <i>(Nov 11, 1916,Grey River Argus,3</i>)

>  A small portion was asked to cut prospecting tracks. <i>(Sep 23, 1867,Southland Times,2</i>)

>  It would not be wise to follow the rash and profitless example of our neighbors in Westland, in times gone by, who sought to anticipate prosperity, by indiscriminately cutting prospecting tracks - from nowhere in particular to Ultima Thule. <i>(Feb 8, 1877,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

>  A resolution was carried for Government to reinstate the subsidy voted last session for deep level mining at the Thames; also aid for the opening up of prospecting tracks, contructing trunk and branch roads in the north; for the repeal of the Shops and Shops Assistants' Act. <i>(Jun 15, 1895,Grey River Argus,4</i>)

>  A prospecting-track has been cut along the range for some twenty odd miles ... <i>(1907,TrNZI,39/396</i>)

### prospector
 
 <i>n.</i> a gold-seeker

>  [From the <i>New Zealander</i>] ".. Fees will be demanded only when the prospector has succeeded in finding a place sufficiently auriferous to induce him to "establish a claim" to dig there ..." <i>(Feb 23, 1853,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  I might mention that the finding of the gold is due to M'Lean, an old Coromandel prospector, who now has charge of the workings. <i>(Nov 13, 1880,Waikato Times,2</i>)

>  Larger pieces sometimes occur, even to several pounds in weight .. thus occasionally enabling the "prospector" to form a shrewd guess of the neighbourhood in which to look for a rich mine, with some hope of success. <i>(Jul 13, 1852,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  <b>SEWELL</b> ... we found much excitement from the report of gold diggings on the plains. A party of prospectors had gone up the country, ... <i>(1853,Journal,1/135,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Prospector, n.</i> one who searches for gold on a new field. See <i>Prospect, v.</i> [1890] <i>(1898,Austral English,371</i>)

>  The coarse nature of the alluvial gold ... and the fact that many of the `specks' had fragments of gold attached, early induced prospectors to seek for lodes. <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/12</i>)

>  His Excellency the Lieutenant-Governor proceeded to the island [Waiheke] on a Wednesday night, and left it at an early hour of the following Saturday, - the intermediate Thursday and Friday being days of a most tempestuous character - so that were his Excellency the most astucious of prospectors, he could scarcely have bestowed a superficial glance upon a very minute superficies. - And then, with regard to those of our citizens who flew on the wings of the morning <i/>to pick up gold</i>, we imagine they were in no case to prosecute a sustained or scientific inquiry. <i>(Jan 16, 1852,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  The pressure of the rush has rendered it necessary for the Government to employ prospectors, not only to find new diggings, but especially to ascertain the value of reported new fields, in order, as far as possible, to prevent that perpetual rushing from point to point to which gold miners are given. <i>(Oct 12, 1861,Otago Witness,4</i>)

>  The seekers of oil are imbued with the same spirit as the prospectors for gold, namely, the eternal belief that success will crown their efforts to-morrow, though to-day be barren. <i>(Nov 21, 1929,NZ Truth,20</i>)

>  Claiming to be a composer of popular songs, erstwhile member of the Fisk Jubilee Singers, one-time manager of Fullers' in Wellington, gold prospector who mushed on with the other "sourdoughs" in Alaska, oil man in California, gold prospector in New Zealand, and "professor" of music, this versatile individual might also have included in his list of accomplishments the gift of the glib tongue, and ability to talk his way into and out of anything at all. <i>(Jul 17, 1930,NZ Truth,1</i>)

>  A prospector in the Urewera writes: "I am confident that a large goldfield will be opened up in the Urewera Country ..." <i>(Aug 5, 1908,Poverty Bay Herald,2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Out-of-towners and new-comers in the gang - `prospectors' still earning their patch - tend to be an unknown quantity. <i>(May 1986,More Magazine,26</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

>  In the Totara claim, adjoining the Prospectors' claim on the east, GBP200 is refused. <i>(Jul 12, 1884,Waikato Times,2</i>)

 <i>n.</i> [prospectors reef, prospector's reef]

 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>kowhai, prostrate [s] Sophora prostrata</i> Buchan. <i>Ibid.</i> 61 <i> prostrate kowhai [s] Sophora prostrata</i> Buchan. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,42</i>)

### protected
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  Any miner wishing to retain possession of quartz or other auriferous substances may have the same protected for a period not exceeding three (3) months, provided that such substance is properly stacked on ground not supposed to be adapted to mining purposes, and that a board with the owner's name, address, and date of stacking, legibly written or painted thereon, be posted close to such stack. <i>(Mar 6, 1868,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

>  The late Christmas Box crushing has not given satsfaction to the directors and shareholders. They have consequently protected the claim for the present, for the purpose of re-organising the management and putting the working of the mine on a different footing. <i>(Aug 15, 1871,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  A most promising looking claim if protected from the necessity of being worked, turns out nothing and does nothing for the advancement of the field, but if compelled to develope [sic] its resources, might be made of use to the community generally, as well as to its owners. <i>(Apr 2, 1881,Waikato Times,2</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> [The] following [was] employed by those who served in the [Great] War ... <i>to be protected,</i> uncannily lucky, as in `Well, I'm protected' said after a narrow escape or in `You must be protected' spoken by friends after one has a lucky bet. <i>Ibid.</i> 287 I reckon I was pretty tinny. ... <i>B</i>. Yes, you were protected, Joe <i>(1935,Slang Today,286</i>) [NOTES: 1935 Mitchell and Strong in Partridge Slang Today 286]

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Protected</i>. Lucky; uncannily or very lucky: Australian and New Zealand: C. 20, but not gen. before G.W. Prob. <i>protected by the gods or by one's superiors.</i> Cf.: <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,663</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> The Regulations provided for this contingency [of not working beach claims during summer floods]; and the miners, having duly registered their claims as `protected', scattered themselves over the country ... <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,Bk III ch i 74,1889 ed.</i>)

### protection order
 
 <i>n.</i> a court order aimed at protecting persons who are or have been in a domestic relationship, and their children, from violence, abuse or harassment by or on behalf of their partners. [Jim Cameron writes : Introduced by the Domestic Violence Act 1995, it replaces the <i>non-molestation order</i> under the Domestic Protection Act 1982.]

>  Even the Dictionary of New Zealand English (DNZE) (1997) is patchy in its coverage of legal terms, having for example no entry for disputes tribunal, family protection, incorporated society, Maori option, protection order, resource consent. The most recent edition of Butterworths New Zealand Law Dictionary (1995) is useful but commonly gives specialist definitions taken from the interpretation sections of statutes <i>(Jan 1999,NZWords,1</i>)

### Protectionist
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The statement that I [sc. Sir Robert Stout] am among the most vehement of Protectionists is also wide of the truth. I am not even a member of the Protectionist Association. <i>(Apr 17, 1890,Otago Witness,14</i>)

### protective covenant
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a legal agreement drawn up to protect significant natural areas and indigenous vegetation on farm properties.

>  Although the crown negotiated a nga whenui rahui protective covenant in 2003 over The Needles block..DOC has so far been unable to come up with a 'wilderness covenant' proposition acceptable to the owners... <i>(Mar 2006,FMC - (Federated Mountain Clubs of NZ) Bulletin,32</i>)

>  The fencing is part of the protective covenant work being done by the Council's Significant Natural Areas officer, Neil Phillips. Protective covenants are legal agreements drawn up to protect significant natural areas and indigenous vegetation. A growing number of farmers and landowners in the district have been approaching Neil Phillips for advice regarding drawing up protective covenants. <i>(Aug 8, 2002,Hawera Star,17</i>)

>  Farmers were allowed to keep tracks through the forest, or could allow for house sites as part of a covenant. <i>(Aug 21, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,4</i>)

>  .. these vistas will remain unobstructed and unspoiled forever by virtue of sensible protective covenants presently in place. <i>(Nov 29, 2002,Dominion Post,C9</i>)

### Protector of (the) Aborigines
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MILLER</b> 'I believe.' wrote George Clarke, the Protector of Aborigines, five years later, 'in no single instance of the Company's purchases have they been fully explained. Had they been so, I think no purchase would ever have taken place.' <i>(1958,Early Victorian NZ,26</i>)

>  In 1843, Edward Shortland, who bore the curious title "Sub-protector of Aborigines," said of the concretions that some were "round enough to serve for canon balls and of all sizes from the twelve-pounder upwards." <i>(Nov 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 66/16</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> One of them, who had been taken as a slave from Manukao, went with us chiefly for the purpose of applying to the <i>Protector of the Aborigines</i> appointed by the Government, to obtain back some of the land which formerly belonged to his relatives. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/398</i>)

>  Amongst the practical measures ... for the protection of the aborigines, the most important are, the support of the missions and the missionaries ... [28] The official protection of the aborigines will be committed to one principal officer ... The general duty of protector would be to watch over the execution of the laws, in ... the rights and interests of the natives; ... <i>(Dec 9, 1840,GBPP,(No.311) 27</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Dec 9 Lord John Russell to Governor Hobson in GBPP House of Commons 1841 (No.311) 27]

>  <b>WILY & MAUNSELL</b> On Sunday, July 4, 1840, His Excellency Lieutenant-Governor Hobson, accompanied by Mr. George Clarke, Protector of the Aborigines, and two other officers in His Excellency's suite, paid a visit to Maraetai and attended English evening service. <i>(1938,Robert Maunsell,58</i>)

>  <b>DILLON</b> I am now a kind of `protector of aborigines' tho [sic] that name is abolished. <i>(Aug 6, 1848,Dillon Letters,74</i>)

>  All such [land-purchase] contracts should be made by yourself, through the intervention of an Officer expressly appointed to watch over the interests of the Aborigines as their Protector. [FN] Created 1840 abolished at end of first quarter of 1846 by Grey. Grey to Stanley 10 May 1846 CO 209/43 P.137 -[FN] Created 1840 abolished at end of first quarter of 1846 by Grey. Grey to Stanley 10 May 1846 CO 209/43 P.137 -[FN] Created 1840 abolished at end of first quarter of 1846 by Grey. >  <b>SEWELL</b> <i>(1980,Journal,1/341</i>) [NOTES: >cf 1980 McIntyre (ed) Journ. H. Sewell 1/341]

>  <b>DEAS</b> Either the official protector of aborigines, or some person appointed in his stead ..., must be present at all their investigations in order to protect the rights and interests of the natives. <i>(Oct 1840,Report</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Oct E.Deas Thomson to Commissioners ... to report on Claims to Grants of Land in GBPP 1842 (No.569) 81 4.]

>  <b>SEL,WYN</b> Our party then consisted of Mr. Puckey, Mr. Kemp (Assistant Protector of Aborigines,) Mr. Nihill, myself, and four natives. <i>(1843,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1843 Selwyn Journal in 1845 NZ Part II (Church in the Colonies VII) 8]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> Looking to the case of the protector of the aborigines, there is no check to prevent them from abusing their power, and abusing his [Hobson's] influence. <i>(1844,NZ Journal,52,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> Supposing this intended with a view of giving full effect to the system of amalgamation, there should be no exceptional law for natives within the municipalities, Native Protectors, for example, are an exceptional class of officers, whose very appointment suggests the idea of a natural hostility between the colonists and the Natives; ... letters addressed to `Squire Clarke', the chief Protector of the Aborigines under Captain Hobson ... <i>(Jan 20, 1846,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1846 Jan 20 E.G. Wakefield to Gladstone in 1949 Rutherford ed. Select Documents 15A]

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> ... the missionaries ... by the appointment of one of their number to be Protector of Aborigines, were identified with the native party. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,72</i>)

>  <b>IRVINE & ALPERS</b> The appointment by Hobson of a catechist,George Clarke, as Protector of theAborigines, was ill advised, for it gaveground for suspicion that much of theobstruction made by the Maoris to landoccupation was traceable to his partisaninfluence. <i>(1902,Progress of NZ in the 20th Century,133</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a statutory official charged under the ... Act (184 ) with protecting Maori (esp. land) interests. ### Protectorate Department
 
 <i>n.</i>

### proud
 
 <i>adj.</i> sc of an edge

>  <b>REED</b> Edward Thompson writes: `Quite a lot of squared timber was taken out of Hokianga and Whangape in the fifties & sixties ... About 1906 I became acquainted with Captain Keane. ... I remember him telling me how they got the squared spars out of the bush before jacks & bullocks were thought of ...' ... The method is described by Dan Lewis: `In my early days in the bush, logs were squared to a `proud' edge, as it was called, the finished log being absolutely square ...' <i>(1953,Story of the Kauri, The,164</i>)

 <i>v.</i>

### province
 
 <i>n.</i> [see DNZE (not from Orsman q.v.)]

>  On the division of New Zealand into provinces. 1. For the present, and until further order be made in that behalf, the islands of New Zealand shall be divided into two provinces, to be known respectively by the designations of the "Province of New Ulster", and the "Province of New Munster". 2. The Province of New Ulster shall comprise the whole of the island hitherto called the Island of New Ulster, with the exception of those parts of the said island adjacent to Cook's Straits, which the Governor-in-Chief of New Zealand may, by any such proclamation as aforesaid, except and exclude from the province of New Ulster. <i>(Dec 18, 1847,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> ... Earl Grey decided in mid-1846 to confer on the colony a most complex constitution which, inter alia, provided for the creation of two provinces, New Ulster and New Munster, whose boundary line was to run due east across the North Island from the mouth of the Patea River'. [Then you'll have the later constitution, the abolition of the provinces and its extension in meaning today in sporting circles to include Bush, Waikato, King Country etc. which were never provinces. J. Winchester 1963] <i>(1958,Crown Colony Government in New Zealand,214</i>)

### provincial
 
 <i>adj.</i> of the provinces or a province; `a provincial newspaper'.

>  In this way the stream of immigration to Otago would have been suddenly stopped, but for Mr Macandrew having telegraphed to the joint Home Agents for the Province to continue sending out emigrants at Provincial expense. <i>(Jan 1, 1876,Otago Witness,8</i>)

>  [From the <i>Canterbury Times</i>] That one failure must have had a most startling effect on the nerves of the provincial authorities for when asked the other day to join Nelson in opening up communication with the Coast, they actually declined. <i>(Jan 3, 1876,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  100 acres of country land, 50 suburban acres and a town allotment of ONE ACRE in Nelson will be sold for GBP300 - Nelson will not be an obsure provincial town but in all probability will be the capital of the South Island. <i>(Dec 15, 1841,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,2</i>)

>  Upwards of [pounds]22,000 are in the Provincial Treasury ... Several local ordinances have been passed by the Provincial Council ... <i>(Dec 16, 1854,Lyttelton Times</i>) [NOTES: 1854 Dec 16 Lyttelton Times in 1856 Fitton NZ ?p[211-215]]

>  <b>BURDON</b> Today a man from Invercargill may get into a train or service car and, within twenty-four hours, propose to and be accepted by a girl in Blenheim. ... Surely it is not to be wondered at that provincial distinctions have disappeared. <i>(1938,High Country,166</i>)

>  <b>BASSET</b> Provincial Superintendent: In the provincial system, the Superintendent was usually the man who led the majority group in the provincial assembly. <i>(1969,Sir Harry Atkinson,31</i>)

>  ... it may be necessary to make some arrangements as to [the prisoner's] rations ... the gaol being I suppose a provincial affair. A Provincial seal will have to be procured if the bills King forwarded passed without alteration in that respect. <i>(Aug 7, 1854,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/153,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (C.W. Richmond)]

<b>2</b> <i>adj.</i> . [sport]
