>  <i>provincial ... 4. N.Z.</i> denoting a football team representing a province, one of the historical administrative areas of New Zealand. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,916</i>) [NOTES: <i>provincial ... 4. N.Z.</i> denoting a football team representing a province, one of the historical administrative areas of New Zealand.]

### Provincial
 
 <i>n.</i> a Provincialist [1857 RA Paps 1/265. 271, 272, 276, 312]

>  Can they not discover some mode of "painless extinction," .. so that the bereaved parents, the Provincial Councilors, may not be too violently afflicted at the loss of their bantlings - as seems to be the case with the Wellington Provincials, whose last crop of laws has been dreadfully worried by those wild dogs of the General Government? <i>(Jun 20, 1857,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,2</i>)

>  But we are at last beginning to perceive that Governor Grey began at the wrong end when he enforced the prior summoning of the Provincials, who are checked in their endeavours to advance the several Provinces, through want of those additional powers, which only the Assembly can confer. <i>(Feb 4, 1854,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,7</i>)

>  The provincial taxes, (already much too oppressive) are all subject to the disposal of a General Legislative Council, according to the enactments which it may please such a body to pass;- the remainder only, if any, is at the control of the Provincials. <i>(Aug 15, 1851,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  You Provincials have all the legislation. <i>(1855,Stafford Papers,2/13</i>) [NOTES: (Domett)]

>  <b>CRESSWELL</b> These now had control of the central government, and when the Provinces hampered their aims they swept them away. ... For these Provincials had aims other than acquisition and the destruction of all that stood in the way of the most rapid settlement for the sake of what could be gained. <i>(1939,Present Without Leave,9</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i> [?only Christchurch]

 <i>n.</i> [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Adverting to my despatch, No. 123, of the 24th October 1850, in which Itransmitted .. the draft of a Bill for the constitution of Provincial Councils whichI intended to introduce into the General Legislative Council of these islands. <i>(1851,GB Parliamentary Papers (Irish Univ. series),VIII 1475/18</i>)

>  Provincial Councils Ordinance (Passed the 9th day of July 1851). An Ordinance toprovide for the Establishment of Provincial Legislative Councils. <i>(1851,GB Parliamentary Papers (Irish Univ. series),VIII 179/34</i>) [NOTES: This was passed in Session XI, No.6 Ord. Gen. Ass. NZ.]

>  I issued, upon the 21st May last, the first of the enclosed proclamations forthe purpose of bringing the Provincial Councils Ordinance into full operation inthat province [sc. New Ulster]. <i>(1854,GB Parliamentary Papers (Irish Univ. series),1854</i>) [NOTES: Dated 1852]

>  That honourable member was one of the great "Truly Rural" party which was tobring about an entire revolution in their political system by sweeping away the Provincial Councils altogether. <i>(1873,NZ Parliamentary Debates,14/98</i>)

>  <b>SHAW</b> At Wellington a member of the Provincial Council was my boatman. <i>(1858,Gallop to the Antipodes, A,202</i>)

>  How this will operate on our future fortunes will greatly depend on the practical arangement of the details of the measure which have been left to the judgement and discretion of the Governor; much will also depend on those who are elected under its provisions to the Municipal and Provincial Councils, upon whom will devolve the responsibility of making the New Constitution in its operation acceptable to their fellow settlers, and beneficial to the interests of the settlement. <i>(Jan 5, 1848,New Zealand Spectator & Cook's Strait Guardian,2</i>)

>  <b>BUDDLE</b> The hon. gentleman said, `Hitherto there has been no attempt to meet the special and local wants of the Aborigines .. Between the provincial Councils and the General Assembly the wants of the Natives in [17] these respects have .. been wholly neglected.' <i>(1860,Maori King Movement,16</i>)

>  The Seal of the Taranaki Provincial Council. <i>(May 29, 1939,NZ Centennial News,10</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE</b> "We have our Provincial Councils now, and soon we shall have a General Assembly." <i>(1936,So They Began,97</i>)

### Provincial Government
 
 <i>n.</i>

Executive, it was within the power of the Provincial Legislature ... <i>(Jun 13, 1857,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/273,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (C.W.Richmond)]

>  On the question of Provincialism v. Centralism, he expresses our own views. As to whether Provincial Governments should be abolished or not, he thinks ... <i>(Jan 4, 1868,Marlborough Express,6</i>)

### Provincial Legislative Council
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical) [incl attrib in Provincial Legislative Council/s Bill]

>  The municipal representatives it is true are to be elected by a wide constituency, but the members of the Provincial Legislative Councils are to be elected by the municipal representatives, and not by the people; and the members of the General Assembly to be elected by the members of the Provincial Legislative Councils, to the exclusion of direct popular interference also. <i>(Jun 26, 1847,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,63</i>)

>  Proclamation of Her Majesty's allowance of the Ordninance to provide for Provincial Legislative Councils. <i>(Jul 19, 1850,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  <b>RUTHERFORD</b> 6. The large powers which will thus be vested in the Local Legislatures, will enable the Provincial Legislative Council of New Munster, not only to devise and frame all those measures ... <i>(1949,Selected Documents,34</i>) [NOTES: 1848 Nov 16 Grey's Opening Address to the Legislative Council of NZ in 1949 Rutherford ed. Select Documents 34]

### provincialism
 
 <i>n.</i> [the attitude of provincialism as opposed to the system of government]

>  It is not now remarked for the first time that the islands of New Zealand will not be settled from one centre; that the character of the country will enforce a different mode; and that most of its towns will be each the nucleus of a district especially its own, and bound to it by ties which will substitute for a long time, and perhaps for ever, the feeling of provincialism for that of nationalism, or rather patriotism in its limited sense. <i>(Oct 22, 1842,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,130</i>)

>  I see you are a very prominent figure in the G.A. I expect to see the intense provincialism of you Aucklanders a good deal softened down. <i>(Jul 22, 1854,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/150,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (C.W.Richmond) [to T.S.Forsaith, Auckland]]

>  As regards Centralism and provincialism, do not let us run into extremes either way. Provincialism up to a certain point is indispensable for the progress of the Colony. ... Pruned and reduced to right dimensions our provincial governments may be made admirable institutions. <i>(Apr 7, 1858,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/379,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (H. Sewell)]



 
 <i>n.</i> the political system of control by Provinces as distinct from central Government (1856-1878?) [the system of provincial government as opposed to attitude of provincialism]

>  .. I allude, sir, to the two principles of centralization and provincialism, these principles like most others that influnce the minds of men, have both much of truth in them, it is the extremes only that are erroneous. <i>(Jul 14, 1854,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  It is intended to bring forward a provincialist candidate to oppose opinion on the question of centralism versus provincialism. <i>(Dec 10, 1874,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  In a speech of over an hour Mr. Rees pulled the bills to pieces unmercifully, arguing that they did not abolish provincialism but only Superintendents and Councils, while we obtained in their place, nominees of Government ... <i>(Aug 16, 1875,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  Under the wretched system of Provincialism, educational measures in our own Province ha[ve] been shamefully neglected. <i>(Feb 28, 1871,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  Mr Moss, with his extreme views, would all but abolish the central Legislature in his zeal for local self-government, and would revive all the difficulties of Provincialism in an intensified form. <i>(Aug 20, 1880,Hawke's Bay Herald,2</i>)

>  The debate on Sir M. O'Forke's motion for the restoration of provincialism was continued for half and hour, and the motion was rejected by 20 to 7. <i>(Sep 28, 1906,Nelson Evening Mail,2</i>)

>  After all, the names `Centralist' and `Provincialist' do not in most cases give a truer impression than party nick-names generally convey. `Centralism', properly so called, is repugnant to all the political instincts of Englishmen. `Provincialism' is as repugnant their educated sympathisers who are called or erroneously call themselves Centralists are truer friends of local self-government than many of the defenders of `Provincialism', ... <i>(Sep 11, 1858,Journal</i>)

>  After all, the names `Centralist' and `Provincialist' do not in most cases give a truer impression than party nick-names generally convey. `Centralism', properly so called, is repugnant to all the political instincts of Englishmen. `Provincialism' is as repugnant their educated sympathisers who are called or erroneously call themselves Centralists are truer friends of local self-government than many of the defenders of `Provincialism', ... <i>(Sep 11, 1858,Lyttelton Times,(Editorial)</i>) [NOTES: 1858 <i>Lyttelton Times</i> 11 Sept in 1971 Speeches and Documents 105]

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> <i>(1865,England's NZ War,49 FN</i>) [NOTES: [see CENTRALIST]]

>  On the question of Provincialism v. Centralism, he expresses our own views. As to whether Provincial Governments should be abolished or not, he thinks ... <i>(Jan 4, 1868,Marlborough Express,6</i>)

>  The rotten system of Provincialism <i>(Dec 21, 1870,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> In 1873 Ballance entered politics ... The main issue was provincialism versus centralism. As supporter of Stafford's ... `Centralist' policy, Ballance retired ... <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,1/144</i>)

>  <b>RUTHERFORD</b> <i>(1971,Speeches & Documents,96, 105</i>) [NOTES: [the terms defined.]]

>  I should be sorry to see one of the hydra heads of provincialism lift its head for want of scotching. <i>(Aug 7, 1875,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/399,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (C. Brown)]

>  <b>FITZGERALD</b> Half the recent session was occupied in a struggle between Centralism and Provincialism. <i>(1856,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1856 Fitzgerald Letter to the Electors of Lyttelton 5]

>  <b>SEWELL</b> I can see through all this, a distinct policy which would settle in a great measure the question of Centralism and Provincialism. <i>(1856,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1856 Journ. H. Sewell (1980) 2/207]

>  There is still some heat left in the smouldering ashes of Provincialism. <i>(Jan 4, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,10</i>)

>  <b>FROBISHER</b> Let us now, for argument's sake, suppose Provincialism to be the best form of internal Government for New Zealand. <i>(1893,Sketches of Gossipton,37</i>)

### Provincialist
 
 <i>n.</i> Someone favouring Provincial Government

>  It is intended to bring forward a provincialist candidate to oppose opinion on the question of centralism versus provincialism. <i>(Dec 10, 1874,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  Mr. De Latour is returned for Mt Ida by a majority of 235. He is a Provincialist. <i>(Jan 19, 1876,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  ... if he resigns his seat a rank Provincialist will get in for New Plymouth. <i>Ibid.</i> 2/413 Of course, that the abolition of the provinces is a <i>fait accompli</i> is hardly denied now by even the hottest Provincialist, but how can Auckland and Otago work together harmoniously now the provincialist war cry cannot be used to rally them? <i>(May 10, 1868,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/269,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Jane M. Atkinson)]

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> On both sides are to be found Provincialists and Centralists. <i>(1874,Colonial Experiences,280</i>)

>  For the Wellington Country District there were two candidates, Mr. C.D.R. Ward, a provincialist, and Mr. W. Allen, who is, we believe, a supporter of the Wakefield party. <i>(Nov 28, 1855,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,2</i>)

>  Hall of Canterbury, a clever young man and a rather strong provincialist. <i>(May 7, 1856,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/216,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (C.W.Richmond)]

>  After all, the names `Centralist' and `Provincialist' do not in most cases give a truer impression than party nick-names generally convey. `Centralism', properly so called, is repugnant to all the political instincts of Englishmen. `Provincialism' is as repugnant their educated sympathisers who are called or erroneously call themselves Centralists are truer friends of local self-government than many of the defenders of `Provincialism', while some who find themselves classed under the name of Provincialists have the most profound contempt for the assumption and local tyranny that have distinguished one or two of our little centralisms. <i>(Sep 11, 1858,Lyttelton Times</i>) [NOTES: 1858 <i>Lyttelton Times</i> 11 Sept (Editorial) in 1971 Speeches and Documents 105]

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Two parties were now [mid 1850s] developed in the colony; one was called Centralists, the other Provincialists. The former aimed at the incorporation of the provincial governments, with a view to their ultimate absorption, and maintained the right of the General Assembly to intermeddle in all provincial affairs. The latter, on the other hand, insisted that the Imperial Parliament had established two independent governments in New Zealand, each for its own sphere, the provinces having exclusive jurisdiction in all provincial matters, and only under the control of the General Assembly on matters of general concern. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,/223</i>)

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> <i>(1865,England's NZ War,49</i>) [NOTES: [F.N.] [see CENTRALIST]

>  The Provincialists consider themselves that they gained a triumph ... by limiting the Bridges and Ferries Bill to districts outside Provinces. <i>(Jul 25, 1868,Marlborough Express,3</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> There were in the House of Representatives in the fifties, less clearly thereafter, two loose groups which were called the `Centralist' and the `Provincialist' parties. No one has succeeded in giving a convincing explanation of the nature of this division, nor made clear to what extent these labels were meaningful. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,106</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> A strong provincialist, Clifford did all possible to assist Wellington. He was re-elected Speaker ..., and was knighted in 1858. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,1/358</i>)

>  Harry Atkinson and Charles Bowen, representing Stafford's centralist faction in the government, the opposition was made up of an angry rump of provincialists, mainly from Auckland and led by Grey. <i>(Apr 1987,NZ Journal of History,48</i>) [NOTES: (Dalziel)]

### pu/poo
 
 <i>n.</i> a gun

>  When the Ngati Awa arrived in the late 1820s, the people of Karaka knew nothing of guns, and when some fugitives from the various settlements in Kura Te Au (Tory Channel), brought tidings that a taua from the North Island were killing people with 'Pu'(which meant a strong wind or explosive breath), they scoffed, "Let them come!" "We have plenty 'pu'"... The deadly effect of the 'pu' caused great consternation in the Pa. <i>(Apr 2003,NZ Memories,No. 41/39</i>)

>  <b>sinc</b> [Maori glossary] <i>pu</i> Gun <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,302</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> Good poos or muskets. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),60</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> A musket was called a Poo (from the peculiar sound emitted). <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,2/219</i>)

### pua o te reinga
 
 <i>n.</i> = pua reinga q.v. [aka pua-o-te-reinga, wood rose, woodrose; <i>dactylanthus taylorii</i>]

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Tepuaotereinga, <i>Taylor. Dactylanthus Taylorii. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

>  &#65279;It has neither roots nor green leaves, and is found only in New Zealand. Maori named it Pua o te reinga, Europeans call it the Flower of Hades, or Wood Rose, and to the botanist it is Dactylanthus taylorii. <i>(Jun 1992,North & South,18</i>)

>  Its Maori name, pua o te reinga, means flower of the underworld - and refers to the way the flowers emerge from underground. <i>(Jan 22, 2011,Dominion Post,A13</i>)

>  Dactylanthus taylorii has a special place n New Zealand's native flora as the country's only fully parasitic flowering plant. Also known as pua o te reinga, wae-wae-atua, the wood rose or flower of Hades, it is hard for the untrained eye to spot as it nestles among tree roots. <i>(Apr 9, 1997,Waikato Times,21</i>)

### pua reinga
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) an endangered parasitic native plant, <i>Dactylanthus taylorii </i>. [ORIGIN: Maori, aka pua o te reinga, pua-o-te-reinga, wood rose, woodrose; <i>dactylanthus taylorii</i>] (See [also wood rose](../A#also-wood-rose))

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>[?] D[actylanthus] Taylori, Hook.f.</i> ... Auckland ... <i>Puareinga. </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,625</i>)

>  Pua Reinga is New Zealand's only truly parasitic plant, attaching itself to the roots of many shrubs and trees .. Pua Reinga has a unique relationship with the short-tailed bat, which pollinates the flowers. <i>(Jan 11, 2007,NZ Herald,A7</i>)

>  Here may be found the fantastic and legendary "Pua Reinga," which means, literally, the flower of the underworld. <i>(Sep 14, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,14</i>)

### puakarimu
 
 <i>n.</i> a fern

>  Puakarimu (<i>Lycopodium deuterodensum</i>) is largely confined to kauri forest, and the bog clubmoss (<i>L. serpentinum</i>) to a few swamps in the far north, where it is now a very rare plant. <i>(2001,NZ Geographic,No. 49/72</i>)

### Puanga
 
 <i>n.</i> the constellation Rigel in Orion

 <i>n.</i>

### puapua-a-atutahi
 
 <i>n.</i> a poisonous fungus

### puarangi
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Hibiscus trionum </i>L. Common name: <i>Starry hibiscus</i> Maori name: <i>Puarangi </i>... Mr A.H. Watt obtained the Maori name from older people at Te Kao, when the New Zealand Post Ofice wished to use an illustration of <i>Hibiscus</i> on a postage stamp. <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,64</i>)

>  4d Puarangi (Hibiscus) . <i>(Dec 3, 1959,NZ Philatelic Bulletin,XIX : 1</i>)

It belongs to the northern frost-free part of the country and can now be seen only in remote places and on some off-shore islands. <i>(1964,Postage Stamps of NZ,IV/186</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Puawhananga, <i>Lindsay. Clematis indivisa. </i> ... Puwhananga,<i> Col[enso]. Clematis indivisa. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)

>  ... the elegant climbing <i>Puawananga (Clematis, sp.) ... </i> <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/16</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Maori races)]

>  <b>FEATON</b> CLEMATIS INDIVISA (<i>Willd.</i>) The Entire-leaved Clematis. ... The `PUAWANANGA'. <i>Ibid.</i> 178 Puawananga [Maori name] .. Large Clematis [Settlers' name] .. Clematis indivisa ..Climber <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,2</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>C[lematis]</i> indivisa, <i>Willd.</i> ... A large woody climber ... <i>Puawhananga.</i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,2</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Clematis indivisa (The Entire-leaved Clematis)</i> ... Maori names, <i>Pua-wananga, Pikiarero. </i> <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,164,1940, p. 173 ed.</i>)

>  ... many had twined the moony-white clematis in their hair - the <i>pua wananga</i>, child of two bright stars, ... <i>(1930,NZ Short Stories,31</i>) [NOTES: J.C.Andersen]

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> The large-flowered clematis (<i>Clematis indivisa</i>, puawhananga) is esteemed by all, ... <i>(1910,NZ Plants,38</i>)

>  <i>puawana[a]nga</i>, a climbing plant (Clematis indivisa), a child of the stars Rehua (Antares) and Ruanga (Rigel). <i>(Jun 1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/157</i>) [NOTES: (J.C.Andersen)]

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> <i>`Pua wananga,' </i>Sam said. `That's the old Maori name for those flowers. [bush-clematis] It was supposed to be sacred. <i>(1965,Among the Cinders,33</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Clematis paniculata</i> CLEMATIS ... <i>Puawaananga </i> <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,38</i>)

>  You can see it, clematic paniculata, Child of the Stars, puawhananga, sprawling over the trees in the bush now with its mass of white starry flowers. <i>(Sep 23, 1983,Evening Post Weekender,4</i>)

>  In most lowland forest areas of New Zealand spring is heralded by the flowering of the climber now called clematis paniculata but formerly known as clematis indivisa... In his original drawing Mr Fuller showed the Maori name of the flower as "puawananga''. The Botany Division of the Department of Scientific and Industrial Research advised that it should be shown as "pikiarero". <i>(1964,Postage Stamps of NZ,IV/186</i>)

>  He would have shown him the clematis then in bud, puawananga, the child of the stars. <i>(Mar 2, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,60</i>)

### pubbery
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) a pub

>  One day [the colonial barmaid] is calling out "Beef-steak pudding, two!" in the grubby dining-room of a pubbery, the next she coruscates before rows of shining bottles, asking "What's yours?" with more or less alluring sweetness, and the morrow may see her on the visiting list at Government House by the quick evolution of a matrimonial coup with some guilded Johnny. <i>(Dec 9, 1905,NZ Free Lance,22</i>)

>  Martindale, represented by Mr. H. F. O'Leary, was charged with carrying on the business of a bookmaker and with using the pubbery as a gaming house. <i>(Jan 8, 1921,NZ Truth,5</i>)

>  Williamson's procedure is a daring one; he simply walks into a store or pubbery, buys goods, and hands over a cheque which gives him a handful of ready money to move on to the next town and do likewise. <i>(May 28, 1910,NZ Truth,257/7/3</i>)

>  <b>MARKS</b> ... a motherly old lady running a `pubbery' in the Lyell Gorge. <i>(1934,Memories (Mainly Merry),182</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> Another memory that lingered on the trip, was that from Napier to Gisborne, to inspect a property known as `Te Rapa' and nearby was a `pubbery' (certainly not a hotel, for anybody would wonder why the Licensed Victuallers Association ever granted a licence for meals or liquor to be dispensed), and was typically designated `The Trap'. Truly, this pubbery was just short of shanty, typical of the `Outer Barcoo', but where Maories, their Wahines, or broken down `Remittancers' could drown their sorrows, wander into the `arms of Bacchus' or chase pink elephants. <i>(1916,Memories of Maoriland,157</i>)

>  Much large dampness during the week put Dunedin's wickets out of action, so to speak, and players and public last Saturday afternoon spent their time in their favorite pubbery, or the Y.M.C.A., or meditating over the elections. <i>(Dec 16, 1911,NZ Truth,3</i>)

### public domain
 
 <i>n.</i> land vested in the people of New Zealand

>  The much-maligned "public domain" concept has also been refined. Under the proposals, a public domain title would be created with all existing crown titles repealed and ownership vested "in the people of New Zealand". Only private land transfer titles will be exempt. <i>(Dec 17, 2003,Dominion Post,A1</i>)

>  Public domain: The dropped idea that would have created a new concept of legal ownership by all New Zealanders rather than the Crown. <i>(Apr 8, 2004,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  United demanded that the term "public domain" be left in the legislation, because by its legal reading, this would prevent future governments from parcelling up bits of coastline to favoured tribes to grease the wheels of future Treaty settlements... [15] No one yet knows what "public domain" really means in a legal sense, and no group has yet come forward that might claim ancestral connections back to 1840. <i>(Apr 24, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

>  So the artfully crafted notion of a "public domain" ringing our jewelled isles shouldn't be too easily disparaged. <i>(Aug 30, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),5</i>)

>  The term "Crown ownership" having been deemed offensive to Maori, it came up with a euphemism, "public domain". <i>(Aug 30, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>)

### public school
 
 <i>n.</i> 1. British: a private school. 2. New Zealand and American: a school maintained at public expense.

>  Mr. Grace takes this opportunity to inform the public of Port Nicholson, that he intends to open a public School for all the branches of a liberal education, and also to take Boarders, so soon as he has built a suitable house in the intended town at Thorndon. <i>(May 9, 1840,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,2</i>)

>  CONCERT AND DANCE. - Raingiahoia Public School. <i>(Dec 11, 1884,Waikato Times,2</i>)

>  PARTIAL DESTRUCTION OF A PUBLIC SCHOOL .. About 2.30am a fire broke out in the Bridge-street Boys' School - one of the principal Government schools in town. <i>(Nov 24, 1892,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  OUR PUBLIC SCHOOLS COLUMN. <i>(Sep 30, 1908,Otago Witness,86</i>)

>  <b>SCANLAN</b> The term `public school' has a different connotation in New Zealand. It implies the Borough or County school; the school provided by the State. <i>(1932,Pencarrow,256</i>)

>  I would include among New Zealand English words: <i>public-school</i>: state school, not private school as in England <i>(May 6, 1952,VUC First Year English Class</i>)

### public servant
 
 <i>n.</i> = state servant

>  FORGERY BY A PUBLIC SERVANT .. Two informations for forgery were laid to-day against Thos. Gibson, late town clerk, and collector for the Petone Town Board. <i>(Oct 9, 1884,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)

>  While we are glad that so valuable a public servant is meeting with reward, we regret that Western Australia, at the moment a new and bright career was opening to her, should be deprived of his valuable services, and protection. <i>(Mar 20, 1841,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,2</i>)

The opinion is held that a prominent Wellington business man will be appointed to the position. <i>(Nov 11, 1903,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  [Hdg] Public Servants and Gambling. <i>(Feb 8, 1890,NZ Observer,4</i>)

>  No public servant had been more devoted in the discharge of his duties, and none had shewn more skill in the conduct of a department. <i>(Jan 2, 1867,Timaru Herald,2</i>)

>  He had studied to work himself up from porter to clerk; he was not pushed up, but climbed with his own ability and that was the kind of Public Servant that was wanted. <i>(Jun 28, 1932,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,3</i>)

>  THE PUBLIC SERVICE JOURNAL. A monthly publication devoted primarily to the interests of the Public Servants of New Zealand. <i>(Jan 15, 1914,Public Service Journal,1</i>)

>  That Public Servants in remote places be allowed extra leave to enable them to reach the railway. <i>(Apr 15, 1914,Public Service Journal,29</i>)

>  I think some provision should be made to meet the question of tips, and more especially if the Public Servant is to visit the same hotel again. <i>(Aug 15, 1914,Public Service Journal,37</i>)

>  The Public Servant is at the mercy of the market. <i>(Sep 15, 1914,Public Service Journal,3</i>)

>  Public Servants have nothing to gain from inflation. <i>(Feb 1951,Public Service Journal,4</i>)

>  <i>public servant ... 2. Austral. & N.Z.</i> a civil servant. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,920</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1986 NZPOD 610; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 391]]

>  One important reform would be for Parliament to set some rules about how MPs may criticise public servants. <i>(Aug 28, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),54</i>)

>  As the link which has bound you to Westland for the last eight years is about to be severed, we, the undersigned residents of Hokitika, express our very sincere regret that Westland should lose one of her best public servants. <i>(Oct 8, 1874,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  In the past there has been doubt whether the Finance Act makes compulsory the prosecution of all public servants guilty of misappropriation of public money. <i>(Jun 21, 1921,Ashburton Guardian,2</i>)

>  Many thousands of Public Servants.. find themselves quite unable to live decently. <i>(Feb 21, 1945,People's Voice,5</i>)

### Public Service
 
 <i>n.</i> government departments and their personnel; the state service(s) [[=British <i>Civil Service</i>, occas. used in NZ].]

>  The Public Service Appeal Board - Messrs. P. Barr (chairman), J.H. Richardson, Mount, and Maryatt - resumed today consideration of appeals against the original classification. <i>(Jan 14, 1914,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  The following contributions have been received by the Under-Secretary, Department of Internal Affairs, from the members of the Public Service during the month of May:- Belgian Relief Fund GBP1476 12s. 9d., Relief of the Poor of Great Britain and Ireland and Belgium Fund GBP13 16s. 6d. <i>(Jun 2, 1915,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  <i>Obituary: The New Zealand Public Service. Born 1912; Matured 1962; Eviscerated 1988; Died 1999. </i>The death is announced of the New Zealand Public Service after a long illness. In 1912 the Public Service Act was significant in that for the first time the responsibility for appointments and promotions in the service was invested in a statutory officer, the Public Service Commissioner <i>(Aug 6, 1999,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  My lords have again before them the letter from Mr Stephen, of the 13th ultimo, adverting to certain circumstances which appeared to the Marquis of Normanby and to the Viscount Palerston to force upon her Majesty's government the adoption of measures for establishing some Britsh authority in New Zealand for the government of the Queen's subjects resident in, or resorting to, those islands; and, with that view, proposing that the officer about to proceed to New Zealand as consul, should be appointed lieutenant-governor of this dependency; and that the expenses which must neccessarily be incurred for his passage, and for the purchase of articles which will be required for his immediate use in the public service, or for presents to the native chiefs, should be defrayed by advances from the funds of the government of New South Wales. <i>(Aug 21, 1839,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,4</i>)

>  An Act to Amend the Public Service Classification and Superannuation Act. <i>(1908,NZ Statutes,8 Edw. VII 250/206</i>)

>  An Act for the Regulation of the Public Service. <i>Ibid.</i> 82 "Public Service" means the service of His Majesty in respect of the Government of New Zealand other than honorary service. <i>(1912,NZ Statutes,3 Geo. V 23/81</i>)

>  If any gentlemen were present who did not belong to the Public Service, he trusted they would have the good taste to withdraw. <i>(Apr 1951,Public Service Journal,20</i>) [NOTES: Meeting of July 31, 1890, reported in 1890 Evening Post]

>  With the approach of the holiday season, the Public Service becomes disintegrated. <i>(Feb 16, 1914,Public Service Journal,21</i>)

>  As a newcomer to the Public Service I have just worked my first overtime. <i>(Jan 1951,Public Service Journal,4</i>)

>  An Act to Provide a Superannuation Fund for the Public Service .. <i>Ibid.</i> 287 "Public Service" includes the High Commissioner's Office, the Legislative Branch, and every Department of the Government Service except the Government Railways Department, so much of the Police Department as is included in the Police Provident Fund Act, 1899, and so much of the Education Department as is included in the Teachers' Superannuation Act. <i>(1907,NZ Statutes,7 Edw. 63/286</i>)

>  <i>public service n.</i> the New Zealand and Australian term for the civil service. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,920</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1986 NZPOD 610; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 391]]

>  [M.O.W. engineer discussing `Thermal Heating at Chateau Tongariro'.] [The thermostat] was found to be working far too frequently, the sensing device measuring the loads taken by the hotel being much too sensitive. Its sensitivity had been reduced by means of what had been nicknamed, `a Public Service relay', a slugging device which, when something happened, did nothing about it until absolutely necessary. <i>(May 15, 1962,NZ Engineering,177</i>) [NOTES: jocular or contemptuous (cf. government stroke)]

>  <b>BRYCE</b> THE PUBLIC SERVICE [title] In few countries, if in any, is the proportion of members of the public service to the whole nation so large as in New Zealand ... <i>(1921,Modern Democracies,2/307,1929 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HALL</b> In those days, as now, we were known as the Public Service. <i>(1977,Glide Time,94</i>)

>  The welfare of the public service is identical with the welfare of the colony. <i>(Aug 7, 1890,Otago Witness,23</i>)

>  The picture of the public service is of a ship of state that keeps springing leaks. <i>(Sep 28, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),54</i>)

>  When they asked me why I wanted to work in the public service, I said because you get security for life. That was the wrong answer, I should have said 'because of the opportunities'. <i>(Jul 30, 2003,Capital Times,7</i>)

>  It is notified in our advertising columns that a Public Service entrance examination for male candidates will be held on or about 18th November and following days. <i>(Aug 25, 1913,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  It is notified by the Public Service Commissioner that as the number of female applicants already qualified for cadetships in the Public Service very greatly exceeds the number of suitable vacancies likely to occur for them in 1913 and 1914, it has been decided not to admit female candidates to the Public Service entrance examination of November, 1913. <i>(Jul 25, 1913,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  The Public Service Commissioner gazettes a notice that a public service entrance examination, formerly known as Civil Service Junior, will be held on or about November 18th and the following days. <i>(May 2, 1913,Grey River Argus,5</i>)

>  Miss Mary Brooker secured her Senior Free place and Public Service Entrance, Master F. Gordon, attending at Invercargill, also securing the latter placings. <i>(Feb 2, 1932,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,2</i>)

>  <b>MIDDLETON</b> .. I agreed to sit for the Public Service exam. <i>(1964,Walk on the Beach,165</i>) [NOTES: (Not for a Seagull)]

### Public Service Investment Society
 
 <i>n.</i> originally a public servants' co-operative investment organisation and shop [usually as the PSIS]

>  In fact, the former Public Service Investment Society has been open to anyone for about 15 years. <i>(Jun 3, 2004,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

### Public Service Manual
 
 <i>n.</i> the official set of instructions and regulations used in the Public Service

>  <b>HASMAN-JUDD</b> She is impressed by the conscientious way he monitors her progress, regularly asking if she's settling in all right,.. and offering to explain any bits of the Public Service Manual she can't understand. <i>(2000,Woman Who Loved Plants & Other Stories, The,88</i>)

### Public Trust
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  That Act provides that all moneys payable to the Government must be paid in to the credit of one or other of the four branches into which the Public Account is divided. These are the Consolidated Fund, the Special Fund, the Land Fund, and the Public Trust Fund. <i>(Apr 9, 1870,Otago Witness,1</i>)

>  AUDIT DEPARTMENT CALLED ON TO EXPLAIN. .. it is considered the evidence taken by the Public Trust Commissioners shows that the accounts are not properly audited, and the Commissioners intend to summon the Audit officer to give evidence. <i>(May 4, 1891,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  LAWYER G. G. ROSE (Wellington Public Trust Solicitor). <i>(Aug 13, 1921,NZ Truth,5</i>)

>  The fine new office for the Department had come none too soon and at the present rate of growth he could say that before long the tenants who filled the rooms unoccupied by the Public Trust Department would have to go. <i>(Feb 18, 1910,Grey River Argus,4</i>)

### public trust
 
 <i>n.</i> rust [rhyming slang]

>  Car dealers have a collection of jargon they use on the yard, so if you are thinking of taking up selling cars as a career, there are a few terms you should know. ... Nowadays most cars have `fires,' or heaters, but not all of them have `public trust,' or rust. <i>(Jan 22, 1986,NZ Herald,Sect. 2/1</i>)

### Public Trust (Office)
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  H. Oswin, a clerk in the Public Trust Office, New Plymouth, was committed for sentence this morning on a charge of the theft of GBP450, moneys [sic] of the Public Trust. <i>(Jul 3, 1906,Hawera & Normanby Star,7</i>)

>  The transactions of the Public Trust Office for the year just closed show the enormous total of over GBP3,500,000. <i>(Apr 16, 1906,West Coast Times,3</i>)

>  .. the remaining financial requirements for land pur-[35]chase and establishment of farms being obtained mainly from private individuals, the State Advances Office and the Public Trust. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,34</i>)

>  The Public Trust Office was constituted by `The Public Trust Office Act, 1872' ... <i>(1894,NZ Official Yearbook,270</i>)

>  The Public Trust office has a Common fund which is state-guaranteed and pays competitive rates of interest. <i>(Jul 29, 1989,Listener (New Zealand Listener),6</i>)

>  The Government .. had made a provision which merely left it open to the trust office to make such investments, if desirable, and if an agreement could be made with the colonial government by the public trust office. <i>(1870,NZ Parliamentary Debates,IX/109</i>)

>  The "Public Trust Office Act, 1872 .. There shall be an office called the Public Trust Office, administered by an officer called the Public Trustee, who shall be appointed by the Governor. <i>(1872,NZ Statutes,36 Vict. 26/124</i>)

>  In one of the last clauses of the Bill, power was given to the Public Trustee to audit the accounts of the Curators of Intestate Estates and the accounts of lunatic estates .. The Bill enable Friendly Societies and Building Societies to intrust their securities and properties to the Public Trust Office. <i>(1872,NZ Parliamentary Debates,XII/33</i>)

### Public Trustee
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Hon. Mr VOGEL moved, - For leave to introduce a bill to provide for the appointment of a Public Trustee. <i>(Jul 30, 1870,Wellington Independent,5</i>)

>  It is understood that Mr R. C. Hammerton, Secretary of the Stamp Department, has been appointed Public Trustee also, and will discharge the duties of both offices. <i>(Jul 16, 1880,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

>  In the Appeal Court case, Public Trustee versus Sheath, interpretation of a will, decision was reserved. <i>(Sep 28, 1917,Grey River Argus,3</i>)

>  The Public Trustee wrote in respect to a deceased estate and stated that no offers had been received for the property. <i>(Jan 5, 1932,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,3</i>)

>  The Public Trustee is also authorised by `The Lunacy Act, 1882,' to undertake the administration of the estates of lunatics ... <i>(1894,NZ Official Yearbook,269</i>)

>  Recently a correspondent wrote to "Truth" representing that the Public Trustee had failed, through apparent neglect, to collect rent owing on a house let by the Public Trustee. <i>(Jun 21, 1924,NZ Truth,5</i>)

>  I find it astonishing that anyone could possibly contemplate that the Public Trustee is expected to be a guarantor or insurer of such investments where a loss occurs. The Public Trustee would certainly not expect to share in or retain any gains where they arise. <i>(Jul 29, 1989,Listener (New Zealand Listener),6</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> He can entrust it to the enervating mediation of that passe invention, The Public Trustee, whose very mention dries up the blood in their veins. <i>(1995,Where the White Man Treads,235</i>) [NOTES: check page 235, 236 or 237?]

>  In one of the last clauses of the Bill, power was given to the Public Trustee to audit the accounts of the Curators of Intestate Estates and the accounts of lunatic estates... The Bill enable Friendly Societies and Building Societies to intrust their securities and properties to the Public Trust Office. <i>(1872,NZ Parliamentary Debates,XII/33</i>)

>  Mr Vogel, in moving for leave to introduce a Bill to provide for the appointment of a Public Trustee, said the Bill was one of so large a nature that he should be doing an injustice to it by making a brief explanation. <i>(1870,NZ Parliamentary Debates,VIII/148</i>)

>  The Bill would authorize public trust property to be placed in charge of a public trustee. Private persons, as well as charitable bodies, might also vest property in the trustee, subject to the approval of a board, consisting of the Colonial Treasurer, the Government Annuities Commissioner, the Auditor-General, and the Public Trustee. <i>(1870,NZ Parliamentary Debates,IX/107</i>)

>  The "Public Trust Office Act, 1872... There shall be an office called the Public Trust Office, administered by an officer called the Public Trustee, who shall be appointed by the Governor. <i>(1872,NZ Statutes,36 Vict. 26/124</i>)

>  The "Public Trust Office Act, 1872... There shall be an office called the Public Trust Office, administered by an officer called the Public Trustee, who shall be appointed by the Governor. <i>(1872,NZ Statutes,36 Vict. 26/124</i>)

### Public Works (Department)
 
 <i>n.</i> a state organisation (now defunct) responsible for contructing and maintaining public roads, bridges, buildings, etc.; an important provider of largely unskilled employment in remote rural regions during mid and late 20th century and hence also often a source of pilfered equipment or materials

>  I think the Public Works cottages are adisgrace on any farm. <i>(Nov 1, 1945,NZ Dairy Exporter,74</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> It was a lady's bike to he kept his old Public Works shovel tied along the frame to make it look right. <i>(1961,One of Us</i>)

>  <b>WALLACE</b> <i>(1891,Rural Economy of Aust. & NZ,488</i>) [NOTES: (also 207 Aust)]

>  <i>(Nov 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,408/1</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> <i>(1969,Descriptive Atlas,39</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> I limped on to the Public Works camp. <i>(1960,Good Keen Man, A</i>) [NOTES: cf W3]

>  <b>CRUMP</b> After a few days on good grub and in the company of the comparatively intellectual Public Works Maoris who were camped at the road a mile away, I bailed our way round to Te Puna Bay where I reckoned the deer would be feeding after spending the winter in the Huiarau range. <i>(1960,Good Keen Man, A,94</i>)

>  <b>FORRESTER</b> Until I became a Government field officer at Ruatahuna I didn'tknow a thing about catching trout the legal way. Bullets, worms ... and that good old Public Works bait, gelignite... all these methods I could use with deadly results. <i>(1965,Hunter for Hire,171</i>)

>  The most capable officers of the Public Works Department were from time to time instructed to report on it. <i>(Mar 25, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

>  It cannot be that the Public Works Department has changed its opinion as to the resources of the country through which the line passes. <i>(Feb 25, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

>  <b>COLE</b> Here the Public Works gangs still use the pick and shovel. The rattle of their implements, and the occasional echoing wham of a charge of gelly, and the deepening engine note of a passing vehicle as it changes into low gear on the ... slippery bush-shadowed bends, seem only to nibble at the vast silence. <i>(1949,It Was So Late,63,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <b>NOONAN</b> There was no Public Works Department inNew Zealand until 1870. <i>(1975,By Design,1</i>)

>  <b>NOONAN</b> Under date 26 May 1948, Cabinet approvedof the Public Works Department beingmerged in the Ministry of Works for alladministrative purposes under theCommissioner of Works as permanent head. <i>(1975,By Design,196</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Driving along the Wairoa-Napier road, I was brought to a halt by the Ministry of Works... I myself had worked for the Public Works (to use its old name) on the Rotorua-Tirau road. <i>(1971,Our Open Country,133</i>)

>  My father, out of work as a consequence of the Great Depression, desperate to find employment ended up in a Public Works relief scheme on the construction of the Mangatiti Road junction in the same area. <i>(Oct 2003,NZ Memories,No. 44/31</i>)

### public works camp
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> The Works camp was in a small patch of bush. <i>(1971,Our Open Country,133</i>)

>  <b>COLE</b> The re-opened Public Works camp, where Iam engaged as a clerk, is on the flat amile distant. <i>(1949,It Was So Late,89</i>)

### public works gang
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>COLE</b> &#65279;Here the Public Works gangs still use thepick and shovel. <i>(1949,It Was So Late,90</i>)

### publican's shout
 
 <i>n.</i> drinks shouted by the publican in his turn. (A dying custom, and often used ironically). - [ca1920 at least (Havelock and elsewhere).]

### publican-fish
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> GLOBE-FISH <i>Contusus richei</i> (Freminville) ... It has several other popular names such as Balloon-fish and Mouse-fish. In America it is known as Rabbit-fish and Stink-fish. The most humorous name is that of Publican-fish. ... The most witty name, Publican-fish given to it by Australian fishermen, is said to have originated from the resemblance of an inflated Globe-fish to the rotundity of many successful publicans. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,378</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> PUBLICANFISH: <i>see Globefish. </i> <i>Ibid.</i> 182 PUBLICAN-FISH Another name for the Globe-fish, given apparently because of its round, bloated resemblance to early New Zealand publicans. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,176</i>)

### puckaroo
 
 <i>adj.</i> (See [pakaru](p-per.md#pakaru))

### puckarooed
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  The most active thing, the only thing in your body, is the mind, your brain. And if anything affects that, then you're puckarooed. You've had it. <i>(Oct 12, 1991,Dominion,13</i>)

### puckeroo
 
 <i>v.</i> break, ruin (an Anglicisation of PAKARU q.v.) [26/11/88] spg buckeroo (North Auckland, or ?Bay of Plenty)]

>  His command of the Native tongue was pleasantly accurate. The words <i>pakeha, kapi, waipero, hoea</i> and <i>puckaroo</i> came rolling out of him like ribaldry from Uriah when he found the trick David had played on him. <i>(Apr 16, 1884,Tuapeka Times,5</i>)

>  <b>MUIR</b> <i>'I</i> don't know. His old man was pretty strict, of course. ... Probably pukarooed any romances.' <i>(1960,Word for Word,174</i>)

>  <b>CASEY</b> It used to be a real crackerjack, but I'd puckerooed it completely, <i>eh. </i> <i>(1963,As Short a Spring,284</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> The New Zealand equivalent [to go bung `<i>break down</i>'], puckeroo<i> or</i> be puckerooed <i>sound like an English word used by the maoris and returned with some damage to the voicing of the consonants; but is is actually from the </i> Maori <i>pakaru</i> `break'. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,135</i>)

>  <i>pukaru v.</i> break [Maori pakaru, broken]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),16</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> WHA. Haangi puckerooed by now, I bet. Kumaras like bullets, pigs all burnt up. [note 99] <i>puckerooed</i>: English version of Maori <i>pakaru</i>, meaning shattered, or broken or rendered useless. The word is used several times in the play, both in its Maori and Pakeha form; it is much stronger in Maori. <i>(1969,Awatea,43</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>puckeroo</i> ruin, Maori `pakaru', broken or shattered pieces, used WWI soldiers; eg `This torch is puckerooed!' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,88</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> ... until recently he never would have mislaid his maul or used his axe [to bash in iron wedges] instead, which is the surest way to buckeroo an axe Uncle Ted says. <i>(1948,Tidal Creek,126,1979 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> [Adj.]

>  <i>puckeroo</i>: broken <i>(1980,Lynfield College, Auckland</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  <i>puckeroo NZ sl. 1 a</i>. useless, broken. <i>2 v.t.</i> (esp. in p.p.) ruin. [M <i>pakaru</i> break, broken] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,610</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 391]]

>  "He was scooped and there was nothing to suggest that he was puckeroo," trainer John Boon said. <i>(Nov 28, 2000,Dominion Post,D6</i>)

### puckerooed
 
 <i>adj.</i> broken, ruined; exhausted [also puckeroo'd] (See [puckeroo](pop-pyo.md#puckeroo))

>  `Before long he was fair-dinkum bushed. He had a compass ... but it was puckeroo'd. Well, he decided it was a dead loss ...' <i>(Apr 19, 1971,Listener (New Zealand Listener),56</i>)

>  <b>McGill</b> He...said he had heard nothing, bit puckerooed after all that mucking about looking for gold in the quarry. <i>(1995,Gold in the Creek,136</i>)

### pud-puller
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) masturbator

>  <b>BAXTER</b> <i>Pud-puller!</i> he whispered bitterly to his reflection. <i>(1985,Horse,6,1962 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Ghost in Trousers)]

### pudding
 
 <i>n.</i> blankets, bedclothes [??nonce]

>  <b>FRAME</b> I pulled more pudding over my side, and I curled myself up to fit in with her ... And then I fell asleep. <i>(1961,Lagoon, The,18,1951 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (The Secret)]

### pudding-stone
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [cf. Moeraki Boulders](mob-mys.md#moeraki-boulders))

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> We walked on to the top of the bank towards the sea about a mile [near Thames], but soon got off the good land, and passed over an entire bed of pudding-stones from 10 to 50 lb. weight. <i>Ibid.</i> 342 The low land did not exceed a quarter of a mile, when we came to rising ground, covered with Kaikatoa - very ordinary. <i>(Nov 7, 1833,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,341,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>COLENSO</b> The rocks in this locality [East cape], were chiefly composed of sand-and-pudding-stone; the latter containing immensely large oyster shells. <i>(1844,Excursion in the Northern Island of NZ</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> The eastern extremity of this bay is formed by a rocky peninsula of pudding-stone, worn by the action of the waves <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/202</i>)

>  The miner took the naming of rocks into his own hands, and pronounced the softer country "sandstone," where this had not to be called "puddingstone," "mullock-bands," or hard-bars." <i>(Mar 16, 1900,NZ Mines Record,305</i>)

### puddle
 
 <i>v.</i> [[AND 1852] OED 6. Gold-mining: to work [washdirt] with water in a tub so as to separate the ore (1859 Austr. 1864 US.)]

>  Also, a small parcel of nine and a-half tons for the Tapu Gold-Mining Company, which, after being puddled, gave forty-two ounces of retorted gold and some five ounces of very rich specimens from the stamper boxes. <i>(Apr 8, 1869,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

>  Now money is your only friend, / So if you want some tin to spend, / To paddocks rich your footsteps bend, / And your time there well employ; / When no one's looking fill your dish, /And you may wash out what you wish, / Puddling mullock's knocked into a `mish,' / Hard work will but annoy. <i>(1875,NZ Songster,No. 5/64</i>) [NOTES: c1875? Thatcher The Leary Boy]

>  <b>PYKE</b> There was nothing to `puddle' - anything like the stiff-clays of Bendigo being non-existent. <i>(1887,History of the Early Gold Discoveries  in Otago,111</i>)

>  <b>THATCHER</b> The Chinese swarm around, my boys, / Puddling every bit of ground. <i>(1865,Colonial Songster,4</i>)

### puddle erosion
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the loss of fine particles in muddy water run-off.

>  <b>CONNELL & HADFIELD</b> On the more gently sloping land, especially where it is cultivated, puddle erosion, the loss of fine particles in muddy water run-off, and rilling, small gullies in cultivated hillsides, also occur. <i>(1961,Agriculture,251</i>)

### puddle-jumper
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) a high wheel-base truck

>  [Chief Ranger speaks] Instead of a new chev. truck we found we had an old chev. puddle-jumper - a Second World War puddle-jumper - and if any of you have ever driven a puddle-jumper ... <i>(Jul 26, 1986,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: 9.00 am (Spectrum Documentary about Tongariro National Park)]



 
 <i>n.</i> a microlite plane [OED citations seem all to refer to "light" aeroplanes, rather than the smaller microlites]

>  With a little training, the average Joe can get in one of these puddle-jumpers and have some fun. <i>(Feb 1997,North & South,22</i>)

### puddler
 
 <i>n.</i> a machine that puddles washdirt [goldmining; cf. puddler, a person who puddles washdirt]

>  <b>BLYTH</b> <i>Puddlers:</i> Circular machines used for washing paydirt. <i>(1935,Gold Mining Year Book,11</i>)

>  The puddler they found in capital condition, and a very good asset to the company. <i>(Jul 25, 1889,Otago Witness,12</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a person who puddles washdirt [goldmining; the person who puddles washdirt; cf puddler, the machine for that purpose]

>  The advocates for sluicing believe that this is the only way that anything can be made out of the ground, while the puddler thinks if he can make, as a few are now doing, GBP8 or GBP10 a week ... <i>(Apr 23, 1862,Nelson Examiner,4</i>)

>  [From the <i>Otago Daily Times</i>] Next came the puddler, with his puddling machine, making fortunes of quite handsome dimensions, digging further still into the hill and reducing its bulk. <i>(Mar 29, 1895,West Coast Times,4</i>)

>  I may mention, in proof of the permanent character of the gold-field, that Major Croker has had numerous applications for permission to erect puddling machines. .. I find that the applicants are for the most part old Bendigo puddlers, who, no doubt, remember well the extraordinary success that waited on the efforts of those who first established this method of extracting the gold from the soil in Victoria. <i>(Mar 12, 1862,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,5</i>)

>  The Greytown correspondent of the <i>Heathcote Advertiser</i> states that a miner in that town, named John Duvain, has come into the possession of GBP450,000. There are also two brothers, puddlers, of the same place, who have lately received the intelligence from America of a third brother having made provision for the annuity of GBP250 per annum for each of them for life. <i>(Oct 6, 1875,Nelson Evening Mail,2</i>)

>  <b>HISLOP</b> A day with a prospecting dish is real good fun, and will often give the novice puddler visions of gold that will put to shame the [21] goose that laid the golden eggs. <i>(1943,Pure Gold,20</i>)

### puddling
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MAY</b> PUDDLING: separating gold from clay. <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,528,1962 ed.</i>)

### puddling machine
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [also puddler](../A#also-puddler))

>  [From the <i>Otago Daily Times</i>] Next came the puddler, with his puddling machine, making fortunes of quite handsome dimensions, digging still further into the hill and reducing its bulk. <i>(Mar 29, 1895,West Coast Times,4</i>)

>  The discoverers are also the proprietors of the first puddling machine erected on these diggings ... <i>(Jun 11, 1864,Otago Witness,19</i>)

>  I may mention, in proof of the permanent character of the gold-field, that Major Croker has had numerous applications for permission to erect puddling machines. .. I find that the applicants are for the most part old Bendigo puddlers, who, no doubt, remember well the extraordinary success that waited on the efforts of those who first established this method of extracting the gold from the soil in Victoria. <i>(Mar 12, 1862,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,5</i>)

>  <b>WEKEY</b> (quoting Otago Gfds Regs) Claims shall be classified thus;- 1. Ordinary Claims, <i>i.e.</i>, alluvial claims worked without the aid of sluices or machines. 2. Sluice and machine claims, <i>i.e.</i>, alluvial claims worked with the aid of sluices or puddling machines. 3. Wet Claims, <i>i.e.</i>., alluvial claims in flooded ground ... 4. Creek or river claims, <i>i.e.</i>, alluvial claims in the bed of creeks or rivers. 5. Quartz claims. <i>(1862,Otago As It Is,61</i>)

>  The puddling machine interest is stronger here than in other parts of the Province, but the results ... offer no very cheering assurance of its adaptability to these gold fields. <i>(1862,Otago: Its Goldfields and Resources,18</i>)

 <i>n.</i>
