### puff-pipe
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>Puff-pipe</i>- An anti-siphonage pipe from a single waste water fixture carried up on the outside of a building and terminating at least 150 mm above the overflow level of the fixture. <i>(1979,SANZ Glossary Building Terminology,89</i>)

### puffball
 
 <i>n.</i> one of several species of ball-shaped fungi which, though solid when young, are full of dust-like spores when mature.

>  <b>CROWE</b> Both the giant and common puffball are recommended as being amongst the best of the puffballs, so long as they are solid and white inside. <i>(1997,Field Guide to Native Edible Plants of NZ,136</i>)

>  <b>CROWE</b> The Maori ate young puffballs.. after steaming. <i>(1997,Field Guide to Native Edible Plants of NZ,136</i>)

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Calvatia caelata</i> (Bull.) Morg. Common name: <i>Puffball </i> Maori name: <i>Pukurau.</i> In Hooker's `Handbook ...', the name `pukurau' or `pukuvau' is <i>applied to</i> Lycoperdon fontanesei ... <i>which is now </i>known as<i> Calvatia caelata</i>. In Williams ... the name `pukurau' is identified as <i>Clathrus cibarium</i> ... a net-like fungus ... [C. caelata is found in Europe ...as well as NZ.] <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,23</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Tank Tarrant once led his gang across the paddocks ..., kicking the puffballs and the sheep turds, throwing the dry cow pats at any tarts he met. Chanting the gang haka. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,230</i>)

>  The common puffball is also finely flavoured. This is nearer a golfball size than a softball, and it has a stem. <i>(Jan 11, 2003,Dominion Post,F7</i>)

>  The giant puffball grows in any soil with high organic matter, often on bare earth and on back lawns, including my own. <i>(Jan 11, 2003,Dominion Post,F7</i>)

>  I well remember the strict warnings never to pick the puffball, and it was always a source of great satisfaction to trample the puffballs that had gone brown and watch the evil yellow cloud of spores spurt forth. <i>(Jan 11, 2003,Dominion Post,F7</i>)

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> Arthur picked up a large puff-ball and punted it high into the air. "One for the forwards," he cried, and laughed as the khaki dust drifted in the sky and the broken fungus dropped back to the ground. <i>(1979,Supper Waltz Wilson,23</i>)

### puffer
 
 <i>n.</i> globefish

>  <b>MORELAND</b> GLOBEFISH [<i>Spheroides jaculiferus</i>] ... Puffer, toado, common toado, are alternative names sometimes heard here. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,24</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> GLOBEFISH ... <i>Other names: Spheroides richei</i>; puffer, common toado, sand toado. <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,296</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] puffer 2354-73 PUFFERFISHES 2353-83 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### pufferfish
 
 <i>n.</i> globefish

>  Council staff sprang into action yesterday morning when more than 250 puffer fish were found scattered over two kilometres between Karaka Bay and Fort Dorset... National Institute of Water and Atmosphere spokesman Malcolm Francis said quick action was necessary as the fish were extremely toxic... Puffer fish were common in New Zealand waters, but they rarely washed up on the beaches. <i>(Apr 23, 2004,Dominion Post,A7</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>GLOBEFISH</i> (Pufferfish, prickly toadfish) <i>Contusus richei (Sphoeroides richei)</i> ... The globefish is a round bodied puffer ... [317] They prefer shallow coastal waters and are found in large numbers just off sandy beaches. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,316,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> Flathead Kathetostoma giganteum Puffer-fish <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,169</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Pufferfish Contusus richei</i> Alternatively globefish, or toadfish. Also present in southern Australia. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,147</i>)

>  [species name] pufferfish .. [scientific name]<i> Sphoeroides</i> sp. .. [other name(s)] -- <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Clown toado Canthigaster callisternus </i>Alternatively sharpnosed pufferfish. Present off south-east Australia, regularly seen around north-eastern New Zealand. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,147</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>CLOWN TOAD</i> (Sharpnosed pufferfish) <i>Canthigaster callisternus </i>...The clown toado is a small puffer ... <i>(1982,Collins Guide,316,1984 ed.</i>)

### puffin
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> There is also a sort of <i>Puffin</i> or <i>pelicanoides</i>, commonly called the <i>mutton-bird</i>, which the natives take in large quantities by lighting fires on the seaward face of the hills near the coast, into which these birds, called <i>titi </i>by the natives, fly, and are killed. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,165</i>)

>  As we hear the short-tailed puffin at night flying overhead, I count it also as one of our Tutira birds. It is called by the bushmen the `mutton-bird,' but can be only ... <i>Puffinus caudus. </i> <i>(1895,TrNZI,28/375</i>)

### puffing pig
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Fishermen have nicknamed Hector'sdolphins "puffing pigs." <i>(Jan 2, 1993,Listener (New Zealand Listener),29</i>)

### puftaloon
 
 <i>n.</i> [also spelt pufftaloon below]

>  <b>FUTTER</b> <i>PUFTALOONS</i> ... Flour ... Butter ... Baking Powder ... Milk. Salt ... roll out lightly as thin as a wafer, cut in rounds ... fry a golden brown ... Serve very hot ... eaten with golden syrup. <i>(1926,Home Cookery for NZ,206</i>)

>  <b>McKAY</b> <i>PUFFTALOONS</i> Drain on ... paper, serve at once with jam, or honey. <i>(1929,Practical Home Cookery,178</i>)

>  <i>pufftaloons, pufftaloonies ...n. pl.</i> hot fried cakes spread with jam, sugar, or honey. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),16</i>)

### pug
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  An undetermined kaolinic product - the `pug' of the miners - is fairly abundant in the reefs. <i>Ibid.</i> 113 (Coromandel) The actual vein-walls are frequently smooth and slickensided, being separated from the wass-rock by a narrow selvage of plaster finely comminated rock (locally termed `pug') <i>Ibid </i>114 They are in almost all cases fissures filled, as the result of rock-movement and decomposition processes, with clayey material (`pug') <i>(1907,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No. 4 (N.S.)/102</i>) [NOTES: (Fraser and Adams)]

>  The more steeply dipping contacts between these andesites and the older land-surface ... are usually marked by a heavy of band slickensided pug. <i>(1912,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No. 15 (N.S.)/127</i>) [NOTES: (Bell and Fraser, Waihi-Tairua)]

>  ... there was merely a narrow vein of pug, showing no scheelite, but a little gold. <i>(1918,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No. 19 (N.S.)/45</i>) [NOTES: (Marshall, Tuapeka)]

>  This silica settled in pockets, forming the siliceous `pug' common in the workings of the New Zealand quick-silver mines ... <i>(1922,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,5/174</i>) [NOTES: (Cropp)]

>  <b>STRONACH</b> The house itself was built of pug, that mixture of clay and tussock which makes the most comfortable of all dwellings, cool in summer and warm in winter. .. The chimneys were huge, each one four feet of solid pug all round. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,5</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [used elsewhere but sign. in NZ with stock kept out of doors year-round] [land & soils] to become heavy and sticky.

>  There are certain paddocks that pug badly in winter ... <i>(Mar 1932,NZ Dairy Exporter,29</i>)

>  Wintering is in two mobs, and care is needed to avoid pugging the pakihi. <i>(Mar 6, 1987,NZ Dairy Exporter,26</i>)

>  If they're pugging the verge, get them off as you could be responsible for any damage to the District Council. <i>(May 29, 2001,Lifestyleblock.co.nz</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a characteristic NZ sticky mud.

>  The nature of the sinking is 40ft through pug. <i>(Sep 9, 1893,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  On my return on Friday I found the party labouring in soft blue pug which behaved like quick sand. <i>(Sep 7, 1894,West Coast Times,4</i>)

>  The present face is showing quartz grit mixed up in pug seams. <i>(Dec 22, 1909,Grey River Argus,1</i>)

>  <b>STEVENSON</b> The blue pug in which they [bones] are usually found .. is impervious to both air and water. <i>(1947,Maori and Pakeha in North Otago,13</i>)

>  <b>TAIT</b> A hard clay pug subsoil has required heavy draining. <i>(1961,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol III,119</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> Suddenly the blue pug released its grip on all four legs and, spraying a bow wave of mud, the cow shot across the top of the bog like a speed boat.. <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,114</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> It is backed by a large hill of unstable blue and white pug which in the wet season, becomes like a mass of soft putty. <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,134</i>)

### pug ring
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HISLOP</b> Some of the lads made their [sun-dried] bricks in what they called a pug ring, formed by driving the horses round in a circle as with the old-time chaffcutter, but Cecil made his on what they called a straight. A strip of ground about 30 yards long by four or five wide was scooped free of top soil, which was dumped on the garden nearby. It was then ploughed about ten inches deep, or according to the number of bricks required, and very well watered, which was usually done with a shovel from a tank on a sledge for even distribution, and left to soak all night. A good layer of chopped-up tussocks was then evenly spread over the top. After more watering a six-horse team was driven up and down the strip until the pug was well mixed. <i>(1943,Pure Gold,116</i>) [NOTES: [c1910, Otago]]

### pug up
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [used elsewhere but sign. in NZ with stock kept out of doors year-round] 1. [land & soils] to become heavy and sticky. 2. [animals] to cause hoof damage to pasture.

>  .. if the pigs have a fairly large run and the shelter is shifted about as soon as the ground in front shows signs of 'pugging up'... <i>(Aug 12, 1936,Weekly News,73</i>)

>  Wet land is not pugged up in the winter months. <i>(Aug 15, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,163</i>)

>  If you abuse your pastures by allowing them to pug up excessively, you will have a bumper crop of buttercup and pennyroyal ... <i>(Oct 1, 1949,NZ Dairy Exporter,23</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR & TROTTER</b> I put on a lot of lime in the early days. I might have overdone some of the paddocks, it made them very free on top, in the winter they used to pug up. 2. <i>(1994,From Caithness to Southland,47</i>)

>  By July the weather is getting wetter and the cows will pug up the breaks they graze on rainy days. <i>(Mar 1967,NZ Journal of Agriculture,37</i>)

### pug wall
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>STRONACH</b> Nails would stick in those brittle pug walls. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,5</i>)

### pug-heaving
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McKEON</b> One day [ca1905] after school hours I was engaged in the forbidden sport of `pug-heaving', which consisted of rolling small balls of clay, impaling them on a whippy stick and letting fly at some convenient object, the fun being to watch the clay balls flatten on impact. <i>(1971,Fruitful Years, The,16</i>)

### pugged
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [used elsewhere but sign.in NZ with stock kept out of doors year-round][of land & soils] damaged by stock hooves in wet conditions or during winter.

>  In season pugged areas become a sea of yellow buttercup. <i>(Jan 15, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,10</i>)

>  <b>CONNELL & HADFIELD</b> On heavy soils in wet weather, however, soil compaction is undesirable because the soil structure is broken down, or 'pugged', reducing water penetration and aeration. <i>(1961,Agriculture,307</i>)

>  Even though stock concentrations reached more than 400 cows an acre, only two areas were pugged, amounting to half an acre in all. <i>(Apr 1968,NZ Journal of Agriculture,41</i>)

### pugging
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [used elsewhere but sign. in NZ with stock kept out of doors] of damaged pasture, the creation of wet, sticky holes where animals have walked.

>  Low-lying portions are liable to "pugging" if stocked to any extent in the winter. <i>(Aug 15, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,161</i>)

>  <b>CONNELL & HADFIELD</b> .. sod-seeding may be practised with advantages not only of .. but also .. less pugging of the soil during the first winter grazing. <i>(1961,Agriculture,315</i>)

>  Care has been essential to avoid serious pugging, but now it could be worse. <i>(Mar 1987,NZ Dairy Exporter,6</i>)

>  .. the swear word on Taranaki farms at this time of the year is pugging. <i>(Aug 22, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,17</i>)

### pugh
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  List of Gear Lost: 38 Irons - .. 8/- ea. ... 14 Lances - .. 8/- ea. ... 1 Cutting Spade - .. 8/- ... 2 Boat Spades - .. .16. 4 Boat axes - .. 4/- ea. ... 5 Boat Knives - .. 2/- ea. ... 3 Pughs - .. .9. ... 2 Blubber Knives - .. . 8. ... 1 [Boat] Traveller - .. .4. <i>(1837,Piraki Log,49</i>)

### pugmill
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SALMOND</b> <i>Pugmill.</i> a mechanical device used to break clay down in preparation for brickmaking. <i>(1986,Glossary of Building Terminology,233</i>)

### puha
 
 <i>n.</i> (Cuisine) [ORIGIN: Maori, [Ma. <b>puha</b> sow-thistle <i>(sonchus spp.)</i> cf. P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 197.]

>  <b>KOHERE</b> They were given poor mutton boiled withkumara and puha. <i>(1951,Autobiography of a Maori,117</i>)

>  'Allo, kia ora, bon jour, tena koe,. Would you like puha on your baguette? <i>(Jun 19, 2002,Capital Times,13</i>)

>  .. served on a colcannon of puha and cybies [sic]. <i>(Jul 2005,Capital Times,13-19 July / 13</i>)

>  T'e cabbage, t'e 'puha', and t'e spudgrow up from the ponedust made from t'eman. <i>(May 30, 1917,Chronicles NZEF,II/19 : 151</i>)

>  <b>EDWARDS</b> The tables themselves are loaded: hangifood, kaimoana, trout, tuna, titi, puha,karengo. <i>(1992,Mihipeka,21</i>)

>  <b>EDWARDS</b> There is puha, lovely cooked... But it isalso used as a steriliser for freshwounds. You must wash the puha, thenbruise the leaves. <i>(1992,Mihipeka,56</i>)

>  <b>McNAUGHTON</b> And Mum got down off the horse when she got home and said, 'Oh Daddy, I was hungry for puha.' <i>(1986,Countless Signs,41</i>) [NOTES: From Patricia Grace (1975): Waiariki]

>  <b>PARK</b> I took her advice and every day gathered half a sugarbag full of puha (sow thistle) and korau, the wild turnip. <i>(1992,Fence Around the Cuckoo,220</i>)

>  Amongst [the weeds] were very large quantities of sow thistle (puha), large quantities of which we boiled. <i>(Sep 8, 1917,NZ Truth,8</i>)

>  A Maori woman is raffling a parcel of puha, a tray of eggs and a meat pack, but there are few takers. <i>(Aug 1991,Metro,67</i>)

>  Whether it's an Irish stew, a delicious French pot-au-feu, or an old-fashioned Kiwi pork and puha, a boil-up will satisfy winter appetites. <i>(Jun 8, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),38</i>)

>  <b>PARK</b> I took her advice and every day gathered half a sugarbag full of puha (sow thistle) and korau, the wild turnip. <i>(1992,Fence Around the Cuckoo,220</i>)

>  For vegetables there was plenty of pora, the Maori turnip, and puwha (rauriki) or sowthistle. <i>(1988,Tales of Pioneer Women,207</i>) [NOTES: Written of 1940]

>  Basic English is as .. useless.. as the recent report to the Son of Heaven on "The Immediate Practicality of Supplementing the Japanese Diet of Rice and More Rice with Pouha (sic) andToheroa Soup." <i>(Dec 21, 1942,NZEF Times,II/78 : 17</i>)

>  We'd go each weekend to gather pipis and pick watercress and puha in Howick and that would last us till pay day. <i>(May 9, 2004,Sunday Star Times,D9</i>)

>  As you rightly point out, the different types of puha or sow thistles are ubiquitous throughout the world. From Gisborne I went to live in Greece, where picking "horta" (puha) is akin to a national pastime. <i>(Aug 22, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),9</i>)

>  [Caption] Sonchus oleraceus, pororua or common puha; S. kirkii, raurorua or NZ native puha. Ibid. The original Maori puha was rauroroa or S. kirkii - a ntive which has a whitish bloom on the leaf surfaces and is usually found near the coast... Puha leaves were put on cuts and wounds to prevent infection, and waipuha, or water in which puha had been boiled, was drunk to cure stomach complains and loss of appetite. <i>(Nov 6, 1998,Dominion,16</i>)

>  Spread an old newspaper on the floor andon it place a steaming pot of puha,kamo-kamo, riwai, brisket-on-the-bone anddumplings what we call: doughboys. <i>(Sep 7, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),12</i>)

>  We became great mates. We'd stop in thestreet for a yack- how's it going andwhatever - and they'd bring me in feedsof puha. <i>(Aug 29, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

>  Get your puha-picking hands off our man! <i>(Nov 24, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),59</i>)

>  <i>Puwha</i> is a popular green vegetable and can also be used to treat warts by slapping it onto the skin. <i>(Jul 24, 2002,Capital Times,25</i>)

>  Cooked like this, puha tastes a little like cooked lettuce, and unlike the usual slow-boiled version. <i>(Sep 1999,Cuisine,No. 76 : 57</i>)

>  Puwha tiotio (<i>Sonchus asper</i>), the prickly leaf sow thistle and Puwha pororua (<i>Sonchus oleraceus</i>), the smooth leaf sow thistle are actually not natives but were probably brought here by early Maori. The Maori name is often spelt as puha. <i>(Oct 2000,Growing Today,39</i>)

>  For vegetables [in early Banks Peninsula] there was plenty of pora, the Maori turnip, and puwha (rauriki) or sowthistle ... <i>(1940,Tales of Pioneer Women,207,1988 ed.</i>)

>  The Puwha, or [31] Milk-thistle <i>(Sonchus oleraceus)</i>, the large-leaved variety was common, though not (it is reasonably suspected) too plentiful; and this was abandoned for the smaller-leaved European kind (after its introduction) as being less bitter and more palatable. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/30-31</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  The next is the <i>puwha</i>, or comon sow-thistle <i>(Sonchus oleraceus</i>, var., or two varieties, exclusive of the later introduced British one). <i>(1881,TrNZI,13/27</i>)

>  The Rev. W. Colenso ... considers this var. [Sonchus oleraceus L. asper ... leaves clasping, sharply toothed] to be the puwha of the Maoris, who formerly used it for food, but abandoned it for the introduced European plant, which is less bitter. <i>(1894,TrNZI,26/264</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>electric puha</i> marijuana. <i>(1989,Dinkum Kiwi Dictionary, The,28</i>)

>  There are parsley and puha on the section up the road, seaweeds and pipi at the beach. <i>(Jun 6, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),54</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> There would be kiwi and pigeon, wild duck and trout, pork and puha, potatoes and kumara - a great feast, with plum pudding to follow. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,109</i>)

>  <b>PARK</b> Mrs Hush herself preferred to cook the wild sow thistle that was called puha in those parts, a prickly dark green weed that contained so much harsh iron it could pull even the perniciously anaemic out of the furrow. <i>(1951,Witch's Thorn, The,152</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> Pork bones at $3.99 a kilo and puha at $2.79 a bunch are the local diary blackboard specials. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,82</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [noun & attr.]

>  Another little flash of inspiration were [sic]the dribbles of puha oil, just to keep the outdoorsy theme alive. <i>(Jul 14, 2001,Dominion,6</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> We passed ... three Maori women ... stooping to cut puha from a manuka burn-off. `A readymade Van Gogh, ... Or rather The Puha Gatherers.' <i>(1976,Send Somebody Nice,18</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> PRICKLY SOWTHISTLE, RAURIKI (not always distinguished from 1 above)

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> PRICKLY SOUR THISTLE [SOWTHISTLE] (<i>Sonchus asper</i>) is also called rough sour thistle [sowthistle,] and also by its Maori name of Puwha. This is very similar to the former [S.oleraceus, sowthistle] in all respects, except that the margins of the leaves are much more prickly, ... This form seems scarcely as common as the former, though it was found in New Zealand when Cook arrived here. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,210,1926 ed.</i>)

>  <i>puwha [=] prickly sow thistle: sow thistle</i> [s] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,62</i>)

>  <b>PARK</b> <i>(1951,Witch's Thorn, The,152</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>[Sonchus asper, Hill,] Sow-thistle; Rauroroa; Tawheke; Puwha</i>. ... As this was collected by Banks and Solander it must be regarded as indigenous. <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,388</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> sowthistle (as a green vegetable; Maori cabbage) [also rauriki ca1935 Havelock Marlborough puha [pu:a:], Maori cabbage, sowthistle.] (See [also rauriki](../A#also-rauriki))

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> [Maori speaks] Don't ask me. I suppose they frightened we eat them with pork and puha, eh? <i>Ibid.</i> 220 `you never see watercress here, eh? And puha, with a bit of brisket or pork-bones'. <i>(1960,Maori Girl,75</i>)

>  <i>puha n</i> -s [Maori] <i>New Zealand</i> : a sow thistle (<i>Sonchus oleraceus</i>) that is commonly used as a potherb <~ turned up with practically everything, corned beef, boiled bacon, baked rabbit - Ruth Park> <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1838</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSTON</b> On making New Zealand, Cook was pleased to find wild celery growing in profusion as well as `scurvy grass' of the cress family and the native sowthistle, <i>puwha. </i> <i>(1968,Turn of the Tide, The,74</i>)

>  A WEED that was ruthlessly removed by hand from corn crops in the Gisborne area has now become a crop itself with a market that covers the East Coast and Rotorua districts. This is the sow thistle, better known as `rauriki' in the Wellington district, which, marketed as `puha,' finds a ready sale at 1/- a bundle. There are two varieties, puha tiotio or prickly-leaved, and puha pororua, or smooth - leaved. [So Williams Dict 318] <i>(Nov 22, 1962,Evening Post,12</i>)

>  <b>McCALLUM</b> As easy as pulling puha out of wet ground. <i>Ibid.</i> 98 <i>Puha</i>: Maori cabbage. <i>(1963,Half-Gallon Jar,17</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> Sea-eggs, puha, pork, and kumara:/ The Maori owned the land. I have a camera. <i>(1979,Collected Poems,279,1963 ed.</i>)

>  For example, puha - a plant still commonly eaten by Maori - had more than three times the level of antioxidants than blueberries. <i>(Feb 28, 2006,Dominion Post,A3</i>)

>  <i>(Nov 1989,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,86/47</i>)

>  It was while on the Italian segment of his travels that Martin sampled the local version of puha, found growing amidst some Roman ruins along the Appian [sic] Way - and on boiling it up on his trusty primus camping stove to pronounce it was on a par with the Kiwi variety! <i>(Nov 1989,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,86/47</i>)

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> A sow thistle Poowha <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/338</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> P|ua, <i>s</i>. A sow-thistle. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,196</i>)

>  greens - pu'lla [puha, pora] <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,113</i>) [NOTES: c1826-1827 list of Maori words in c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) 113; also 1826-28 Boultbee Vocabulary in 1979 Begg 286]

>  <b>BURNS</b> A sow thistle Poowha <i>(1844,NZ Chief,24</i>) [NOTES: [Vocabulary of English and NZ Words]]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> When I reproved a chief for not compelling his children to attend school, his excuse was, that as soon as they arose in the morning, they went and gathered little bundles of <i>Puwha</i>, sow thistles or sticks, which they carried to the town and sold, either for a few pence or a little food, saying, they were like the fowls which went scratching about for food. <i>(1868,Past and Present of NZ,88</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Puwha, <i>Col[enso]. Sonchus oleraceus. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> It [sc pigeon] also feeds on the tender shoots of the puwha, a kind of sow-thistle; and the flesh then partakes of the bitterness of that plant. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,159</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> The sow-thistle (<i>puwha</i>: Sonchus oleracus[?sic: or -eus]) was used fresh as a vegetable ... <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,102</i>)

>  <i>PUHA OR SOW THISTLE </i>...throw out stalks and branches, clea ean thoroughly ... squeezing out the bitter milky juice, cook with dripping and meat ... When puha becomes popular, it will be like the once despised potato, a national boon. <i>(1908,Souvenir of All Nations Fair,25</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> ... and all was ready for the contents of the haangi - pork, puha, and potatoes; and an appetite stating its case with great fluency <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,177</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> When eaten, the whole package is placed before the diner, who eats the steamed fern-leaves and <i>puwha</i> as well as the fish. <i>(1940,Fishes NZ,20</i>)

>  <b>BEAGLEHOLE</b> <i>puha</i> a wild green vegetable, the sow thistle <i>Ibid.</i> 97 By vegetables, the Maori ... tends to think of wild turnip-tops, wild water-cress, or puha (known to pakehas as the sow-thistle weed or rauriki) boiled up with meat and potatoes ... <i>(1946,Some Modern Maoris,xxii</i>)

>  <b>ZIMMERMAN</b> ... almost always there was <i>puha</i>, a green, leafy weed that grows in profusion over the whole countryside, ... <i>(1946,Where the People Sing,66</i>)

>  <i>(Jan 7, 1953,Otago Star</i>) [NOTES: [I.W.T. Munro] [see <i>Rauriki</i> for long quot. and description.]]

>  <b>MASON</b> AROHA: Here are corn cobs and tomatoes, And some <i>puha</i> from the orchard. <i>(1963,Pohutukawa Tree,20</i>)

>  <b>BRATHWAITE</b> PUHA, PUWHA Green vegetables. <i>(1961,Evil Day,381</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i> puha</i>: A native (Maori) SPINACH. Very tasty with a white sauce. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,83</i>)

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> Demons were also exorcised by taking a piece of one of the plants of the generic group `Puwha' or `Puha' and a dead ember from the fire. <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,16</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> puha <i>sowthistle; (used as a vegetable) </i> <i>(1982,Selected Poems,189</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> Then one night I added [to the soup stock] two silverbelly eels. After that, more water, a pigeon, cresses, puwha, more potato, o the soup grew. <i>Ibid.</i> 466 110 Puha/Puwha = edible weed <i>(1983,Bone People, The,366</i>)

>  <i>puha n. NZ</i> sow thistle, cooked and eaten as a vegetable by Maoris, rauriki. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,611</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 392]]

>  Then, I'd roll out my threadbare Whaariki/ - to help you remember to take your boots/ off - spread an old newspaper on the floor/ and on it place a steaming pot of puha,/ kamo-kamo, riwai, brisket-on-the-bone and/ dumplings what we call: doughboys. <i>(Sep 7, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),12</i>) [NOTES: (Hone Tuwhare)]



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> prickly-leaved puha

>  <b>BURTON</b> The smooth-leaved puha rauriki (<i>Sonchus oleraceus</i>) is the most favoured for eating, though the slight bitterness of the prickly-leaved <i>S.asper</i> (puha tiotio) is not unpleasant. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,7</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> smooth-leaved puha

>  <b>BURTON</b> The smooth-leaved puha rauriki (<i>Sonchus oleraceus</i>) is the most favoured for eating, though the slight bitterness of the prickly-leaved <i>S.asper</i> (puha tiotio) is not unpleasant. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,7</i>) [NOTES: [Burton is surely mistaken in name here]]

<b>4</b> <i>n.</i> a native puha; the others introduced

These leaves could be stamped and sent through the post-office a few years ago, but the practice is now interdicted. <i>(1926,Travel in NZ,Vol II South Island/ 169</i>)

### puheretaiko
 
 <i>n.</i> also called senecio a New Zealand tree daisy

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Puheritaiko, <i>Lyall. Senecio rotundifolius. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)

>  The traveller who visits Stewart Island in December or January will have his attention arrested by the blaze of crimson presented by the rata (<i>Metrosideros lucida</i>), often flecked with the pale racemes of the kamahi (<i>Weinmannia racemosa</i>), the deep green leaves of the puheretaiko and the tupari (<i>Senecio rotundifolius</i> and <i>Olearia colensoi</i>), and the grass-like leaves of the inaka (<i>Drachophyllum longifolium</i>), all of which form a fringe of greater or less breadth at the water's edge, and (except the puheretaiko) often expand into large masses ascending to the crests of the hills. <i>(1884,TrNZI,17/216</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk)]

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> Where the coastal scrub of Stewart Island is densest, it has received the name of `mutton-bird scrub'. This consists largely of the puheritaiko, a very fine shrubby groundsel (<i>Senecio rotundifolius</i>) ... <i>(1910,NZ Plants,76</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> ... they creep over the thinly scatterd flat, fresh fallen <i>Puheretaiko</i> leaves [on the Snares island]. <i>(1936,NZ Naturalist,149</i>)

>  <b>HOWARD</b> Along the coastal fringes [of Stewart Island] it consists of a characteristic scrub known locally as `Mutton-bird scrub', the principal constituents ... being Puheretaiko (<i>Senecio Rotundifolius</i>), Tete-a-weka (<i>Olearia Angustifolia</i>) and Daisy Tree (<i>Olearia Colensoi</i>). <i>(1940,Rakiura,xvii</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Senecio reinoldii</i> MUTTONBIRD SCRUB ... <i>Puuheretaaiko</i> ... Muttonwood ... Shrub or small tree. Leaves leathery ... <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,102</i>)

>  S. rotundifollius [sic] can be seen under cultivation in many parts of the town, its Maori name being "puheritaiko." <i>(Feb 12, 1902,Otago Witness,10</i>)

>  Puheritaiko Senecio rotundifolius, Hook. f. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/45</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

 <i>n.</i> virgin, eligible young woman

 <i>n.</i> a typographical error, or confusion of POE and TUI. (cf TOI)

>  The Kaka or Southern Nestor, the Kakarika, the Pui or New Zealand mocking bird, ... makomako. <i>(1841,NZ Journal,2/31/82</i>) [NOTES: (Dr. Dieffenbach)]



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a bubbling mud pool. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>FEARON</b> Mention must also be made of the 'puis' which caused problems for roadmen here in the early days. The puis were bubbling mud pools situated on the hillside past Kummerstein. <i>(1980,Te Wharau,212</i>)

### puia
 
 <i>n.</i> a geyser; a boiling spring

>  [From the Auckland Provincial <i>Gazette</i>.] .. The principal "puia" at Tokanu is called Pirori, an intermittent fountain, whose column of boiling water, of two feet in diameter, sometimes reaches a height of more than 40 feet. <i>(Aug 3, 1859,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,1</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> Hochstetter divides the steam jets into three kinds: 1. <i>Puias</i>, which are geysers, continuously or intermittently active. 2. <i>Ngawas</i>, which are inactive <i>Puias</i> - emitting steam, but not throwing up columns of water. 3. <i>Waiarikis</i>, which mean any sort of cistern of hot water suitable for bathing. <i>(1878,Forty Years in NZ,11</i>)

>  The word Puia is especially used in the Taupo country, to designate the intermittent, geyser-like fountains of Tokanu ... and of Whakarewarewa on Lake Rotorua. Puia has ... the more general meaning of crater or volcano, and is applied to active as well as extinct volcanoes. ... Ngawhas [is] a term specially used for non-intermittent springs, for the solfataras and sulphurous hot-springs on the Rotomahana, Rotorua, and Rotoiti. Finally, the springs suited to bathing purposes, the water of which never reaches the boiling point, and all naturally warm baths are called `Waiariki,' corresponding to the Laugar of Iceland. <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/171</i>)

>  In her passage underground she set fire to the world below, hence the hot springs, puias, fumaroles ... in the line of route. <i>(1903,TrNZI,35/172</i>)

>  ... strange to say, several large masses of waterworn gravel cemented by puia-deposit into a compact rock are to be seen a short distance below the bridge at Taupo. <i>(1905,TrNZI,37/459</i>) [NOTES: (Fletcher quoted by Hill)]

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> About three miles from this place I saw masses of white vapours rising in jets, and the natives told us that they were caused by the hot-springs (puhia). <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/327</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> I was told these were images of two ... men who some years ago were surprized by a hostile party in their own houses, and were cooked in one of these puias to form a repast for their savage murderers. <i>(Mar 1850,Journal,7/81</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> We caught a pig ... which is to be cooked in the puia. <i>(Feb 6, 1853,Journal,8/74</i>)

>  [see quot <i>ngawha</i>] <i>(1911,TrNZI,43/278</i>) [NOTES: (Hill)]

>  `'Tell you what, Jim. We'll get up in the cool of the dawn, and have a dip in O'Rorke's <i>puia</i>. <i>(Dec 1911,Triad, The,22</i>) [NOTES: 1911 Dec 11 A.A.Grace (The Fate of Morland) in The Triad 22]

>  The rheumaticky greybeard was bending over his own little mud steam-hole, a boxed-in `puia' covered with a sackin which his pot of potatoes was boiling. <i>(Sep 10, 1920,Quick March,47</i>)

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> ... during the time I was in New Zealand practically none but Maoris enjoyed the salutary effects of the puias. <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,298,1933 ed.</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a young girl [ORIGIN: Maori, glossed as 'pussy' in Ryan]

 ([puk]) <i>n.</i> puku; stomach

### puka
 
 <i>n.</i> a spade; a cabbage

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> P|uka, <i>s</i>. A spade; cabbage. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,196</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BAKER</b> ... a familiarity, at once disturbing and comforting, brought into focus ponga ferns and a large-leaf puka. <i>(2000,Kokopu Dreams,32</i>)

>  Some things still get nipped - I used to have a puka (<i>Meryta sinclairii</i>) in a tub, but a minus 6C frost got that. <i>(Aug 8, 2004,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),47</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> Puka ... Kopakopa <i>(1878,Forty Years in NZ,503</i>)

>  <i>puka</i>, an ornamental tree (Meryta Sinclairii), paddle-leaf. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/157</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i>An account of the</i> PUKA (Meryta Sinclairii, <i>Seem</i>.) By T. KIRK. <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/100</i>)

>  <i>Pu&#769;.ka</i> (pu&#769;.k&adot.), <i>n</i>. [Maori.]<i> 1. (Bot.)</i> A New Zealand tree <i>(Meryta Sinclairii)</i>, of the order <i>Araliaceae</i>; - called also <i>broadleaf. [Local, North Island of N. Z.] 2. (Bot.)</i> Either of two species of New Zealand trees <i>Griselina lucida</i> and <i>G. littoralis</i>) of the order <i>Corneae. G. littoralis</i> is usually called <i>broadleaf</i> by the settlers.<i> [Local, N. Z.] </i> <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2029</i>)

>  In his own garden, Sanson has planted cordyline banksii and australis (cabbage trees), astelia, leptocarpus similis (oioi), scleranthus (native mounding moss), libertia, and puka with a karaka hedge. <i>(Jul 6, 2000,Evening Post,15</i>)

>  The puka was another rare shrub, growing naturally only on the Hen and Chickens Islands, the Three Kings, and at one or two points on the North Auckland coast. <i>(Jan 26, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,19</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> The big leaves of the puka tree by the cowshed turned their backs to the westand rattled in discomfort. <i>(1969,Open Country Calling,79</i>)

>  [Caption] At last: Alistair Turnbull with.. Three Kings puka berries that have taken more than a year to ripen... <i>(Nov 1, 2003,Dominion Post,E13</i>)

>  Over the back of the principal mirrors.. are six panels of totara knot, moki, pukaki, and figwood, studded with moki buttons. <i>(Oct 4, 1897,Auckland Weekly News,7</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> an informal shortening of <i>hapuka</i>, hapuku

>  [species name] hapuku .. [scientific name] <i>Polyprion oxygeneios</i> .. [other name(s)] groper, puka <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> broadleaf

>  The English common name -- shining broadleaf -- derives from the puka's bright green leaves, which are thick with a glossy sheen. ... Puka often starts life with other epiphytes, such as colospermum or astelia, high up in the crown of a mature rimu, Pukatea or rata. <i>(Jun 25, 2005,Dominion Post,E13</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> The other native species [of <i>Griselinia</i>] is a handsome large-leaved tree, <i>G. lucida</i> or puka, which occurs throughout lowland forest ... <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,1/252</i>)

>  [?genus] [Native name] Puka .. [Settlers' name] Broadleaf .. [Family] Corneae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,122</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 122]

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> [<i>Griselinia lucida, Forst.] Puka. </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,239</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Griselinia lucida (The Shining Broadleaf)</i> ... (Native name <i>Puka</i>. A name given to any broad-leaved tree). <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,316</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> paddle leaf

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Puka, <i>Col[enso]. Polygonum australe</i> and <i>Meryta Sinclairii</i>. <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>M[eryta] Sinclairii, Seem. Puka. </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,232</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> Very interesting, too, must be the Three Kings where Mr. T.F. Cheeseman found abundance of that magnificent tree, supposed to be almost extinct, the puka (<i>Meryta Sinclairii<i>). <i>(1910,NZ Plants,83</i>)

>  Another notable thing about these off-shore islands the Chickens ... is the fact that they are the original habitat of that gigantic broadleaf the puka, ... meryta sinclairii ... <i>(Sep 11, 1922,Quick March,15</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> The huge leafed <i>Puka</i> ... was the most conspicuous small tree of [the Hen and Chicken group] island. <i>(1936,NZ Naturalist,154</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Meryta Sinclairii (The Puka) </i>... A handsome tree, from 12ft.- 24ft. in height. <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,331</i>)

>  <i>puka</i>, an ornamental tree (Meryta Sinclairii), paddle-leaf. <i>(Jun 1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/157</i>) [NOTES: J.C.Andersen]

>  <b>MARTIN</b> 6. Puka (<i>Meryta sinclairii<i>) outlying islands [north NI] only. <i>(1929,Flora NZ,162,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <i>(1951,Post-Primary School Bulletin,v/12/276</i>) [NOTES: [check quot]]

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Meryta sinclairii<i>(Hook.f.) Seem. Maori name: <i>Puka</i> ... This handsome tree grows on small islands off the coast ... It has been planted widely as an ornamental. <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,37</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Elingamita johnsoni</i>

>  For more than a year now, Alastair has been watching berries slowly - extremely slowly - ripen on a rare tree, the Three Knights {sic] puka, <i>Elingamita johnsoni</i>. This glossy-leaved tree resembling a small karaka is on the endangered list.... [Caption] At last: Alistair Turnbull with.. Three Kings puka berries that have taken more than a year to ripen... <i>(Nov 1, 2003,Dominion Post,E13</i>)

### pukaea
 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori trumpet

>  [Caption] Dion Peita plays the pukaea, a Maori musical instrument that will be used to usher in Matariki at Te Papa. <i>(May 27, 2004,Wellington Contact,1</i>)

>  It's about passing on knowledge. ... Knowledge that pukaea is for war, koauau for songs of love and putatara to call our tupuna. <i>(2008,Mana,85/15</i>)

>  On Sunday, he will play the koauau - an end-blown flute - the putorino - an instrument that is both flute and trumpet at the same time - the pukaea - a "lusty and spectacular" six-foot long trumpet - and the nguru - a tiny wooden flute played with the nose. <i>(Sep 24, 2004,Dominion Post,B10</i>)

### pukaha
 
 <i>n.</i> a cloak

>  <b>LEDYARD</b> ... they add what they call the Bugabuga or the Toga, and sometimes both. The Bugabuga is a very coarse covering made with little trouble, and affords a good shelter from cold or wet weather : The Toga is different and is equally calculated for use and elegance : The Bugabuga is formed round and converging to the top where there is an apperture just sufficient to admit the head to pass through, and when on, covers the body as low as the hip : If it storms, or they have occasion to stop where they have no other shelter they squat down upon their hams, and then the bottom of the Bugabuga reaching the ground, forms a shelter to the whole body (the head excepted) which looks in that situation as if it had been severed from a human form, and fixed upon a hay-cock. <i>(Feb 1777,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1777 Feb in 1783 Ledyard Journal (1963) 14]



 
 <i>n.</i> a tree

>  {Hdg] New wheels for Pukaha team. The team of five undertaking predator-control in the Mount Bruce pukaha forest have the use of a new quad bike... <i>(May 2004,Footnotes,No. 21/9</i>)

### pukahu
 
 <i>n.</i> any matted fibrous formation especially that found under kauri trees. [Maori]

>  The lake floor is formed of a species of pukahu, similar to that found in kauri forests, and termed "bukau" by bushmen. <i>(Sep 18, 1909,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  ... the floor of the kauri bush is covered with a thick coating of vegetable humus (<i>pukahu</i>), which is rendered highly inflammable by a mixture of dead leaves, particles of gum, and scales dropped from the resinous bark. <i>Ibid.</i> 493 Wherever the kauri-trees grow ... the ground is covered with a thick [493] layer of humus (<i>pukahau</i>), composed of vegetable fibre intemingled with fallen leaves, particles of gum, and scales from the resinous bark, the latter helping to form a solid bank, sometimes several feet in height, round the base of the trees. <i>(1899,TrNZI,31/479</i>) [NOTES: Walsh]

>  <b>LEVY</b> Really hot, white-ash burns of the felled forest were rarely secured, [15] which meant that large numbers of the felled trees did not burn, much raw pukahu was left merely blackened, ... <i>Ibid.</i> 94 In high humidity forests, ... pukahu - undecomposed dead forest litter - may accumulate on the forest floor, feet in depth. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,14,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> In early days the volume of the timber would not have warranted the time and expense involved clearing away the accumulated debris, known as pukahu (Maori: masses of rootlets), and usually referred to by the bushmen as `bookau'. <i>Ibid.</i> 208 The platform enabled a man to go from side to side, in order to pour <i>pukahu </i>down the face of the dam as a preventive of leaks in the planking. This pukahu (bookau to the bushman) was the matted, fibrous material which gathered at the foot of large Kauris. <i>(1953,Story of the Kauri, The,84</i>)

>  PUKAU Short for <i>pukahukau</i>. The mound at the base of mature kauri trees. syn. CONE <i>(1952,NZ Forest Glossary</i>)

>  Stumps can vary in height up to 3.2 metres above ground level ... When the stumps are covered by a pukahu - a mound of shed bark and other forestlitter - it is necessary to dig down to find the stump height. <i>(Aug 20, 1975,Evening Post,32/1-3</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to stop leaks in a dam with pukahu [(see 1 above, 1953 p.208)]

>  <b>REED</b> Some of the men were sent to pukahu it [the dam]. <i>(1953,Story of the Kauri, The,219 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>REED</b> `I have known these bookau fires to stay alight through fairly heavy falls of rain, and after a few fine days to get going again'. <i>(1953,Story of the Kauri, The,272</i>) [NOTES: (Thompson)]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [Ma.] a matted fibrous material that accumulates on a forest floor.

>  <b>LEVY</b> Good burns were hard to obtain and most burns were not hot enough to consume all timber, so that much pukahu was left on the forest floor. <i>(1970,Grasslands of NZ,48</i>)

### pukakaho
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> They dwelt in whares [11] (huts) built in the traditional shape of an inverted V, roofed with grass, and lined inside with the reeds of the toetoe or New Zealand pampas grass. The orthodox Maori name for these reeds is pukakaho, but the white men called them `bukaka'. <i>Ibid.</i> 44 A potato was hollowed and a bukaka stem was inserted and the [pipe] owner puffed away. <i>Ibid.</i> 139-140 One spot was marked by a lot of bukakas [140] (toitois) and when they drained it they found the skeleton of a saddled horse. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,10</i>)

### pukaki
 
 <i>n.</i> . [a mainly South island mainly pl form of PUKEKO 2]

>  The total bag consists of 54 grey ducks, seven puk[a]ki and two swans. <i>(Jun 17, 1908,Otago Witness,46</i>)

>  "Thanks, sonny, but I'll push on to my lonely cot by the mossy rill and Dan's stewed pukaki." <i>(Dec 23, 1903,Otago Witness,2</i>)

>  Mrs M'Killop had already received so many compliments and remmembrances from him, varying from recipes for potting pukaki to panaceas for rheumatism, that her faithful Celtic heart was almost resigned to the probable loss of her mistress and nursling. <i>(Dec 23, 1903,Otago Witness,2</i>)

>  <b>HART</b> At the back ... was a large raupo swamp, which was a rare resort for wild ducks and pukaki. <i>(1886,Stray Leaves,13</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> Pukeko (Pukaki or Swamp Hen) - Fair number, holding their own. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,241,1954 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HART</b> ... a rare present for wild ducks and pukaki <i>(1886,Stray Leaves,13,1889 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> My nerves are wild: a pukaki screaming. <i>(1972,High Country Anthology,216</i>) [NOTES: 1889 Jessie McKay The Spirit of Rangatira (The Boundary Dog's Complaint) in 1972 McLeod High Country Anthology 216]

>  <b>WILLIAMS & REEVES</b> And the swamp hen [fn] on the rail/ Pertly tilts her scanty tail,/ [fn] `swamp-hen' - The `pukaki', it wants jugging ... <i>(1889,Colonial Couplets,26</i>)

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> And the swamp hen [fn 3] on the rail/ Pertly tilts her scanty tail,/ [20] [fn 3] The `pukaki', it wants jugging. <i>(1950,Farm & Station Verse,19</i>) [NOTES: 1889 `An Idyll of The Canterbury Plains Twenty Years Ago' in 1950 Woodhouse Farm and Station Verse 19, 20]

>  <b>SEA RLE</b> I may shoot pukakis <i>(1890,Angela,47</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> Pukaki also called swamp-hen or pukeko <i>(1890,Far South Fancies,154</i>)

>  Two <i>pukaki</i> [sic] flew across their path <i>(Jun 11, 1896,Otago Witness,51</i>) [NOTES: (Morris)]

>  <b>ACLAND</b> pukaki -->a mainly South island mainly pl form of PUKEKO <i>(1933,Early Canterbury Runs,175</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> There were sandpipers and waders along the Molyneux and pukaki and fern birds were to be found in the swamps. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,120</i>)

>  <b>STEVENSON</b> Notornis (named Notornis mantelli after Mantell) resembled the pukaki, but was a much heavier bird. <i>(1947,Maori and Pakeha in North Otago</i>)

>  <b>MACFARLANE</b> Pukaki, the swamp hen, treats man rather as a joke and helps himself to whatever he wants. <i>(1954,Te Raka,58</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> The `pukaki' or swamp hen, with its shrill cry and flashing white down, [sic] under its short tail was an instinctive understanding of danger. <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,46</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> PUKEKO <i>Porphyrio melanotus Other names:</i> Swamp-hen, Pukaki, Pook. ... A variety of sounds emerges from a raupo-swamp which holds a colony of Pukekos: a drawn-out, almost booming <i>poo-koo-koo-koo ... </i> <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,108</i>)

>  <b>HOOPER</b> A bird or two rustled somewhere, a pukaki screeched across the stream, but otherwise it was very still. <i>(1981,Goat Paddock,17</i>)

>  <i>Pukeko Porphyrio porphyrio melanotus </i>(TEMMINCK, 1820) ... OTHER NAMES: </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,171</i>)

>  <i>STEWED PUKAKI</i> stew or jug like wild duck or hare <i>(1910,Colonial Everyday Cookery,99</i>)

>  <i>STEWED PUKAKI </i>... cut into pieces ... lay in stewpan ... bacon ... pukaki ... onion ... thyme ... salt and pepper ... water ... Cook ... slowly ... strain gravy ... put gravy with ... salt, cayenne, Harvey's sauce ... lemon ... pour over the pukaki ... <i>(1936,Recipe Book [St Mary's],33</i>)

>  `Stewed pukaki'. <i>(1953,Whitcombe's Cookery Book,1953 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> The pukaki or swamp-turkey <i>(1874,Colonial Experiences,85</i>) [NOTES: (Otago)]

### pukako
 
 <i>n.</i> [no citations] (See [pukeko](pop-pyo.md#pukeko))

### pukana
 
 <i>v.</i> poke out the tongue and roll the eyes

>  He said he would continue to pukana. <i>(Oct 25, 2005,NZ Herald,A3</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> rolling of the eyes and poking out of the tongue (as in the haka) [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>IHIMAERA & LONG</b> Everyone was clapping and cheering for Uncle Hirini and my cousin Hoani who kept jumping from the truck to the road, patterning with their taiaha, springing on their toes and doing the pukana, making high piping noises with their voices. <i>(1982,Into the World of Light,205</i>)

>  Thompson allegedly walked over to another diner and performed a pukana - the part of the haka that involves rolling of the eyes, poking of the tongue, and thrusting the upper body towards an opponent. <i>(Dec 9, 2000,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  [Noel Harris] was so chuffed at having won the race for the first time in his 35 years in the saddle that he let rip the Maori pukana which only last month landed fellow rider Michael Walker in trouble with racing officials. <i>(Dec 4, 2005,Dominion Post,B12</i>)

>  As for her reaction to the pukana, she should learn what it means instead of accusing someone clever enough to do one of threatening her. [sic] <i>(May 16, 2006,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  The women are smiling and doing the pukana, the men are looking suitably fierce. <i>(Dec 1996,Mana,No. 11 : 22</i>)

>  While the controversial 22-year-old did not deliver a pukana (poking out tongue and rolling eyes) at yesterday's Te Rapa meeting, he said he would carry on but "only at the big races". ... Victory: Michael Walker celebrates his winning ways with a pukana. ... Walker earned a warning from officials for a pukana delivered after riding La Sizeranne to victory in the Hawkeye Stoney Bridge Stakes at Hawera. <i>(Oct 25, 2005,NZ Herald,A3</i>)

>  Are not Wellington's racing stipendary stewards guilty of a heavy dose of over-correctness in issuing a warning to Michael Walker for delivering a pukana as he passed the post first at a recent meeting? <i>(Oct 27, 2005,NZ Herald,A12</i>)

>  Walker's pukana (poking out his tongue and rolling his eyes) is exactly the sort of colour the sport needs as it seeks to attract people back to the nation's racecourses. <i>(Oct 26, 2005,NZ Herald,A14</i>)

>  "It wasn't until I saw the Hindu dancer Tara do the pukana with ther eyes - then, straight away, I saw behind her an old woman.. doing the same thing." <i>(Jan 21, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),75</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a manner of tying a tie to resemble the facial pukana

>  Will the "Pukana" tie knot as modelled by Hone Harawira take the fashion world by storm? <i>(Jan 22, 2006,Sunday Star Times,A10</i>)

>  "I'm happy to wear the pukana but I'm even happier just to be me," he said. <i>(Dec 12, 2005,Dominion Post,A2</i>)

### pukana-style
 
 <i>adv.</i> like a tongue poking out (See [pukana](pop-pyo.md#pukana))

>  Mr Harawira said he had worn his tie pukana-style when he was at St Stephen's Maori boys school in Auckland in 1970. <i>(Dec 12, 2005,Dominion Post,A2</i>)

### pukanui
 
 <i>n.</i> puka, <i>Meryta sinclairii</i> [nf Williams]

>  Pukanui, Meryta Snclairii[sic] (Paddle leaf)was claimed to both "stem blood flow in women" and procure abortions (though not, presumably, by Mother Aubert) and in bowel and urinary treatments. <i>(Aug 24, 2002,Dominion Post,F16</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>MERYTA SINCLAIRII, puka, pukanui. Meryta </i>is a genus of the south Pacific. Its southernmost occurrence is in New Zealand, where its natural distribution is limited to two groups of islands, the Hen and Chickens, and the Three Kings. ... [<i>M.sinclairii</i>] is widely cultivated as an ornamental tree ...in warmer parts of the country. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,94</i>)

>  <b>DAWSON</b> Pukanui (<i>Meryta sinclairii</i>): a small tree with very large simple leaves which is also found on the Hen and Chicken Islands off the Northland east coast. <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,110</i>)

>  Another to protect from the family florist is the huge-leafed Meryta sinclairii, Pukanui. <i>(Sep 15, 2001,Dominion Weekend Magazine,10</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> formerly <i>Pisonia brunoniana</i>, parapara

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>PISONIA BRUNONIANA.</i> Parapara is a small tree of quick, rather soft growth carrying leaves up to 40cm long, of dark glossy green ... Nodular ridges down the length of the `fruit' ... secrete a glutinous substance ... Most spectacular is the ability of this tarry substance to hold small birds. ... anyone who has seen a waxeye struggling to free itself from amongst a cluster of these fruits can easily understand why parapara has earned the name `bird-catching tree'. ... Where it grows with pukanui (<i>Meryta sinclairii</i>) two of our largest-leaved trees come together. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,60</i>)

### pukapuka
 
 <i>n.</i> something written, a book; a cheque; the Treaty Williams Appendix <i>Pukapuka</i>,a book.

>  Potato baskets of matting made of flax, mats, fishing nets, an iron pot, and the "Puka-puka," or Testament and prayer book, were the principle furniture. <i>(Oct 1, 1842,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,120</i>)

>  Nikorima came into a country store and borrowed pen, ink, pukapuka, and kawha (envelope) from the storekeeper. <i>(May 2, 1901,Grey River Argus,4</i>)

>  Mr.Thompson said "I represent the Queen. I have got the Queen's puka-puka." <i>(Dec 23, 1843,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,4</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> ... it was agreed that a canoe ... [180] should be lent to us, for the amount of a blanket and some tobacco, for which I instantly wrote a check (pukapuka) [a's-acute], on the spot, payable in Hokianga. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/179</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> [Notes of a meeting at Kaitai] <i>Matieu</i>. ... I never heard the meaning of the booka-booka (i.e. the treaty) before a different explanation was given me by those who heard it read. <i>(1840,Journal,2/197</i>)

>  <b>SARAH MATHEW</b> After some further discussion ... the three principal chiefs signed the agreement or `puka-puka', as they call all writings, which was also signed by the Police Magistrate and one or two other Government officers. <i>(Sep 18, 1840,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Sep 18 Sarah Mathew Journal in 1940 Founding of NZ 192]

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Pukapuka - book, paper, a tree so called, the lungs <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/380</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> ... he [the old tohunga recently converted] almost invariably kept his puka puka (hymn and prayer books) upside down when he pretended to sing his psalms or read the service, ... <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/141</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> ... others [sc. Maoris] again said that they had turned `Mitanari', ... and that the white Mitanaris said the `Pukapuka' would be strong against the heathen. <i>(Feb 20, 1841,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1841 Feb 20 E.J.Wakefield Journal in 1842 Heaphy NZ 58]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> <i>Pukapuka</i> - the word applied either to paper or books - at first sight appears to be derived from our English word <i>book</i>; it is not so, but from the large white leaf of the Pukapuka Rangiora (Brachyglottis repanda), to which the natives likened paper when they first saw it. They said, `He rau pukapuka tenei!' `Oh! this is a leaf of the Pukapuka.' The Greeks derived their [<i>biblos</i>] book, and the Latins their <i>Liber,</i> book, in a similar way, from the inner bark of the Linden or Teil tree, and the Egyptians theirs from the leaf of the Papyrus, whence comes our word paper. <i>(1868,Past and Present of NZ,16 (fn)</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> rangiora, <i>Brachyglottis repanda</i>

>  You may recall the recent TV commercial featuring a boy floating pukapuka leaves down a river. <i>(Feb 3, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),25</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> There are many other woods of much service to Europeans, differing in quality, among others: the ... 39 <i>Kaikamoko</i> - 40 <i>Pukapuka</i>... - 45 <i>Parekireki,</i> &c. Of all these woods there are abundant quantities. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/400</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Senecio (Brachyglottis) <i>Forsteri</i>, Hook. fil. ... Nat. name, `Puka Puka', <i>Colenso.</i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 148</i>)

>  <b>LUSH</b> ... succeeded in getting a number of Pohutukawas, Pukapukas, and several handsome evergreens <i>(Jun 9, 1852,Auckland Journals,1171,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Pukapuka, <i>Col[enso]. Brachyglottis repanda. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)

>  Puka puka or Rangiora. <i>(1868,TrNZI,36</i>) [NOTES: Botany of North Island.]

>  Pukapuka ... is listed with koareare, but the translator is not sure of its exact nature in Canterbury, but it is a kind of bread made from the pollen of raupo in Southland, and presumably the same further north. <i>(1880,South Island Maori Lists</i>) [NOTES: c1880 South Island Maori Lists `Wahi Mahika Kai' in 1945 Beattie Maori Place-names of Canterbury 65]

>  The few clumps of trees consist of pohutukawa, mapou, manuka, rewarewa, akeake, whau or corkwood, pukapuka, and a few puriri, which, however, is of little value, being very scattered and ruined by fire. <i>(1884,TrNZI,17/419</i>) [NOTES: (Gold-Smith)]

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>B[rachyglottis] repanda, Forst. ... Pukapuka; Wharangi-tawhito. </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,367</i>)

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Brachyglottis repanda</i> J.R. & G. Forst. Maori names: <i>Rangiora, pukapuka, wharangi </i> <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,40</i>)

>  Of our shrubs and smaller timber trees, several are of strikingly beautiful growth ... (e.g.) the Houhere, <i>Hoheria populnea </i>...; the Pukapuka, <i>Brachyglottis repanda</i>;... the Karo, <i>Pittosporum crassifolium</i>; and the truly evergreen Ngaio, <i>Myoporum laetum</i> ... <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/iii/5</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  The puka-puka with its hoary leaves. <i>(Dec 1900,Auckland Weekly News,Xmas No. : 6</i>)

>  He carried piles of pukapuka leaves. <i>(Dec 14, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,17</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [Ma.] the nectar-bearing flowers of raupo, valued by early apiarists.

>  There are [sic] a number of native trees in blossom at the same time as pukapuka, including puriri, tea-tree, 'bush-lawyer' clematis, cabbage-tree, mingi and ohoho. Of these, 'bush lawyer,' cabbage-tree and ohoho bloom only at the same time as pukapuka and all three yield more nectar than pukapuka does. [pungapunga] <i>(Jul 1911,NZ Farmer,765</i>)

### pukariao
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Pukariao, <i>Geolog. Surv. Brachyglottis repanda</i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [pakaru [puckeroo]](../P#pakaru-[puckeroo]))

 <i>n.</i> (See [pakaru [puckeroo]](../P#pakaru-[puckeroo]))

### pukatea
 
 <i>n.</i> a large native tree growing in swampy areas, valued for boat building; the tree and its wood (=Hooker f. <i>Atherosperma</i>) [[1919 Jul 29] Ver. (WB).= Vernacular names used by settlers supplied by W. Best, Otaki (And) <i>Laurelia novae zelandiae [Buckerteer] </i>]

>  Mr A. Mras, Cabinetmaker, shows a handsome wardrobe, with panel glass door, and inlaid with pukatea, with honeysuckle facings. <i>(Nov 20, 1901,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

>  <b>REEVES</b> Even in the North Island the average bushman will speak of the pukatea tree as "bucketeer", and not to call the poroporo shrub a "bull-a-bull" would be considered affectation. <i>(1898,Long White Cloud, The,404</i>)

>  The pukatea is easily known by its roots forming a pedestal for the tree above the ground. Its leaves are thick, serrated, and of a glossy green; the wood is said to be close-grained, and to resemble a pine. <i>(Sep 12, 1840,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,3</i>)

>  <i>(Sep 14, 1878,Taranaki Herald,2</i>) [NOTES: It is considered a good sign in this province if fuschia, whitewood, and pukatea (often called buckateer) abound.]

>  The bush is composed of pukatae and tawa, and is overgrown with kicke. <i>(May 1, 1880,NZ Mail,00</i>) [NOTES: Check page]

>  Pukatea, 12s. per 100 feet. <i>(May 19, 1866,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  WOOD BROTHERS are now prepared to deliver in Foxton, TAWA, RATA, and PUKATEA, in 2 and 4 feet lengths. <i>(Feb 26, 1891,Manawatu Herald,3</i>)

>  The kahikatea stands next in abundance, and is closely approached by the kamai, while the miro, matai, totara, Hall's totara, are generally distributed; but the northern rata and pukatea scarcely occur south of Greymouth. <i>(1894,NZ Official Yearbook,330</i>) [NOTES: (T. Kirk)]

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> [<i>Laurelia novae-zealandiae, A.Cunn.] Pukatea. </i>... The wood is pale-brown ... <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,601</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Laurelia novae-zelandiae (The Pukatea).</i> One of the loftiest of New Zealand forest trees, sometimes reaching the height of 150 ft. ... . Maori name <i>Pukatea</i>[, often misnamed <i>Bukatea</i>]. <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,174,1940 p. 180 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BUICK</b> Apparatus for lighting fire by circular motion, made of pukatea (<i>Atherosperma novae-zealandiae</i>) .. 2 <i>(1937,Moa-Hunters of NZ,85</i>)

>  <i>pukatea</i>: large tree. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,175</i>)

>  <i>(May 21, 1954,Listener (New Zealand Listener),22</i>)

>  Bushmen usually called a puketea, buckyteer; a kowhai, goey; and a kaponga, bunger. <i>(Aug 12, 1957,Dominion,[corresp. col.]</i>)

>  <i>pukatea n</i> -s [Maori] : a lofty New Zealand forest tree (<i>Laurelia novae-zelandiae</i>) of the family Monimiaceae having a light-colored wood that is soft but strong <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1839</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> <i>pukatea .. .. pukatea .. .. Laurelia novae- zelandiae </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <i>pukatea n.</i> tall NZ timber tree of laurel family, with pale wood. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <i>pukatea</i>, a tree (Laurelia novae zealandiae), a tall tree with deeply-buttressed trunk. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/157</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  Pukatea. Griselinia littoralis, Raoul. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/iii/43</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  ... he might find the Pukatea (<i>Atherosperma Novae Zelandiae</i>) with its bright green foliage ... considerable quantities of Pukatea (<i>Antherosperma Novae Zelandiae</i>) occur ... <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/iii/10</i>) [NOTES: (Monro)]

>  The largest trees on the range are tawhero (<i>Weinmannia sylvicola</i>) and pukatea (<i>Laurelia novae-zelandiae.</i>) <i>(1889,TrNZI,21/38</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> If he [a bushfelling contractor] struck ill-luck, bad weather, puketia swamp or a thoughtless owner ... he might well lose ... money on the contract. <i>(1955,Land of My Children,5</i>)

>  The bark of the forest tree <i>pukatea (Laurelia)</i> has a reputation as a backblocks remedy for toothache and neuralgia. <i>(Jun 1936,NZ Railways Magazine,29</i>)

>  <b>BROOKES</b> ... whitewood, fern trees, bush karaka ... puketea. <i>(1892,Frontier Life,20</i>)

>  Pukatea .. Laurelia Novae-Zelandiae .. Timber a pale brown colour, soft and light; ... <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,13</i>)

>  <b>BELL</b> Thus, the three trees still stood, the two tawas resting on the pukatea at different angles, ... <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,94</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>(1898,Austral English</i>)

>  ... buccateer <i>(1841,NZ Journal,2/43/213</i>)

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> The <i>Bukitea</i> is also used for building; it is of a darker colour, and rather a softer nature than [Kahikatea]. <i>(1842,Narrative of a Residence in  Various Parts of NZ,41</i>)

>  The pukatea is another wood much in use for weather boarding . . .; but it is soft and spongy, and absorbs much wet. <i>(1843,Letters from New Plymouth,58,1968 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Nov 27 in 1843 Letters from New Plymouth (1968) 58]

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Another tree common in this part of the valley is the pukatea, and is easily known by its trunk, which rapidly diminishes in diameter from the ground upwards. <i>Ibid.</i> 1/147 Several species [of rata] were common; but the pukatea was the most frequent. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/75</i>)

>  <b>GOLDER</b> Buckateer <i>(1845,Pigeon's Parliament,5</i>)

>  In the [alluvial bush land], which is wooded chiefly with <i>pukateas, kaikateas,</i> and <i>rata</i> trees, and thick underbrush of <i>konine</i> (fuchsia) or <i>roe-roe, kaua kaua </i>(or pepper), &c., entwined with kuriwau or supple-jack, ... <i>(1852,Wellington Gazette</i>) [NOTES: 1852 <i>Wellington Gazette</i> in Earp Hand-Book for Intending Emigrants 230]

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> 16. Pukatea, (<i>Laurus pukatea</i>.) This is a very common tree, growing generally in low, marshy situations, ... <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,143</i>)

>  <b>MACKIE</b> One tree the `pukatea' Laurelia Novae Zealandiae has a singularly shaped butt. <i>(Feb 15, 1853,Traveller under Concern,79,1973 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Laurelia <i>Novae-Zelandiae</i>, Cunn. ... Nat. name, `Pukatea', <i>Col. </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 218</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Pukatea,<i> Col[enso]. Atherosperma novae-Zelandiae</i> and <i>Griselinia lucida.</i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> <i>Atherosperma novae-zelandiae</i>, Hook. Pukatea.--Height, 150 feet, with buttressed trunk 3-7 feet in diameter; <i>(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,129</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Pukatea ... is a tree of [192] the second largest class. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/191</i>)

>  [Native name] Pukatea .. [Settlers' name] Pukatea .. [Family] Monimiaceae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,121</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 121]



 
 <i>n.</i> broadleaf [[?are H&Hector mistaken:]

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Griselinia <i>lucida</i>, Forst. ... Nat. name, `Poukater', <i>Raoul. </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 98</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Pukatea, <i>Col[enso]. Atherosperma novae-Zelandiae</i> and <i>Griselinia lucida. </i> ...[spg] Poukatea, <i>Raoul. Griselinia littoralis. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> <i>Griselinia littoralis</i>, Raoul. Pukatea, Broadleaf.--An erect and thickly-branched bush-tree, 50-60 feet high; 3-10 feet in diameter. Grows chiefly in the South Island and near the coasts. <i>(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,128</i>)

### pukatea weed
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  PUKA-TEA WEED. - This weed is boiled (roots and all), and applied hot as a poultice to cuts, bruises, or wounds. <i>(1902,Brett's Colonists Guide,744</i>)

### pukateine
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> In 1910 he [Aston] isolated pukateine, the poisonous principle of pukatea bark, ... [ref. 1909 TrNZI 41/56-58] <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,11</i>)

### pukatuola
 
 <i>n.</i> ? =?<i>[punga]/ [?pungu] tuoro </i>

>  <b>MONRO</b> The pukatuola is another wonderful animal of the southward [sc. Otago], told of by the old men. Under a different name he is heard of in the north. A gigantic animal of the lizard species, most dangerous to humanity. A very shrewd man, whom we met to the southward, was of opinion that these hairy men and crocodiles had their origin in the Maories seeing pictures of animals in books belonging to Europeans, and then persuading themselves that they existed in their woods: but I cannot take this view of the case. <i>(1844,Notes of a Journey</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Monro Notes of a Journey (Nelson Examiner July 20 et seq.) in 1898 Hocken Contributions 263]

 <i>n.</i> (See [pukahu](pop-pyo.md#pukahu))

>  The managing director, Mr. G.E. Alderton, said that the oil produced from the pukau of the gum swamps was more valuable than shale oil, giving as its fractions motor spirit, a solvent oil, a turpentine substitute, and paint and varnish oils. <i>(Nov 12, 1919,Grey River Argus,3</i>)
