### pukeko
 
 <i>n.</i> [pukeka-bird; one citation]

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> At this stage of its existence [early pioneer days] New Zealand seemed specially made for lovers; ... with blue waters and mangroves along the rivers ..., and stalking, red-legged pukeka-birds would never tell. <i>(1938,Promenade,44</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a brightly coloured native swamp-hen; the pukeko is called TANGATA TAWHITO `old, ancient, original, primeval' (Williams 409), but it may have introduced itself from Australia <i>Porphyrio melanotus</i>, swamphen; also pook, red-bill, sultana bird, (swamp-)turkey; pukaki is a common mainly South Island mainly pl form; a Maori variant (of Ngati-Pukeko tribe) =pakura Besides having possibly the greatest number of descriptive names of any NZ bird, it has many chiefly oral, anglicised, variants of the original Maori <i>pu[u]keko</i>, based on these alternations: /p/ as [p] or [b] /u:/ as [u:], [ju:], [u], [ju], [?], [j?]; and after [b], [ou], [o] /e/ as [E], [ae] /o/ as [ou], [?], [i]

>  Kaka and pukeko are to be protected game in the Wellington district all this year. <i>(Apr 22, 1909,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  It is notified in a Gazette issued yesterday that the pukeko, teal, grey duck, spoonbill duck, black swan, and wild geese will not be "protected" this season. <i>(Apr 15, 1915,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  <b>WALKER</b> pukakas <i>(1902,Zealandia's Guerdon,52</i>)

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> <i>Pukeko (Poo-kek-ko)</i>: The New Zealand swamp-hen. <i>(1908,Shingle-Short,190</i>)

>  <b>FOSTON</b> In the swamp `pukake' abound. <i>(1911,Bell Bird's Lair,47</i>)

>  <i>Pukeko. A bokakker out of season. </i> <i>(1913,Auckland Capping Book,27</i>)

>  <b>BARLOW</b> He also told me there were great numbers of P&umacr.k&ecaron.ko, or swamp-hens there, and that ... we might be able to have some P&umacr.k&ecaron.ko, shooting. <i>(1888,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,122</i>)

>  PORPHYRIO MELANOTUS, <i>Temm.</i> - Swamp-hen <i>(Pokako)</i>.[sic] <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/99</i>) [NOTES: (Reischek)]

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> [The] native rat still lingers here and there [in the central North Island], although it has disappeared on the coast; the native dog has become a mongrel; ducks, teals, the red-billed porphyrio or pukeko, a large bittern, a tern, two kinds of gulls, cormorants ... are found here. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/362</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> They also killed 2 pukeko's one of which I supped of and found it well flavoured. <i>(Mar 4, 1844,Journal,3/81</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> <i>Pukeko</i> - A species of water-hen, the size of a well-grown capon. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),62</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> ... pukeka ... <i>(Mar 19, 1840,Diary</i>)

>  ... pursuing a few pukekas. <i>(1840,NZ Journal,23/288</i>)

>  The end of the lake abounds with wild duck I also saw a fine Bittern a small white Gull a wader like a Godwit and shot a Pukako. <i>(May 15, 1841,Journal of Ensign Best,311,1966 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> 58. <i>Porphyrio melanotus, Temm</i>. Pukeko of the natives. - <i>Yate, Dieffenbach</i>. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/197</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> <i>Pukeko (Porphyrio melanotus)</i>.- A species of water-hen, the size of a well-grown capon. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,162</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Pukeko - a bird (<i>Porphyrio</i>) <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/380</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> ... pukeko <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,228</i>)

>  <b>POWER</b> There is a splendid Rail in the swamps, called `Pukeko' by the natives; it is as large as an English pheasant, with plumage of splendid mazarine blue, and with a vermillion-coloured beak and topknot. <i>(1849,Sketches in NZ,72</i>)

>  <b>GODLEY</b> ... teal, ducks, and pukakos innumerable <i>(Jun 4, 1850,Letters,170,1951 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Canterbury)]

>  I amused myself with shooting wild duck and Pukeko which are very abundant. <i>(Nov 14, 1854,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/136</i>) [NOTES: (W.R.Bridges)]

>  <b>SMITH</b> The pukekos seem to highly approve of [potatoes], as we heard several in the cultivations ... <i>(Jan 12, 1858,Notes of a Journey</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Jan 12 Smith Notes of a Journey in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 359]

>  Then the blue Pukeko, prince of Rails, often stalked through the raupo ..., or the brown-streaked Bittern ... rose with deliberate flight ... <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/47</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> [Fn] The Sultan-hen of brilliant plumage (<i>Porphyris melanotus</i>), or the Pukeko. <i>(1867,New Zealand (Hochstetter),293</i>)

>  ... wood-hens (<i>weka</i>), and swamp-hens (<i>Pukeko</i>) are plentiful ... <i>(1868,Province of Otago in NZ, The,31</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> PORPHYRIO MELANOTUS. (SWAMP-HEN.) ... <i>Native names</i>.- Pukeko and Pakura*. ... [186] The Swamp-hen is widely distributed over Tasmania, the greater part of the continent of Australia, New Zealand, and the Chatham Islands. *[fn: 1888: Pakura] So called by the Ngatipukeko tribe of Whakatane; ... <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,185,1888 2nd edn 2/79 ed.</i>)

>  <b>EVANS</b> ... pokekhas .... swamp hens <i>(1874,Over the Hills,2</i>)

>  <b>meredith</b> The only birds [at Tolaga Bay] are wild ducks of many varieties, pheasants, and <i>pokeko</i> (bald-coots). <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,134,1935 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> The place was populous with large black birds, called by the Maoris pu-ke-ko. <i>(1884,Our Maoris,114</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> Sultana-birds<i> (Pukeko) ..... F.</i> Rallidae; <i>G.</i> Porphyrio. The `Poule Sultane' of the French, Pollo Sultano,<i> It. </i>Porphyrio Melanotus,<i>Ibid.</i> The New Zealand species has crimson bill; red legs; rich deep blue breast; rest of plumage velvet-black. <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/306</i>)

>  Rail and pukeka were caught by snares set in their sphagnum moss runs, ... Tui and mako (bell birds) were killed by the birder hutching beneath a cone of fern fronds ... <i>(1928,BP Bishop Memoirs,Vol. IX No. 5 pp. 343-382) 362</i>) [NOTES: 1928 Baucke Manuscript in Skinner and Baucke The Morioris]

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> On the 12th I hunted in the neighbouring swamps, where many duck, swamp-hens (<i>pokeko</i>), and swamp-lark were splashing about. <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,174,1933 ed.</i>)

>  <i>pukeko n.</i> a handsome rail that lives in swamps; the swamp hen. [Maori] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,9</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> But here, a little stream meandered under cream-plumed shimmer of huge toi-toi bushes, crimson-billed pukekos with thoughtful expressions waded downstream, and a dart of wings meant riri-riro, the Maori's little bird of laughter, adventuring in the sunlit spaces. <i>(1934,Journalese,109</i>)

>  <i>pukeko</i>: swamp hen. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,175</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> SWAMP HEN OR PUKEKO (<i>Porphyrio melanotus</i>). This is a truly handsome bird over 20 inches in height; <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,82</i>)

>  <i>pukeko</i> also <i>pukeka n</i> -s [Maori] : a handsome blue, black, and white gallinule (<i>Porphyrio melanotus</i>) of New Zealand, Australia, and adjacent islands <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1839</i>)

>  <b>BACON</b> <i>pukeko</i>-The swamp hen; about the size of a domestic fowl, but with longer legs, a red beak, a blue body, black wings and tail, and dark pink feet. <i>(1963,In the Sticks,182</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> PUKEKO <i>Porphyrio melanotus Other names</i>: Swamp-hen, Pukaki, Pook. ... A variety of sounds emerges from a raupo-swamp which holds a colony of Pukekos: a drawn-out, almost booming <i>poo-koo-koo-koo</i> ... <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,108</i>)

>  <i>137. Porphyrio porphyrio melanotus</i> Temminck, 1820 <i>Pukeko</i> Australia, Tasmania and New Zealand ... Some authors use the subspecific name <i>stanleyi</i> Rowley, 1871 for New Zealand birds. <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,1980,42 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> [Quoting an anonymous early poem <i>The Candidate</i>] And can you really make, man, Proper pukeko soup? <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,22</i>)

>  <i>pukeko n</i>. a New Zealand wading bird with bright plumage. [< Maori] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,921</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 611; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 392]]

>  <b>BAXTER</b> pukeko <i>swamp-hen </i> <i>(1982,Selected Poems,189</i>)

>  <i>Pukeko Porphyrio porphyrio melanotus </i>(TEMMINCK, 1820) ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Swamp hen, pakura, puke, pukaki. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,171</i>)

>  <i>pu[u]keko</i>, a swamp-bird (Porphyrio melanotus), the swamp-hen; makes a comical pet, but inquisitive and destructive in the garden. Pukaki, name in the south, not recognized by Williams. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/157</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  Of the boys' funny sayings [in the 1930s] two live on in my memory. ... And if he was asked where he was going, he would answer: `Up the boo-ai in a matchbox hunting pukakers [pukekos] with a long-handled shovel.' <i>(1988,Through the Looking Glass,126</i>) [NOTES: (Meikle)]

>  The swamp hen, or pukaki, has been noticed by some of your writers. <i>(Apr 1, 1924,Otago Witness,71</i>)

>  Farmers left only small pockets of manuka and kanuka, some up to 12 metres tall, and a narrow band of broad-leaved trees - karaka, two large-leaved trees, one kohekohe and one wharangi - in the steep Forest Valley on the island's east side. <i>(Oct 12, 2000,Dominion,9</i>)

>  In a quaint old-world lagoonThe wild pukaki breeds. <i>(Oct 29, 1919,Hawke's Bay Herald,6</i>)

>  The road winds into the countryside past mailboxes, barking dogs, slack-uddered cows, milking sheds, pukeko stalking through long wet grass, beige cows chewing contentedly, kids on sweating ponies, haybarns and still more cows until finally there it is. <i>(Aug 8, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),50</i>)

>  He also sees pukekos. What a brilliant bird! <i>Porphyrio porphyrio melanotus</i>, otherwise known as the purple swamp-hen. <i>(Aug 26, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

>  We tried using pukekos as surrogate mothers for them, but, as they grew up, they thought they were pukekos and ran away from other takahe. <i>(Sep 30, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),47</i>)

>  There are a few art enthusiasts here and there amongst us, but.. their voices have been as the voice of the pukako crying in the wilderness. <i>(Apr 6, 1912,NZ Free Lance,6</i>)

>  That handsome bird, the pukeko, with its brilliant purple neck and breast and crimson beak, is often seen. <i>(Aug 27, 1842,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,3</i>)

>  Before Messrs Thomas Bamber and Morton Jones, J.P.'s, this morning R. E. M. Campbell pleaded guilty to a charge of killing a pukeka out of season. <i>(Mar 16, 1892,Wanganui Herald,3</i>)

>  Next to the plant is a paddock, puggy now in mid-winter, four pukeko pecking desultorily, empty save for the birds. <i>(Sep 2000,North & South,37</i>)

>  Stewed Pukaki. Hang for some time, then skin like a rabbit or hare, and stew or jug like wild duck or hare. <i>(1908,Colonial Everyday Cookery,99</i>)

>  <b>POWER</b> There is a splendid Rail in the swamps, called `Pukeko' by the natives; it is as large as an English pheasant, with plumage of splendid mazarine blue, and with a vermillion-coloured beak and topknot. <i>(1849,Sketches in NZ,72</i>)

>  <b>GRACE</b> "A half-fledged young <i>pukeko</i> like you, presumes to cut me out!" <i>(1914,Tale of a Timber Town,12</i>)

>  The existence of the pukaki in the sanctuary at Lake Hayes was similarly threatened owing to the heavy snowfall. <i>(Aug 6, 1918,Lake Wakatip Mail,4</i>)

>  The existence of the pukaki in the sanctuary at Lake Hayes was similarly threatened owing to the heavy snowfall. <i>(Aug 6, 1918,Lake Wakatip Mail,4</i>)

>  I could hear pukeko shrieking down below in the mudflats... <i>(Jan 11, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),60</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> Ducks flapped and pukeko burst from the raupo. <i>(2000,Kokopu Dreams,214</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> The raupo spread of great reds in a green army.. where the glossy blue kakapo or bo-kaker peered out, winced, and bolted back into cover. <i>(1983,Dominion Infotech Weekly,100</i>)

>  Besides themselves there were none to dispute possession save only the sprightly pukaki of the raupo swamp. <i>(Sep 4, 1890,Otago Witness,36</i>)

>  We awoke to four plump pukeko preening in a line outside the window. <i>(Nov 2001,AA Directions,Summer 50 : 29</i>)

>  The pakeha boat.. gliding across raupo-fringed lakes, where the wild duck and pukaki, the black swan and the red bill muster in countless mobs. <i>(Apr 13, 1900,Auckland Weekly News,Suppl. 7</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> A fairly straight road ran between dykes and tall marsh-grass, where the red-legged pukakis rose and flapped away, their blue-black plumage shining in the sun. <i>(1911,Through South Westland,27</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Pu k|eko; A flute made of the bird <i>K|eko.</i> Name of a person. <i>(1825,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,196</i>)

>  [pl] I noticed the following:- Pigeon, tui, korimako, kaka, ruru, piwakawaka, toutouwai, kingfisher, duck and teal in the lakes, and pukeko in the swamp, and various sea birds. <i>(1884,TrNZI,17/419</i>) [NOTES: (Gold-Smith)]

>  <b>WALLACE</b> The Swamp-hen or Pukeko (<i>Porphyrio melanotus</i>,. . .) of New Zealand is in the habit of pulling up the affected grass [sc. with grass-grub] to get at the grub ... <i>(1891,Rural Economy of Aust. & NZ,52</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Pukeko, n.</i> Maori name for the bird <i>Porphyrio melanonotus</i>, the <i>Swamp-Hen</i>(q.v.). <i>(1898,Austral English,371</i>)

>  ' Two <i>pukaki</i>[sic] flew across their path.' <i>(Jun 2, 1896,Otago Witness,51</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [c1890 and spg. bowcacker used c1890 in Turakina-Bulls district by D.M. Mason. Used in Pohangine district by N.T. Moar's father (see bowie) (R. Mason) ca1935 Havelock, Marlborough [pju:kaek?] [pu.'kaek?] etc [bokaek] [`boukaek?] etc [kaek?] (children) [boui:]] spg <i>bo-kakka </i> bowcacker: (R.M.) `Used ca1890 Turakina-Bulls district (B.M. Mason), and in Pohangina district.']

>  <b>PASCOE</b> [Birds S.W.] Besides being a jack of all trades, this bird has a variety of names, no two people being agreed as to which is correct. The Maories call it the bu-ka-ka - is this spelling correct? Some people call it the coote, from some fanciful resemblance to the water hen of the Old Country. Swamp hen it is generally called in Westland, but I have heard it also called bald pate and New Zealand Turkey. <i>(1957,Mr Explorer Douglas,234</i>) [NOTES: c1899 (1957) Pascoe Mr Explorer Douglas 234]

>  Warriors, Wahines, Kuri, Poaka, Tamariki, Kumeras, Karos, Interpreters (1st, 2nd and 3rd grade), Poets Laureate, Poi Dancers, Guides, &c. on application to the Registrar. ...WAIPIRO - - An acursed drink ... TAIHOA - - The Police have the matter in hand POAKA - -a pig PUKEKO A bokakker out of season KORERO A long talk UTU - - The spike HAPU - - A Tribe KUMERA - - A sweet Spud <i>(1913,Auckland University Carnival Book,27</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> `No - it's more like a big bo-kakka' the local [Southland] slang for pukeko. <i>(1975,Milford Road,99</i>)

### pukeko disease
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a salmonella disease causing brown scours in sheep and thought to be carried by pukeko or swamp-hens.

>  <b>NELSON</b> pukeko disease: salmonella disease in sheep carried by pukeko. <i>(1999,Eugene Nelson Glossary,5</i>)

### Pukenui Limestone
 
 <i>n.</i> Geol. definition

>  <i>Gephyrocapsa</i> sinuosa has been identified by Tony Edwards (1976) in the Pukenui Limestone in the upper part of a long interval with <i>Chlamys delicatula</i> which is comparable to the Totara Road sequence. <i>(Nov 1978,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,46/4</i>)

 <i>v.</i> (See [pakaru](p-per.md#pakaru))

### Pukewilliam
 
 <i>n.</i> PM William Massey

>  We call him "Pukewilliam" - he's addressed as "Pitchfork Bill";The soldiers' boots are "Masseys", and there's very little drillAbout who's willingTo spend NZed's last shillingIf to spend it will be stilling the grisly, ghastly Hun. <i>(Dec 1918,NZ Observer,10</i>)
