 <i>n.</i> (See [pakaru](p-per.md#pakaru))

### puku
 
 <i>n.</i> ?cloak [ORIGIN: Maori, cf Buck Coming of Maori 2/170 check]]



 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) the stomach, especially when large and distended. [ORIGIN: Maori, ca1935 (Havelock) frequent:a full puku; how's your little pukie ?]

>  The "puku" (stomach) contained the carcase of a retriever dog, which had been bitten in two .. and both portions were then apparently swallowed. <i>(Feb 12, 1908,Otago Witness,39</i>)

>  You got a pain in the puku! <i>(Dec 21, 1944,Northern News,1</i>)

>  "They sit like a ball of anxiety in your puku and with me I think that had a lot to do with feeding a condition like cancer."... The legacies of his illness - aside from "one wicked scar running down the entirety of my puku" - are a greater consciousness of his health and the importance of family. <i>(May 5, 2002,Sunday Star Times,C3</i>)

>  I well recall him patting his protuberant tummy that he called his 'puku' fondly after a good feed of spaghetti and his famous meat sauce. <i>(Jun 2005,NZ Memories,No. 54, p. 13</i>)

>  I rubbed my slightly sticky-outie hairy puku. <i>(Jul 12, 2000,Capital Times,4</i>)

>  Poet Hinewirangi said poetry came from her puku; fellow poet Robert Sullivan said it came from his puku and his heart; and a third poet, Apirana Taylor, said it came from his puku, his heart and his sex, which appeared to cover all options. <i>(May 1, 1993,Listener (New Zealand Listener),35</i>)

>  There had been some wicked kai that day and Miro's puku was feeling a little delicate. <i>(2001,Huia 4: Contemp. Maori Fiction,29</i>)

>  ...at least I had a captive audience to read to: my 14 month old son who, as long as his puku is full, is unconcerned at my lack of reading riddim (sic). <i>(Apr 28, 2004,Capital Times,12</i>)

>  You know, you buy bulk food, fill your puku up, until the next time you can get something to eat. <i>(May 12, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),21</i>)

>  <b>DAVIS</b> Haven't noticed much shrinkage in the puku yet. <i>(1964,Watersiders,126</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> P|uku, <i>s</i>. The stomach; <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,197</i>)

>  belly - buk [puku] <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,113,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: c1826-1827 list of Maori words in c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) 113; also 1826-28 Boultbee Vocabulary in 1979 Begg 286]

>  <b>WESTON</b> The Medical Officer ... injected the serum in what the Maories call my <i>puku</i> ... <i>(1918,Three Years with the NZers,70</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> A stomach-ache became ... a <i>pain in the puku </i> <i>(1941,NZ Slang,42</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> <i>puku</i>, stomach, and <i>pukunui</i>, big stomach (used jocularly) <i>(1943,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,93</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> `I'm all right now,' I said, although my <i>puku</i> was feeling very sore. <i>(1948,Taina,176</i>)

>  <b>McCALLUM</b> [stage Maori] I then give him a good poke in the puku with the stem of my pipe. <i>Ibid</i> 98 [glossary] <i>Puku</i>: stomach. <i>(1963,Half-Gallon Jar,19</i>)

>  <i>(1954,Numbers,1/5</i>)

>  <b>ROGERS</b> That `good name' business seemed to hit the old man in the <i>puku</i>. <i>(1960,Long White Cloud, The,17</i>)

>  <b>IRVINE</b> Again, the New Zealander has drawn less upon Maori words than the Australian has called upon the Aborigine. However `puku' (stomach) and `potae' (hat) are freely used in New Zealand. <i>(1962,Australian Universal Dictionary,3</i>)

>  <i>puku n.</i> stomach; hence pain in the puku [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),16</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> The words <i>potae</i> `hat' and <i>puku</i> `stomach' are still current, but North Island words mainly. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,170</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> `I should be saving my puku for our kai tonight.' <i>(1969,Green River,15</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Puku, a pain in the</i>. A stomach-ache: New Zealand coll.: late C. 19-20. B., 1941. <i>Puku</i>: a Maori word. ? <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1145</i>)

>  <b>125</b> My brothers' slang and `funny' sayings delighted me. ... I remember [126] such words as `batty', `hoot' and `spondulicks' and many that ... were corruptions of Maori words. I spell them here as they sounded to me then: <i>ehoa, harka, kupai, kure, munga munga </i>typo, markuta, pooku, tangy <i>(1988,Through the Looking Glass</i>) [NOTES: Meikle]

>  <b>BAXTER</b> ... when Nikki/ goes down to have a wash/ ... with her little bulging puku <i>(1979,Collected Poems,516,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <i>puku</i> <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  <i>puku n. NZ colloq</i>. stomach. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,611</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>COX</b> `Nah, just slapped on some shades, stuck out the puku, stood really big in the doorway with a bad attitude. [a bouncer speaks] <i>(1987,Dirty Work,59</i>)

>  this thin tall Margaret walking / beside her short "tubby" friend with / the puku. <i>(Nov 12, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),114</i>)

>  Another old saying I like is a purely local one, applied to the planting of flax' "always plant his puku to the sun" - his puku(stomach) being that slightly bulbous clumped swelling at the base of the leaves. <i>(May 13, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),53</i>)

>  My 'puku' feel werry bad. <i>(May 30, 1917,Chronicles NZEF,II/19 : 151</i>)

>  "Look at that puk! Big fat slob." [Fn] Puk: contraction of <i>puku</i>, a Maori word meaning belly; much used by both races. <i>(1970,End of the Golden Weather, The,48</i>) [NOTES: 2nd edition]

>  The <i>Women's Day</i> cover handily includes arrows labelled "baby bump" pointing to Kidman's puku. <i>(Jun 19, 2006,NZ Herald,A14</i>)

### puku poaka
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>EDWARDS</b> We would eat boiled kai too, like pukupoaka. <i>(1992,Mihipeka,39</i>)

### pukunui
 
 <i>n.</i> a fat stomach, pot belly; also used as a nickname. [ORIGIN: Maori] (See [puku](pop-pyo.md#puku))

>  <b>POWER</b> [Maoris' nicknames for the pakehas in Power's party] A stalwart officer of the grenadiers, for instance, is generally known as Puku-nui, or Big-belly; an artillery officer is Biggy-bung; one wearing spectacles is Four-eyes, or No-eyes. <i>(Jan 30, 1848,Sketches in NZ,159,1849 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BENTLEY & FRASER</b> A novel librarian at Tui/ Found a way to avoid `puku nui'/ Was rotating male members/ From July to December/ On a system according to Dewey. <i>(1985,Grand Limerick Tour, A,16</i>)

>  "Taihoa," said Wi, with a seraphic smile, "let this yarn about him stay by while I talk about the man with the pukunui (big stomach)[.]" <i>(Jul 2, 1903,Poverty Bay Herald,4</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> Charadrius obscurus, New Zealand dotterel (See [also tuturiwhatu](../A#also-tuturiwhatu))

>  Mr. E. Tregear, in his <i> Maori Polynesian Dictionary</i>, and the Rev. H. W. Williams in the <i>Polynesian Journal</i>, give "Pukunui" as the Maori name of the dotterel. <i>(Aug 1, 1908,Evening Post,13</i>)

>  Has anybody ever seen the bird Pukunui (<i>"Big Stomach"</i>)? It was once supposed to exist in the North Island, but no European has reported it to science, and its existence has now become very problematical and perhaps mythical. <i>(Aug 1, 1908,Evening Post,13</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> <i>Pukunui</i> - A bird so called from the largeness and rotundity of its breast, about the size of the crow, and remarkable for the deep red with which the feathers are tinged upon the back and under the wings. (Williams 309 2.n) <i>Pluvialis obscurus</i>, dotterel : see TURIWHATI <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),68</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> 48. <i>Charadrius obscurus. Gmel</i>. ... Ha-poko-wera of the natives of Dusky Bay. <i>Forst</i> Tuturiwatu of the natives. <i>Yate. </i> To this order may also be referred two other birds spoken of by Mr. Yate under the names of Pukunui, Pututo. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/195</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/195]

>  <i>New Zealand Dotterel Charadrius obscurus</i> (GMELIN, 1789) ... [183] OTHER NAMES: <i>Tuturiwhatu pukunui, paturiwhata, red-breasted dotterel. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,182</i>)

### pukupuku
 
 <i>n.</i> a flax cloak

>  <b>FORSTER</b> ... they had bits of dog-skin at the four corners of their cloaks ... The <i>boghee-boghee</i>, or shaggy-cloak, which hangs round their necks like a thatch of straw, was almost constantly worn by them ... <i>(1777,Voyage Round the World,1/214</i>)

>  <b>THOMAS EDGAR</b> That Night they [the two young New Zealanders] lay in the Steerage on the bare Deck cover'd with their Cloaks or Buggee buggees - In the Morning they wept as before and repeated the same Mournful Song - <i>(Feb 26, 1777,Log</i>) [NOTES: 1777 feb 26 Thomas Edgar's Log in 1908 McNab Historical Records 2/226]

>  <b>BUCK</b> War cloaks (-->pukupuku). These were plain without any decoration and were said to give protection against spear thrusts. <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,170</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Pelargonium</i>

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>Pelargonium ... inodorum </i>... Scentless Pelargonium. KOPATA, PUKU-PUKU. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,32</i>)

### pukurau
 
 <i>n.</i> a fungus

>  Several fungi were also eaten ... such as the two large terrestrial species called <i>pukurau (Lycoperdon fontanesei</i> and <i>L. giganteum</i>); the <i>harore (Agaricus adiposus)</i>; the <i>hakekakeka (Hirneola auricula judae)</i>; and the <i>paruwhatitiri (Ileodictyon cibarium). </i> <i>(1881,TrNZI,13/30</i>)

>  [Also eaten were] several <i>Fungi</i> [especially] ... four ... which grow on trees, - the Harori <i>(Agaricus adiposus)</i>, the Hakeke, and the Popoiahakeke (<i>Polyporus</i> species, and the Pekepekekiore (<i>Hydnum clathroides</i>); also, three terrestrial ones, - the Paruwhatitiri (<i>Ileodictyon cibarium), the Pukurau</i> (Lycoperdon Fontainesii), ... <i>Ibid.</i> 57 ... for severe burns ... [Maoris] applied ... the fine reddish dust of the large decaying fungus pukirau (<i>Lycoperdon fontainesii</i>). <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/iii/32</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd); in 1981 Brooker et al. NZ Medicinal Plants 15]

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Pukurau, <i>Col[enso]. Lycoperdon Fontainesii. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Maori also ate the Pukurau (<i>Lycoperdon Fontainesii</i>), and possibly other species of puff-balls besides. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/212</i>)

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Calvatia caelata</i> (Bull.) Morg. Common name: <i>Puffball </i>Maori name: <i>Pukurau</i>. In Hooker's `Handbook ...', the name `pukurau' or `pukuvau' is applied to <i>Lycoperdon fontanesei </i>... which is now known as <i>Calvatia caelata</i>. In Williams ... the name `pukurau' is identified as <i>Clathrus cibarium</i> ... a net-like fungus ... [C. caelata is found in Europe ...as well as NZ.] <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,23</i>)

### pull
 
 <i>v.</i> to bring (sheep) towards (the shepherd); to bring sheep directly to its handler (occa. used of a shepherd)

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> He covered more ground than was necessary, by running wide, but eventually `pulled' the mob on the slope of a hill, and sat down wagging his tail, looking quite pleased with his performance. ... I might have to pull every mob of sheep on the run. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,47,1935 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Pull</i>.- (1) A dog <i>pulls</i> sheep when he brings them towards his master. (C.) (2) Hard walk, a long climb. (C.) <i>(Nov 18, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Gloss]

>  <b>BURDON</b> The silent dog is used to `pull' or bring sheep back to the shepherd. <i>(1938,High Country,107</i>)

>  <b>HARTLEY</b> As the young dog begins to pull sheep the trainer should go forward and work matters so that the sheep will be brought along a fence line. <i>(1949,Shepherd's Dogs, The,26</i>)

>  <b>STRONACH</b> The eye dog, on sheep, has a different way of commanding them. ... [44] It is said by some that on rough country he spends too much time eyeing the sheep and not enough `pulling' them. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,43</i>)

>  <b>LILICO</b> ... once you have a strong-eyed dog trained to pull he does so with care and control of sheep which the plain worker can never approach. <i>(1934,Sheep Dog Memoirs,4</i>)

>  <b>HARTLEY</b> The system I have devised ... gives the trainer greater command where there is a likelihood of the dog `setting' sheep and failing to lift or pull. <i>(1949,Shepherd's Dogs, The,3</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> He was a very `strong-eyed' dog and had immense power over sheep. Once he had headed them he `pulled' relentlessly, following hard on the heels of the mesmerised animals.... <i>(1970,Glorious Morning,141</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a dog trial event for heading dogs.

>  There is a background of gorse-flecked hills, a good pull, a nice flat for yarding, and a natural terrace affords an excellent vantage ground for spectators. <i>(Jun 16, 1909,Otago Witness,35</i>)

>  It was considered by many to be about the longest run-out in the country, and the pull also is far from easy. <i>(Mar 9, 1967,NZ Farmer,37</i>)

>  The pull was a bit loose although the dog was in reasonable control and held a good line. His sheep refused to settle and, with a loose pull across the flat, they entered the ring moving too freely and the dog had some difficulty in getting his hold. <i>(Jul 22, 1971,NZ Farmer,65</i>)

>  The pull is completed when the sheep enter the ring. <i>(1991,Constitution of NZ Dog Trial Assoc,33</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (local) to pick, pluck (flowers)

>  <b>DEMPSEY</b> And we don't pick flowers on the Island, but <i>pull</i> them, which sounds rather rough on the flowers. <i>(1964,Little World of Stewart island,6</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> bringing sheep directly to its handler

>  <b>McNAUGHTON</b> It was a perfect head and pull. Tat got them into the twelve o'clock hold in the ring. <i>(1970,Tat,122</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> <i>Heading dog</i>: On which casts wide to reach the `head' of sheep, and brings them back. Their work divides into the `head' and the `pull'. <i>(1970,Glorious Morning,253</i>)

>  <i>Pull</i>: Action of dog bringing sheep directly to its handler. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,25</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [heading dogs] a delivery of sheep to a handler or shepherd.

>  <b>MILLS ET AL</b> Any rash tactic likely to upset the sheep can be fatal, not only to a good 'pull' but also for later work at hand. Although it isn't always possible, try to avoid a jerky, stop-and-start kind of pull. <i>(1964,Practical Guide to Handling Dogs and Stock,37</i>)

>  Pull - when a heading dog brings - pulls - sheep to the shepherd. Should be as straight as possible and at walking pace. <i>(Nov 15, 1983,NZ Journal of Agriculture,21</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [heading dogs] to deliver sheep to a shepherd, handler or triallist.

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> He covered more ground than was necessary, by running wide, but eventually 'pulled' the mob on the slope of a hill and sat down ... <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,47</i>) [NOTES: (1935)]

>  [The dog] Pulled straight and true and put in some fine work at the ring. <i>(Jul 2, 1923,NZ Farmer,951</i>)

>  <b>HARTLEY</b> .. in the case of excessively strong-eyed dogs, it gives the trainer greater command where there is a likelihood of the dog 'setting' sheep and failing to lift or pull. <i>(1949,Shepherd's Dogs, The,3</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> Once he had headed them, he pulled relentlessly, following hard on the heels of the mesmerised animals until they were galloping in a mad panic. <i>(1970,Many a Glorious Morning,141</i>)

>  <b>MCMILLAN</b> He was fifty I guess .. but he could still pull wild wethers to him with the top heading dog ... <i>(1975,More From the High Country,19</i>)

>  <b>JARDINE</b> She pulled too hard and nearly put them [the sheep] over a bluff.. <i>(1978,Shadows on the Hill,7</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a hard walk

>  <i>Pull</i>.- (1) A dog <i>pulls</i> sheep when he brings them towards his master. (C.) (2) Hard walk, a long climb. (C.) <i>(Nov 18, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: (Acland Gloss.)]



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i>

>  These crosses [sc. greyhound-collie/terrier, etc], sometimes euphemistically called lurchers, are excellent deer-pullers. ... Only with great luck and fitness ... can the hunter be in on the pull with the knife. On the whole it is better not to have dogs so fast as to be able to pull deer unless you are going in for that line exclusively ... <i>(Dec 1951,Here & Now,15</i>)

### pull a train
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  An 11-man and one woman jury had found Jones guilty of the rape of a girl picked up from a city bus terminal on the night of Friday, April 23, and taken to a house on the Ellerslie Main Highway where she was forced to `pull a train' of Hell's Angels and Storm Troopers gangsters. <i>(Jul 20, 1971,NZ Truth,14(3401)</i>)

### pull and tie weasand
 
 <i>phr.</i> freezing-worker

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> PULL AND TIE WEASAND (Rotates with scalp man and tongue man) <i>(1975,Log of a Superfluous Son, The,42</i>)

### pull back
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>pull back</i>: Tool for pulling shears when setting them up. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/26</i>) [NOTES: (Wallace Shearing Glossary)]

### pull finger
 
 <i>phr.</i> ellipsis of pull your finger out. -->1988 July Pull finger! as an addressed ejaculation to someone who was saying something the hearer thought silly: used by women: p.c. Laurie Bauer (Wellington) -->cf P( pp.1199-1200 -->cf Cohen, J.M. and M.J. Penguin Dictionary of Modern Quotations 1971 Harmondsworth 67 Duke of Edinburgh speech 17 Oct 1961 ... I think it is about time we pulled our finger out

>  <b>McGill</b> pull finger command to hurry up; eg `Right, let's pull finger and see if we can catch the fleet.' Contraction of English phr. `pull one's finger out.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,88</i>)

### pull in
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to muster.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Sam Welsh first mustered on Waitangi well before the First World War and he tells me they always pulled in a big swag of Haldon and Hakataramea sheep in the autumn muster. <i>(1975,Sixty Thousand on the Hoof,91</i>)

### pull one's head in
 
 <i>phr.</i> to desist, refrain from doing something, mind one's own business

>  pull your head in: a saying applied to any person or persons to stop doing anything; e.g., `Settle down son and pull your head in.' <i>(1981,NZ Police National Headquarters</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  <b>McGILL</b> head, phr. need your head read you are insane or stupid; eg `That's the third time this week you've put on odd socks. You need your head read, you do. Mostly NZA. <i>Ibid.</i> 88 pull your headin advice to mind one's own business; eg `I'd prefer to handle this my way. Why don't you just pull your head in?' Partridge traces to army men sticking heads out of troop trains and being advised to pull them in lest they be mistaken for cattle; c. 1930. ANZ. ] <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,55</i>)

### pull out
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to draft or to separate. (See [run off](roa-ruv.md#run-off))

>  <b>MOWAT</b> Pulled woollies out at Red Gate. <i>(Feb 19, 1908,Alexander Mowat Diaries</i>)

>  Pulled woollies out of mob. <i>(Jun 2, 1908,Alexander Mowat Diaries</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> [The] following [was] employed by those who served in the [Great] War ... <i>pull out</i>, to depart, from the artillery's pulling out the guns from the emplacements; <i>(1935,Slang Today,286</i>) [NOTES: 1935 Mitchell and Strong in Partridge Slang Today 286]

### pull someone's tit
 
 <i>phr.</i> to have (a person) on, pull someone's leg

>  <b>DAVIN</b> `Yer; yes, don't get knotted, Franky boy. I'm just pulling your tit'. <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,331</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>pull someone's tit</i> to make a fool of or tease; eg `Take it easy, Moss. Can't you see he's just pulling your tit?' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,88</i>)

### pull the finger on
 
 <i>phr.</i> to inform on

>  finger ... 11. put the finger on. Inf., U.S. & N.Z. to inform on or identify, esp. for the police. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,416</i>) [NOTES: -->1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 416 [nf. 1986 NZPOD]]

### pull the other tit
 
 <i>exclam.</i> said as an expression of disbelief

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Didn't know him from a bottle of detergent - Pull the other tit, boy <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,163</i>) [NOTES: nf P8 -->P: low Aust; since ca1910. -]

### pull the wind
 
 <i>phr.</i> to row against the wind

>  <b>SARGESON</b> Going back, Ken said, we'll be pulling the wind. <i>(1946,That Summer,44</i>) [NOTES: Great Day]

### pull up
 
 <i>v.</i> stop, stop running

>  As long as he pulls up OK from this run, he will run next in the Yalumba... <i>(Sep 26, 2004,Sunday Star Times,B16</i>)

### Pull Yourself Together Department
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  If ever there was a tailor-made branding image to publicise the Government's new mega Pull Yourself Together Department (official title Work and Income Department) it has to be Christine Rankin, at present boss of Income Support. <i>(Jul 4, 1998,Dominion,20</i>)

### Pull Yourself Together Party
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  Then McCormick will indulge in his political and social satire, with maybe a plug or two for his enduring Pull Yourself Together Party. <i>(Sep 25, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

### pull-finger
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  "It was a bit of shock when it came to an end, but it was the big pull-finger that I needed. I was feeling bored and complacent." <i>(Aug 13, 2000,Sunday Star Times,D3</i>)

### puller
 
 <i>n.</i> one who removes wool from carcases

>  <b>SMITH</b> The men employed [by fellmongers] pulling the wool off the skins are [186] called `pullers'. <i>(1927,Sheep & Wool Industry,184</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> ... you were on hourly rates shoving the heavy-laden trolleys of sheepskins from the painters to the pullers ... <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,67</i>)

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> The wool is removed from the [depilatory] painted skins by the `pullers' who sort it. Sixty years ago nearly all the wool was spread out in padocks to dry ... <i>(1957,Canterbury Frozen Meat Company,69</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The [central North Island] hunters have what they call `pullers'; these are simply big strong horses and as the captured animal recovers from the tranquiliser it is tied to this horse's tail. ...as long as the hunters position themselves so that the captured horse pulls straight back this has little effect on the puller's tail. <i>(1972,Wake of The Axe,118</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a fish

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Puller <i>Chromis dispilus </i>Griffin <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,408</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 408]

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Demoiselle</i> Chromis dispilus <i>Family Pomacentridae (damselfishes). Apparently restricted to New Zealand. ... from Bay of Plenty northwards ... A similar species, the single-spot demoiselle (or puller), C. hypsilepis,</i> has a yellowish tinge. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,98</i>)

>  [index to common names 283-293] puller 1543 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a working dog that pulls sheep towards its handler.

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> Kingdom in the Hills 68 Lady was a safe header, but, like most plain-eyed dogs, a poor puller. <i>(1974,Kingdom in the Hills,68</i>)

### pulling
 
 <i>n.</i> [[? is this a special use: cf PULL n.]]

>  <i>Pulling:</i> A musterer's term for climbing i.e. pulling up a spar. <i>(1947,Wayleggo</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the delivery of sheep by a heading dog to its handler.

>  [Photo caption] A group of collie enthusiasts watching an interesting pulling and yarding event. <i>(Apr 19, 1905,Otago Witness,48</i>)

>  Clutha Sheep Dog Trial Club .. Head, Pull, and Yard. About 500 yds; time, 10 minutes. Prizes - 1st GBP4, 2nd GBP3, 3rd GBP2, 4th GBP1. Points - Heading 8, command 8, pulling 8, style at yards 6, yarding 4. <i>(Mar 11, 1908,Tuapeka Times,3</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> <i>Pulling</i>: the act of a heading dog bringing sheep back to his master is termed pulling. <i>(1947,Wayleggo</i>)

>  The dogs generally cast and headed well, and although the pulling was sometimes rash and the sheep split, there was not a single case of crossing. <i>(Jul 2, 1923,NZ Farmer,950</i>)

>  The handy dog is is used for all heading and pulling, and to be of real value, must get close to the sheep he is working. <i>(Nov 5, 1953,NZ Farmer,45</i>)

>  <b>MILLS ET AL</b> ... Such natural headers are generally natural pulling dogs also. <i>(1964,Practical Guide to Handling Dogs and Stock,33</i>)

### pulling the coat
 
 <i>phr.</i> see citation

>  <i>pulling the coat:</i> Walking out of a shed before the shearing has finished. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/26</i>) [NOTES: (Wallace Shearing Glossary)]

### pullthrough
 
 <i>n.</i> an instrument for cleaning a rifle barrel

>  <b>THOMSON</b> The essentials are : carrying bag, rifle and sling, pullthrough, flannelette and oil for cleaning, two skinning knives and a steel, two pairs of boots, ... <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,25</i>)

### pulp
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McARA</b> The minus-ten-mesh pulverised ore and water from the stamps (then called pulp) flowed into a large, common, wooden launder ... <i>Ibid.</i> 330 <i>Pulp</i>: The finely-crushed ore mixed with water after passing through the 10-mesh stamp screens. <i>(1878,Gold Mining at Waihi,217</i>)

### pulpie
 
 <i>n.</i> a pulp and paper worker; ?pulpies spec. Pulp and Paperworkers Federation

>  Pulp and paper workers had been locked out by management ... Mr Knox reported to 464 `pulpies' at the meeting on his efforts to end the row. <i>(Sep 5, 1986,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  Claims that her husband received a .... loan from the Pulp and Paper Federation have prompted the wife of controversial pulpies secretary John Murphy to go public. <i>(Sep 21, 1986,Sunday News,3</i>)

### pulpit-puncher
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) preacher

>  Of course, the pinch-penny pulpit-puncher always depends on woman [sic] to fill the pews before him, and cannot realise congregation without the silly gulls. <i>(Sep 1, 1906,NZ Truth,63/1/7</i>)

### pulsator
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The main milk and air pipes ran along the bails above the cows and the pulsator was in the middle. <i>(Dec 2003,NZ Memories,No. 45/11</i>)

>  When the slide pulsator uncovers the air line so that air can rush in, the inflation collapses with a slap against the teat. <i>(Aug 1959,NZ Dairy Exporter,69</i>)

>  The Game Releaser.. is automatic in action, being operated by and working simultaneously with the pulsator. <i>(Aug 28, 1926,NZ Dairy Produce Exporter,5</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> The parts of the [milking] machines are named, the <i>cups</i> which attach to the cow's teats, the rubber <i>inflations</i> inside the cups, the <i>pulsators</i> which regulate the vacuum and the <i>releaser</i>. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,149</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> This slapping, the choo-shik-choo of the pulsator ... made a confusing noise for John. <i>(1978,Elver, The,37</i>)

### pum land
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) short for pumice land

>  In a leading article recently a daily paper referred to the gum lands and the `pum' lands (pumice) selected by the Government for returned soldiers. <i>(Jun 10, 1919,Quick March,45</i>)

### pumice country
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Mr Clifton days that as far as the growing of root crops is concerned, the pumice country has passed the experimental stage. <i>(Jan 24, 1913,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  Thanks to Dr Reakes.. a tremendousstretch of pumice country has been freedfrom its bad reputation. <i>(Jul 17, 1912,Otago Witness,15</i>)

>  There are about 40 varieties of the eucalyptus family grown, but only four or five out of this number are well adapted for the pumice country. <i>(Feb 19, 1908,Otago Witness,11</i>)

>  Perhaps pumice country farmers could follow the practice adopted by those South Taranaki farmers who have no summer problem. <i>(Sep 1959,NZ Dairy Exporter,63</i>)

>  Another great stretch of pumice country in which Aucklanders are or should be interested lies between Rotorua and Taupo. <i>(Jan 27, 1916,Auckland Weekly News,64</i>)

>  A large area adjacent to the lake [Taupo] is at present worthless pumice-country. <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,8</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> `The going, once we got onto the pumice country beyond the Gentle Annie, wasn't so bad.' [From an account of the 1897 flood by Charlie Golding] <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,67</i>)

>  This posed interesting situations in places such as the central pumice country of the North Island which was suitable for dairying, and he wondered if the Government would go to the extend of stopping dairy conversions from forestry land. <i>(May 31, 2002,Dominion,21</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) farmland with a pumice base.

>  At Fort Alfred, the plain is left, and the horseman enters by a gap into the hummocky pumice country, separating Te Teko form [sic] the Kaingaroa plateau. <i>(Aug 6, 1869,Hawke's Bay Herald,3</i>)

>  The high road between Wairoa and Ohinemutu runs closely by both the above lakes. It is made through pumice country, so exceedingly friable that the wind blows down the sides of the cuttings in tons. <i>(Dec 29, 1877,Otago Witness,6</i>)

>  By skilful farming, he has turned a large area of this pumice country into high-class dairying and fattening land. <i>(Apr 13, 1920,Poverty Bay Herald,6</i>)

>  There is no record of bush sickness having developed on any save pumice country. <i>(Sep 15, 1913,NZ Journal of Agriculture,121</i>)

>  In developing pumice country a great saving can be effected in clearing costs if the manuka and manaoa scrub are burnt standing two or three years ahead of cultivation. <i>(Jul 16, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,17</i>)

>  <b>TAIT</b> 500 acres of strong, healthy country, 675 acres of warm, sweet pumice country. <i>(1958,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol II,410</i>)

>  Don Ellett, who farms a 770-acre pumice country unit at almost 3000 ft near Pureora Forest.. <i>(Aug 24, 1972,NZ Farmer,8</i>)

### pumice dust
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  It was necessary to sit still, too, for each movement raised a cloud of pumice-dust, and there was always an exciting possibility of falling down the steep bank. <i>(Jan 16, 1904,Evening Post,11</i>)

>  " .. The trunks and lower branches appeared white, like the ground, partly owing to the light-coloured lichens and pumice dust with which they were covered, and partly owing to reflection ..." <i>(Jun 19, 1886,Te Aroha News,6</i>)

>  Recollections of the Tarawera eruption were vividly brought to mind this morning when a fine shower of pumice dust, carried from the volcanic region by the wind, settled over the town. <i>(Dec 16, 1896,Poverty Bay Herald,2</i>)

>  Thousands of acres of bare hillside and barren country have been covered with millions of trees, and the aspect of miles of country which was at one time covered with manuka scrub, fern, and pumice dust, has been changed. <i>(Jun 20, 1913,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  Patches of snow covered with pumice-dust were passed. <i>(Nov 29, 1872,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  Patches of snow covered with pumice dust were passed. <i>(Dec 7, 1872,Auckland Weekly News,20</i>)

>  I prefer drawing a kindly veil over the awful experiences some of us have had during the last week's howling hurricane, for while pebbles have been hurled at our devoted heads, the citizens of Wanganui must have eaten some pounds' weight of pumice dust, mixed with various unsavory compounds! <i>(Nov 3, 1900,Wanganui Chronicle,2</i>)

### pumice land(s)
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) the region of the North Island around the volcanic plateau

>  In a third category may be placed the more undulating pumice lands, which in time will be broken in much in the same way as the flats are coming in now. <i>(Jan 24, 1913,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  It is stated that the late manager of the Puponga sawmill at Manunui, who is also a practical agriculturist, had been experimenting with different grass seeds during the past two years on land after the trees had been felled and he was surprised at the growth of grass on the pumice land. <i>(Jul 4, 1908,Taranaki Herald,5</i>)

>  But whether we turn our eyes further north or further south, hot springs meet our gaze; pumice lands, as in the Waikato, or volcanic lands, as at Whangarei, tempt our settlers. <i>(May 9, 1871,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  It is nearly 100 miles shorter than that <i>via</i> Mokau; it passes through good country almost the whole of its length, avoiding the inferior "pumice" land, and with the exception of a short distance it runs chiefly through ground purchased or leased by the Crown. <i>(Jun 9, 1882,Wanganui Chronicle,3</i>)

>  Immigration properly controlled must benefit every country that has land available for production, but while there is much pumice land in the Dominion to be had, there is not a great deal of rich country that is not in the hands of big interests and in the form of large estates. <i>(Apr 10, 1930,NZ Truth,8</i>)

>  1. <i>Pumice Lands Settlement</i>. [heading] <i>(Jan 4, 1929,Na To Hoa Aroha,1/162,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Ngata)]

>  <b>EVANS</b> He was ... able to prolong the toughness by taking on a new block in the sparse pumice land of New Zealand's North Island. <i>(1986,Change Agent,19</i>)

>  Timber grown on pumice soil is not as good as that grown in the north. Totara, for instance, grown on pumice land is not as heavy as that grown in the north. <i>(1913,AJHR,C-12/46</i>)

>  PUMICE LANDS. [heading] Pumice country varies in quality in just the same degree as do the more fertile lands on the Canterbury Plain ... <i>(Jan 15, 1912,NZ Journal of Agriculture,24</i>)

>  My experience.. may be of some use in suggesting one or two plants which are worthy of extended trial.. on.. the pumice land of Waikato and Taupo. <i>(Mar 24, 1892,Auckland Weekly News,31</i>)

>  Norman and Bob Evans and their four children were strangers to the North Island when they arrived in this harsh pumice-land area in 1955. <i>(Aug 2002,NZ Memories,37/50</i>)

>  Vogel imagines this happening after artesian wells are bored to supply water to more than a quarter of a million acres (617,000 hectares) of pumice-land, which is drenched with a mixture of "soil, clay and fertilising agents capable to being held in water by suspension." <i>(Jun 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 52/50</i>)

### pumice soil
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  According to the Government Agricultural Chemist, Mr. B. C. Aston, this pumice soil has all the elements of fertility save one - nitrogen. <i>(Jan 24, 1913,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  These extensive tracts of country of light pumice soil would, I think, make fine sheep-runs. <i>(Jul 10, 1855,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  These trees have been found to suit the Hot Lakes district, as they do well in the pumice soil. <i>(Oct 9, 1901,Feilding Star,2</i>)

>  Situated on the banks of the Waikato, on pumice soil, rain has no terrors for the officials of the Hamilton Club. <i>(Aug 9, 1924,NZ Truth,9</i>)

>  Timber grown on pumice soil is not as good as that grown in the north. Totara, for instance, grown on pumice land is not as heavy as that grown in the north. <i>(1913,AJHR,C-12/46</i>)

>  Much of the road has never been metalled, though there are convenient quarries, and the result is that the soft pumice soil washes away with every heavy downpour of rain. <i>(Feb 18, 1897,Hawke's Bay Herald,2</i>)

>  It is a very erroneous opinion some have that by traffic the pumice soil will consolidate; evidence to the contrary is apparent everywhere in that locality; the horse tracks, instead of consolidating, are worn down in many places 18 - 24 inches. <i>(Oct 29, 1869,Hawke's Bay Herald,2</i>)

>  .. the soil between the river bank and the swamp is pumice soil ... <i>(May 1, 1875,Wanganui Chronicle,2</i>)

>  Nature having deposited an impervious coating of volcanic mud on the eastern hills and slopes, all the rains, instead of percolating through as formerly, the porus [sic] and pumice soil, now comes rushing down as if from the iron roof of a building, without loosing [sic] a droop [sic] and it is more than the present natural outlet can do to carry off the sudden influx of this surplus supply. <i>(Sep 13, 1886,Timaru Herald,2</i>)

>  Pumice soils are clasified as coarse sands, but the word `sand' conveys rather a wrong impression ... <i>(Jan 15, 1912,NZ Journal of Agriculture,374</i>)

### pumice-whitened
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>DOMETT</b> She staggers, falls - upon the pumice-whitened shore./ <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/247</i>)

### pumicestone
 
 <i>n.</i> [aka pumice-stone, pumice stone]

>  The Waikato had a very deep bed, and its banks showed cliffs of pumicestone. <i>(Mar 16, 1844,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

>  The soil consisted of a black mould, intermixed with pumice-stone; no other description of wood than stunted manuka presenting itself. <i>(Aug 26, 1842,New Zealand Colonist & Port Nicholson Advertiser,4</i>)

>  The soil is a rich brown loam, mingled, however, with a large proportion of powdered pumice-stone. <i>(Mar 19, 1842,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,3</i>)

>  The tunnel runs thro' a very strange volcanic formation, consisting of clay, rotten pumice stone, and a large amount of malachite, which is found throughout the whole length of the tunnel. <i>(Dec 13, 1877,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

>  Next day rub off all surplus tissue and fat [from the rabbit skins] with a piece of pumice stone. <i>(Nov 11, 1916,NZ Truth,2</i>)

>  CIGARETTE-STAINED nails may be prevented by using a holder, or the stains removed with a mixture of ground pumice stone and peroxide. <i>(Feb 6, 1930,NZ Truth,21</i>)

>  Pumice stone will unquestionably injure the enamel. <i>(Feb 23, 1889,Observer (Auckland),4</i>)

>  If for a short period, the simple packing the [sic] eggs, with the end having the air chamber uppermost, in wooden trays full of powdered calcined pumice stone, will be amply sufficient. <i>(Feb 16, 1867,Hawke's Bay Herald,3</i>)

>  Powdered pumice stone is very useful for removing stains from the hands. If it is mixed with a little lemon juice it is still more effective. <i>(Jun 21, 1924,NZ Truth,14</i>)

>  Turea Moana, uncle of Ngapaki, corroborated the evidence of the previous witness, also deposing to find a block of pumice stone. <i>(Dec 14, 1897,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  As for the pumice stone, it should be depended upon to smooth away any hard parts which may later develop into corns or painful ridges. <i>(Nov 21, 1903,Star, The,3</i>)

>  It comes out on a country covered with scrubby tea-tree, with a poor soil of sand and pumicestone. <i>(May 10, 1873,Auckland Weekly News,20</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> Among the loose Shingles on the beach two pieces of <i>Pumice-stone</i> were found. [ed. fn] Rounded pumice pebbles commonly cast on New Zealand beaches were mainly produced by the prehistoric Taupo eruption (circa 1800 years ago), ... <i>(Jun 8, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/296,1982 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ROCHFORT</b> A small depth of soil brings you to pumice-stone, much used for building chimneys to wooden houses. <i>(1853,Adventures of a Surveyor,26</i>)

>  <b>Hochstetter</b> We had reached a pumicestone-plateau,called by the Maoris Moerangi. <i>(1867,New Zealand (Heaphy),358</i>)

### pumicey
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of farmland soils] composed or part-composed of pumice.

>  They [soils] get more pumicey farther back. <i>(Jun 1982,NZ Dairy Exporter,34</i>)

>  <b>HINDMARSH</b> The soil was a pumicey loam overlaying a mudstone base. <i>(1997,Come Be a Pioneer,197</i>)

### pumotomoto
 
 <i>n.</i> a wooden flute

>  Nunns played a Putatara, a shell trumpet; a Pumotomoto, a wooden flute and a Putorino, both a trumpet and a flute... The Pumotomoto is played directly into the fontanelle of an infant, the soft spot where skull bones do not completely cover the brain. <i>(Sep 30, 2004,Wellingtonian,4</i>)

### pump
 
 <i>n.</i> aggression

>  He was a player full of pump, and whether that was good or bad depended on what side of the fence you were on. <i>(Jul 19, 2002,Dominion Post,C11</i>)

### pump up
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>GRADY</b> <i>Pump-up</i> - To inflate a whale with compressed air after a hollow-nosed air-spear had been thrust into its body. <i>(1982,Perano Whalers,228</i>)

### pumper
 
 <i>n.</i> heart

>  <b>THOMSON</b> <i>(1905,Bush Boys of NZ,13</i>) [NOTES: [see <i>propped up</i> and note]]

### pumpie
 
 <i>n.</i> a service station attendant

>  The service stations only had `pumpies' working, ... and the traveller did not belong to the Automobile Association. <i>(Dec 28, 1987,Evening Post,3</i>)

### pumpkin-head
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WISNIOWSKI</b> `I didn't like him at all. A real pumpkin head.' This is the name Australian colonists of English extraction give to German settlers, on account of their round features. Now that even the natives have learned to despise the miserly Teuton land grabbers, the name pumpkin head is often used by people who speak only pidgin English. [1972 ed. note] `Pumpkin head in English in the original. ... may be a confusion between two Americanisms, `pumpkin head' meaning a stupid person and `square head' or a similar term for a German. <i>(1877,Tikera,150,(trans. Podstolski 1972) ed.</i>)

>  <b>THOMPSON</b> Yah, it's luck,/ .../ Dat gifs der pumpkin-head a chance to blay der vinnin' game,/ <i>(1909,Ballads about Business,38</i>)

### puna
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a New Zealand-bred cultivar of chicory (Cichorium intybus). [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Puna - higher in most important minerals than ryegrasses, clovers and lucerne. Very high animal performance recorded on this cultivar. <i>(1995,Grasslands Range of Forage and Conservation Plants,28</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a spring

>  Two of these punas, beautiful clear little fountains, may be seen today; one is by the side of the Motumako road <i>(May 10, 1920,Quick March,13</i>)

>  This involved withdrawal from normal activities, relieving of karakia and bathing in sacred waters. Special puna (springs) were set aside for this purpose. <i>(Dec 6, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),86</i>)

### puna-weta
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [putaputaweta](pop-pyo.md#putaputaweta))

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Carpodetus serratus (The Serrate Carpodetus).</i>.. The native name <i>Puta-puta-weta</i> signifies full of weta holes. In the North Island trees of this species are rarely cut down unperforated by longitudinal insect galleries. Another name by which it is known is <i>Puna-weta</i>. In the Urewera country it is called <i>Kai-weta</i> (i.e., weta food). [That ugly innocent the weta (<i>Deinacrida</i>) has thus been accused of perforating the tree; but as a matter of fact though often used as convenient residences [195] by the weta, the holes are made by the caterpillar of the large moth <i>Hepialus virescens</i> ...] <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,188,1940 p. 194 ed.</i>)

### punch
 
 <i>v.</i> (applied to sheep rather than cows)

>  <i>Punch</i>.-To drive [sheep] forcibly up hill or when they are tired. <i>(Nov 18, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Gloss]

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> They were punching the last mob down to the shearing shed this day when two merino wethers broke and set off back the trail `going like the hammers.' <i>(1953,Golden Bush, The,154,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McNAUGHTON</b> This meant another two hours of dogging and punching the several different mobs to safe higher ground. <i>(1970,Tat,108</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> When I hear the old men talk I have to grin,/ And often wonder how they got the dam sheep in./ When punching sheep down through the snow/ <i>(1975,Eight Daughters,90</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to carry or move with effort or difficulty

>  Not very well today and punching two swags very nearly made me brake [sic] down, so camped early. ... Punching up remainder of Swag, what a weary heart braking [sic] thing it is, staging swags going over the same ground twice or thrice. <i>(Feb 10, 1891,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1891 Feb 10 Diary in 1957 Pascoe Mr Explorer Douglas 124]

>  <b>HARPER</b> .. taken the risk - rather than going down the river and `punching' up more stores over that rough ground. <i>(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,249</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to work or drive cattle (espec. or chiefly, bullocks) as a team); (in NZ the term with dairy cows is spank, occas. bang)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> That's the team of working bullocks we have just passed; we have names for them, as you'll know when you come to try your hand at punching them. <i>Ibid.</i> 67 He can punch the bullocks almost as well as an old hand already, even to the swearing at them. <i>(1881,Waitaruna,53</i>)

>  <b>D'AVIGDOR</b> A <i>bullock puncher </i>is a man engaged to drive bullocks to market or port. The expression is painfully true, as these men, who receive but very poor wages, are recruited from the worst class, ill-treat the poor animals by <i>punching</i> their sides with pointed stick. <i>(1888,Antipodean Notes,170</i>)

>  <b>SKEY</b> Puncher of the stolid beast,/ Punch him not from morning feast; [179] .../ Goad him not until he reel,/ ... / Fling that cruel whip from thee,/ [the burlesque addresses shepherd, swagman, ploughman, scrubber] <i>(1889,Pirate Chief and the Mummy's Complaint..., The,178</i>)

>  I punched cattle a little, and prospected a little, and made out fairly well, but had to keep moving. <i>(Jun 1, 1903,Star, The,4</i>)

>  They are roughing it pretty much as we are, but came over steerage instead of punching cattle, and intend travelling third-class by rail instead of on wheels. <i>(Sep 14, 1912,Evening Post,10</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> sc. briskets (See [cf. puncher](pop-pyo.md#puncher))

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Lambs hooked on to the chain and progressing slowly along while the row of men standing alongside slash and tear and nick with knives while others punch briskets or pull the skins from the swinging carcases. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,67</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to make the final direct (blows) to sever the block or log in a chopping contest. [1983 TV1 axemen's chops to make the final direct blow(s) to sever the block or log (?also n.)]



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to muster or drove stock in a large mob.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> punch: to drive sheep, or cattle for that matter, up a hill, or when they are too tired to move freely. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,64</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> We'd heard tales from blokes who had,[worked in the North Island] of punching big mobs of romneys, the predominance of huntaways and beauty of Maori women. Mustering for Gordon Lucas through Tarras way, punching 2000 lambs short of water, getting in the river, I was generally having a hard day of it. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,168</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> I punched sheep for a crust, and want no appellation beyond 'musterer' on my grave. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,200</i>)

### punch cows
 
 <i>phr.</i> to dairy-farm, be a dairy farmer

>  <i>to punch cows</i>: to be dairy farming <i>(1980,Lynfield College, Auckland</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

### Punch King
 
 <i>n.</i> a children's game

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Punch King was similar to Bar the Door, but more violent. The game was called Punch King Sinio. ... Five were left uncrowned. ... The five had their backs to the wall, and woe betide any luckless mite who dared to come within reach of their punches, so indiscriminantly were they delivered. Any punch was legal, rabbit or otherwise. ... (1876; Wellington) <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,57</i>)

### punch out
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>punch out</i> to beat up; eg `I intend to punch out that so-and-so.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,88</i>)

### punch over
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  `... this has spoilt a good day. I have been raging all day. I punched him over because he tried to punch me over, and put me in my place. He reckoned he was a Liverpudlian seaman who had been here for 20 years, a big toughie,' Gilbert had told him... Detective Rex Barnett said that Gilbert admitted that he had `punched over' a man in the toilets `because he was trying to buy something I do not like.' <i>(Dec 7, 1984,Press, The,4</i>)

### punched out
 
 <i>adj.</i> beaten up

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>punched out</i>: BEATEN UP <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,83</i>)

### punched, bored, or countersunk
 
 <i>phr.</i> not to know whether one is...: to be not with it

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Doesn't know if he's punched, bored, or countersunk - A tiger for punishment <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,162</i>)

### puncher
 
 <i>n.</i> [(freezing works)] (See [cf. punch v](../P#punch-v))

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> The legger slits as a surgeon does,/ And the puncher strips the pelt, <i>(1949,Farm & Station Verse,188</i>) [NOTES: 1949 Keith Stronach `Slaughter Chain'sc in 1950 Woodhouse Farm and Station Verse 188]

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> The legger slits as a surgeon does,/ And the puncher strips the pelt, <i>(1949,Farm & Station Verse,188</i>) [NOTES: 1949 Keith Stronach `Slaughter Chain'sc in 1950 Woodhouse Farm and Station Verse 188]

>  <b>GORDON</b> <i>Brisket-puncher, -punching</i>: punching or pulling pelts off briskets. [check] <i>(May 17, 1951,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: ca1951 May 17 Feilding Freezing Works terms p.c. COLIN GORDON]



 
 <i>n.</i> bullock-puncher; drover

>  A rouseabout who insulted a bullock puncher was tied behind the waggon from the station to Invercargill, but was allowed to ride upon it on the homeward journey, and he then resumed work as if nothing had happened. <i>(Mar 27, 1901,Otago Witness,29</i>)

>  <b>SKEY</b> Puncher of the stolid beast,/ Punch him not from morning feast; [179] .../ Goad him not until he reel,/ ... / Fling that cruel whip from thee,/ <i>(1889,Pirate Chief and the Mummy's Complaint..., The,178</i>)

>  <b>SCOTT</b> Using the whip with all the skill of a professional `puncher' <i>(1900,Colonial Turf,76</i>)

>  "The puncher sometimes has to erect a flagstaff with a red flag on the bullocks' horns, to let him know whereabouts they are; so long as the horns are visible, there is no danger." <i>(Jul 1, 1870,North Otago Times,2</i>)

>  Mr. Editor, the late Judge Gillies was at one time a bullock puncher, his education soon made him leave it. <i>(Dec 5, 1892,Timaru Herald,4</i>)

>  .. for, although a gentleman armed with a stout walking stick and backed up by one assistant armed with a dumpy level and a second with a painted staff, may get round a sharp corner on the side of a steep hill, it in no way necessarily follows that Long Jem the <i>bull puncher</i> can accomplish the same feat with five or six yoke of bullocks and the dray load of stores ... <i>(Apr 20, 1861,Hawke's Bay Herald,2</i>)

### punching
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the driving of stock.

>  <b>McNAUGHTON</b> It was a slow process, getting those sheep down. The hillside was benchy and rough. But once they were started on the downward journey, the danger of a breakaway was past, and all that remained was hard punching to keep them together. <i>(1970,Tat,99</i>)

>  <b>CRESSWELL</b> There were no tracks - just hard punching all the way, with a fair number of sheep dying from tutu poisoning. <i>(1949,Early NZ Families,5</i>)

>  <b>NELSON</b> punching: setting the dog to drive the mob along. <i>(1999,Eugene Nelson Glossary,7</i>)

### puncture vine
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i> vine, puncture [s] Tribulus terrestris </i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,83</i>)

### pung
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HARVEY</b> We tried to find some water somewhere to fix the pung, ... so we settled for the lavatory bowl. We soon found out where the bubbles were coming from and fixed the thing up so's Nuts could put his wheel back on. <i>(1964,Any Old Dollars Mister?,113</i>)

### punga
 
 <i>n.</i> an anchor

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> <i>(1928,Where the White Man Treads,130. 169</i>)

>  <b>BUCK</b> <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,2/207</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a common misspelling of ponga (See [ponga](per-pop.md#ponga))

>  .. by the felling and uprooting of a punga tree, they accidentally came across the leader referred to. <i>(Jul 29, 1868,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  The first thing that called my attention was a punga which fell near me. <i>(Oct 19, 1867,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> It was now that I heard the popping of corks ..., and when we arrived at a punga summerhouse ..., Betty herself reached beneath the seat and placed on the table a tiny bottle of cherry brandy ... . <i>(1965,Memoirs of a Peon,66</i>)

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> They would.. uncrown the graceful punga, or "bungy", as it is called in local parlance. <i>(1928,Dawn Beloved,27</i>)

>  A crumbling chimney, the uprights of a boat landing and the moss-encrusted punga of a temporary bird-holding pen are all that's left of Henry's residence. <i>(Aug 3, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),34</i>)

>  Naturally, clients who actually leave messages don't need you urgently, or they would have spoken to someone else in your office by now - someone who wasn't up a punga at Anakiwa or empathising on a marae. <i>(Aug 7, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),13</i>)

>  In Karamea the locals decorated the beautiful wooden hall with punga. <i>(Jul 17, 1993,Listener (New Zealand Listener),38</i>)

>  In the North Island, it is better known by its Maori name, ponga, sometimes corrupted into punga. <i>(May 11, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,15</i>)

>  He says the most interesting inhabitants were the "spooky but friendly punga people". The who? "They're carved into punga logs with paua shell eyes. They live in the forest,"... <i>(Sep 5, 2004,Sunday  Star Times: Escape,12</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> We crossed the flat and entered a narrow path where high grasses brushed against our knees as we rode past, and every few yards streams ran across it in inverted culverts of punga logs. <i>(1911,Speaking for Ourselves,156</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> They would first rig up a platform.. and on top of it they put trunks of punga fern, because they gave grip to the feet. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,156</i>)

### punga fence
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a fence comprised of ponga logs. (See [punga; scrub fence](../P#punga;-scrub-fence))

>  <b>CHUDLEIGH</b> Made a cow bale in the morning, worked at the punga fence in the afternoon. <i>(Nov 4, 1882,Chudleigh Diary,314</i>) [NOTES: (1950)]

### punga house
 
 <i>n.</i> [see punga]

>  <b>LANGDALE-HUNT</b> It was only a punga house with a corrugated iron roof, but beautifully kept inside and the cooking was done with a colonial oven. <i>(1985,Last Entail Male,130</i>)

>  (The Coroner took down that the footprints led from the direction of the punga house, the nearest point in the direction indicated.) <i>(Jul 8, 1898,Colonist (Nelson),2</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> We drove to the little accommodation-house at the edge of the bush - a punga house, i.e. built of fern logs on end, filled in with moss and grass. <i>(1911,Through South Westland,42</i>)

### punga hut
 
 <i>n.</i> [see ponga]

>  A few trees were left standing about the tents for shade, but since the felling had been finished to some distance from the camps, I have advised the men to build for themselves rough punga huts, not that I thought they were in any danger where they were, but that I thought they would be more comfortable, and be nearer their work if they made huts further up the road. <i>(Feb 27, 1875,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

### punga whare
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [punga](pop-pyo.md#punga))

>  Mr. F. A. Bremer said he had known Mr. Pearce in the days when the latter lived in a tent and a punga whare. <i>(Oct 31, 1908,Hawera & Normanby Star,5</i>)

>  This is a sure sign that the district is emerging from what may be called the packhorse and punga whare stage. <i>(Feb 7, 1899,Taranaki Herald,3</i>)

>  First a punga whare would be erected, to be replaced later by a two-roomed house.. from pit-sawn timber. <i>(Dec 26, 1928,NZ Free Lance,36</i>)

>  In early 1853 the Taita settlement was a sporadic settlement of punga whares amongst thick forest and man-made clearings. <i>(Feb 2004,NZ Memories,No. 46/55</i>)

>  It was an ordinary punga whare. <i>(Jul 21, 1888,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  For the first year or two he is compelled to live in a punga whare or a tent. <i>(Oct 7, 1904,Taranaki Herald,8</i>)

### punga-punga
 
 <i>n.</i> ponga

>  <b>Reischek</b> The first night in this bush I camped under a punga-punga (fern-tree). <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,193,1933 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN & FENWICK</b> We made our way .... over the well made track of punga-pungas (or in the vernacular of the camp `bungies'.) <i>(1892,Holiday Trip to the Catlins District, A,15</i>)

### pungapunga
 
 <i>n.</i> pollen of raupo

>  The pollen also of the flowers of the large Bulrush (<i>Typha angustifolia</i>) was ... collected ... by the Southern tribes, and made into large gingerbread like cakes, called Pungapunga. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/31</i>) [NOTES: Colenso Botany North Isd]

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Pungapunga, <i>Geolog. Surv.</i> Pollen of <i>Typha angustifolia</i>. <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The root of the raupo ..., the swamp-grass of which the Maori construct their whare[e-grave], is edible ... Punga-punga, the pollen of the raupo, used to be made into bread. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/210</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Punga-punga, n.</i> Maori name for the pollen of the <i>raupo</i> (q.v.). <i>(1898,Austral English,372</i>)

>  <i>~ n.</i> the pollen of a plant used by the Maoris for making a kind of cake; the cake so made. [Maori] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,9</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> The inner parts and roots of the raupo (<i>Typha orientalis</i>) provided a meagre amount of food, but the seed heads were prized for their bright yellow pollen which was made into loaves known as<i> pungapunga. </i> <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,5</i>)

>  Another highly curious article of vegetable food was the <i>pungapunga</i>, the yellow pollen of the <i>raupo</i> flowers- the common bulrush, or cat's reed mace (<i>Typha augustifolia</i>... To use it as food it is mixed up with water into cakes and baked. It is sweetish and light, and reminds one strongly of London gingerbread. <i>(1881,TrNZI,XIII/28</i>)

### puniu
 
 <i>n.</i> a fern

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Polystichum vestitum</i> PRICKLY SHIELD FERN ... Puuniu ...Large spreading crowns of long, harsh-textured, glossy dark green fronds, a metre or more long on the largest plants ... <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,424</i>)

### punk
 
 <i>n.</i> putawa

>  It is a good plan to put some wood or coal ashes in the pig pen every week, for the hogs to root over and eat, or throw in some chunks of charcoal and punk or rotten wood for them to gnaw at pleasure. <i>(Mar 18, 1876,Otago Witness,18</i>)

>  A lecturer aptly demonstrates the theory that heat generates motion by pointing to a boy who accidentally sat down on a piece of lighted punk. <i>(Nov 4, 1881,Taranaki Herald,4</i>)

>  <b>MACKIE</b> We had no lucifer matches but my friend had some fungus called `punk' which is very inflammable and shooting this from a pistol it ignited. <i>(Jul 23, 1853,Traveller under Concern,1973,125 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> [W.H.S. Roberts writes] we also carried `touch-paper' ..., as well as a fungus that grew on the black birch called punk, or by the Maoris putawa. <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,155</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> `Punk' [a fungus] taken from the trees was at hand everywhere in the bush, and was used for a [home-made cricket] ball. Large pieces of punk two or three pounds in weight would cut out three balls. ... [Carterton, Wairarapa, 1880] <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,156</i>)

>  <b>FULLER</b> ... a dried bracket fungus - <i>Polyporus hasularis</i>, called punk by the European settlers and bushmen, was collected and prized for its slow-burning properties. <i>(1978,Maori Food and Cookery,15</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> Little has been recorded of the bracket fungus <i>[Polyporus] </i>and its importance in the generation of fire. ... Dr G.B. Cone has spoken to an old Pakeha bushman who has used it in the Thames district where it was known as `punk' to the early settlers, who learned of its use from the Maoris. Punk burns very slowly and persistently. It can be put out only when smothered. ... Punk has now been recognised as the dried flesh of the <i>Polyporus hosularus<i> which may be found in many parts of the North Island. <i>(1966,Maori Life and Custom,61</i>)

### punnet
 
 <i>n.</i> also called pottle, chip a small squarish container, such as is used to hold strawberries. a `strawberry box'; originally a small chip basket for holding (usually) berry fruit; chip; pottle

>  <i>(1944,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),72</i>)

>  <b>MATTHEWS</b> As many as twenty punnets of fruit have been picked from a single [boysenberry] vine. <i>(1943,NZ Gardening Dictionary, The,25</i>)

>  <i>(1944,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),72</i>)

>  <b>HALL</b> Square basket in which strawberries are sold. In Dunedin they are <i>`pottles'</i>, in Christchurch `punnets', and in the North Island `chips'. <i>(May 1951,Letter</i>) [NOTES: ca1951 May Letter Miss G Hall]

>  <b>TURNER</b> The words<i> pottle, punnet</i> and <i>chip</i> are all used for a container for strawberries and investigation of the regional distribution of these words might be of interest.... <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,178</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>punnet</i>: The North Island term for a SMALL BOX OF BERRIES, e.g. strawberries (see: <i>pottle, chip</i>) <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,83</i>)

>  <i>punnet </i> <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: Goldie Brown Collection]

>  <i>punnet n. Chiefly Brit</i>. a small basket for fruit. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,9I24</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 613; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 393]]

>  <b>BIRKENHEAD CITY COUNCIL</b> Later they switched to a cardboard punnet [for strawberries] and I think it's still a cardboard punnet. A punnet held approximately a pound. <i>(1988,Back Then..,I : 19</i>)

>  There was an instance where customers told a retailer they didn't like buying strawberries in punnets because they couldn't tell if the ones at the bottom were squishy or not. <i>(Jan 27, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),22</i>)

### punt
 
 <i>n.</i> have/take a punt at/on, to have a go, to try out; to take a chance on [ca1978 Dec 4 (Karl Du Fresue) (from? rugby; or? racing) ca1983 August (Teachers' Club, Wellington) I'll take a punt on that [raffle].]

>  My sister took a punt by asking me, and I think I surprised a lot of people that day, including myself. <i>(May 30, 2001,Capital Times,5</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a flat-bottomed barge with no internal engine-power

>  <b>MARSDEN</b> On the following day (August 21st) all our works went on well, and every preparation was made for completing the punt with all possible dispatch, in order that the stores might be landed and the settlements formed at Kiddee Kiddee and Manowowra. <i>(1819,Letters & Journals,151,1932 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HIGGINS</b> ... the other is crossed by a floating bridge or punt as we call them pulled by pulleys and ropes ... <i>(Dec 27, 1858,Letters</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  <b>MANDER</b> Bruce had stopped punt building on the beach, .. <i>(1920,Story of a NZ River, The,67,1974 ed.</i>)

>  This vessel, the first Marsden says, `ever built upon the northern island of New Zealand,' was a 20-ton flat-bottom punt. <i>(1948,Our Open Country,5</i>)

>  <b>MUNDY</b> ... Mr Jim Ryan, who at one time used to be a punt man crossing the Buller for traffic between Westport and Reefton. <i>(1953,Days That Are No More,30</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> The[??] deciding that local school 4-1/2 miles away (and across the mighty Molyneaux River) was there at Moa Flat, with access to the Punt, a two boated iron contraption, running on two iron pulleys, on heavy steel cables and with a wide wooden deck, could be used daily. <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,26</i>)

### punt out
 
 <i>v.</i> to kick out; to sack

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> Punt him out. [sc. a useless person] <i>(1905,Sons o' Men,132</i>)

### punter
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BAKER</b> [Our Underworld slang includes] <i>punter</i>, an assistant of a pickpocket who diverts the victim's attention while robbery is committed. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,52</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Punter</i>. 6. pickpocket's assistant: New Zealand c.: C. 20. B., 1941. Ex sense 1. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1146</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> That pickpocket's assistant who, while the pickpocket operates, diverts the victim's attention: New Zealand: since ca. 1910. Sidney J. Baker, new Zealand Slang, 1941. Ex the sense of punter recorded in prec. entry. <i>(1949,Dictionary of the Underworld,538,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <i>punter n</i>. now, one who backs racehorses; once, pickpocket's assistant who diverted one's attention. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),16</i>)

### punua
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BULLER</b> LARUS DOMINICANUS. (SOUTHERN BLACK-BACKED GULL.) ... <i>Native names.</i> Karoro; the young bird distinguished as Ngoiro, {Koiro, and Punua}. [271] This fine Gull, which ranges over the whole Southern Hemisphere, is extremely plentiful. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,270,1888 2nd edn 2/47 ed.</i>)

### punui
 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Stilbocarpa lyallii</i>, a plant most commonly growing in islands south of the New Zealand mainland.

>  The punui [on the Snares] is a strong growing herb, which resembles ... the punui of Stewart's island. <i>Ibid.</i> 1/163 The most striking herbaceous plant is ... the punui, <i>Aralia Lyallii</i> T. Kirk, var. <i>robusta</i>, the large orbicular leaves of which are sometimes two feet in diameter. <i>(1891,NZ Journal of Science,1/143</i>)

>  For the sake of conciseness, in the following paragraphs the native name<i> punui</i> will be restricted to the Stewart Island plant; the generic name <i>Stilbocarpa</i> to that from the Auckland Islands. <i>(1884,TrNZI,17/293</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk)]

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Stilbocarpa polaris (The Polar Stilbocarpa)</i> ... Auckland, Antipodes, Macquarie, and Campbell Islands. ... Maori name <i>Punui. </i> <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,317,1940 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WODZICKI</b> Goats have also been placed...on small mutton bird islands in order to destroy punui <i>(Stilbocarpa lyallii)</i> and thus improve the islands as mutton bird grounds (R. Trail, <i>in litt.</i>, 1947) <i>(1950,Introduced Mammals of NZ,155</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> The PUNUI (<i>Stilbocarpa</i>), an araliad with circular leaves measuring on occasion two feet across, ... occupies [the Auckland and Campbell Islands] as well as the coast of Stewart Island ... . <i>(1961,Flora NZ,240,1929 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DEMPSEY</b> A deft shake ... and the [muttonbird] bodies were ... neatly packed into casks lined with the large leaves of the punui, a plant which grew near the hut. <i>(1964,Little World of Stewart island,50</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>Stilbocarpa lyallii</i>, the punui of Stewart Island, has less hairy leaves and reddish-purple flowers. On the mutton-bird islands where there is low <i>Olearia </i>forest, these plants can form a dense undergrowth ... <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,96</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Stilbocarpa lyalli</i> PUUNUI ...Robust herb forming large clumps up to 1 m or more tall. <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,126</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Stilbocarpa lyalli</i> PUUNUI ...Robust herb forming large clumps up to 1 m or more tall. <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,126</i>)

>  <b>SANSOM</b> Herekopare and Woman's Island are the only two beneficiary muttonbird islands. All the rest are Crown lands. ... I know ... that the fierce nettle also grows on Herekopare. Other lovely ... plants are there too - the Stewart Island yellow senecio, teteaweka of course, ... Cook's health-giving scurvy grass, soft native tatahi grass and punui, the giant-leafed umbrella-like araliad. <i>(1982,In the Grip of an Island,69</i>)

>  While my punui (<i>Stilbocarpa lyallii</i>) is out of its comfort zone, it had grown well for three years since it arrived as a seed from Putauhinu, one of the most southern titi (muttonbird) islands. Back on the mainland, my punui was growing well under the cover of a sinuous brachyglottis - until the heat of our La Nina summer overwhelmed it. <i>(Apr 2008,NZ Gardener,91</i>)

>  The punui of the Auckland Islands, with leaves sometimes 12 in. broad, and purple-eyed flowers, and its near relation on Stewart Island, are important members of this family. <i>(Oct 12, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,17</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> wheki-ponga, a tree-fern

>  <b>MARTIN</b> The second species [of <i>Dicksonia</i>] known as the PUNUI or WHEKI-PONGA (<i>D. fibrosa</i>?) ... <i>(1961,Flora NZ,109,1929 ed.</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Cyathea <i>Cunninghamii</i>, Hook.fil. Nat. name, `Punui', <i>Col. </i> <i>(1855,Flora NZ,II Flowerless Plants 7</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Punui, <i>Col[enso]. Cyathea Cunninghamii. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)

### pup
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a small round sheaf-stack.

>  <b>HAMILTON</b> A machine under the charge of an inexperienced man would deliver sheaves that greatly varied in size .. some extra large, others, called pups by the stookers, extra small. <i>(1952,History of Northern Southland,44</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> pup: a miniature of the round, conical-topped sheaf-stack. Sheaves, sheaf-stacks, and stooks are all rarely seen now. What could not be included in the last full-sized stack to be built was made into a pup. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,64</i>)

### pupu
 
 <i>n.</i> a trumpet made from a shell

>  It's Tama Iti who picks up the pupu and blows a low note into the morning. <i>(Jun 28, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>KOHERE</b> We get sea-foods: crayfish, paua, pupu,limpet and karengo the edible seaweed andsea-eggs. <i>(1951,Autobiography of a Maori,127</i>)

>  The frame is cobbled with dark-green opercula from the shellfish known as bubu and is as much a part of the coast as of the picture. <i>(Jul 12, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),61</i>)

>  <b>MALCOLM</b> Shell fish, such as pipis and pupu, would be taken out of their shells and strung up to dry in the sun for two or three days. <i>(1982,Where it all Began,93</i>)

>  <b>HULME & MORRISON</b> You learn it from the omnipresent fact of death - bubu eats seaweed, gull eats bubu, cat gets gull. <i>(1989,Homeplaces,62</i>)

>  There was a mussel bed there, too, just near the Kakaho Stream, but we mainly went after pupu [cat's eyes]. We used to rake them up into a pile, but you couldn't do it now. <i>(Feb 15, 2000,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  In between the ordinary was a new array of extraordinary - wasp caviar, stinging nettle soup, bubu sea snails, and beef-brain fritters. <i>(Mar 12, 2001,Dominion,1</i>)

>  <b>COLLINS</b> E-poo-poo, <i>Shells. </i> <i>(1804,English Colony in NSW,561</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,197</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> The common name for all fish is <i>ika</i>, or <i>ngohengohe</i>; of all univalve shells, <i>pupu</i>; and of bivalves, <i>pipi</i> and <i>anga</i>, which includes both kinds. <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui,415</i>)

>  <b>McNAUGHTON</b> The younger children.. would look about in the rock pools for pupu, each one of them hoping to find the biggest and the best. <i>(1986,Countless Signs,42</i>) [NOTES: From Patricia Grace (1975) : Waiariki]

>  She'd have admired his every kina, even his pupu, he loved her gentle praise... <i>(Nov 20, 1989,Listener (New Zealand Listener),35</i>)

>  [It was stated in the Marine Department Annual Report] that 12,116,000 borers and 10,660 pupus had been destroyed during the year. Mr. Broadfoot wanted to know how this total had been estimated. "Did you count them?" he asked Mr. Semple, "and having got them did you brand or earmark them?" "As a matter of fact, we leg-ironed them," remarked Mr. Semple with complete gravity. <i>(Oct 20, 1941,NZEF Times,I/17 : 8</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> catseye

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> White seashore sandhills, representing the accumulated deaths of ages of pipi, pupu, kuku, and other molluscs, mingled with the disintegrated mountain and volcanic quartz - ... <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,2</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> They ate many kinds of shellfish such as bubu and pipi. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,22</i>)

>  <b>SANSOM</b> They had no gun but they cooked limpets and bu-bus (the catseye shell) and a few pauas to keep them going. <i>(1970,Stewart Islanders,134</i>)

>  <b>HAPI LOVE</b> <i>bubu</i> up North <i>pupu,</i> the local name for a shellfish like the catseye only bigger <i>(Oct 12, 1972,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: ca1972 Oct 12 Hapi Love New City Hotel, Blenheim]

>  <b>FULLER</b> CATSEYES, PRESERVED (PUPU) The catseye (<i>Lunella smarada</i>) is a small helical shellfish common on rocky beaches. <i>(1978,Maori Food and Cookery,49</i>)

>  She would've admired his every kina, even his pupu ... <i>(Nov 20, 1989,Listener (New Zealand Listener),35</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> The rich marine life of the New Zealand shoreline provided the Maori with a vast array of shellfish: mussels, paua, pipi, toheroa, tuatua, tuangi, oysters, scallops, kina (sea eggs) and pupu (catseyes). <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,12</i>)

>  <b>SANSOM</b> No knob [on the supplejack stick] ... so green ... fitted on a suitable shell, usually a whelk or a cat's eye shell, the lunella (`Bu-bu' to us) for a hand-grip ... <i>(1982,In the Grip of an Island,144</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> 196 Pupu = edible green snail, also called a catseye <i>(1983,Bone People, The,467</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> the shellfish periwinkle (<i>turbo granosus</i>).

>  Pupu - the shellfish periwinkle (turbo granosus). <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,62</i>) [NOTES: c1880 South Island Maori Lists `Wahi Mahika Kai' in 1945 Beattie Maori Place-names of Canterbury 62]

>  I tell them.. how to make a kete, how to target the pupu [periwinkle], how to turn the rocks back to their usual position, why you don't yell and carry on. <i>(May 1, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (place name) (See [NZOD Waikoropupu Springs](../N#nzod-waikoropupu-springs))

### pupuharakeke
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Pu[u]pu[u]-harakeke, Placostylus hongii.</i>, flax snail

>  <b>POWELL</b> PUPUHARAKEKE or FLAX SNAIL (<i>Placostylus hongii</i>). ... reddish-brown coloured snail about 3 inches in height. These snails are vegetarian and feed largely upon fallen karaka leaves. They are found hidden under leaves and around sedges, but only in flax when there is no other cover. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,33</i>)

>  The flax snail, pupuharakeke, a species endemic to the Te Paki region. <i>(May 1985,Forest & Bird,10</i>)

>  Unuwhao has its own unique fauna. Best known are the large land snails: two sub-species of pupuharakeke (<i> Placostylus ambagiosus </i>) survive only there, along with pupurangi (<i> Paryphanta busbyi watti </i>). <i>(May 1985,Forest & Bird,9</i>)

### pupurangi
 
 <i>n.</i> kauri snail one of a group of large carnivouous snails found only in New Zealand. [Maori pupurangi[u's-macron]]

>  FOR SALE At the Stores of the Undersigned, a few good specimens of the New Zealand Pupurangi Shell. <i>(Nov 20, 1850,New Zealander, The,1</i>)

>  Sir, - I am informed that there exists in New Zealand a whistling snail, called by the Maoris a pupurangi. .. I am told this snail is a tree-climber, and that the natives procure them by posting themselves below the trees in which the pupurangi are located, and that on their whistling to them the snails fall to the ground, and are then greedily devoured by their captors, as their flavour is said to be curiously delicious. <i>(Oct 7, 1871,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> PUPURANGI or KAURI SNAIL (<i>Paryphanta busbyi</i>). A North Auckland representative of a group of large carnivorous snails found only in New Zealand. The shell is a flattened spiral of dark greenish colour, about 2 1/2 to 3 inches in diameter. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,32</i>)

>  <i>(Sep 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,285/2</i>) [NOTES: (OEDS)]

>  For the benefit of the inquirer, I may state that the pupurangi of the Maoris is Helix busber, and the largest land-snail in New Zealand. I have three specimens, and am willing to sell one or two at the price of GBP1 5s. each, having refused GBP1 1s. repeatedly. <i>(Nov 18, 1871,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  CONGIOPODUS LEUCOPAECILUS (Richardson). <i>Pigfish; Puramorua.</i> Large numbers commonly sold throughout the year in Christchurch under the name of `silver trumpeter.' Rarely seen in Dunedin markets unless other fish are scarce. ... Distribution: South Island and southern coasts of North Island. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/121</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  The Maoris call it pupurangi, snail of the skies - I suppose because the shell is sky-blue inside or because they found it up in the trees. <i>(1945,Korero (AEWS Background Bulletin),3(9) : 6</i>)

 <i>phr.</i>

### purge
 
 <i>n.</i> alcohol, booze, grog, liquor [ca1940 Les Cleveland (esp soldiers') ca1945-50 Common among Wellington University students]

>  Piously Pulverise Purge-Purveying Publicans. <i>(Nov 11, 1905,NZ Truth,21/5/4</i>)

>  <i>Plenty of Purge</i> [Title of a report of slygrogging at Ashburton]. <i>(Jan 18, 1907,NZ Truth</i>)

>  Josephine was only too willing to sell to the stage navvy, and there were in the house at the time three other men under the influence of purge. One man was helplessly `inked,' and other man was in bed with a woman. <i>(Sep 4, 1909,NZ Truth,219/6/1</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> `Come on now, force that purge down ya gullet and have a man's drink with me.' <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,43</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Great drop of purge this ... . Down the hatch. <i>Ibid.</i> 173 Green Band, Red Band, Gold Top, Double Brown, Nut Brown, ... all the colours of purge. Karitane yellow. What was he babysitting for on Friday night ...? <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,150</i>)

>  <b>GIFFORD</b> As many a joker sufferin' a return trip lunch ... will tell you, the old purge will put you flat on your back ... <i>(1976,Loosehead Len's Bumper Thump Book,31</i>)

>  One of the army of eternal swishers of the unholy purge which steals a man's brains - or, more correctly, which takes charge of the fool who has no brains - came before the Sphinx-like Riddell, S.M., who presides daily at the drunks levee in Wellington, on Wednesday. <i>(May 9, 1908,NZ Truth,4</i>)

>  <b>LACEY</b> As far as pourin' the purge down the neck there's only one way for that. <i>(1976,Loosehead Len's Bumper Thump Book,34</i>)
