### puriri
 
 <i>n.</i> formerly called New Zealand teak a large native hardwood tree growing in the North Island; the fruit forms in large clusters of red berries. [Maori puriri[u-macron]] [[PO and B.. VARIANTS X-REF]

>  ACCEPTED TENDER. - Marsden and Penwarden: Fencing, 1s 8d per chain. Posts: Rimu, GBP1 17s 6d per 100; puriri, 5s each. <i>(Sep 1, 1887,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  The posts, if possible, should be made of puriri, a wood much resembling English oak. <i>(Oct 28, 1843,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

>  Puriri Posts, house blocks and firewood(puriri, tea-tree, matai and tarariri). <i>(Sep 1907,Wairoa Bell & Northern Advertiser,Unpaged</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Pud|idi, <i>s</i>. A shrub so called. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,196</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> Pouriedee [<i>puriri</i>] is a fine hard wood like Lignum Vitae, grows into immense trees, but you can not get planks out of it as the Worms ... make places like Nail holes .... <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),33,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McDONNELL</b> The boride[e-acute] is a fine-limbed, large, and spreading tree; very crooked, close-grained, stringy, and tough; much resembling teak; of a darker [20] colour, harder, and of an oily nature. <i>(1834,Extracts From a Journal,19,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> Puriri (<i>Vitex littoralis</i>) - This tree, from its hardness and durability, has been denominated the New-Zealand Oak; and indeed it seems to answer all the purposes of that prince of trees. ... On first examining a Puriri log, you would be inclined to reject it, on account of the many large holes that at once present themselves to notice, ... <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),44</i>)

>  Many other trees abounded in Pamaki [a's-acute], such as the kaikatea [a2-acute], towai, rewa-rewa, totara [a's-acute], puriri, rata [a's-acute], kahika [a2-acute], tipow, tanikaha [a2-acute], rimu, and various kinds of akkas, all of which will be found described in the Appendix (Note 7). <i>Ibid.</i> 2/393 The <i>Puriri (Metrocideros florida or quercus Australis</i>), is a wood whose durability equals any of the timbers in the country; it is very tough and close grained, and has been termed the Oak of the Pacific; but the Teak would be more appropriate, ... <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/109</i>)

>  <b>WADE</b> The Puriri has been improperly called the New Zealand Oak. <i>(1841,Journey in NZ,40</i>)

>  <b>BRIGHT</b> .. poo-ree-dee <i>(1841,Handbook for Emigrants,54</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> About half way between Parenga-renga and the western coast there is a puriri-tree (Vitex litoralis) half buried in the sand, but still growing; ... <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/202</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> The Perriri [Puriri] <i>(1844,NZ Journal,102,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Vitex lucens</i> puredi <i>(Jul 29, 1844,Report of Select Committee on NZ</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  ... and leave me alone in the forest there is some danger that I would take root and turn into a puriri or rime. <i>(Jul 15, 1851,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/100</i>) [NOTES: (H.R. Richardson)]

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Vitex<i> littoralis</i>, Cunn. ... Nat. names, `Puriri' and Kaneree', south of the Thames, <i> Colenso.</i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 203</i>)

>  <i>puriri n. </i>New Zealand tree, the New Zealand oak or teak [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),623</i>)

>  .<i>puriri n. </i> N.Z. tree with dense, dark timber, formerly called N.Z. oak or teak. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),16</i>)

>  <i>puriri n. </i>a New Zealand tree with hard timber and red berries. [< Maori] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,925</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 613; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 393]]

>  <i>puriri</i>, a tree (Vitex lucens), said to be the only tree the rata-vine cannot strangle. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/145</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i>Pu&#769;.ri-ri</i> (pu&#769;.re.-re.; <i>colloq.</i> pu-r&emacr.&#769;.re.), <i>n</i>. [Maori.] <i>(Bot.)</i> A New Zealand verbenaceous tree (<i>vitex littoralis</i>); called also <i>bulreedy. [Local, N. Z.] </i> <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2029</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>puriri</i>: New Zealand oak, a large, handsome tree with hard timber. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,194</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> [PUREDI] <i>Vitex lucens </i> <i>(1860,Gatherings of a Naturalist</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>MANING</b> He would sit whole days on a fallen puriri near the house ... <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,(xiv) 213</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Puriri, <i>Col[enso]. Vitex littoralis. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Puriri (<i>Vitex littoralis</i>) is sometimes called `teak', or `ironwood'. ... The timber is hard, heavy, very durable, very hard to work, and of a greenish colour. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/190</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> <i>Vitex littoralis</i>, A. Cunn. Puriri.-- A large tree, 50-60 feet high; trunk 20 feet in girth. Wood hard, dark olive-brown, much used; said to be indestructible under all conditions. Grows in the northern parts of the North Island only. <i>(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,128</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> Puriri ..... <i>Order,</i> Verbenaceae; <i>Genus</i>, Vitex; <i>Species,</i> Vitex Littoralis <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,2/336</i>)

>  First come the deep glens of native bush, where great old puriri trees form homes for a myriad of birds. <i>(Dec 27, 1928,NZ Truth,15</i>)

>  Those visiting the reserve on a branch field trip are also able to climb many metres up a ladder to a tree platform high in a puriri tree... <i>(Nov 2002,Forest & Bird,306/37</i>)

>  [Native name] Puriri .. [Settlers' name] New Zealand teak .. [Family] Verbenaceae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,121</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 121]

>  <b>KIRK</b> The Puriri .... on account of the strength of its timber is sometimes termed by the settlers `N.Z. Oak', but it would be far more correct to name it `N.Z. Teak'. <i>(1889,Forest Flora,209</i>) [NOTES: (Morris)]

>  Puriri .. Vitex littoralis .. Timber a dark brown colour, very hard, heavy, strong, and durable; ... <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,13</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Puriri, n. </i>Maori name for the New Zealand tree, <i>Vitex littoralis</i>, A Cunn., <i>N.O. Verbenaceae; </i>called also <i>New Zealand Oak, New Zealand Teak</i>, and <i>Ironwood</i>. It is very hard. <i>(1898,Austral English,372</i>)

>  <b>WADE</b> <i>(1842,Journey in NZ,200</i>) [NOTES: (Hobart Town)]

>  Booreedy Jack was a specimen of the old-time bushman ... kauri-cutting, timber-jacking, pit-sawing, rafting and mill-work, etc. - ... [178] ... he acquired the soubriquet of `Booreedy' (settlers' name for puriri), which stuck to him ... <i>(Dec 1904,NZ Illustrated Magazine,177</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>V[itex] lucens, T.Kirk. ... Puriri; Kauere. </i>... A well-known tree, producing the most valuable hardwood in the colony, ... <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,565</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> Poroporo ... becomes Bulli-bulli, and Puriri becomes Boradi. <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,202</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Vitex lucens (The Puriri)</i>. A fine tree, from 50ft. to 60ft. in height, often called the <i>New Zealand Oak</i>, on account of the strength and durability of/ its timber. <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,350,1940 p., 372 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> The lancewood blades, narrow black scarred with orange, shot straight up among the tangle; many trees were old and in decay, and the little rosy apples of the <i>pyriri</i> [sic: ?erron] tree smothered the moss. <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,149</i>)

>  <i>puriri</i>: tree, sometimes known as New Zealand oak. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,175</i>)

>  <i>puriri n</i> -s [Maori] : a New Zealand ironwood (<i>Vitex littoralis</i>) yielding a very durable hard strong dark brown wood . <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1847</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Puriri .. .. puriri .. ..<i> Vitex lucens </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> ... wiwi, blackberry, clusters of puriri, a patch of solid bush, a creek, a swamp of raupo and toetoe - no patch of grass visible sufficient to graze a solitary cow. <i>(1960,Maori Girl,10</i>)

>  Who you gonna call when wasps build a huge nest at the top of a tall puriri tree just metres away from a health clinic, a school bus stop and community centre? <i>(Dec 28, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

>  They call this place Cathedral Grove, where the puriri cast deep shadows. <i>Ibid.</i> 106 Puriri groves are still used as a burial place. <i>(Jan 1992,NZ Geographic,No. 13/103</i>)

>  Generations of farmers have bent fencingstaples trying to attach their fencingwire to puriri strainer posts. So hardwas the timber.. that wire manufacturerscreated special puriri staples, which arestill in production today. <i>(Jan 1992,NZ Geographic,111</i>)

>  Well watered and Subdivided with first-class Fencing (all Puriri). <i>(Dec 30, 1919,Poverty Bay Herald,2</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> [attrib; made of puriri timber]

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> He used Gum tree from Sydney for Keels, Coudy planks, Hoc-car [aka] Timber and Kicaitore Knees, sometimes Pouriedie Keels. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),34,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LUSK</b> I took with me two stout puriri planks. <i>(May 10, 1853,Auckland Journals,136,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>Z.W. WELLS</b> Paid Brooking for puriri fencing [pounds]5.9.6. <i>(Nov 1863,Diary,62,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <i>(1952,Landfall,6/32</i>) [NOTES: E.Lee-Johnson]

>  Head piece, figured puriri veneer. <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,1632</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> AROHA: The pakeha captain got as far as here: placed a ladder against the puriri wall and climbed half way up. <i>(1963,Pohutukawa Tree,24</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> [so called 'bastard' puriri - single citation]

### puriri moth
 
 <i>n.</i> Aenetus virescens, aka ghost moth

>  The Puriri, or Ghost Moth, as it is sometimes called, is confined to the North Island where it is widely distributed, although more commonly seen in northern districts. It is New Zealand's largest moth, attaining a wing span of eight inches. <i>(Sep 16, 1972,Press, The,11/10</i>)

>  [caption] The puriri moth, or ghost moth, is a swift and powerful flier. <i>(1983,Land of the Mist,71</i>)

>  <b>KIRK</b> The growing tree is subject to the attacks of the larva of the puriri moth, which bores holes sometimes three-eighths of an inch in diameter ... <i>(1875,Durability NZ Timbers,15</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> These holes [in the puriri logs] are made by a soft-bodied grub, which develops into the puriri moth. <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,350,1940, p. 373 ed.</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> GHOST MOTH (<i>Hepialus virescens</i>), often called the Puriri Moth. This is the largest of our native moths, and it is confined to the North Island .... <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,56</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> PURIRI MOTH .. .. see Moth, Ghost ... Moth, ghost.. .. pepetuna .. .. <i>Hepialus virescens </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  The density of puriri timber doesn't stopthe grubs of the puriri moth (or "ghostmoth") from invading the living trees andriddling them with burrows. <i>(Jan 1992,NZ Geographic,113</i>)

### purler
 
 <i>n.</i> a heavy fall, a cropper (esp. come a purler) [1951 14 M 10 Wellington H.S. 16 PEARLER (2) -->1951 14 M 10 Wellington H.S. 16 PEARLER, WENT FOR A]

>  The Idler, while doing his preliminary, came a great purler, and Holmes, his jockey, was severely shaken. <i>(Aug 15, 1892,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  R.G DEANS, NEW ZEALAND. (By an Opponent.) .. I tried to get him low - / By waist, or hips, you know - / And give the youth a purler by those means, / But who e'er supplied the mirth / As a body went to earth, / I'm confident of this - it wasn't Deans. <i>(Feb 24, 1906,NZ Free Lance,20</i>)

>  Ballyhooley came a cropper at the first of the set, Songster followed suit at the second, and Waterloo turned a regular purler over the last of the three. <i>(Aug 27, 1896,Otago Witness,34</i>)

>  The grassing of Mana [football team] ... raised a hurricane of plaudits from the ... barrackers. Mana went a `perler' that time. <i>(May 19, 1906,NZ Truth,48/2/8</i>)

>  <b>ABRAHAM</b> [The Bishop [Selwyn] fell in head over ears at once. The only remarks he made ... the other, was that he now understood the full meaning of the poet's language about `<i>purling</i> brooks'. ] <i>(1856,Walk with the Bishop,3</i>) [NOTES: [? Play on <i>purl </i>to tumble, turn head-over-heels]]

>  H. ... when in mid-stream tripped, landing feat - a `purler'. <i>(Aug 1934,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,37</i>)

>  <b>COMBS</b> On the first two essays [at jumping] he came absolute `pearlers'. <i>(1939,Harrowed Toad,86</i>)

>  <b>48</b> The police seemed to have come a purler in trying to invoke the aid of doctors in tracing pregnant women. <i>(Sep 17, 1963,NZ Truth</i>)

>  ONLY one horse fell in the National Hurdles - Comedy Prince. He came down a "purler" and gave young Towler a nasty fall. <i>(Aug 19, 1926,NZ Truth,11</i>)

>  Enter Venice's finest, Commissario Brunetti, a most civilised and engaging fellow whom Leon gifts with some purlers. <i>(Apr 12, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),64</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> something fine or exceptional [1960 17C F B10 Napier Girls H.S. 18 PEARLER [delight]; ca1935 (Havelock) She's a purler. 1984 16 M E89 Pakuranga Coll. 8 PERLA [something good] 1987 Australia-West Indies cricket: a banner with `PEARLA' written on it]

>  <b>BAKER</b> In constant use by our youngsters ... swinger, pearler, stunner, ... <i>(1941,NZ Slang,51</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> One day wobbling along in a rainstorm Bert had come a purler from his grid right into the middle of a huge puddle ... <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,153</i>)

>  <i>pearler n</i>. anything exceptional. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),15</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> `Isn't she a little pearler, Dick?' Tom said `She's a beauty, all right,' I said. `One out of the box. The best looking piece I've sen since I got back. Who is she?' <i>(1976,Pacific Star,152</i>)

>  <b>MANTELL</b> `Never thought he'd ever get around to having any then they produce a purler like that. She's a real little darling.' <i>(1980,Murder or Three, A,45</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>pearler</i> superb person or thing; eg `Thanks for helping out, <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,83</i>)

### purourou
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>YATE</b> <i>Tiaki</i>, or <i>Purourou </i>- This elegant bird is about the size of the sky-lark; and its plumage ... is of glossy black; ... <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),56</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> The <i>purourou</i> agrees with our lark; but with a plumage of glossy black. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/301</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> 33. <i>Creadion carunculatus. G.R.Gray,</i> ... Tieke of the natives of Tasman Bay. <i>Quoy and Gaim. </i>Tiaka or Purourou of the natives. <i>Yate</i>. Tira-oua-ke [e-acute] of the natives. <i>Less. Tierawaki</i>, Cook's Straits. <i>Dieffenbach. Ibid</i> 192 - 35. ... Mr Polack also mentions a lark-like bird, of a black colour, under the native name of Purourou, which I do not think belongs to this genus. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/192</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/192]

>  <b>BULLER</b> CREADION CARUNCULATUS. (THE SADDLE-BACK.) ... <i>Native names.</i> Tieke, Tiraweke, {Tirauweke,} and Purourou. [151] It is naturally a noisy bird, and when excited or alarmed becomes very clamorous, hurrying through the woods with cries of `tiaki-rere', quickly repeated. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,150,1888 2nd edn 1/18 ed.</i>)

>  [Names used for Air NZ fleet aircraft include:] ZK-NAW `PUROUROU' (Saddleback) <i>(Jun 1988,Pacific Way,98</i>)

### purple death
 
 <i>n.</i> cheap red wine (cf. rooster's blood; crocodile's tears, camel's bile)

>  Any soil with the taste of this purple death couldn't even support a poisonous cactus. <i>(Sep 18, 1944,NZEF Times,IV/169 : 5</i>)

>  A squid supper, a few gallons of Purple Death, and Furbo's bid would just about do the trick. <i>(Oct 2, 1944,NZEF Times,IV/171 : 10</i>)

>  "He should be able to hold his liquor... Even Purple Death, Crocodile's Tears and Camel's Bile". "I doubt whether they drink Purple Death at Bellamy's.".. <i> Ibid.</i> 5 Every time the joker hit the copper arrangement, old Purple Death [sc. a ballet dancer] moved. <i>(Jan 22, 1945,NZEF Times,IV/187</i>)

>  He should be able to hold his liquor ... even Purple Death, [Crocodiles' Tears and Camels's Bile] <i>Ibid.</i> [glossary] Purple <i>Death</i>: Italian overproof vino. <i>(1946,Johnny Enzed in Italy,53</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Mugs up men. ... Tear into the good stuff. ... And we drank the purple death because there were more rivers to cross and more men to be maimed up there where the arty was throbbing the sky. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,92</i>)

>  <b>GEE</b> When she came in she was carrying a half-gallon jar of wine. `Purple death,' she grinned at her father. <i>(1965,Special Flower,37</i>)

### puru
 
 <i>n.</i> leg protection, a legging

>  Herries Beattie describes a different method: "This tussock was placed in the paraerae (sandal) and the foot inserted. Then the tussock was brought up the leg and tied above the ankle. This protection was called puru and the sandals were then known as tupuru." Patiti was also regarded as "a fine thing to put in the paraerae to keep the feet warm and if one was wading it was warmer with patiti around the feet than without it." <i>(Nov 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 40</i>)

>  Scirpus maritimus, the tall leafy swamp plant that favours salty estuarial situations which are subject to periodical flooding is known to the Maoris of the Northern estuaries as 'purua grass', cattle browsing it readily when allowed. <i>(May 20, 1933,NZ Journal of Agriculture,263</i>)

### Puruhauan
 
 <i>n.</i> (Geology)

>  Some of the fossils seem as old as Puruhauan: others, unpublished but secured in Otago University collections, suggest Triasic. <i>(Nov 1996,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,111/43</i>)

### purupuru
 
 <i>n.</i> variant of poroporo

>  How many readers know the native shrub, with its dainty blue flower, nasty-smelling foliage and bright orange berries - purupuru by name but commonlycalled bullabull? <i>(Mar 11, 1943,NZ Farmers' Weekly,40</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori marlinspike [ORIGIN: Maori, Williams purupuru 'caulking of a canoe']

>  By the natives the spike was, Dr Newman explained, called an ahao or purupuru. <i>(Jun 11, 1908,Grey River Argus,3</i>)

>  [The Maori implement's] genuineness has been questioned, one sceptic asserting that it was neither more nor less than a British marlinspike; but the carving was of Native pattern, and the pointed portion, instead of being conical as is common in European, was also characteristically Maori in type ... The late Archdeacon Williams had told him it was called an ahao, and he found that another name was purupuru. Only recently, through the assistance of Mr. Percy Smith and the knowledge of some old-time Maoris at New Plymouth, had he found its purpose. It was really a marlinspike used for the caulking of sea-going canoes, and having a like end in view, the Maori had developed an implement almost exactly resembling that of the Europeans. <i>(Jun 10, 1908,Otago Witness,11</i>)

### pururi
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Mr. David Goldie and myself were appointed.. to investigate the numerous complaints made by certain settlers in Pukekohe.. as to the price paid them for puriri junk timber. <i>(Mar 1, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,15</i>)

>  This lathe was at work turning puriri rollers for straining fencing wire. <i>(Sep 13, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

### push
 
 <i>n.</i> a gang of larrikins; a crowd or clique

>  <b>HARPER</b> Mention of the above term in 1890 reminds me of another side of Christchurch in those days - namely the gangs, or `pushes' as they were called - of larrikins. They wore bell-bottomed trousers, fancy shoes and well-oiled forelocks. These young ruffians frequently assaulted any lone pedestrian who happened to come their way. <i>(1946,Memories of Mountains,53</i>) [NOTES: [1890]]

>  <b>A TRAMP</b> The sporting `push' <i>(1891,Casual Ramblings,94</i>)

>  In spite of my being in with a clever `push' <i>Ibid.</i> 40 When he did see that the cad was going to chuck the push, he tried to get up. <i>(1896,Tom Bracken's Annual,38</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Push, n.</i> gang. The word is of late, very common in Australia. It was once a prison term. Barrere and Leland quote from M. Davitt's `Leaves from a Prison Diary,' `the upper ten push.' In thieves' English it is (I) a crowd; (2) an association for a particular robbery. In Australia, its use began with the larrikins (q.v.), and spread, until now it often means clique, set, party, and even jocularly so far as `the Government House Push.' [1890] <i>(1898,Austral English,372</i>)

>  I used to pack the tucker to the `Perseverance Push', ... <i>(1960,Penguin Book of NZ Verse,93</i>) [NOTES: c1890-1900 Edwin Edwards `Waitekauri Every Time']

>  There used to be much more of the push element around Christchurch than there is now, and it frequently occurred that pushes [ie youths belonging to these groups] like the `Tigers,' the `Diehards,' the `Scalpers,' and the `Skull-Draggers,' faced the music before the beak. <i>(Feb 3, 1912,NZ Truth,345/7/3</i>)

>  Don't pledge your votes to anyone till you've heard McCardy's address to the people, not to the push. <i>(Sep 25, 1913,Hutt Valley Independent,4</i>)

>  <b>GRACE</b> Jake would say to the youth of his `push' ... <i>(1914,Tale of Timbertown,12</i>)

>  <i>push n.</i> a clique; a set; a political party; a gang of roughs. <i>(1912,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,9</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> <i>(1941,NZ Slang</i>)

>  <i>push n</i>. gang of larrikins (q.v.); by extension, a clique, set, party. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),16</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> 7. We struggled through the noisy push/ And had a friendly booze,/ ... <i>(1947,Pioneer Recollections,55,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Push</i> (push), <i>n. [Add.] 5.</i> A gang of larrikins. <[Colloq.] 6. </i>A company; a group; a set of people. <i>[Slang] </i> <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2029</i>)

>  Rumour was current on Tuesday night that an old man had been set upon by a horde of "louts" and submitted to a course of treatment similar to that which might be expected from the members of a Sydney or Melbourne "push." <i>(Aug 17, 1893,Wanganui Chronicle,2</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  `Push', `bash', `crash' occur pretty frequently in describing progress in accounts of tramping trips, and tell a tale of scrub and tangled bush. <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,30</i>) [NOTES: Ruth Mason `Track and Trail]

### Push Play
 
 <i>n.</i> a fitness campaign by Sport and Recreation New Zealand (SPARC) to promote the benefits of regular physical activity. [from <i>push</i> the <i>play</i> button on a video recorder; also as verb + noun (see 2008 cit.)]

>  National Push Play Day on Friday marked the end of a month-long campaign to get Kiwis more active and Chronicle reporter Mike Hutson went along to the Levin Mall on Saturday lunchtime to find out if the campaign was successful. <i>(Nov 8, 2005,Horowhenua Chronicle,3</i>) [NOTES: Check re paper title: this is from Horowhenu-Kapiti Chronicle]

>  "The Push Play message is about having fun while you exercise." <i>(Nov 8, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,7</i>)

>  Push play day for Kawerau women ... Today, Friday, November 4, form 12.05pm to 12.50pm is push play day. <i>(Nov 4, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,8</i>)

>  People don't have to be in line for a gold medal to feel good when they push play. <i>(2008,Mana,84/50</i>)

 <i>phr.</i> [and variants, see McGill]

### pussy's tit
 
 <i>exclam.</i> an exclamation of disbelief, esp. when one suspects that another is attempting to pull one's leg.  <i>n.</i>

### put (one's) pot on
 
 <i>phr.</i> to inform on, to tell on; cf. put (one's) weights up

>  `Tongue-murdering' appears to be a new term applied to slandering a man, or `putting his pot on' with intent to do him harm. <i>(Nov 11, 1905,NZ Truth,21/5/8</i>)

>  The first [indecent post-] card was enough to put his pot on. <i>(Sep 8, 1906,NZ Truth,64/5/6</i>)

>  Walsh was abusing Mrs. Kerr, the landlady, when she said she would `put his pot on' with the police. The term is familiar, but is believed to have a highly respectable origin, `Put his pot on!' being the death warrant uttered by the chief when the early cannibals proposed having boiled missionary for dinner. <i>(Jun 13, 1908,NZ Truth,156/6/1</i>)

>  <b>REES</b> You ought to put the Liquor Party's pot on. <i>(1913,Merry Marauders,206</i>)

>  Squarehead and Co. had a try to rope in Tom Wilford to put our pot on. Tom W. smelt a rat and wasn't to be had that way. <i>(Apr 17, 1915,Hutt Valley Independent,3</i>)

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> Next time I saw him with a girl I was ... not to come deliberately and `put his pot on' <i>(1938,Me and Gus,65,1925 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SMYTH</b> Dalton has put your pot on. [i.e., told the boss] <i>(1928,Jean of the Tussock Country,59</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> ... I played one of my [prison] pals for the life of a girl `who had done him wrong' or, even in more classical language, had `put his pot on with the police'. <i>(1945,In Prison,103</i>)

>  `I been there about the same time as you ... and I haven't had a rise yet. Wonder if Myers put my pot on.' <i>(1962,Landfall Country,114</i>) [NOTES: (Ballantyne `And the Glory')]

>  <b>TURNER</b> Reliable news is <i>the dinkum oil</i>, to inform on a person is <i>to put his pot on,</i> ... <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,48</i>)

>  `If Mrs Moore and her child are living in the gully no one would put her pot on. They would just accept it and say nothing about it,' she said. <i>(Aug 4, 1974,Sunday Times (NZ),3/4</i>)

>  <b>MARSH</b> `Reckonin' she'll put my pot on with the boss.' <i>(1980,Photo-Finish,173</i>)

>  put his pot on with the boss <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  <i>pot ... put a person's pot on NZ & Austral. sl. </i>inform on him. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,588</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>MITCALFE</b> The cheek of them! I didn't `put their pot on', ... <i>(1985,Hey Hey Hey,121</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>phr.</i> to do (one or oneself) a disadvantage; to ruin things (for one or oneself)

>  <b>HISLOP</b> It also shows how a smart Alec, by saying too much, can put his own pot on if he is not careful. [He lost a sale.] <i>(1943,Pure Gold,91</i>)

>  <b>Grace</b> ...the falling-down house in the trees that Rua was giving up for the Friday night so that Maina could arrange a meeting that would put her husband's pot on, and, she hoped, teach the no-good crook a lesson. <i>(2001,Dogside Story,113</i>)

### put (one's) weights up
 
 <i>phr.</i> to inform on, tell on, expose; get (one) into trouble [possibly from racing or bookmaking referring to the putting up or publishing the weights to be carried by horses so that punters can read the facts for themselves; or perhaps, from raising the handicap weights carried so making things more difficult both for the horse and for the bettor who fancies it: perhaps (though very unlikely) from a general analogy of tipping the balance, making the true weight (or, metaphorically, character) of somebody known, as one finds out a true weight literally when one `tips' old-fashioned scales by raising the weights and the thing to be weighed off the surface into a weighing position; ca1952 June Wellington I'll put his weights up with his cobbers tomorrow about his flirting.]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Got the pricker with me because I put his weights up - A terrible stick man, that Tank - Woppit Upper - <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,163</i>)

>  <i>weight ... 3. put person's weights up NZ colloq.</i> inform on (someone). <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,875</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>McGILL</b> weights, phr. -->putting your weights up putting someone in trouble; possibly from weights carried in handicap horse races; eg `I'd watch Monk if I was you, he'd put your weights up with the cops soon as look at you.' Australian miners c.1925. ANZ. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,121</i>)

>  <b>Grace</b> ...they'd been given everything they needed from Maina, who, once she'd decided to put her husband's weights up, had done it in a big way. <i>(2001,Dogside Story,187</i>)

### put (one) crook
 
 <i>phr.</i> to put (one) in the wrong, on the wrong track; to lead astray

>  Give me three bob. I'll put you crook the first time you ask me for a tip on a racecourse, you beauty. <i>(Dec 19, 1914,NZ Truth,12</i>)

>  "I put you crook last week when I told you Dodgers had won the comp. There was another week remaining - and just as well .. it was the game of the season.." <i>(Mar 19, 2010,Hawke's Bay Today,A11</i>)

>  Of a wrong answer given by a contestant: `The audience put you crook, boy.' <i>(1960,2ZB</i>) [NOTES: Wellington Selwyn Toogood]

>  <b>CRUMP</b> We'll follow the pack-horse from here on and hope he doesn't put us crook. <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,86</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> `I might of known it,' he said. `He's put us crook. ... That's not Gum Bay.' <i>(1965,Among the Cinders,187</i>)

>  "Jeez, mate, sounds like someone's put you crook don't blame you for packing a sad..." <i>(Feb 14, 2009,Bay of Plenty Times,/on NZ Herald website/Knowledge Basket</i>)

>  Tarakahi are on the popular possies and it seems I may have put you crook with the GPS co-ordinates for The Bubbles inshore and behind White Island. The correct ones are 37-51.981S 176-58.678E. <i>(Oct 20, 2010,Daily Post (Rotorua),A19</i>)

>  I'm, afraid, Mr Browne, that your friend has put you crook. I think. As yet, no decision has been made that might remove the use of rainwater tanks and force Auckland residents to use the reticulated water, at a cost, of course. <i>(Aug 24, 2010,NZ Herald,A7</i>)

### put (one) in crook with
 
 <i>phr.</i> to put someone in bad with, to reduce one's standing in the eyes of someone else, also be in crook with. [ca1930 (Harold Griffiths, p.c. Dec 12, 1959) `You wouldn't put me crook with him, would you?']

>  <b>SLATTER</b> I had shaken him off as soon as we arrived in London on the U.K. leave scheme from Italy because, when he gets on it, he is likely to put a man in crook with the provosts. <i>(1970,On the Ball,134</i>)

>  [Aussie speaks] `Stone the crows. I've got no monkey suit. I dont want to put you in crook, boy, but ...' [sic] <i>(1978,Listener Short Stories,2/20</i>) [NOTES: 1951 Young in 1978 Listener Short Stories Vol 2 20]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Got a snitch on me and put me in crook with the boss. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,91</i>)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> To refer to a person, to his disadvantage, with the third person. .in 4;`You'd made it look as if I'd been having a good time with Val while she was away. You silly bastard, now you've put me in crook with her.' <i>(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],895</i>) [NOTES: (1962-63)1974 Syder and Hodgetts Aust and NZ English [TS] 895]

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> He's me cobber, and right now he's in crook with his bosses in Wellington on account of him being too lenient on the after-hours liquor trade round here. <i>(1965,Among the Cinders,202</i>) [NOTES: [be in crook with]]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Tried to put me in crook with the missus - I'm awake up to that one - <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,164</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> I had shaken him off as soon as we arrived in London on the U.K. leave scheme from Italy because, when he gets on it, he is likely to put a man in crook with the provosts. <i>(1970,On the Ball,134</i>)

### put (one) off the board
 
 <i>phr.</i> to dismiss or stand down someone from shearing

>  <b>BOWEN</b> No learner has ever been put off the board because he has shorn sheep well, but many have lost their run through over-fast, hurried, slip-shod, and rough shearing. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,46</i>)

### put (one) on the coat
 
 <i>phr.</i> to ostracise, excommunicate someone (in prison)

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> This is one of the reasons that informal social control mechanisms at Paremoremo are so strong. Anybody who deviates from the required standard of thought or behaviour - the criminal standard - is quickly observed and `put on the coat' by the rest of the community. At Paremoremo you have to learn quickly to conform to criminal norms or live the life of a pariah. <i>Ibid.</i> 130 A lot of my old mates ... disapproved totally of what was happening and put me on the coat. We remained on speaking terms, but it wasn't the same as it had been before. . <i>Ibid.</i> 246 Coat (n) Put on the Coat. Ostracise, excommunicate. In the sense of reprimand, British prison slang early in this century. <i>See also</i> JACKET. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,66</i>)

### put a ring around
 
 <i>phr.</i> (informal) expressing certainty, strong agreement, etc. [? from hoop-la; or from ringing e.g. a calendar or diary date; or marking off something as one's own] [esp. in 2nd person you can put a ring around it/that]

>  <b>McALPINE</b> There are so many writers trying to get in [sc. to Mills and Boon publications], but with a number of people you can put a ring around it: they write what they want and it's not what the publishers want. <i>(1998,Passionate Pen, The,104</i>)

>  <b>KEINZLY</b> `There's still a couple of hundred that haven't told me yet - but they will tonight. You can put a ring around it!' <i>(1960,Tangahano,131</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Up there will be all those Wairarapa supporters with drooling green ribbons, coming down to see their team win the Ranfurly Shield. You can put a ring around that one they kept on saying in the saloon bar last night. <i>Ibid.</i> 60 Gotta get a good possie on the bank and see the boys win. Ya can put a ring around that one. <i>Ibid.</i>... if it had been she'd have had kids all right. You can put a ring around that one. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,17</i>)

>  <b>O'SULLIVAN</b> `It had better be good weather then,' Latty said. `You can put a ring round that,' Len said. <i>(1978,Boy, The Bridge, The River, The,130</i>)

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> '.. Made recently by a truck, I'd say' `Yes, I reckon you can put the ring aroud that.' ... . `I beg your pardon.' ... ` `Put the ring aroud that' ? ` / `Only that I agree with you ... Like, you hit the nail on the head.' <i>(1980,Penfriend,13</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> George pulled a wry face, `You can put a ring around that, matey!' [agreeing that the pig couldn't be transported] <i>(1982,Wild Pig,45</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> But we'll nail the bastard [sc a rogue boar]. You can put a ring around that! <i>(1984,Razorback,63</i>)

>  If I kept a diary, today would have a red ring round it. Not because it's anyone's birthday, ...] <i>(Apr 4, 1987,Dominion,8</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> you can put a ring around that; from ringing some item with a pencil so it stands out on the page; ref. Gordon Slatter; c.1925 <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,127</i>)

>  Sir Robert Muldoon says: `We'll get back. You can put a ring round it.' <i>(Jul 14, 1984,TV1</i>)

>  [Hdg] You can put a ring round that. <i>(Feb 5, 1977,Listener (New Zealand Listener),84</i>)

### put a shit on
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>put a shit on</i> to denigrate or deceive; eg `Look, Pat's a past master at this, he really put a shit on you, no sweat.' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,89</i>)

### put across
 
 <i>v.</i> put (a good, nasty) one across a person; to score (fair or unfair) points off: esp. put across a beauty, to execute a smart move

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Put across a beauty.</i> To execute a smart move: coll., mostly New Zealanders': from ca. 1911. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,672</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> put:<i> put me right off the tucker</i>, completely deterred me from eating the food; <i>put nasty ones across him</i>, score unfair points off him. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,302</i>)

### put across a beaut
 
 <i>phr.</i> put a trick across [also put across a beauty q.v.]

>  To be a dag at,' `to put across a beaut,' `to jerry to,' ... need no explanation. <i>(Apr 2, 1938,Press, The,18</i>) [NOTES: McNab NZ Slang]

>  I put a beaut [sc. trick] across him. <i>(1950,Landfall,13/39</i>) [NOTES: also 1950 Gaskell in 1984 Some Other Country 97]

### put across a beauty
 
 <i>phr.</i> put something across, trick (a person or persons) [1951 14 M 14 St Bede's, Chch 16 BEAUTY, COME A [have a fall] 3.]

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> Anyhow, to give you the fair dinkum guts I put across a beauty when I found the double-headed penny in the ring, and that's how I won 200 francs. <i>(1935,Slang Today,287</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>put across a beauty</i> make a smart or tricky move; eg `Sims put across a beauty with that double dummy.' c.1910. Mostly NZ. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,89</i>)

### put butter on the bacon
 
 <i>phr.</i> see cit.

>  <b>SANSOM</b> My cousin's husband ... a schoolboy at The Neck at the beginning of this century, has memories that are deep and probing. Neither is there any putting butter on the bacon with him. <i>(1982,In the Grip of an Island,63</i>)

### put down
 
 <i>v.</i> to preserve, to `bottle' [ca1910 (W.H.B. Orsman)]

>  <b>McCALLUM</b> Do you prepare and put down your own pickled onions ? <i>(1963,Half-Gallon Jar,78</i>)

>  <i>putangitangi</i> : paradise duck. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,175</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to pack down



 
 <i>v.</i> to press

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> Kane and his mates worked from 4a.m. to 9p.m. and put down seventy three bales. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,76</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to sow or establish crop or pasture.

>  My own bed [asparagus] was put down in 1907 and has had no stable or farmyard manure since. <i>(Sep 15, 1913,NZ Journal of Agriculture,160</i>)

>  <b>HAYES</b> He put down pedigree ryegrass and Mother white clover, stock cocksfoot and stock Montgomery clover, stock white clover and stock ryegrass, in large acreages, which meant concentrated harvesting. <i>(1978,From the Toss of a Coin,125</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND & TAYLOR</b> Bill liked fencing; and he soon got the knack of putting down a good paddock. <i>(1981,Sunrise on Hikurangi,81</i>)

### put in
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>put in</i> to betray or propose; eg `He put in word that Perkins had been seen twice in the pub during office hours.'/`He put in Perkins for the thankless job of secretary.' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,89</i>)

>  <i>put in ... 6. N.Z. (tr.)</i> to establish or appoint, esp. in relation to a farm: <i>he put in a manager.</i> <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,927</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1986 NZPOD; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 394]]

### put in neutral
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) to castrate.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> neutral, to put in: to castrate. The expression seems to apply especially to a litter of wild sucking pigs which musterers delight in operating on and then releasing with a view to catching up again later in the year. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,55</i>)

>  To castrate stock: to put into neutral. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

>  Dry humour runs through this collection .. to put into neutral - to castrate male lambs or calves. <i>(Nov 22, 2002,Dominion,C6</i>)

### put in the bite
 
 <i>phr.</i> to put on the pressure (for a favour)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> To request a loan of money. .in 4;`Jim put in the bite for a couple of dollars. I let him have it, but this is the last time; he's too regular.' <i>(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],894,1962 ed.</i>)

### put into neutral
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) to castrate male stock.

>  To castrate stock: to put into neutral. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

>  Dry humour runs through this collection .. to put into neutral - to castrate male lambs or calves .. . <i>(Nov 22, 2002,Dominion Post,C6</i>)

### put noses together
 
 <i>phr.</i> to hongi

>  <b>PARKINSON</b> We observed that the natives mode of salutation was by putting their noses together. <i>(Nov 19, 1769,Journal,106</i>)

### put on
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to place a person, stock, or fertiliser etc on the land.

>  If put on hard feed, these teeth easily come out and leave the animals 'gummies' in the course of a few weeks. <i>(1919,Livestock in New Zealand,15</i>)

>  Sheep and cattle should now be put on their winter blocks, keeping any animals not up to standard in a separate paddock. <i>(Apr 20, 1922,NZ Journal of Agriculture,220</i>)

>  Mares in foal should be put on flat paddocks with no drains or creeks ... <i>(Apr 20, 1922,NZ Journal of Agriculture,221</i>)

>  The young stock is put on to paddocks that become available as the lambs get away fat. <i>(May 15, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,503</i>)

>  <b>ASPINALL</b> The hoggets have to be drenched and crutched before they are put on hill blocks. <i>(1993,Farming Under Aspiring,148</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR & TROTTER</b> I put on a lot of lime in the early days. <i>(1994,From Caithness to Southland,47</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to prepare food for shearing, dog trials, stock sales, mustering or docking teams.

>  <b>FOSTER & WRIGHT</b> At Paua the ladies have put on a feed in the woolshed. <i>(1983,Stockman Country,33</i>)

### put on the acid
 
 <i>phr.</i> (as put the acid on, without object specified)

>  <b>VIRTUE</b> You get it, sonny Jim, cripple boy, hundreds. ... I'm putting on the acid, get it, should've done it ages ago. <i>(1989,Then Upon the Evil Season,33</i>)

### put on the head
 
 <i>phr.</i> to suspend (from a Union) (phs from head, the sailors' term for `privy'). [(watersiders', ?obs)]

>  The Lyttelton Stevedores' Union seems a pretty strong joint. When a member of the Union is suspended or `put on the head', as the slang term had it ... <i>(1905,NZ Truth,17-1-8</i>)

### put one around
 
 <i>phr.</i> to trick, put one across

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> I did not intend to be caught with `roughies' left in my pen when the pressure was on. A shearer will always try to put one `around' his mate when he has a full pen of sheep to pick from. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,93</i>)

### put one on (someone)
 
 <i>v.</i> to punch

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>to punch; eg `One more squeak out of you and I'll put one on ya.' ANZ. </i> <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,89</i>)

### put one on the heavy jacket
 
 <i>phr.</i> exclude, ostracise (var. of put on the coat) [(prisoners')]

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> Kapua ... had been put on the heavy jacket by the Maoris in the can, and was sitting at a table all by himself. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,198</i>)

### put out
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to move rams into a paddock with ewes at tupping season.

>  During the month of April dipping should be concluded in the South Island, and the rams should be put out. <i>(Apr 1, 1920,NZ Farmer,439</i>)

>  Rams are put out towards the end of April ... <i>(Aug 16, 1948,NZ Journal of Agriculture,176</i>)

>  The rams are put out to ensure lambing from the middle of August. <i>(Apr 1956,NZ Journal of Agriculture,16</i>)

>  <b>GASCOIGNE</b> If too many rams are put out and the ewes are not coming into 'season' in numbers then the males fight amongst themselves and there is a waste of their powers. <i>(1965,From the Top of a Hill,48</i>)

>  Putting the rams out used to be a fairly simple art. If you put your rams out around March 10, you could expect to get lambs from around August 6 on. You may be like one of our friends up the coast who was asked when he put his rams out. <i>(Feb 10, 1966,NZ Farmer,29</i>)

>  Have you decided what date you are going to put the rams out? <i>(Feb 10, 1966,NZ Farmer,36</i>)

>  We used to put the rams out on April 10 but we put it back a week this year because September's still our pinch period for feed. <i>(May 13, 1982,NZ Farmer,96</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR & TROTTER</b> One of the farmers said to me 'I suppose you'll soon be thinking of putting your rams out. ' <i>(1994,From Caithness to Southland,55</i>)

### put over
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [stock breeding] to serve a cow or ewe with a bull or ram.

>  He began by putting Cheviot rams over his Romneys and then introduced Perendale rams to the crossbreds to cement in the genetic changes. <i>(May 27, 2004,Dominion Post,C5</i>)

>  Coopworths cut 4lb of wool off (the fleece weights of progeny of) any Romney they're put over and endanger meat production by encouraging Omega grade lambs. <i>(Sep 13, 1973,NZ Farmer,9</i>)

>  A Charolais bull is put over the cows and the calves are sold at the Culverden calf sales. <i>(May 24, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,16</i>)

>  Churchill is a beefy-type Dexter and putting him over our Dexter/Jersey cross heifers will breed some of the characteristics out of their offspring. <i>(Dec 2001,Smallfarmer, The,4</i>)

### put the acid on
 
 <i>phr.</i> (informal) [AND 1906: possibly f. acid test of gold.] to make a sharp (or final) demand on; to put the bite, juice on (a person for money, favours, etc). Cf. hard word.

>  Put the Acid On [title of a story of fake gold jewelry with a pun on testing it with acid aqua fortis, and puttingthe pressure on buyers with high-pressure sales tactics] <i>(Feb 24, 1906,NZ Truth,36</i>)

>  Gibson replied that both Golding and Goram were putting the acid on to a drunk, or a half-drunk, in the bar, other-wise `telling him a tale.' <i>(Jul 23, 1910,NZ Truth,265/6/1</i>)

>  <b>BAILEY & ROTH</b> But the bloke that puts the acid on/ Got up and shouted : `Bosh!...' <i>(Oct 1918,Shanties,116</i>) [NOTES: NZ Watersider (`The Mixer')[also c1926 Transport Workers' Song Book 141]]

>  `Too much acid,' Bill roared, `I'm telling you, you didn't put the acid on her enough.' <i>(Feb 10, 1920,Quick March,31</i>)

>  To put `the nips in' and `the acid on' are clear enough. <i>(Apr 2, 1938,Press, The,18</i>) [NOTES: McNab NZ Slang]

>  <b>CRUMP</b> A passing fisherman called in for a brew of tea and I put the acid on him for his spare spark plug. <i>(1960,Good Keen Man, A,93</i>)

>  <b>HELMER</b> You could have knocked me down with a flagon when my mate Helmer put the acid on me for my opinion of a book he'd written about the time he spent in New Zealand with us. <i>(1964,Stag Party,5</i>)

>  put the acid on, hard word on, the nips in (Sl Phr.) ask for a loan or favour. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),16</i>)

>  <b>MORRIESON</b> Instead he puts the acid on the Bramwells and they slung some gold to keep his mouth shut. <i>(1974,Predicament,73</i>) [NOTES: 1981 edition]

>  ENGLAND skipper Bob Willis yesterday put the acid on his batsmen to perform in the remaining two days of the third cricket test with New Zealand at Eden Park. <i>(Feb 14, 1984,Dominion,22</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> acid, phr. to put the acid on to demand something; derived from `the acid test'; eg `I see this new union joker's putting the acid on Lange and Douglas for more jobs.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,9</i>)

>  <b>CHERRILL</b> But although you had tickets and no mistake on Ju. at the time you didn't forget to put the acid on him proper afterwards. <i>(1950,Sheepfarm,99</i>)

>  <b>VIRTUE</b> You get it, sonny Jim, cripple boy, hundreds. ... I'm putting on the acid, get it, should've done it ages ago. <i>(1989,Then Upon the Evil Season,33</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>phr.</i> to treat acidly, to snub; ??to brush (a person) off (possibly confused with Brit. come to the acid to speakin an unpleasant or sarcastic way).

>  <b>CHERRILL</b> But although you had tickets and no mistake on Ju. at the time you didn't forget to put the acid on him proper afterwards. <i>(1950,Sheepfarm,99</i>)

### put the back up
 
<b>1</b> <i>phr.</i> (Rural) [saddle horses] to buck.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> put the back up: to buck. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,64</i>)

### put the bite on
 
 <i>phr.</i> to borrow from; to pressure (a person) for money etc.

>  <b>IRELAND</b> 'I'm as good as broke and I'd just come all the way back from England; and what's the first thing that happens?' my father says to me years later. 'A homey comes up and puts the bite on me.' <i>(1998,Under the Bridge & Over the Moon,41</i>)

>  Lola comes up and tries to put the bite on me because I am a stranger. <i>(1951,Landfall,17/21</i>) [NOTES: (Middleton)]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> So then it was Australia ... ending up door to door with an unopened attache case trying to put the bite on housewives. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,38</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> Tony would have the kale. But he did not like putting the bite on Tony. <i>(1965,Horse,43,1982 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Quick and the Dead)]

>  `You'd have more show trying to put the bite on the Pope.' <i>(1966,NZ Short Stories,131,1976 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Middleton `Doss House')]

>  He's come down to put the bite on me for a few thousand. <i>(1967,NZ Short Stories III,153</i>) [NOTES: Dibble `In It']

>  <b>BAXTER</b> [a down-and-out speaks] Who's going to put the bite on - who'll ... <i>(1982,Collected Plays,104,1968 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Band Rotunda)]

>  <b>68</b> From him I learned ... that it was smart to drop your swag in small towns and put the bite on the butcher, baker and grocer. <i>(1974,Loner, The</i>)

### put the boot in
 
 <i>phr.</i> to kick a prostrate foe, to kick (when down). [ca1951 R. Barraclough (Wellington) `especially in a fight, to use ungentlemanly tactics'.]

>  They have no notion of what is called British fair play, but like many Britons of similar disposition, such as Lancashire miners and Australian larrikins, the moment a man is down they put the boot into him and so doing willingly incur the risk of committing brutal murder. <i>(Nov 24, 1906,NZ Truth,75/5/2</i>)

>  A youth named Albert Beaumont was charged with inciting Helmling to resist the arresting copper. According to McCowan and Detective Osborn, he remarked, with vehemence, `Put in the boot low down; don't go any further.' <i>(Aug 26, 1911,NZ Truth,322/5/8</i>)

>  Raymond attacked him `putting the boot in'. <i>(Mar 3, 1953,NZ Truth,9</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> `Sonny's dead for ages. Flashing his roll after the shearing season. At a party or something. Some pakehas got him outside and into him with the boot.' <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,205</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> And then he dropped two of the blokes who butted in to stop him putting the boot into Ted while he was on the ground. <i>(1971,Bastards I Have Met,19</i>)

>  <i>to put the boot in</i>: to play rough or unfairly (rugby) <i>(1980,Lynfield College, Auckland</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>boot</i> in phrs. <i>put in the boot/put the boot in</i> to kick brutally, literally or metaphorically; eg `He put the boot into Happy's chances of becoming matron by spreading the old rumour about her youthful experimant with dope.' Mostly NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,18</i>)

>  His helmet was off, his baton had been taken from him, and the crowd was in the mood to put the boot in. <i>(May 1987,Metro,146</i>)

>  <b>CRANE</b> The police had found him sleeping out in one of the city gardens and had `put the boot in'. <i>(1986,I Can Do No Other,100</i>)



 
 <i>phr.</i> to treat unfairly; to attack a disadvantaged opponent

>  All of them keeping the worker down, by putting in the boot. <i>(1926,Transport Workers' Song Book,107</i>) [NOTES: [fig use]]

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> ... she had done him dirty. She had `put in the boot.' <i>(1929,Riven,293</i>)

>  <b>part</b> [The] following [was] employed by those who served in the [Great] War ... <i>put the boot in,</i> to take an unfair advantage; <i>Ibid.</i> 287 In London, for preference; there I can have a mag to a tabby, or if any of you fellows put the boot into me in any way, I'll parade sick and join Colonel Peerless's Light Infantry at the base. <i>(1935,Slang Today,286</i>)

>  <b>LEE</b> <i>(Dec 10, 1980,John A. Lee Diaries,50,1937 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> ... all the half-pie pannikin bosses ordering me about because there's no-one like the working man for putting the boot into the working man. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,227</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>boot</i>: can be used for the things you hike in, and there is an expression `to put the boot in' which means metaphorically to kick someone, as `then he really put the boot in and sent him down the road' (fired him); or gave him the boot. How ever, the commonest use of boot refers to what you would call the TRUNK of your car. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,16</i>)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> To aim a blow at the morale or the material position of him who is already at a loss. .in 4;`He's dropped the ball. The mug! Oh the useless bleedin' mug! They'd be better off without him.' `Well, he's not going too good today, but there's no need to stick in the boot. You didn't say that last Saturday when he went like a train.' -->to PUT THE BOOT IN means the same thing. <i>(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],90 [987]</i>) [NOTES: (1962-63) 1974 Syder and Hodgetts Aust and NZ English [TS] 90 [987]]

>  Lois puts boot into Union [heading] Rugby enthusiast Lois Henare is claiming foul play by one of the country's leading unions for selling expensive match tickets. <i>(Sep 14, 1986,Sunday News,29</i>)

>  In full evangelistic cry Marcus is likely to shout `hallelujah', and urge his audience to sink the boot into Satan. <i>(Oct 8, 1972,Sunday Times (NZ),2/7</i>)

### put the capper on
 
 <i>phr.</i> to put the finishing stroke or an end to (infreq.: cf. the more usual put the lid on, put the stopper on).

>  Mac's insinuation put the `capper' on it and fetched them inside. <i>(1905,Bush Boys of NZ</i>)

### put the fangs into
 
 <i>phr.</i> to press for a loan or handout; to put the bite on.

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> To put the fangs into. To request a loan of money or a donation. i .in 4;`Don't give him anything. He's always putting the fangs into someone.' <i>(1962,Half Caste,336,1974 ed.</i>)

### put the hard word on
 
 <i>phr.</i> to ask a favour or a loan; (to a woman, or homosexual) to ask for sexual favours; cf. put the bite on. [ca1949 (Wellington) The way they're wooing in the bedroom looks like he'll soon be putting the hard word across her.]

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> .. he thinks she is putting the `hard word' on him <i>(1927,Old Savage,144</i>)

>  <b>PARK</b> [in a context of an assumed US accent] ... a cinch to get into the big-time. But what I calculate you oughta do, bo, is to see this Thrush of Erin when he drops into this burg, and put the hard word on him [to help you]. Yeah, bo. <i>(1951,Witch's Thorn, The,24</i>)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> A request for a loan of money. .in 4;`So I said to him, `Well, what you want is enough cashto pay the deposit, and you'd be right from there on. Why don't you put the hard word on your father-on-law? I think he'd lend you that much without a murmur.' <i>(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],890</i>)

>  (phrase) put the hard word on, to touch for a loan or favour. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),617</i>)

>  hard word n. firm request, demand; specific amatory proposal. <i>Ibid.</i> 16 put theacid on, hard word on, the nips in (Sl Phr.) ask for a loan or favour. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),10</i>)

>  I put the hard word on the man I believed to be the father and he arranged to get some stuff to take. <i>(Oct 17, 1972,NZ Truth,7/1</i>)

>  <b>MANTELL</b> `Difficulty was finding somewhere to live ... When he got back, he put the hard word on Siggy. Fixed that, too.' <i>(1978,Murder in Fancydress,134</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> hard word, put the: see PUT THE. . . put the hard word on: To INSIST that a difficult DECISION be MADE. Usually used in the context of pressuring someone to grant sexual favours or, `put their money where their mouth is' in the case of a business deal or an offer of marriage. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,83</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> So Morrie put the hard word on Hank [ ...who] hadn't the heart to refuse. - <i>(1982,Wild Pig,177</i>)

>  Ormsby spotted them as he headed back to Turangi after helping one of nine stranded trucks to get underway. ... He gave the driver the hard word and told him to head back to Rangipo, and take State Highway 4, which was still open. <i>(Jun 17, 1986,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  put the hard word on NZ & Austral. ask (person) for loan or (woman) for sexual favours. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,342</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 225]

>  I hummed and haa-ed for a couple of weeks [about having a cancer operation], then I really got the hard word [from the doctor]. <i>(Dec 24, 1987,Dominion,6</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> hard word, the demand, often for sex, often phr. put the hard word on; eg `Jimmy had been putting the hard word on Helen for years, but she wouldn't come across. It wasn't as if she had them queuing up outside her door, but she had her standards.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,55</i>)

>  <b>Owen Marshall</b> Michael told Rik that he'd thought he'd take in mainly women as flatmates, because if they got a bit behind in payments then he could put the hard word on them. <i>(2005,Watch of Gryphons,69</i>)

### put the needle in
 
 <i>phr.</i> to put the acid/juice on.

>  When one of our clients puts the needle in we have to do his job before outside work. <i>(Jun 1953,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: H.S. Gajadhar quoting a Hutt Timber and Hardware Co. contract builder]

### put the nips in(to)
 
 <i>phr.</i> to ask a favour; to ask for a loan (of money); (cf. put the acid on, touch)

>  I put the nips into one the other night 'n he come to light with a couple o' dener <i>(Sep 19, 1917,NZEF Chronicles,63</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> I put the nips into the fellow in charge for a feed <i>(1935,Slang Today,564</i>) [NOTES: A.E. Strong WW1 scene in Partridge SlangToday]

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> Nips in(to), put the. To ask a loan (from a person): Australian and New Zealand: from ca. 1908. Cf. sting, q.v., and put the hard word on. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,564</i>)

>  To put `the nips in' and `the acid on' are clear enough. <i>(Apr 2, 1938,Press, The,18</i>) [NOTES: McNab NZ Slang]

>  <b>PEARSON</b> <i>(1963,Coal Flat,190</i>)

>  Put the acid on, hard word on, the nips in (Sl Phr.) ask for a loan or favour. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),16</i>)

>  nip ... 11. put the nips in (to). Austral. & N.Z. sl. to try to borrow money (from). <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,761</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 507; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 328]]

>  <b>McGILL</b> put the nips in/squeeze on seek or extract money or a favour; eg `Watch out, lads, Badger's doing the rounds again putting the nips in/squeeze on.' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,89</i>)

>  And it's anybody's guess what Japanese tourists make of such Kiwisms [sic] as `She's apples `put the nips in' `feeling crook' or `give him some curry'. <i>(1989,Listener (New Zealand Listener),13</i>)

### put the red collar on
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) to condemn or slit the throat of an animal.

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> I've heard a head shepherd say to a man "go down to the shed and put the red collar on those two dog tuckers in the yard." He meant the man to cut the throats of the sheep and skin them for the dogs. <i>(1951,NZ High Country,42</i>)

### put the set on
 
 <i>n.</i> to straighten a person up; to set a person to rights (See [set](sa-sho.md#set))

>  <b>COTTLE</b> It took the parson to `put the set on him' <i>(1891,Frank Melton's Luck,147</i>) [NOTES: -]

### put the wood in the hole
 
 <i>phr.</i> to close the door

>  <i> to put the wood in the hole</i>: to shut the door <i>(1980,Lynfield College, Auckland</i>) [NOTES: <i> to put the wood in the hole</i>: to shut the door]

### put through
 
 <i>v.</i> to complete a milking (of a dairy herd) [dairy farming, milking]

>  Mr Linn expects to put through a herd of 100 Jersey cows in ninety minutes. <i>(Oct 12, 1909,Hawera & Normanby Star,4</i>)

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> Being but an indifferent milker it used to take me quite three hours ... to put my herd through. <i>(1924,Follow the Call,9,1975 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> sc. proceedings for bankruptcy [legal process]

>  Former top telly cop, Don Selwyn, has been adjudged bankrupt over an alleged $8000 maintenance bill. The actor who starred in Mortimer's Patch as the popular Sergeant Bob Storey was `put through' on July 2 on an application by Auckland lawyer, Pauline Kingi. <i>(Jul 20, 1986,Sunday News,18</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to process stock through a dip, drafting yards, shearing board. [cattle, dairy, sheep farming; ie any animal stock]

>  The number of sheep and lambs put through the Waingawa freezing works for the past year was 260,957, which was an increase of about 6000 on the previous year. <i>(Jul 4, 1914,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  [Photo caption] Interior of the Slaughterhouse, In which 1000 sheep a day is often put through. <i>(Aug 7, 1901,Otago Witness,40</i>)

>  The Undersigned having enlarged and improved his Plant for "Boiling Down," begs to intimate to Sheepowners that he is now a PURCHASER OF FAT OR LEAN SHEEP FOR BOILING DOWN PURPOSES, or will "put through" Sheep for the owners on terms which may be ascertained on application. <i>(Mar 17, 1887,Southland Times,1</i>)

>  Principally hoggets have been put through the sheds, but in a couple of weeks shearing will be general throughout the district. <i>(Nov 21, 1902,Bruce Herald,5</i>)

>  Put about 5000 sheep through the yards of which nearly 3000 must have been ewes. <i>(Mar 8, 1878,Chudleigh Diary,267,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>AYSON</b> They got a good muster of cattle, took them to the yards, put them through, kept those that were to be tamed in the paddock, and let the rest out in a mob, each to find its way to its own beat. <i>(1937,Thomas,86</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> He had been overseas and he had been up and down the island picking up jobs ... Crayfishing and scrubcutting. Contract fencing. Poisoning rabbits. Even collecting agar. On the chain at Whakatu [166] when they put through 13,000 lambs a day. Share-milking for a while. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,165</i>)

>  An effective remedy that is being used by some farmers is a bath of sheep dip. After being put through this, the sheep are run into a pen that has been well sprinkled with dry unslaked lime, the mosture [sic] on the sheep's feet causing it to slacken and effect a cure in conjunction with the dip. <i>(Jul 12, 1909,Feilding Star,1</i>)

>  <b>PEAT</b> .. he is putting sheep through every bit as quick as the others ... <i>(1992,Falcon and the Lark, The,50</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to mate or tup. [sheep breeding]

>  <b>TAIT</b> One cross of Lincoln has been put through the ewe flock. <i>(1956,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol I,22</i>)

### put to bed with a shovel
 
 <i>phr.</i> to bury

>  <b>SANSOM</b> I can hear my grandfather saying; `Do something. Don't give in or the next thing you know we'll be putting him to bed with a shovel.' <i>(1982,In the Grip of an Island,100</i>)

### put up
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to prepare a paddock lunch or crib.

>  <b>HOSKEN</b> No-one had mentioned lunches to me and I had none put up. <i>(1968,Turn Back the Clock,52</i>)

>  <b>SPENCER</b> Eva, Nancy and Jean decided to accompany Alick and put up lunch to enjoy a picnic at some chosen place. <i>(1992,Waioeka Pioneering Saga,114</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to hold up and rob, to stick up

>  <b>BURGESS</b> While we were at the township [in Otago, c1862 March], one of our acquaintances came to me and told me there was a man in a disreputable shanty, that had plenty of money and would I come and put him up? Now the Wetherstone diggings were very quiet, so that I objected to men being put up and let go to alarm the place - for I anticipated robbing this banker sooner or later, and that would do me. <i>(1866,Confessions,76,1983 ed.</i>)

>  Sullivan [the bushranger] said, `Here's a young fellow and a woman carrying a swag; I'll put them up.' <i>Ibid.</i> 18 `I'll put them [the victims] up and you give me your gun while you tie them.' <i>(1866,Maungatapu Murders,17</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to have up (before court), to charge with an offence

>  <b>SARGESON</b> He's been sacked and put up for it. <i>(1945,When the Wind Blows,75</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> Anyhow he'd been sacked and put up for it, and he'd only got six months probation, but there'd been a bit in the newspaper. <i>(1949,I Saw in My Dream,67,1974 ed.</i>)

### put up (a) smoke
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) [mustering] to light a tussock to signal readiness to muster off or to signal one's whereabouts.

>  [?1951 ECR OEDS] <i>Put up a smoke.</i>- To light a snowgrass bush, etc., as a signal when mustering. <i>(Nov 18, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> put up smoke: musterer's means of signalling his whereabouts. For example, the top-beat man would set light to a tussock to indicate that he was in position and the muster could begin. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,64</i>)

### put up a blue
 
 <i>phr.</i> to make a mistake

>  <b>CHARLES</b> Young Bradshaw's the boy who is flying this plane,/ She is as full as a tick and it's pouring with rain./ Things are not going good - his donk's on the blink* -/ Then he puts up a blue and she dives in the drink./ [fn] *his donk's on the blink: his engine is giving trouble (airman's slang) [Charles had served in the RNZAF in WW2] <i>(1981,Black Billy Tea,89</i>)

### put up a run
 
<b>1</b> <i>phr.</i> (Rural) [mustering and trial dogs] to hunt or head sheep.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Rock had put up a run in which he fairly excelled himself ... <i>(1947,Wayleggo,27</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> At Lake Coleridge also I witnessed the most extraordinary run I have known a dog to put up. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,50</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> During that trip Flirt put up a run that was the equal of any head I have seen. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,61</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> But for the fact that his out-run was excessively wide he put up a good run ... <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,195</i>)

### put-in
 
 <i>n.</i> (Sport) entry point on a river (in kayaking)

>  With a few eager multisporters and newer paddlers keen to experience some grade 2-3 whitewater, we thought that a weekend at the Raukawa Adventure Centre (RAC) would be ideal. Most of us headed up on Friday night for an early start on Saturday. So, by about 9 am our team was assembling at the middle put-in for the Whangaehu River. <i>(2005,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.huttkayakers.org.nz/tripIndex.php*Accessed 17/04/2006. No date for site construction or posting.]

>  Craig figured that we should try a new put-in, on the little known Makotuku River... <i>(2005,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.huttkayakers.org.nz/tripIndex.php *Accessed 17/04/06. No date for site construction or posting.]

### putaitai
 
 <i>n.</i> spoon-bill duck

>  <b>BULLER</b> SPATULA {RHYNCAPSIS} VARIEGATA. (NEW-ZEALAND SHOVELLER.) ... <i>Native name</i>. Tete, {Pateke,} Putaitai, Kuriwhengi, {Kuruwhengu, Papaungungu,} Kahoho, and Wetawetangu; `Spoon-bill Duck' of the colonists. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,252,1888 2nd edn 2/269 ed.</i>)

### putakitaki
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [putangitangi](pop-pyo.md#putangitangi))

### putangi
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [putangitangi](pop-pyo.md#putangitangi))

>  <b>HAY</b> The species we have identified in the Kaipara and Hokianga are - the Putangi, or `paradise-duck' (<i>Casarca variegata</i>); the grey duck, or Parera (<i>Anas superciliosa</i>); the brown duck, an allied species or variety of the last; the Papanga, or `teal,' or `widgeon' (<i>Fuligula N.Z.</i>), and some other varieties that may be imported birds, or crosses, or other native species. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/225</i>)

### putangitangi
 
 <i>n.</i> paradise duck (See [also paradise duck](../A#also-paradise-duck))

>  Both sanctuaries in the Botanical andWoodhaugh Gardens are live withwaterfowl, the grey duck (parera) beingespecially numerous though there are alsothe Paradise Duck (Putangitangi). <i>(Aug 16, 1933,NZ Tablet,27</i>)

>  There are many of the duck tribe, and amongst them the paradise duck (putangitangi) is particularly abundant. <i>(Aug 27, 1842,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,3</i>)

>  I was bathing the other morning in a small river when a brood of young putangitangi rushed impetuously from the thick toitoi bushes on the water margin and splashed in close beside me. <i>(Jan 26, 1865,Wellington Independent,3</i>)

>  Henry left at 6.0 in his yacht, the Putangi. <i>(Mar 6, 1890,Otago Witness,35</i>)

>  The hunters caught poaka (wild pigs), parera (grey duck), putakitaki (paradise duck), kereru or kuku (pigeon), and hurepo (bittern). <i>(1988,Tales of Pioneer Women,249</i>) [NOTES: Written of 1940]

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> 59. Casarca variegata. ... Pooa dugghie dugghie of the natives of Dusky Bay. <i>Forst.</i> Putangi tangi of the natives of Cook's Strait; Paradise Duck of the settlers. <i>Dieffenbach. </i> <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/198</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/198]

>  <b>SELWYN</b> Walked along shingly beach ... to a large pool by the side of the sea, in which were swimming a number of Putangitangi, or Paradise Ducks, ... this being the season of their moulting. <i>(Jan 16, 1844,Journal,NZ Part III (Church in the Colonies VIII) 12</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Putangitangi - Paradise duck <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/381</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> In the afternoon, we ... were fortunate enough to shoot some `putangitangi' for supper. This bird has obtained the name of paradise duck, from the beauty of its plumage: ... <i>(Jan 15, 1844,Southern Districts of NZ,217</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> <i>(1845,Advance NZ,1/57</i>) [NOTES: [take in quot p. 340 under Paradise duck]]

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> ... the <i>paradise duck</i>, or <i>Pu-tangi-tangi, (Caesarea variegata,)</i> is remarkable for its size, for the beauty of its plumage, and the delicacy of its flesh. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,164</i>)

>  <b>McLEAN</b> We caught some young <i>putangitangi</i> or Paradise ducks. <i>(Dec 6, 1850,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1850 Dec 6 Mclean Diary in 1940 Cowan Sir Donald Maclean 59]

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> PUTAKITAKI<i> (carsarca variegata),;a duck with beautiful plumage, rather larger than the common grey variety. It is very common in the south of New Zealand, and is generally known as the paradise duck. </i> <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,312</i>)

>  CASARCA VARIEGATA, Gml. Putangitangi. Paradise Duck. <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/71</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

>  <b>BULLER</b> CASARCA VARIEGATA. (NEW ZEALAND SHELDRAKE.) ... <i>Native names.</i> Putangitangi; Putakitaki in the South Island; `Paradise Duck' of the colonists. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,241,1888 2nd edn 2/264 ed.</i>)

>  Putakitaki - the celebrated paradise duck, once abundant. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,64</i>) [NOTES: c1880 South Island Maori Lists `Wahi Mahika Kai' in 1945 Beattie Maori Place-names of Canterbury 64]

>  <i>Casarca variegata</i>. - Putangitangi, Paradise Duck. Both Islands. <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/113</i>) [NOTES: (Martin)]

>  <b>WILSON</b> ... her precious Paradise ducks [fn Maori <i>Putangi tangi</i>] had discovered that their wings ... were now strong enough for flight ... <i>(1894,In the Land of the Tui,176</i>) [NOTES: 1891 Mar 9 in 1894 Wilson Land of the Tui 176]

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> The paradise duck (<i>putangi-tangi</i>: Casarca variegata) a bird of beautiful plumage, but not of such flavour as the grey duck or teal, was considered a prize. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,105</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> The town [Nelson] itself is sweet ...; there is a pretty park ... with a ... lakelet alive with swan and <i>putangitangi</i> or paradise duck; ... <i>(1926,Travel in NZ,(Vol II South Island) 14</i>)

>  <i>putangitangi</i> a bird (Casarca variegata), a goose but called the paradise-duck; always found in pairs. <i>(Jun 1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/157</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>POWELL</b> PARADISE DUCK (<i>Casarca variegate</i>), Putangitangi of the Maoris, is a handsome bird variously mottled, freckled and lined with brown and white on a brownish to black ground. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,80</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> PARADISE DUCK <i>Tadorna variegata Other names</i>: Putangitangi, Rangitata Goose. <i>Description</i>: 25' Of intermediate size between the ducks and the larger species (Canada Goose and swans). <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,87</i>)

>  <i>107. Tadorna variegata</i> (Gmelin, 1789) <i>Paradise [Shel]duck</i> (Putangitangi) <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,37,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Paradise Shelduck Tadorna variegata </i>(GMELIN, 1789) ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Paradise duck, pari, putangitangi. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,142</i>)

>  <b>MIKAERE</b> There were putakitaki (paradise ducks), parera (grey ducks), tataa (brown ducks) and whio (blue ducks). ... The pukeko (swamphen) were caught only as a last resort, because their flesh was stringy and tough. <i>(1988,Te Maiharoa,75</i>)

>  The native name of this duck, "putangitangi," is said to have reference to the call, which is likened to a lament. <i>(Jan 15, 1910,Evening Post,13</i>)

>  <b>HULME & MORRISON</b> hear the tolling of putangitangiatoa the paradise shelduck trumpeting to its ever-faithful mate <i>(1989,Homeplaces,109</i>)

>  The native name [sc. of the paradise duck] is Putangi-tangi, which means the waterfowl that makes a great funeral crying - and this she does to perfection with her loud clarionet vocal. <i>(Nov 20, 1890,Otago Daily Times,41</i>)

### putaputa
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [putaputaweta](pop-pyo.md#putaputaweta),[marbleleaf](../M#marbleleaf))

>  <b>FEATON</b> Piripiriwhata [Maori name] .. Puta-puta [Settlers' name] .. Carpodetus serratus .. Shrub <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,178</i>)

>  [Native name] Putaputa-weta .. [Family] Saxifrageae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,122</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 122]

### putaputaweta
 
 <i>n.</i> a small tree with a marbled leaf, also called marbleleaf, puna-weta [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>FEATON</b> CARPODETUS SERRATUS (|Forst.) ... <i>The `PUTA-PUTA-WETA'.- This graceful looking shrub, or sometimes small tree, is found in both the Northern and Middle Islands. </i> <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,133</i>)

>  Putaputa-weta (piripiriwhata) .. Carpodetus serratus .. Timber white and open of grain; ... <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,13</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>C[arpodetus] serratus, Forst. ... Piripiriwhata; Putaputawheta. </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,137</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Carpodetus serratus (The Serrate Carpodetus)</i>... The native name <i>Puta-puta-weta</i> signifies full of weta holes. In the North Island trees of this species are rarely cut down unperforated by longitudinal insect galleries. Another name by which it is known is <i>Puna-weta</i>. In the Urewera country it is called <i>Kai-weta</i> (i.e., weta food). [That ugly innocent the weta (<i>Deinacrida</i>) has thus been accused of perforating the tree; but as a matter of fact though often used as convenient residences [195] by the weta, the holes are made by the caterpillar of the large moth <i>Hepialus virescens</i>... ]. <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,188,1940 p. 194 ed.</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> The putaputaweta (<i>Carpodetus serratus</i>) is a rival of the English may. <i>(1910,NZ Plants,37</i>)

>  PUTAPUTAWETA/ (Marble Leaf)/ <i>Carpodetus serratus</i>/ ... Leaves have a marbled effect. <i>(1982,Field Guide to Common NZ Trees & Shrubs,14</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>CARPODETUS SERRATUS, putaputaweta, marble-leaf. </i>... It is one of the species grouped by foresters under the slightly opprobrious term `scrub hardwoods'. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,78</i>)

>  352 Lithograph of putaputaweta (piripiriwhata) foliage. <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,22</i>)

>  ...the drive back to our base at Okuru is made more pleasant by avenues of flowering kamahi and the occasional sweet-smelling putaputaweta. <i>(Feb 8, 2003,Dominion Post,F12</i>)

>  PUTAPUTAWTA or marble leaf (carpodetus serratus) is a handsome small tree with fabulous floiage and fragreant flowers. Much of it's beauty is in the mottled appearance of its leaves, the veins of which are a darker green than the leaf blade, hence the English common name of marble leaf. <i>(Jan 15, 2005,Dominion Post,E13</i>)

>  In exchange they were give [sic] a choice of native plants such as tarata, karamu, totara, kohuhu, broadleaf, mingimingi, putaputaweta, harakeke (flax) and cabbage tree. <i>(Dec 8, 2005,Wellingtonian,14</i>)

### putoto
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Porzana tabuensis plumbea</i>, spotless crake (swamp rail)



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Porzana tabuensis plumbea</i>, spotless crake (swamp rail)

>  Put|oto, <i>s</i>. A bird so called; a partridge. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,198</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> <i>Putoto</i>- A small black bird, about the size of the thrush, found in the swamps of New Zealand ... <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),67</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> 48. <i>Charadrius obscurus. Gmel.</i> ... Ha-poko-wera of the natives of Dusky Bay. <i>Forst</i> Tuturiwatu of the natives. <i>Yate. </i>To this order may also be referred two other birds spoken of by Mr. Yate under the names of Pukunui, Pututo. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/195</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/195]

>  <b>BULLER</b> ORTYGOMETRA TABUENSIS. (SWAMP-CRAKE.) ... <i>Native names.</i>- Pueto and Putoto. [in 1888 replaced with SWAMP-RAIL q.v.] <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,181</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> ORTYGOMETRA TABUENSIS. (SWAMP-RAIL.) ... <i>Native names.</i>- Pueto and Putoto. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,2/101</i>)

>  <i>Ortygometra tabuensis</i>. - Putoto, Swamp Crake. Both Islands; Australia, Tasmania, Polynesia. Sparingly dispersed throughout New Zealand. <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/117</i>) [NOTES: (Martin)]

>  <b>HAY</b> The Putoto (<i>Ortygometra Tabuensis</i>) is a crake, often confounded by settlers with the patatai. It is a smaller bird altogether, having partridge tints on the back, and a grey breast. It chiefly inhabits raupo swamps. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/225</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>P[orzana] tabuensis</i> ... Swamp Rail. PUTOTO. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,79</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Putoto</i> (iii), n. <i>Porzana tabuensis plumbea, spotless crake (swamp rail)</i>; a bird. <i>(1959,Dictionary of the Maori Language,317</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> SPOTLESS CRAKE <i>Porzana tabuensis Other names</i>: Sooty Rail, Putoto, Puweto. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,105</i>)

>  <i>Spotless Crake Porzana tabuensis plumbea </i>(Gray, 1829) ...OTHER NAMES: <i>Putoto, puweto. ... Widespread, more common in the North Island, but rarely seen. Also occurs in Australia. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,167</i>)
